Classification of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one I. Height 1 and 2, II. Descendants with elliptic boundaries Thanks: This project was funded by the National Science Centre, Poland, grant no. 2021/41/B/ST1/02062. For the purpose of Open Access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright license to any Author Accepted Manuscript version arising from this submission.
Karol Palka, Tomasz Pełka

Abstract
. This is the first article in a series aimed at classifying normal del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank one over algebraically closed fields of arbitrary characteristic up to an isomorphism. Our guiding invariant is the height of a del Pezzo surface, defined as the minimal intersection number of the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution and a fiber of some ℙ 1 \mathbb{P}^{1} -fibration. The geometry of del Pezzo surfaces gets more constrained as the height grows; in characteristic 0 0 no example of height bigger than 4 4 is known. In this article, we classify del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank one and height at most 2 2 ; in particular we describe the non-log terminal ones. We also describe a natural class of del Pezzo surfaces which have descendants with elliptic boundary , i.e. whose minimal resolution has a birational morphism onto a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one mapping the exceptional divisor to an anti-canonical curve.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14J10; 14D06, 14J45, 14R05

1.
Introduction

A normal surface
\overline{X}
is
del Pezzo
if its anti-canonical divisor
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. This is the first article in a series aimed at a complete classification of del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank one over any algebraically closed field
{k}
up to an isomorphism. We will provide an explicit construction of every such surface; and a list of singularity types of all log canonical ones. For each log canonical type, we will compute the number of non-isomorphic del Pezzo surfaces realizing it, or, in case this number is infinite, their moduli dimension, see Definition
1.9
(d)
. Here, by the
singularity type
we mean the weighted graph of the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution, see Section
2A
. Non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces are described in Proposition
B
.

Del Pezzo surfaces are possible outcomes of the two-dimensional Minimal Model Program, and as such they play an important role in the birational classification of varieties. They were investigated by many authors from various points of view. For example, in case
{k}=\mathbb{C}
, Keel–McKernan
[
KM99
]
proved that that the smooth locus of any log terminal del Pezzo surface is dominated by images of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and Gurjar–Zhang
[
GZ94
]
showed that its fundamental group is finite.

The proof of Keel–McKernan’s result provides in fact a description of
“all but a bounded family”
of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one. This description was recently completed by Lacini
[
Lac24
]
in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
, who arranged all log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one into 24 series. Nonetheless, these series are non-disjoint and rather broad, hence questions such as the existence of a del Pezzo surface of rank one with prescribed singularities are still not easy to answer. In general the problem of uniqueness remained untouched.
Many other partial classification results were achieved by various authors, see e.g.
[
Zha89
,
Nak07
,
Koj03
,
LS23
,
Bel24
]
. We compare some of them with our classification in Section
7
.

1A.
The height and the classification program

In a series of articles we pursue a new approach, which gives a new geometric insight into the structure of del Pezzo surfaces. Our guiding invariant is the
height
.

Definition 1.1

(The height)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal projective surface and
\overline{D}
a reduced Weil divisor on it. Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. The
height
of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, denoted by
0pt(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, is the infimum of the set of integers
{h}
such that
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D={h}
. We put
0pt(\overline{X})\coloneqq 0pt(\overline{X},0)
.

We note that the height of a normal surface is finite only if
\overline{X}
has negative Kodaira dimension; in particular the height of a singular del Pezzo surface is finite and nonzero, see Remark
2.5
. The key property which makes the height important is that, contrary to other invariants (like the Fano index), it is bounded. This has been observed by the first author several years ago, who, to his surprise, noticed that the bound seems to be very small, and proposed a classification program based of studying the

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations realizing the infimum in Definition
1.1
. The following result will be proved in a forthcoming article.

Conjecture 1.2

(Palka)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a singular del Pezzo surface.
If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
then
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 4
.

The exceptions with
0pt(\overline{X})>4
in
\operatorname{char}{k}=2,3
, cf. Theorem
E
(f)
, are related to the exceptional geometry of canonical del Pezzo surfaces in low characteristics, and will be classified. Crucially, the geometry of del Pezzo surfaces gets more constrained as the height increases, and our knowledge on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations gives strong control over the configurations of possible degenerate fibers and various auxiliary curves, including the cases of low field characteristics. For this reason the height is a natural uniform invariant for the purpose of classification.

In
[
PP24
]
we have already shown that for singular
open
del Pezzo surfaces, i.e. the ones admitting a nonzero reduced boundary
\overline{D}
such that
-K_{\overline{X}}-\overline{D}
remains ample, the height of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, and hence of
\overline{X}
, is bounded by
2
, with some exceptions if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2,3,5
. In this article we obtain the following results.

•

In Theorem
A
we describe all normal del Pezzo surfaces of rank 1 and height at most
2
.

•

In Proposition
B
we show that with some exceptions if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2,3,5
the above class contains all non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
, and we describe their structure.

•

In Propositions
C
and
D
we discuss the issues of uniqueness, rigidity, and moduli for log canonical ones.

•

In Theorem
E
we describe a notable class of del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
which can be constructed directly from canonical ones and singular members of anticanonical linear systems of the latter.

The classification results we achieve are complete, in the sense that we not only concentrate on studying possible singularities of del Pezzo surfaces, but we also carefully control their constructions. This allows us to discuss moduli of surfaces of fixed singularity type or to prove their uniqueness up to an isomorphism.

We warn the reader that the price paid for including in the analysis fields of low characteristic, especially
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, are additional calculations, which sometimes become tedious. There seems to be no royal road here, as the resulting classification contains many exotic cases, see Tables
9
–
14
.

1B.
Del Pezzo surfaces of height at most two

The main part of this article treats the case
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
, so
our
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations are relatively simple, but flexible enough to produce a lot of examples, sometimes with moduli.
Describing their degenerate fibers allows us to arrange the minimal log resolutions of all del Pezzo surfaces of rank 1 and height at most 2 into series whose subsequent elements are connected by
birational transformations called
elementary vertical swaps
.

Definition 1.3

(Swap, see Figure
1
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface, let
D
be a reduced divisor on
X
and let
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

(a)

An
elementary swap
(respectively, an
elementary swap over
B
) is a blowdown
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
such that the
(-1)
-curve
A\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
meets
D
normally and satisfies
A\cdot D\leqslant 2
and
A\cdot C=1
for exactly one
(-2)
-curve
C\subseteq D
(and, respectively,
p_{*}A=p_{*}C=0
). We write
\varphi
as
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, where
D^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}(D-C)
.

-2
-3
-1
A
-2
C
-3
\varphi
-2
-2
-1
\varphi(C)
-3

Figure 1
.
Example of an elementary vertical swap
\varphi\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, see Definition
1.3
.

(b)

A
swap
(respectively, a
swap over
B
) is a composition of a sequence
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
of elementary swaps (respectively, elementary swaps over
B
). We write it shortly as
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. Whenever the fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
X\longrightarrow B
is clear from the context, we refer to a swap over
B
as a
vertical swap
.

(c)

We say that

(X,D)

swaps vertically
to
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
if there is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
X\longrightarrow B
and a swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
over
B
.

(d)

Given two normal surfaces
\overline{X}
,
\overline{X}^{\prime}
, we say that
\overline{X}

swaps vertically to

\overline{X}^{\prime}
if the minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to the minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}^{\prime}
.

(e)

We say that
(X,D)
is
primitive
(over
B
) if it does not admit any elementary vertical swaps (over
B
).
A normal surface
\overline{X}
is
primitive
if so is its minimal log resolution.

If a log surface
(X,D)
with a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
is primitive over
B
(and
(X,D)
is a minimal log resolution of a normal surface
\overline{X}
), we say that
(X,D)
(and
\overline{X}
) are
vertically primitive
, keeping the fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
implicit. This way
\overline{X}
can be vertically primitive but not primitive, see Figure
2(e)
for an example; it can also be vertically primitive with respect to one
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration but not another, see Example
5.4
.

Theorem A

(Classification of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height
\leqslant 2
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal del Pezzo surface of rank
1
and height at most
2
, and let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution. Then the following hold.

(
1

The log surface

(X,D)
swaps vertically to a vertically primitive log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration shown in Figure
2
. Moreover, if
0pt(\overline{X})=2
then
(Y,D_{Y})
is a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{Y}
which is either canonical, or, in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, is a surface constructed in Example
5.5
, see Figure
2(i)
.

\overline{X}

5.17
, see Table
12
.

Remark 1.4

(Cascades of log canonical del Pezzo surfaces)
.

Assume that
(X,D)
is a minimal log resolution of a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, and
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X_{1},D_{1})\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow(X_{n},D_{n})
are elementary swaps. Then by Lemma
2.9
(d)
each
(X_{i},D_{i})
is a minimal log resolution of a log canonical del Pezzo surface, too. Thus Theorem
A
arranges all log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of height at most two in series constructed inductively by blowing up within the degenerate fibers of simple
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations shown in Figure
2
. Similar natural cascades were constructed e.g. by Hwang
[
Hwa24
]
in the toric case, see Section
7C
.

Theorem
A
(2)
gives the following description of moduli for log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height at most
2
in non-exceptional field characteristics. For a complete result see Proposition
C
and Table
13
.

Notation 1.5
.

For a singularity type
\mathcal{S}

we denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
the set of isomorphism classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and type
\mathcal{S}
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
the subset of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
consisting of surfaces of height at most
2
.

Corollary 1.6

(Moduli, see Proposition
C
and Table
13
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3,5
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a log terminal singularity type. Then any two surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
are equivalent under an equisingular deformation. Moreover, if
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})\geqslant 2

then one of the following holds.

(a)

We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})=\infty
, and there is a one-parameter family such that
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
if and only if
\overline{X}
is isomorphic to one of its fibers. Moreover, every surface
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
swaps vertically to a surface of type
2\mathrm{D}_{4}
, see Figure
2(e)
and Example
5.2
(d)
.

(b)

We have
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})=2
, and
\mathcal{S}
is one of the types shown in Figure
3
, see Table
13
.

Remark 1.7

(The number of singularities)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one. Theorem
A
gives a complete list of possible singularity types of
\overline{X}
under the assumption
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
it implies that such
\overline{X}
has at most
4
singularities. For
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3,5
this so-called
Bogomolov bound
is known for any log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one, regardless of the height
[
Lac24
, Corollary 1.2]
. If
{k}=\mathbb{C}
, it can be proved using Bogomolov-type inequalities
[
KM99
, Corollary 1.8.1]
,
[
Bel09
]
.

In turn, if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
then in
[
KM99
, p. 68]
the authors construct del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, height
2
and type
[2\nu-4]+2\nu\cdot\mathrm{A}_{1}
for any
\nu\geqslant 3
, see Figure
2(i)
and Example
5.5
. Theorem
A
implies that in fact every del Pezzo surface of height at most two and at least
5
singular points swaps vertically to one of those surfaces.

For del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{X}
of bigger height the inequality
\#\operatorname{Sing}\overline{X}\leqslant 4
fails for
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
. Indeed, in Theorem
E
below we will see that if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
or
5
there exist del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}+[5]+[3]+[2]
or
2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[5]+[3]+[2]
, respectively (the latter was also constructed in
[
Lac24
, 6.23]
). In our forthcoming articles we will see that these surfaces have the maximal possible number of singular points if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
or
5
, respectively; while if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, every del Pezzo surface of rank
1
with at least
12
singularities swaps vertically to a surface from
[
KM99
, p. 68]
.

There are lots of del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
which are not log terminal. Even if
{k}=\mathbb{C}
they can have arbitrarily many singularities with complicated minimal resolutions, see Section
3B
. Nevertheless, their height is low, and their structure is easy to understand in terms of explicit primitive models and swaps. Indeed, in Section
3
we use the known description of
open
del Pezzo surfaces
[
MT84
,
PP24
]
to prove the following result. For a more detailed version see Proposition
3.1
.

Proposition B

(Non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal del Pezzo surface of rank one. Assume that
\overline{X}
is not log terminal. Then the non-lt singularity of
\overline{X}
is unique, and one of the following holds.

(a)

We have
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
. If
0pt(\overline{X})=2
then
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to one of the canonical surfaces from Examples
5.2
or
5.3
, see Figures
2(b)
–
2(f)
, or, if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, to a surface from Example
5.5
, see Figure
2(i)
.

(b)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
. The surface
\overline{X}
has a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary (see Definition
1.11
) such that
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
is cuspidal, and
\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, not of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
,
\mathrm{E}_{7}
or
\mathrm{E}_{8}
, such that the minimal resolution of
\overline{Y}
has at least
6
exceptional components, cf. Table
15
. In this case we have
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 6
, see Theorem
E
.

(c)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
0pt(\overline{X})=3
, and
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to the surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
3.9
, see Figure
4
.

Moreover, if
\overline{X}
is a non-rational surface then
0pt(\overline{X})=1
, the resolution of the non–log terminal singularity of
\overline{X}
is a tree with exactly one non-rational curve, and the resolutions of the log terminal ones are admissible chains.

-3

Figure 4
.
Del Pezzo surface
\overline{Y}
from Proposition
B
(c)
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, see Example
3.9
.

We note that the uniqueness of a non-lt singularity for log canonical del Pezzo surfaces was proved by Kojima in
[
Koj20
]
, our more detailed result follows a similar approach.

Remark 1.8

(Non-rational del Pezzo surfaces)
.

(a)

The last statement of Proposition
B
, concerning non-rational surfaces, is known, see
[
Băd83
,
Sch01
]
.
It leads to combinatorial descriptions of such surfaces, given in
[
Fuj95
, Theorem 4.9]
,
[
Che97
, §5]
for
{k}=\mathbb{C}
and in
[
Sch01
, §5]
for an arbitrary perfect field
{k}
.

(b)

We note that a del Pezzo surface of rank one has a non-rational singularity if and only if
\overline{X}
itself is non-rational
[
Sch01
, Theorem 2.2]
, cf.
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.7]
. If furthermore
\overline{X}
is log canonical then, as we will see in Lemma
4.4
,
\overline{X}
is an elliptic cone as in
[
Băd01
, §14.38]
, see Example
4.2
.

(c)

In fact, Proposition
B
, as well as more detailed descriptions cited above, hold also for
numerically del Pezzo surfaces
, i.e. without the assumption that
K_{\overline{X}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, see
[
Che97
]
. In this article, however, we focus mostly on rational or log canonical singularities, for which
K_{\overline{X}}
is automatically
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
[
Lip69
, 17.1]
.

1C.
The question of uniqueness of del Pezzo surfaces of a given singularity type

Corollary
1.6
implies that usually two log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank
1
and height at most
2
which share the singularity type are usually isomorphic. Proposition
C
below gives a more precise description of the exceptions, showing in particular that they are equivalent under an equisingular deformation.

Definition 1.9

(Representing families)
.

(a)

A
smooth family

f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
consists of a smooth surjective morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow B
between connected smooth varieties, and a reduced divisor
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{X}
such that for every component
\mathcal{D}_{0}
of
\mathcal{D}
, the restriction
f|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\colon\mathcal{D}_{0}\longrightarrow B
is a smooth surjective morphism with irreducible fibers, cf.
[
CTW17
, Definition 2.13]
.

(b)

A
fiber
of
f
over
b\in B
is a pair
(X_{b},D_{b})
, where
X_{b}
is the scheme-theoretic fiber
f^{-1}(b)
, and
D_{b}
is a reduced divisor on
X_{b}
given by the restriction of
\mathcal{D}
.

(c)

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a set consisting of isomorphism classes of some log smooth log surfaces. We say that a smooth family
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
represents
\mathcal{C}
if
\mathcal{C}
equals the set of isomorphism classes of fibers of
f
.

(d)

Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family.
We say that
f
is
almost faithful
if it is equivariant with respect to the action of some finite group
G
such that two fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
and
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
of
f
are isomorphic if and only if
b
and
b^{\prime}

lie in the same orbit. In this case, we say that
f

has dimension

\dim B
. We call the image of
G
in
\operatorname{Aut}(B)
the
symmetry group
of
f
.

(e)

Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family. We say that
f
is
stratified
, by a stratification
B=\bigsqcup_{i=0}^{d}B_{i}
, if

(i)

\dim B=d
, and each closure
\overline{B}_{i}=\bigsqcup_{j\geqslant i}B_{i}
is a smooth connected subvariety of codimension
j
in
B
,

(ii)

two fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
and
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
of
f
are isomorphic if and only if
b
and
b^{\prime}
lie in the same stratum.

(f)

We say that a set
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
of isomorphism classes of normal projective surfaces or log surfaces is
represented by an (almost faithful, or stratified) family
(of dimension
d
), if the same holds for the set of isomorphism classes of minimal log resolutions of surfaces in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, cf. Lemma
2.15
.

Proposition C

(Exceptions to uniqueness)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rational log canonical singularity type. Let
{\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})}
.

(a)

If
\mathcal{P}
is finite then it has at most three elements and it is represented by a stratified family.

(b)

If
\mathcal{P}
is infinite then it is represented by an almost faithful family.

(c)

If
\#\mathcal{P}\geqslant 2
then the type
\mathcal{S}
, the number
\#\mathcal{P}
in case
\#\mathcal{P}<\infty
, and the dimension of some almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}
in case
\#\mathcal{P}=\infty
, are listed in Table
13
.

If
\#\mathcal{P}=\infty
then the base and symmetry group of some almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}
is described in Table
3
(case
0pt=1
) and Remark
5.18
(case
0pt=2
). For instance, in Corollary
1.6
(a)
the base
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
parametrizes the fourth fiber in Figure
2(e)
, and the symmetry group is a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(B)\cong S_{3}
.

Note that in Proposition
C
we do not claim that the representing family is unique. Proposition
D
below shows that (up to some minor exceptions in positive characteristic, listed in Table
14
) one can choose such a family so that it satisfies certain natural properties, related to infinitesimal deformations of minimal log resolutions of surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
. In case
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})=\infty
it allows to intrinsically define the
moduli dimension
of
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
, see Definition
1.10
(c)
. To state this result, we need to introduce some further terminology.

We denote by
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
the logarithmic tangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
, i.e. the sheaf of vector fields on
X
which are tangent to
D
, see
[
Kaw78
,
FZ94
]
. We write
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=\dim H^{i}(X,\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. The number
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
is the dimension of the tangent space to the base of a semiuniversal formal deformation of
(X,D)
, see
[
Ser06
, Definition 2.2.6]
for a definition (and
[
Kaw78
]
for analytic version). If
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
then this base is smooth, see
[
Ser06
, Proposition 3.4.17]
or
[
Kat96
, 8.6]
.

Definition 1.10

(
h^{1}
-stratified and almost universal families)
.

Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family.

(a)

We say that
f
is
h^{1}
-stratified
if it is stratified by
B=\bigsqcup_{i=0}^{d}B_{i}
, see Definition
1.9
(e)
, and

(i)

for every
i\in\{0,\dots,d\}
and
b\in B_{i}
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))=i
,

(ii)

the formal germ of
f
at the deepest stratum
B_{d}=\{\overline{b}\}
is a semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{\overline{b}},D_{\overline{b}})
.

(b)

We say that
f
is
almost universal
if the following hold:

(i)

f
is almost faithful, see Definition
1.9
(d)
,

(ii)

for every
b\in B
the formal germ of
f
at
b
is a semiuniversal deformation of
(X_{b},D_{b})
.

(c)

We say that a set
\mathcal{C}
of isomorphism classes of log smooth surfaces
has moduli dimension
d
if it is represented by an almost universal family of dimension
d
. In this case,
d=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
for every
(X,D)\in\mathcal{C}
. We say that a set of isomorphism classes of normal projective surfaces, or log surfaces,
has moduli dimension
d
if the same holds for the set of isomorphism classes of their minimal log resolutions, cf. Definition
1.9
(f)
.

Proposition D

(Moduli and rigidity)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rational log canonical singularity type,
{\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})}
.

(a)

If
\mathcal{P}
is infinite then
\mathcal{P}
has moduli dimension as in Table
13
, unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
14
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{P}
is finite and
\#\mathcal{P}\geqslant 2
then
\mathcal{P}
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family, unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\mathcal{S}=\mathrm{E}_{8}
.

(c)

Let
(X,D)
be the minimal log resolution of a surface in
\mathcal{P}
, and let
h^{i}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. Then

(i)

If
\#\mathcal{P}=1
then
h^{1}=0
, unless
\operatorname{char}{k}>0
,
\mathcal{S}
is as in Table
14
, and
h^{1}=1
.

(ii)

We have
h^{2}=0
, unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
\mathcal{S}
is as in Theorem
A
(3.c.ii)
, and
h^{2}=\nu-2
.

For most of the exceptions in Proposition
D
(a)
the number
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
varies among surfaces in
\mathcal{P}
, see Table
14
, so
\mathcal{P}
is not represented by an almost universal family. In the exceptional case of Proposition
D
(b)
, the set
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{E}_{8})
has three elements, with
h^{1}=0,1,3
, so it is not represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family. Note that by the Riemann–Roch theorem the Euler characteristic
\chi\coloneqq h^{0}-h^{1}+h^{2}
depends only on the singularity type
\mathcal{S}
. It is listed in Tables
9
–
12
: together with the above formulas for
h^{1}
and
h^{2}
it allows to compute
h^{0}
.

1D.
Del Pezzo surfaces admitting descendants with elliptic boundary

We now introduce another broad class of del Pezzo surfaces with relatively simple geometry. Just like in case
0pt\leqslant 2
discussed above, these surfaces are constructed from simple (in fact canonical) del Pezzo surfaces of rank one by a sequence of blowups. The difference is that now, instead of resolving a particular
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-pencil on
\overline{Y}
, we resolve the singularity of an elliptic boundary
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
; see Figure
5
for an example. We say that a boundary
\overline{T}
of a log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
is
elliptic
if
\overline{T}
is an irreducible curve of arithmetic genus one (usually rational singular) contained in the smooth locus of
\overline{Y}
.

Definition 1.11

(Descendant)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal projective surface. Let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution. A log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
is a
descendant
of
\overline{X}
if there is a birational morphism
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
such that
\varphi_{*}D=\overline{T}
.

Classification of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one admitting descendants with elliptic boundary is summarized in Theorem
E
below. It is based on the following elementary lemma, proved in Section
6
, which shows that the geometry of their descendants is highly restricted.

Lemma 1.12

(Descendants with elliptic boundary for surfaces of rank one)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal projective surface of rank one which admits a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary. If
\overline{T}
is smooth then
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only if it is an elliptic cone.
Assume that
\overline{T}
is singular. Let
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
be the minimal log resolution, let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be as in Definition
1.11
, and let
T=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one and
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
.

(b)

\operatorname{Exc}\varphi=D-T+L
for some
(-1)
-curve
L\not\subseteq D
; and
\varphi(L)=\operatorname{Sing}\overline{T}
is the only base point of
\varphi^{-1}
in
\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

(c)

If
\overline{Y}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
then
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2\leqslant 6
(and
\leqslant 4
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\not\in\{2,3\}
). If
\overline{Y}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
then
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
.

(d)

The surface
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only if
\operatorname{cf}(T)<1
, where
\operatorname{cf}(T)
is the coefficient of
T
in
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-K_{X}
.

In particular, if
\overline{X}
is log terminal then
\overline{X}
is a del Pezzo surface and
\overline{T}
is singular.

\mathrm{A}_{2}
\mathrm{A}_{2}
\mathrm{A}_{2}
[3,2,3,2]
L
min. res.
of
\overline{X}
-3
L
-3
T
T
3
\mathrm{A}_{2}
\mathrm{A}_{2}
\mathrm{A}_{2}
\overline{T}
3
min. res.
of
\overline{Y}

Figure 5
.
Del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one and type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}+[3,2,3,2]
with a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, cf. Figure
2(b)
. The elliptic boundary
\overline{T}
is nodal.

Log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
as in Lemma
1.12
(a)

are classified in
[
PP24
, Proposition 1.5]
summarized in Table
15
as follows. In the row corresponding to a canonical singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, the entry in the last column contains
\mathrm{N}
(respectively,
\mathrm{C}
or
\mathrm{C}_{d}
) if and only if some del Pezzo surface
\overline{Y}
of rank 1 and type
\mathcal{S}
contains a nodal (respectively, cuspidal) curve
\overline{T}
with
p_{a}(\overline{T})=1
in its smooth locus. Moreover, the log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
is unique up to an isomorphism unless this entry is
\mathrm{C}_{d}
. In the latter case, the set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})
of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
where
\overline{Y}
is a canonical del Pezzo surface of rank 1 and type
\mathcal{S}
and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a cuspidal member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
has moduli dimension
d
. If
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})=1

then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
0
, see Lemma
6.14
.

Lemma
1.12
(b)
shows that to reconstruct
\overline{X}
from its descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, we need to blow up over the singular point of
\overline{T}
in such a way that the exceptional divisor has exactly one
(-1)
-curve
L
, and the total transform of
\overline{T}
, minus
L
, contracts to normal surface singularities. In the most important case, when the resulting surface
\overline{X}
is log terminal, Lemma
1.12
(d)
shows that it is automatically del Pezzo. We note that an analogous result holds for surfaces of height at most
2
, see Lemma
2.6
, but outside these two classes the ampleness of
-K_{\overline{X}}
becomes a much stronger restriction.

The above strategy leads to a classification summarized in Theorem
E
below. Given Theorem
A
and Proposition
B
we may restrict our attention to log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of height at least
3
. For a normal surface
\overline{X}
we denote by
\mathcal{S}(\overline{X})
its singularity type, and we write
\#\mathcal{S}(\overline{X})
for the number of exceptional components of its minimal resolution. For a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
the set of isomorphism classes of of del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{X}
of singularity type
\mathcal{S}
which have a descendant with elliptic boundary.

Theorem E

(Classification of del Pezzo surfaces having descendants with elliptic boundary)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, having a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary, see Definition
1.11
. Let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution, and let
\mathcal{S}
be its singularity type.
If
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
then the following hold.

(a)

The descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary of the surface
\overline{X}
is unique up to an isomorphism. Moreover, the singularity type
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
of
\overline{Y}
, and the singularity type of
\overline{T}
, are uniquely determined by
\mathcal{S}
.

(b)

We have
\#\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})\geqslant 6
, see the classification in Table
15
. Moreover, if
\overline{T}
is cuspidal then
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})\not\in\{\mathrm{E}_{6},\mathrm{E}_{7},\mathrm{E}_{8}\}
.

(c)

We have
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})+\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathcal{T}
is one of the types listed in Lemma
6.2
.

(d)

Assume that
\overline{T}
is nodal. Then
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})=1
, i.e.
\overline{X}
is the unique del Pezzo surface of rank one and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
admitting a descendant with elliptic boundary.
Moreover,
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
.

(e)

Assume that
\overline{T}
is cuspidal, so
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
.
Then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
is represented by an almost faithful family of dimension
d+t
(whose bases and symmetry groups are described in Remark
6.16
), where

(i)

d
is the moduli dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y}))
, listed in Table
15
(i.e.
d
for types with
“
\mathrm{C}_{d}
”
and
0
otherwise),

(ii)

t=1
if the type
\mathcal{T}
is as in Lemma
6.2
(10)
, and
t=0
otherwise.

Moreover, if
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
d+t
and we have
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0,d+t,d+1
for
i=0,1,2
, respectively. For the corresponding result in case
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
see Proposition
6.17
.

(f)

We have
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})+2
, unless
\mathcal{S}
is as in Proposition
6.4
, in which case
0pt(\overline{X})=3
,
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
. In particular, we have optimal bounds
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 6
, and
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 4
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
.

Conversely, if
\#\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})\geqslant 6
then either
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
or
\overline{X}
is one of the exceptions listed in Lemma
6.3
.

Remark 1.13

(Cascades, cf. Remark
1.4
and Proposition
6.22
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, having a descendant with elliptic boundary. If
\overline{X}
is non-primitive then by Proposition
6.22
(b)
it admits an elementary swap onto a log canonical del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
of rank one, which either has a (possibly different) descendant with elliptic boundary or is canonical or, if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, is one of the primitive surfaces from Example
5.5
, see Figure
2(i)
. In turn, if
\overline{X}
is primitive then it is either canonical, or its singularity type is listed in Lemma
6.21
; in particular if
\operatorname{char}{k}\not\in\{2,3\}
then it is of type
2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[3]
or
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}+[3]
.

In most cases, the above elementary swap
\overline{X}\rightsquigarrow\overline{X}^{\prime}
is given by the contraction of the
(-1)
-curve
L
from Lemma
1.12
(b)
. Then
\overline{X}^{\prime}
has the same descendant with elliptic boundary as
\overline{X}
. For example, if
\overline{X}
is as in Figure
5
then contracting
L
we get an elementary swap onto
\overline{X}^{\prime}
of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}+[2,2,3]
. In the remaining cases, when this particular swap is not possible then by Proposition
6.22
(c)

\overline{X}
is rather simple: either it is already canonical, or its minimal log resolution is birationally dominated by the minimal log resolution of its descendant.

Remark 1.14
.

Let
\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}
be del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and the same log canonical singularity type
\mathcal{S}
. In forthcoming articles we will show that
0pt(\overline{X}_{1})=0pt(\overline{X}_{2})
, and if
\overline{X}_{1}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary then so does
\overline{X}_{2}
. This result implies that for each singularity type
\mathcal{S}
as in Theorem
A
and
E
, the sets
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
described there are equal to the sets of isomorphism classes of
all
del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, i.e.
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
.

2.
Preliminaries

We now settle some notation and we recall some known results which will be useful in this and upcoming articles. For more general statements and further motivation see
[
Fuj82
]
and references therein.

2A.
Divisors on surfaces

Curves and surfaces are understood to be irreducible. A
log surface
is a pair
(X,D)
, where
X
is a normal projective surface and the
boundary

D
is a Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
with coefficients between
0
and
1
such that
K_{X}+D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. It is
log smooth
if
X
is smooth and
D
has simple normal crossings. We identify
(X,0)
with
X
. Given a birational morphism of surfaces
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
we write
\varphi\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X,D)
if
D=\varphi_{*}D^{\prime}
. We denote the reduced exceptional divisor of
\varphi
by
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
, the proper transform of a curve
C
on
X^{\prime}
by
\varphi^{-1}_{*}C
, and the base locus of a birational map
\psi
by
\operatorname{Bs}\psi
.

A
log resolution
of a log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a proper birational morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, where
D=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. It is
minimal
if it is not birationally dominated by any other log resolution.

In this article we consider only log surfaces with reduced boundary.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface. A curve
C
on
X
is called an
n
-curve
if
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
C^{2}=n
.
Let
D
be a divisor on
X
. We say that
D
is
connected
if
\operatorname{Supp}D
is connected in the Zariski topology. We write
D_{\mathrm{red}}
for
D
with reduced structure. By a
component
of
D
we mean an
irreducible
component of
D_{\mathrm{red}}
(not a connected one); we denote their number by
\#D
. If divisors
D,T
and
D-T
are effective, we say that
T
is a
subdivisor
of
D
. For two effective divisors
D_{1},D_{2}
we write
D_{1}\cap D_{2}
for the intersection of their supports, and, if they are reduced, we write
D_{1}\wedge D_{2}
for the sum of their common components.

We now recall the definition of a weighted graph of a reduced divisor on a smooth projective surface. Abstractly, by a
weighted graph
we mean a graph
\Gamma
together with a weight function associating a pair of integers
(v\cdot v,p_{a}(v))
to each vertex
v
; and an integer
(v\cdot w)_{e}
to each edge
e
between vertices
v
and
w
. We write
v\cdot w=\sum_{e}(v\cdot w)_{e}
, where the sum runs over all edges
e
between
v
and
w
. The
intersection matrix
of
\Gamma
is
M_{\Gamma}\coloneqq[v_{i}\cdot v_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
, where
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
are the vertices of
\Gamma
. We say that
\Gamma
is
negative definite
if so is
M_{\Gamma}
. The
discriminant
of
\Gamma
is
d(\Gamma)\coloneqq\det(-M_{\Gamma})
, or
1
if
\Gamma
is empty; see
[
Fuj82
, §3]
for its elementary properties.

Let
D
be a reduced divisor on a smooth projective surface. Its
weighted graph

\Gamma(D)
is a weighted graph together with a bijection
\varepsilon
from the set of its vertices to the set of components of
D
such that for each component
C
of
D
, the weight of the vertex
\varepsilon^{-1}(C)
is
(C^{2},p_{a}(C))
, and for two different components
C
,
C^{\prime}
of
D
, the vertices
\varepsilon^{-1}(C)
,
\varepsilon^{-1}(C^{\prime})
are connected by an edge of weight
(C\cdot C^{\prime})_{p}
for each
p\in C\cap C^{\prime}
. We write
D^{v}\coloneqq\varepsilon(v)
,
v_{C}\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-1}(C)
. We often skip the edge weight if it is equal to
1
, and we skip
p_{a}(C)
if it is equal to
0
. A morphism
\varphi\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X,D)
induces a morphism of weighted graphs
\Gamma(D^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Gamma(D)
by
v_{C^{\prime}}\mapsto v_{\varphi(C^{\prime})}
.

We say that
D
is
negative definite
if so is its weighted graph; we put
d(D)\coloneqq d(\Gamma(D))
. The
branching number
of a component
C
of
D
is

\beta_{D}(C)\coloneqq C\cdot(D-C)
,
i.e. the sum of weights of all edges of
\Gamma
adjacent to
v_{C}
.
We say that
C
is a
tip
of
D
if
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 1
, and is
branching
in
D
if
\beta_{D}(C)\geqslant 3
.

A connected snc divisor with no branching component is a
chain
if it has a tip and
circular
if it does not. A
tree
is a connected snc divisor with no circular subdivisor. A tree
T
whose unique branching component
B
has
\beta_{T}(B)=3
is called a
fork
. A
(-2)
-chain
(
(-2)
-fork
) is a chain (fork) whose all components are
(-2)
-curves.

Let
T
be a chain with a chosen
first
tip of
T
, call it
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
. Then the components
T^{(1)},\dots,T^{(\#T)}
of
T
are ordered in a unique way such that
T^{(1)}=\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
and
T^{(i)}\cdot T^{(i+1)}=1
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant\#T-1
. Then
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)\coloneqq T^{(\#T)}
is the
last
tip of
T
. We write
T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}
for the same chain
T
with an opposite order.

A
type
of an ordered rational chain
T
is a sequence of integers
[a_{1},\dots,a_{\#T}]
, where
a_{i}=-(T^{(i)})^{2}
. We often abuse notation and identify a chain with its type. Moreover, for types
T_{1}=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]
,
T_{2}=[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]
we write
[T_{1},T_{2}]=[T_{1},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]
etc. We warn the reader about a slight abuse of notation: if
T
is a zero divisor (
T=0
), then its type is
empty
, as opposed to
[0]
, which refers to a single
0
-curve. Hence if
T=0
then we have
[a,T,b]=[a,b]
,
not

[a,0,b]
.

We write
(m)_{k}
for an integer
m
repeated
k
times. Following
[
Ton12
]
, we define the type
T_{1}*T_{2}
as

(1)

[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]*[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]\coloneqq[a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}+b_{1}-1,b_{2},\dots,b_{l}].

If
T_{1}
is empty, we put
0*[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]=[b_{2},\dots,b_{l}]
. The following convention will be useful:

(2)

[(2)_{-1}]*[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]\coloneqq[b_{2}+1,\dots,b_{l}],\quad[(2)_{-1},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]\coloneqq[b_{2},\dots,b_{l}].

We say that a divisor
D

contracts to
a divisor
D^{\prime}
(or to a singularity
p
), if there is a birational morphism
\varphi
such that
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi\subseteq D
and
\varphi_{*}D=D^{\prime}
(or
\varphi(D)=p
).

Let
T
be an ordered rational chain on a surface
X
. Assume that
T=[1]
or
T
is
admissible
, i.e.
T=[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]
with
a_{j}\geqslant 2
for all
j
. Then there is a unique type
T^{*}
such that
[T,1,T^{*}]
contracts to
[0]
, see
[
Fuj82
, Proposition 4.7]
. For an admissible chain
[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]
,
[
Ton12
, Lemma 5.ii]
gives:

(3)

[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]^{*}=[(2)_{a_{k}-1}]*\dots*[(2)_{a_{1}-1}],

and, clearly,
[1]^{*}
is an empty chain. A chain
[T,1,T^{\prime}]
contracts to a smooth point if and only if
T^{\prime}=T^{*}*[(2)_{k}]
for some
k\geqslant-1
. If a curve
C
meets a chain
[T,1,T^{*}]*[(2)_{k}]
once, in the first tip, then the contraction of that chain increases the self-intersection number of
C
by
k+2
.

Let again
D
be an snc divisor. An ordered subchain
T\leqslant D
is a
twig
of
D
if
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
is a tip of
D
, and no component of
T
is branching in
D
. In this case,
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)
meets
D-T
, or
T
is a connected component of
D
. A
(-2)
-twig
is a twig whose all components are
(-2)
-curves. We say that a twig of
D
is
maximal
if it is not properly contained in any other twig of
D
.

Let
T
be a rational fork. Then
T=B+T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}
, where
B
is the branching component of
T
, and
T_{j}
are maximal twigs of
T
. A
type
of the fork
T
consists of an integer
b\coloneqq-B^{2}
and an (unordered) set of types
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
. We denote this type by

\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle.

2B.
Log terminal and log canonical surface singularities

Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a resolution of a normal surface. Let
D\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\pi
be its reduced exceptional divisor, and let
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
be all components of
D
. Since the intersection matrix of
D
is negative definite, the formula

(4)

\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{cf}(C_{i})\,C_{i}

uniquely defines rational numbers
\operatorname{cf}(C_{i})
called the
coefficients
of
C_{i}
. We note that
\operatorname{cf}(C_{i})
depends only on the valuation of
{k}(\overline{X})
associated to
C_{i}
, and not on
\pi

[
KM98
, Remark 2.23]
. More generally, if
\Gamma
is a negative definite weighted graph, see Section
2A
, then for each vertex
v
of
\Gamma
we define
\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)\in\mathbb{Q}
by the formula

0=2p_{a}(v)-2-v^{2}+\sum_{w}\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(w)\,w\cdot v,

where the sum runs over all vertices
w
of
\Gamma
. Explicit formulas for these coefficients are given e.g. in
[
Kol92
, §3.1.10, §3.2]
. If
D
is a reduced divisor with weighted graph
\Gamma
, and
C
is a component of
D
corresponding to a vertex
v
, we write
\operatorname{cf}_{D}(C)\coloneqq\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)
. This way, if
D
is the exceptional divisor of a resolution of a normal surface
\overline{X}
, we have
\operatorname{cf}_{D}(C)=\operatorname{cf}(C)
for every component
C
of
D
.

A normal surface is called
canonical
,
log terminal
(
lt
), or
log canonical
(
lc
) if for every exceptional divisor
E
of some (equivalently, any) resolution we have
\operatorname{cf}(E)\leqslant 0
,
\operatorname{cf}(E)<1
or
\operatorname{cf}(E)\leqslant 1
, respectively.

A surface singularity is canonical if and only if it is du Val, i.e. its minimal resolution is a
(-2)
-chain or fork
[
Kol13
, 3.26]
. We now recall the classification of lt and lc surface singularities and fix some notation and terminology, see 3.39 and 3.40 loc. cit. A chain
T
on
X
is
admissible
if it is rational, and all its components have self-intersection numbers at most
-2
. Let
F
be rational fork on
X
of type
\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
, with
b\geqslant 2
and admissible twigs
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
. Put
\delta_{F}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}d(T_{i})^{-1}
. We say that
F
is
admissible
if
\delta_{F}>1
; and
F
is
log canonical
if
\delta_{F}=1
and
F
is not a
(-2)
-fork. Every admissible chain or fork contracts to a log terminal singularity, and conversely, every log terminal singularity is of this type
[
Kol13
, 3.40]
. Every log canonical fork contracts to a log canonical singularity, which is not log terminal because the branching component has coefficient
1
. Solving the inequality
\delta_{F}\geqslant 1
, one gets the following well-known description.

Lemma 2.1

(Admissible forks, cf.
[
Miy01
, I.5.3.4]
)
.

Let
F
be a rational fork of type
\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
, with
b\geqslant 2
.

(a)

The fork
F
is admissible if and only if the triple
\{d(T_{1}),d(T_{2}),d(T_{3})\}
is one of the following:
\{2,2,k\}
for some
k\geqslant 2
;
\{2,3,3\}
,
\{2,3,4\}
, or
\{2,3,5\}
.

(b)

The fork
F
is log canonical if and only if
\{d(T_{1}),d(T_{2}),d(T_{3})\}=\{2,3,6\},\{2,4,4\}
or
\{3,3,3\}
.

(c)

Let
T
be an admissible chain, and let
d\coloneqq d(T)
. Then
d\geqslant 2
and:

(i)

if
d\in\{2,3,4,6\}
then
T=[d]
or
[(2)_{d-1}]
.

(ii)

if
d=5
then
T=[5]
,
[3,2]
,
[2,3]
or
[2,2,2,2]
.

In particular, the following hold.

(d)

If the fork
F
is admissible then one of its maximal twigs is
[2]
.

(e)

Assume that
F
is an admissible (respectively, log canonical) fork, and some twig
T
of
F
has
\#T\geqslant 3
. Then either
F=\langle b,[2],[2],T\rangle
, or
T
is a
(-2)
-twig and
\#T\leqslant 4
(respectively,
\#T=5
).

We say that an snc divisor
D
is a
bench
if
D=T+T_{1}^{+}+T_{2}^{+}+T_{1}^{-}+T_{2}^{-}
, where
T
is a rational chain, called the
central chain
, and
T_{i}^{\pm}=[2]
is a twig of
D
meeting
\textnormal{tip}^{\pm}(T)
. If
T=[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
we say that
D
is
a bench of type
[\![a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]\!]
. A bench is
log canonical
if its central chain is admissible, but not a
(-2)
-chain. Every log canonical bench contracts to a log canonical singularity; in this case
\operatorname{cf}(C)=1
for each component
C
of the central chain, and
\operatorname{cf}(T_{i}^{\pm})=\frac{1}{2}
for each tip. Conversely, if
E
is the exceptional divisor of a minimal resolution of a log canonical, but not log terminal surface singularity, then
E
is one of the following: a log canonical fork, a log canonical bench, a smooth elliptic curve, or a circular divisor whose components are rational and have self intersection numbers at most
-2
, with at least one strict inequality
[
Kol13
, 3.39]
. In the last two cases, we have
\operatorname{cf}(C)=1
for each component
C
of
E
.

A normal surface singularity
\overline{X}
is
rational
if some (equivalently, any) resolution
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
satisfies
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
. By Artin’s criterion
[
Art62
]
, cf.
[
Né22
, Theorem 7.1.2]
, a negative definite snc divisor
D
contracts to a rational singularity if and only if its
fundamental cycle

Z
satisfies
p_{a}(Z)=0
. We recall that
Z
is the minimal divisor with support equal to the support of
D
such that
Z\cdot E\leqslant 0
for every component
E
of
D
. It can be computed from the weighted graph of
D
by the Laufer algorithm, see
[
Né22
, Corollary 6.6.7(b)]
. This criterion implies that every log terminal surface singularity is rational, and, more generally, a log canonical surface singularity is rational if and only if the exceptional divisor of its minimal log resolution is an admissible or log canonical chain, fork, or bench,
see
[
Né22
, Example 7.1.5(g),(h)]
.

A
singularity type
of a point
p\in\overline{X}
is the weighted graph of the exceptional divisor of its minimal resolution.
If the latter is a chain, fork or bench of type
T
, we say that
p
is
of type
T
. For
(-2)
-chains and forks we also use notation for Dynkin types
\mathrm{A}_{k}
,
\mathrm{D}_{k}
,
\mathrm{E}_{k}
. Hence we have the following equalities of singularity types:

\mathrm{A}_{k}=[(2)_{k}],\ \mathrm{D}_{k}=\langle 2;[(2)_{k-3}],[2],[2]\rangle,\ \mathrm{E}_{k}=\langle 2;[(2)_{k-4}],[2,2],[2]\rangle.

We remark that if
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
, the type of a log terminal singularity determines the isomorphism type of its local ring
[
KM98
, 4.9(2)]
, but it is not so if
\operatorname{char}{k}>0
, see
[
Art77
]
.
A
singularity type
of a normal surface
\overline{X}
is a formal sum of singularity types of all singular points of
\overline{X}
.

More abstractly, by a
singularity type
we mean a negative definite weighted graph, see Section
2A
. We say that a singularity type
\Gamma
is canonical (respectively, lt, lc) if
\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)\leqslant 0
(respectively,
\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)<1
,
\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)\leqslant 1
) for every vertex
v
of
\Gamma
; and is rational if and only if it satisfies Artin’s criterion. This way, if
\Gamma
is a singularity type of a normal surface
\overline{X}
, then all singularities of
\overline{X}
are canonical, lt, lc, or rational if and only if so is
\Gamma
.

2C.
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface. A
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
of
X
is a morphism
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
whose general fiber
F
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We call
F\cdot D
the
height
of
p
(with respect to
D
). Recall from Definition
1.1
that the
height
of
(X,D)
is the infimum of the heights of all
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of
X
with respect to
D
. A
witnessing

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration is any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration realizing this infimum. Height of a normal surface is the height of its minimal log resolution.

Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
of
X
. A curve
C\subseteq X
is
vertical
if
p(C)
is a point; otherwise
C
is horizontal. Any divisor
T
decomposes uniquely as a sum of its vertical and horizontal parts, i.e.
T=T_{\mathrm{vert}}+T_{\mathrm{hor}}
, where all components of
T_{\mathrm{vert}}
are vertical and all components of
T_{\mathrm{hor}}
are horizontal. We say that
T
is
horizontal
if
T=T_{\mathrm{hor}}
.

A curve
H
is an
n
-section if
H\cdot F=n
for a fiber
F
of
p
. In the course of our classification, whenever
D
contains such a multi-section, we need to consider two cases: when
p|_{H}
is separable, so the Hurwitz formula applies; and, if
\operatorname{char}{k}|n
, when it is not. To avoid such case-splitting, we prefer
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations with as few multi-sections in
D
as possible. Therefore, we first study cases of large width, defined as follows.

Definition 2.2

(Width)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface of finite height.
We define the
width
of
(X,D)
as the maximal value of
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}
among all witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations. We denote it by
0pt(X,D)
.

Clearly,
0pt(X,D)\leqslant 0pt(X,D)
, and the equality holds if and only if
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
0pt(X,D)

1
-sections.

A fiber
F
of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration is
degenerate
if it is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Any such
F
contracts to a
0
-curve, i.e. can be recovered by inductively blowing up over a
0
-curve. This leads to the following description of
F
.

Lemma 2.3

(Degenerate fibers, cf.
[
Fuj82
, §4]
)
.

Let
F
be a degenerate fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of a smooth projective surface. Then
F_{\mathrm{red}}
is a rational tree with no branching
(-1)
-curve, and the following hold.

(a)

If a
(-1)
-curve
L
has multiplicity
1
in
F
then
\beta_{F_{\mathrm{red}}}(L)=1
and
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L
contains another
(-1)
-curve.

(b)

Assume that
F
contains exactly one
(-1)
-curve
L
. Then
F
has exactly two components of multiplicity
1
, they are tips of
F
, and one of the following holds.

(i)

The fiber
F
is
columnar
, i.e.
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[T,1,T^{*}]
for some admissible chain
T
.

(ii)

Both components of multiplicity one belong to the same connected component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L
, say
C
. The divisor
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L-C
is empty or a chain.

Contracting all fibers to
0
-curves yields the following formula, see
[
Fuj82
, 4.16]
, cf.
[
Pal15
, Lemma 2]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. Let
\nu_{\infty}
be the number of fibers contained in
D
. For a fiber
F
let
\sigma(F)
be the number of components of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
not contained in
D
, i.e.
\sigma(F)\coloneqq\#(F_{\mathrm{red}}-D\wedge F_{\mathrm{red}})
. Put
\Sigma=\sum_{F}(\sigma(F)-1)
, where the sum runs over all fibers not contained in
D
. Then

\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}+\nu_{\infty}+\rho(X)=\#D+2+\Sigma.

2D.
Del Pezzo surfaces

We now summarize basic properties of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of the minimal log resolutions of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one. Those properties will be used frequently in our series of articles. Crucially for this one, Lemma
2.6
implies that a log terminal surface of rank one and height at most two is automatically del Pezzo.

Remark 2.5

(Finiteness of height)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one, not isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Its minimal resolution has negative Kodaira dimension, so by the Enriques–Kodaira classification of surfaces it is
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibered, and therefore
0pt(\overline{X})<\infty
. Also,
0pt(\overline{X})>0
, as otherwise a general fiber of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration would map to a
0
-curve on
\overline{X}
, contrary to the assumption
\rho(\overline{X})=1
.

Lemma 2.6

(Criterion for ampleness of
-K_{\overline{X}}
using a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal surface of Picard rank one such that
K_{\overline{X}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be its resolution. Assume that
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, let
F
be its fiber and let
H_{1},\dots,H_{h}
be all horizontal components of
\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Then
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only if

(5)

\sum_{j=1}^{h}\operatorname{cf}(H_{j})\,H_{j}\cdot F<2.

In particular, every log terminal surface of rank one and height at most two is del Pezzo.

If
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo then
-K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(F)>0
, and conversely, if
\rho(\overline{X})=1
,
K_{\overline{X}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and
-K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(F)>0
then
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo. The inequality
-K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(F)>0
is equivalent to (
5
). Indeed, by formula (
4
) we have

-K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(F)=-\Big(K_{X}+\sum_{T}\operatorname{cf}(T)\,T\Big)\cdot F=2-\sum_{j=1}^{h}\operatorname{cf}(H_{j})\,H_{j}\cdot F,

where the first sum runs over all components
T
of
\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. This proves the first part of the lemma.

Assume now that
\overline{X}
is log terminal (hence
K_{\overline{X}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier),
\rho(\overline{X})=1
and
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
. Since
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
, the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
has a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose fiber
F
satisfies
D\cdot F\leqslant 2
. Because
\overline{X}
is log terminal, we have
\sum_{j}\operatorname{cf}(H_{j})\,H_{j}\cdot F<\sum_{j}H_{j}\cdot F=D\cdot F\leqslant 2
, so (
5
) holds, as needed.

∎

Lemma 2.7

(Elementary properties of minimal resolutions of del Pezzo surfaces)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface. Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be its minimal resolution, and let
D\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Then the following hold.

(a)

Every component
C
of
D
satisfies
\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant 0
.

(b)

Assume that
D
has a component
C\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then
\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant 1
and
2p_{a}(C)+\beta_{D}(C)<2-C^{2}
. Moreover,
C
is a
1
-section of some
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D-C
is vertical. In particular,
0pt(\overline{X})=1
.

(c)

The divisor
D
is snc. In particular,
(X,D)
is the minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}
.

(d)

Every curve
L\subseteq X
,
L\not\subseteq D
satisfies
L^{2}\geqslant 2p_{a}(L)-1
. In particular, if
L^{2}<0
then
L
is a
(-1)
-curve.

(a)
This follows from the minimality of
\pi
and from
[
KM98
, Lemma 3.39(1)]
, cf.
[
Pal24
, Lemma 7.3(3)]
.

(b)
Put
g=p_{a}(C)
,
e=-C^{2}
,
\beta=\beta_{D}(C)
. By the adjunction formula
C\cdot K_{X}=2g-2+e\geqslant e
, as
C\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By
[
Kol92
, 3.1.3]
we have
\operatorname{cf}(C)\geqslant\operatorname{cf}_{C}(C)=\frac{C\cdot K_{X}}{e}\geqslant 1
, which proves the first assertion.

Since
\kappa(K_{X})\leqslant\kappa(K_{\overline{X}})=-\infty
and
X\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the surface
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p\colon X\longrightarrow B
. Let
F
be a fiber of
p
. Since
C\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the curve
C
is horizontal, i.e.
C\cdot F\geqslant 1
. We have
C\cdot F\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(C)\,C\cdot F<2
by (
5
), so
C\cdot F=1
.
Suppose
D-C
has a horizontal component
C^{\prime}
. Since
B
has genus
g\geqslant 1
, we have
C^{\prime}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, so
C^{\prime}
is another section of
p
with
\operatorname{cf}(C^{\prime})\geqslant 1
. Thus
\operatorname{cf}(C)+\operatorname{cf}(C^{\prime})\geqslant 2
, a contradiction with the inequality (
5
).

It remains to prove that
2g+\beta<2+e
. Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{\beta}
be the components of
D
meeting
C
, let
R\coloneqq C+\sum_{i}V_{i}
and
\delta\coloneqq\sum_{i}\frac{1}{-V_{i}^{2}}
. Negative definiteness of
R
is equivalent to the inequality
e>\delta
. Using
[
Kol92
, 3.1.10]
we compute
\operatorname{cf}_{R}(C)=1-\frac{2-2g-\beta+\delta}{e-\delta}
. By
[
Kol92
, Lemma 3.1.3]
and (
5
) we have
\operatorname{cf}_{R}(C)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(C)<2
, so
e-\delta-2+2g+\beta-\delta<2e-2\delta
and therefore
2g+\beta<2+e
, as needed.

(c)
Part
(b)
implies that all components of
D
are smooth. Suppose
D
has two components
C_{1},C_{2}
such that
C_{1}\cdot C_{2}\geqslant 2
. If, say,
C_{1}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
then part
(b)
shows that
C_{1}
is a section and
C_{2}
is vertical, so
C_{1}\cdot C_{2}\leqslant 1
, which is false. Thus
C_{1},C_{2}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Because
D
is negative definite, we have, say,
C_{1}^{2}\leqslant-3
and
C_{2}^{2}\leqslant-2
. As before,
[
Kol92
, Lemma 3.1.3]
implies that
\operatorname{cf}(C_{i})\geqslant\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{i})=1
, where
E=E_{1}+E_{2}
,
E_{1}\cdot E_{2}=2
and
p_{a}(E_{i})=0
. Consider any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. If both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are horizontal then the inequality (
5
) fails. Thus, say,
C_{1}
is vertical. Then
C_{2}
is a multi-section, so by (
5
) and part
(a)
we have
\operatorname{cf}(C_{2})<1
, a contradiction.

Similarly, if
D
has three components
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
meeting normally at one point then applying
[
Kol92
, Lemma 3.1.3]
as before we see that
\operatorname{cf}(C_{i})\geqslant 1
for each
i
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. By the inequality (
5
) at most one of the
C_{i}
’s is horizontal, and it is a
1
-section, which is impossible, since it meets two components of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
.

(d)
Part
(a)
and formula (
4
) give
K_{X}\cdot L\leqslant\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}\cdot L=K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(L)<0
, since
-K_{\overline{X}}
is ample. By adjunction,
2p_{a}(L)-2=K_{X}\cdot L+L^{2}<L^{2}
, as needed.

∎

We will also need the following lemma.
Recall that given a reduced divisor
D
on a smooth surface with a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, we write
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
and
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
for horizontal and vertical part of
D
, see Section
2C
.

Lemma 2.8

(
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations on a minimal resolution of a surface of rank one)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal surface of Picard rank one, and let
\pi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
be its minimal log resolution. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, and let
F
be its degenerate fiber. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=\Sigma+1
, where
\Sigma
is as in Lemma
2.4
.

(b)

Assume that
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo. Then a component
L
of
F
is a
(-1)
-curve if and only if
L\not\subseteq D
.

(c)

Assume that a component
R
of
F
satisfies
R\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=F\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Then
R\subseteq D
and
F_{\mathrm{red}}
has exactly one component not contained in
D
.

Parts
(a)
and
(b)
are direct consequences of Lemmas
2.4
and
2.7
(d)
, proved in
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.6]
.

We prove part
(c)
. Put
F^{\prime}=F_{\mathrm{red}}-R
,
D^{\prime}=D-(F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D)
. Since
R\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=F\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}
,
F^{\prime}
is disjoint from
D^{\prime}
and
R
has multiplicity one in
F
. The first condition implies that
F^{\prime}+D^{\prime}
is negative definite, so by the Hodge index theorem
\#(F^{\prime}+D^{\prime})\leqslant\rho(X)-1=\rho(\overline{X})+\#D-1=\#D=\#(D-D\wedge R)+\#(F^{\prime}\wedge D)+\#D^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\#F^{\prime}-\#(F^{\prime}\wedge D)\leqslant\#(D-D\wedge R)\leqslant 1
.

Since
R
has multiplicity one in
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, by Lemma
2.3
(a)

F^{\prime}
contains a
(-1)
-curve, say
L
. If
L\subseteq D
then, since
\pi
is minimal, we have
\beta_{D}(L)\geqslant 3>\beta_{F^{\prime}}(L)
, so
L
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}\subseteq D^{\prime}
, which is false. Thus
L\not\subseteq D
, so
\#F^{\prime}-\#(F^{\prime}\wedge D)\geqslant 1
. It follows that
\#(D-D\wedge R)=1
, i.e.
R\subseteq D
, and
\#F^{\prime}-\#(F^{\prime}\wedge D)=1
, i.e.
L
is the unique component of
F^{\prime}
which is not contained in
D
, as needed.

∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(X,D)
be a minimal resolution of a log canonical del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
. Let
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a swap, see Definition
1.3
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The log surface
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a minimal log resolution of a log canonical surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
.

(b)

We have
\rho(\overline{X}^{\prime})=\rho(\overline{X})
.

(c)

For every component
T^{\prime}
of
D^{\prime}
we have
\operatorname{cf}_{D^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}_{D}(T)
, where
T
is the proper transform of
T^{\prime}
on
X
.

(d)

If
\rho(\overline{X})=1
then
\overline{X}^{\prime}
is a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one.

Denote the contraction of
D
by
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
. By induction, we can assume that the swap
\varphi\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is elementary.
Let
L,C
be as in Definition
1.3
(a)
. Let
B\neq[2]
be the component of
D-C
meeting
L
; put
B=0
if there is no such. Let
\widecheck{D}_{0}
be a connected component of
D+L
containing
L
, let
\widecheck{D}_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi(\widecheck{D}_{0})
, and let
D_{0}=\widecheck{D}_{0}-L
,
D^{\prime}_{0}=\widecheck{D}_{0}^{\prime}-\varphi(C)
; so
D_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}D_{0}\wedge D^{\prime}
and
\varphi
is an isomorphism near
D-D_{0}
. The weighted graph of
D_{0}^{\prime}
is obtained from the one of
D_{0}
by removing the vertex corresponding to
C
, and, if
B\neq 0
, by decreasing the weight of the vertex corresponding to
B
, which is at most
-3
.

(a)
It is enough to prove that every connected component of
D_{0}^{\prime}
is an admissible chain or an admissible fork. Suppose the contrary.
Then the above description shows that
D_{0}
has a connected component
V
which is not an admissible chain or fork. Let
G\in\{B,C\}
be a component of
V
meeting
L
. By the adjunction formula and by Lemma
2.7
(a)
we have
0>K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\pi(L)\geqslant-1+\operatorname{cf}(G)
. Thus
\operatorname{cf}(G)<1
. Since
\overline{X}
is log canonical, we get that
G
lies in a twig of a log canonical fork or bench. The claim now follows from the above description of
D_{0}^{\prime}
.

(b)
We have
\rho(\overline{X}^{\prime})=\rho(X^{\prime})-\#D^{\prime}=(\rho(X)-1)-(\#D-1)=\rho(X)-\#D=\rho(\overline{X})
, as claimed.

(c)
Part
(a)
implies that
\operatorname{cf}_{D^{\prime}}(\varphi(B))\leqslant 1
and that
D^{\prime}
is negative definite, in particular
d(D^{\prime})>0
. Now
(c)
follows from the above description of
D_{0}^{\prime}
and
[
Pal24
, Lemma 7.5]
, cf.
[
KM99
, L.1(2)]
.

(d)
Denote the contraction of
D^{\prime}
by
\pi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\overline{X}^{\prime}
. By
(a)
and
(c)
,
\overline{X}^{\prime}
is a log canonical surface of rank one. It remains to prove that
K_{\overline{X^{\prime}}}\cdot\overline{R}^{\prime}<0
for some curve
\overline{R}^{\prime}
. Take for
\overline{R}^{\prime}
any curve on
\overline{X}^{\prime}
other than
\pi^{\prime}(\varphi(C))
. Let
R^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime})^{-1}_{*}\overline{R}^{\prime}
,
R=\varphi^{*}R^{\prime}
and
\overline{R}=\pi_{*}R
, so
\overline{R}
is a nonzero, effective divisor on
\overline{X}
. Since
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo, we have
0>K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\overline{R}=\varphi_{*}\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}\cdot R^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.7
(a)
we have
\operatorname{cf}_{D}(C)\geqslant 0
. Hence part
(c)
implies that the divisor
\varphi_{*}\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-(\pi^{\prime})^{*}K_{\overline{X}^{\prime}}=\operatorname{cf}_{D}(C)\,\varphi(C)+\sum_{T\subseteq D-C}(\operatorname{cf}_{D}(T)-\operatorname{cf}_{D^{\prime}}(\varphi(T)))\,\varphi(T)
is effective. Since its support does not contain
R^{\prime}
, we infer that
\varphi_{*}\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}\cdot R^{\prime}\geqslant(\pi^{\prime})^{*}K_{\overline{X}}^{\prime}\cdot R^{\prime}=K_{\overline{X}^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{R}^{\prime}
. Hence
0>K_{\overline{X}^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{R}^{\prime}
, as needed.

∎

2E.
Inner and outer blowups, and basic properties of the logarithmic tangent sheaf

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface. Let
\varphi\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X,D)
be a blowup at a point
p\in D
, with
D^{\prime}=(\varphi^{*}D)_{\mathrm{red}}
. The blowup
\varphi
is called
inner
if
p\in\operatorname{Sing}D
and
outer
if
p\in D^{\mathrm{reg}}
, see
[
FKZ07
, Definitions 2.3]
.

To motivate the discussion here and in the next section, we outline a crucial observation underlying our proof of Propositions
C
,
D
. We will formalize it in Lemmas
2.18
and
2.19
below, after introducing suitable notation.

Observation 2.10

(Inner vs. outer blowups, cf. Example
2.13
)
.

Fix a log smooth surface
(X,D)
, and let
\varphi
be a blowup as above. If
\varphi
is inner, then the isomorphism class of
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
depends only on
(X,D)
and on a combinatorial datum: the edge of the weighted graph of
D
corresponding to the center
p
of
\varphi
. On the other hand, if
\varphi
is outer, then
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
depends also on a continuous datum: the position of
p
on the corresponding component of
D
, up to the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
.
Thus, from the viewpoint of our classification, constructions involving only inner blowups are easier, while outer blowups require some control of the automorphism groups.

We call a birational map
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\dashrightarrow(X,D)

inner
if it factors as a composition of inner blowups and blowdowns. We distinguish two useful classes of inner maps.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface such that
D
contains a
0
-curve
F
meeting exactly two components of
D-F
, once each. An
elementary transformation on
F
, see
[
FKZ07
, Definition 2.10]
, is an inner map
\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}^{-1}\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\dashrightarrow(X,D)
, where
\sigma_{1}
is a blowup at one of the two points in
F\cap(D-F)
, and
\sigma_{2}
is the contraction of
(\sigma_{1})^{-1}_{*}F
. Note that if
D
contains a subchain
[a,0,b]
, with
F
being the middle component, then the reduced total transform of this subchain in
D^{\prime}
is of type
[a+1,0,b-1]
or
[a-1,0,b+1]
, see
[
FKZ07
, §2.3]
.

For a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, a
vertical inner snc-minimalization

(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
is the contraction of vertical
(-1)
-curves of branching number
2
in
D
and its subsequent images so that
B
has no such curves.

Following
[
FZ94
, §1]
we now describe the effect of inner and outer blowups on the cohomology of the logarithmic tangent sheaf, and we compute the latter in some basic cases.

Lemma 2.11

(Change of
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
under a blowup)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface, let
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
be a blowup at a point
p\in X
. Let
D^{\prime}
be either
\varphi^{-1}_{*}D
or
(\varphi^{*}D)_{\mathrm{red}}
. Put
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
,
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime})
.

(a)

Let
r\in\{0,1,2\}
be the number of components of
D
containing
p
. We have an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(X^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(X,\mathcal{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\mathcal{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{ev}_{p}}

(b)

We have
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=h^{2}(\mathcal{T})
.

(c)

If
p\in\operatorname{Sing}D
, i.e.
\varphi
is inner, then
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=h^{i}(\mathcal{T})
for every
i
.

(d)

Assume that
p
is a smooth point of
D
, i.e.
\varphi
is outer. If every vector field on
X
tangent to
D
vanishes at
p
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=h^{1}(\mathcal{T})+1
and
h^{0}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=h^{0}(\mathcal{T})
, otherwise
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=h^{1}(\mathcal{T})
and
h^{0}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=h^{0}(\mathcal{T})-1
.

First, we note that by
[
FZ94
, Proposition 1.7(3)]
removing the
(-1)
-curve from
D^{\prime}
does not change
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))
, see Lemma
2.14
(c)
below, so we can assume
D^{\prime}=\varphi^{*}D_{\mathrm{red}}
. A direct computation, see Lemma 1.5(3) loc. cit. or
[
Har10
, Exercise 10.5]
gives an exact sequence of sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k}^{r}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{ev}_{p}}

The lemma follows from the induced exact sequence in cohomology.

∎

Remark 2.12

(Extension of Lemma
2.11
(d)
in case
{k}=\mathbb{C}
)
.

Assume
{k}=\mathbb{C}
. Let
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X,D)
be an outer blowup at
p\in D
as in Lemma
2.11
(d)
, and let
U
be the orbit of
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
containing
p
, so
\dim U\in\{0,1\}
. If
\dim U=1
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
, otherwise
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))+1
.

Indeed, if
\dim U=1
then then the differential at
g=\mathrm{id}
of the morphism
\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)\ni g\mapsto g(p)\in X
is nonzero, hence its image contains a vector field tangent to
D
which does not vanish at
p
, so
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
by Lemma
2.11
(d)
. Conversely, if such a vector field exists then its flow yields a one-dimensional family of biholomorphisms of
(X,D)
, which are regular since
X
is projective, and move
p
, as needed.

This growth of
h^{1}
matches the growth of dimensions of representing families constructed in Lemma
2.19
, see Observation
2.10
and Figure
8
. The following Example
2.13
illustrates the failure of the above statement in positive characteristic, which leads to exceptions in Proposition
D
.

Example 2.13

(Uniqueness and change of
h^{1}
under outer blowups, see Figure
6
)
.

To illustrate Observation
2.10
and Remark
2.12
, we now follow the construction of minimal resolutions of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and types
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
\mathrm{A}_{7}
, and
\mathrm{D}_{8}
, cf. Figure
16
. We outline the computation of
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
and the classification of the resulting surfaces up to an isomorphism: they are unique except for type
\mathrm{D}_{8}
in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, when there is a one-dimensional family of pairwise non-isomorphic ones, see Table
15
. This is a particular instance of Lemma
4.7
, in cases when
\mathcal{Z}
is as in
4.6
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3)
,
(10)
, see Figure
17
and Table
1
.

As a consequence, we will see that if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
the sets
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{A}_{7})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{D}_{8})
are not represented by almost universal families in the sense of Definition
1.10
. Indeed, the surface of type
\mathrm{A}_{7}
is unique up to an isomorphism, but its minimal log resolution has
h^{1}=1
. Similarly, there are infinitely many pairwise non-isomorphic surfaces of type
\mathrm{D}_{8}
, one of which has
h^{1}=2
and the others have
h^{1}=1
. These exceptional cases of Proposition
D
(c)
and
(b)
are listed in Table
14
as
4.4
(6)
with
T,T_{i}=[2]
,
m,r_{i}=2
; and
4.4
(19)
with
T=[2]
,
r,m=2
.
