0
0
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
(3,0)
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
(3,0)
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
(2,0)
\mathrm{A}_{7}
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2:(1,0)
\operatorname{char}{k}=2:(2,1)
\mathrm{D}_{8}
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2:(0,0)
\operatorname{char}{k}=2:(2,2)
or
(1,1)

Figure 6
.
Example
2.13
:
(h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)),h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)))
after subsequent blowups.

We will define a sequence of log surfaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{0},\widecheck{D}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\widecheck{D}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
,
where each
\widecheck{D}_{j+1}
is the reduced total transform of
\widecheck{D}_{j}
; each
X_{j}
for
j=1,2,3,4
is a minimal resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one and type
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
\mathrm{A}_{7}
,
\mathrm{D}_{8}
, respectively, with exceptional divisor
D_{j}
equal to
\widecheck{D}_{j}
with all
(-1)
-curves removed. Put
h^{i}_{j}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{j}}(-\log\widecheck{D}_{j}))
. Then
h^{i}_{j}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{j}}(-\log D_{j}))
by Lemma
2.14
(c)
.

We start with
X_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
D_{0}=V_{0}+H+V_{\infty}
, where
V_{0}=\{x=0\}
,
V_{\infty}=\{x=\infty\}
are vertical lines, and
H=\{y=0\}
is a horizontal line. Clearly, such
(X_{0},D_{0})
is unique up to an isomorphism. We have
h^{0}_{0}=3
,
h^{1}_{0}=0
. In fact, every
\xi\in H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{0}}(-\log D_{0}))
is of the form
ax\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+by\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+cy^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
for some
a,b,c\in{k}
. We denote such
\xi
, or its lift to
X_{j}
, by
\xi(a,b,c)
. The connected component of
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{0},D_{0})
is generated by
G_{1}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\mapsto(\lambda x,y):\lambda\in{k}^{*}\}
,
G_{2}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\mapsto(x,\lambda y):\lambda\in{k}^{*}\}
and
G_{3}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\mapsto(x,\frac{y}{1+\lambda y}):\lambda\in{k}\}
.

Let
\varphi_{1}
be a blowup at each
V_{z}\cap H
,
z=0,\infty
, and at its infinitely near point on the proper transform of
V_{z}
. It is an inner morphism, so
(h^{0}_{1},h^{1}_{1})=(h^{0}_{0},h^{1}_{0})=(3,0)
by Lemma
2.11
(c)
, and we can identify
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{1},D_{1})
with
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{0},D_{0})
. Let
V_{z}^{\prime}=(\varphi_{1}^{-1})_{*}V_{z}
, let
E_{z}
be the
(-1)
-curve in the fiber over
z
, and let
E_{z}^{\circ}=E_{z}\setminus(D_{1}-E_{z})
. In local coordinates
(x,y)
with
V_{0}^{\prime}=\{x=0\}
,
E_{0}=\{y=0\}
we have
\varphi_{1}(x,y)=(xy^{2},y)
. A computation in these local coordinates shows that the vector field
\xi(a,b,c)
vanishes along
E_{0}
if and only if
a=2b
. Similarly,
\xi(a,b,c)
vanishes along
E_{\infty}
if and only if
a=2c
. These two conditions are nontrivial; they are linearly independent if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, and equivalent to
a=0
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Since
G_{3}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
, it acts trivially on
E_{0}^{\circ},E_{\infty}^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
; and a computation shows that the subgroup generated by
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
acts transitively on
E_{0}^{\circ}\times E_{\infty}^{\circ}
.

Fix
p_{0}\in E_{0}^{\circ}
,
p_{\infty}\in E_{\infty}^{\circ}
. Let
\varphi_{2}
be a blowup at
p_{0}
, and let
\varphi_{3}
be a blowup at
\varphi_{2}^{-1}(p_{\infty})
. Lemma
2.11
(a)
and the above computation give
(h^{0}_{2},h^{1}_{2})=(2,0)
, and subsequently
(h^{0}_{3},h^{1}_{3})=(1,0)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and
(2,1)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. The resulting log surfaces
(X_{2},D_{2})
and
(X_{3},D_{3})
are unique up to an isomorphism.

Let
E\subseteq D_{3}
be a
(-1)
-curve in the fiber over
0
, let
E^{\circ}\coloneqq E\setminus(D_{3}-E)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
. We can choose
p\in E^{\circ}
and local coordinates
(x,y)
at
p
such that
E^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
and
\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{3}(x,y)=(xy+1,y)
. The connected component of
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{3},D_{3})
can be identified with
G_{3}
, acting in the above coordinates by
(x,y)\mapsto((1+\lambda y)((1+\lambda y)^{2}x+\lambda^{2}y+2\lambda),\frac{y}{1+\lambda y})
. Thus
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{3},D_{3})
acts transitively on
E^{\circ}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and trivially if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.

If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, we compute that the vector field
\xi(2b,b,b)
on
X_{1}
lifts to
-b(3xy+x+2)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+by(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, so it vanishes along
E^{\circ}
if and only if
b=0
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
then the vector field
\xi(0,b,c)
lifts to
bx(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(by+cy^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, so it always vanishes at
p
, and vanishes along
E^{\circ}
if and only if
b=0
.

Let
\varphi_{4}
be a blowup at
q\in E^{\circ}
. By Observation
2.10
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
we get a unique log surface
(X_{4},D_{4})
, and if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
we get a family of pairwise non-isomorphic ones, parametrized by the choice of
q\in E^{\circ}
.
By Lemma
2.11
(a)
we have
(h^{0}_{4},h^{1}_{4})=(h^{0}_{3},h^{1}_{3}+1)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
q=p
and
(h^{0}_{4},h^{1}_{4})=(h^{0}_{3}-1,h^{1}_{3})
otherwise; so
(h^{0}_{4},h^{1}_{4})
equals
(2,2)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
q=p
;
(1,1)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
q\neq p
; and
(0,0)
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
.

Lemma 2.14

(Elementary properties of
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface.

(a)

For a component
C
of
D
, denote by
\mathcal{N}_{C}
be the normal bundle to
C
. We have exact sequences

\begin{split}0\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)&\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log(D-C))\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{C}\longrightarrow 0,\quad\mbox{and}\\
0\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)&\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}_{C_{i}}\longrightarrow 0\quad\mbox{where }C_{1},\dots,C_{k}\mbox{ are the components of }D.\end{split}

(b)

If
C\subseteq D
is an
m
-curve for some
m\geqslant 0
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))\geqslant h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D-C))
.

(c)

If
C\subseteq D
is a
(-1)
-curve then
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D-C))
.

(d)

We have
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log D))=0
for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
in the following cases:

(i)

X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
D
is a sum of four lines in a general position.

(ii)

X=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D
is a sum of two fibers, a horizontal line, and a diagonal.

(iii)

X=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D
is a sum of
3
horizontal and
3
vertical lines.

(e)

Let
X
be the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
for some
m\geqslant 0
, and let
D
be a sum of
v
fibers, a negative section
\Xi_{m}
, and possibly a section disjoint from
\Xi_{m}
. Then
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=\max\{0,v-3\}
.

(f)

We have
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(2K_{X}+D))
.

(g)

If
X
has a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration with a fiber
F
such that
F\cdot D\leqslant 3
and
F
meets
D
normally then
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
.

The first exact sequence in
(a)
follows from the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)
, the second one follows from the first by induction. For
(b)
and
(c)
note that
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)
, so
h^{i}(\mathcal{N}_{C})=0
for
i>0
in case
(b)
and
i\geqslant 0
in case
(c)
; so the assertions follow from
(a)
. To prove
(d)
, we compute directly that in each case
h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
and
h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=\sum_{i}(C_{i}^{2}+1)=\sum_{i}h^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{C_{i}})
, so by
(a)
for
i>0
we have
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=0
.

Let
(X,D)
be as in
(e)
. Put
h=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant 2
. If
m=0
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2-h))+h^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2-v))=h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(h-4))+h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(v-4))=\max\{0,v-3\}
, and similarly
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
, as needed.

Assume
m\geqslant 1
. Let
F
be a fiber in
D
. If
h=2
write
\{p\}=F\cap(D-\Xi_{m})
; if
h=1
let
p
be any point of
F^{\circ}\coloneqq F\setminus\Xi_{m}
. Let
(X,D)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the elementary transformation given by blowing up
p
and contracting the proper transform of
F
. Then
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is as in
(e)
, too, with
m
replaced by
m-1
. If
h=1
, it is easy to see that
there is a vertical vector field which vanishes only along
\Xi_{m}
and some fiber. Thus in any case Lemma
2.11
gives
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, so
(e)
follows by induction on
m
.

Parts
(f)
and
(g)
are proved in
[
FZ94
, Proposition 6.2]
, as follows. Let
\Omega^{k}(\log D)
be the sheaf of
k
-forms with logarithmic poles along
D
. We have a perfect pairing
\Omega^{1}(\log D)\times\Omega^{1}(\log D)\longrightarrow\Omega^{2}(\log D)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}+D)
. Thus
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)^{\vee}=\Omega^{1}(\log D)=\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}+D)
and
(f)
follows from Serre duality.

Let
F
be as in
(g)
,
\xi\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(2K_{X}+D))
. The sheaf
\mathcal{N}_{F}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(2K_{X}+D)|_{F}\cong\mathcal{O}_{F}(F\cdot D-4)
has no global sections, so by
(a)

\xi|_{F}\in H^{0}(F,\mathcal{T}_{F}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(2K_{X}+D)|_{F})=H^{0}(F,\mathcal{O}_{F}(F\cdot D-2))
. Since
F
meets
D
normally,
\xi|_{F}
has
F\cdot D
zeros, so
\xi|_{F}=0
. Since
F
is a general fiber we get
\xi=0
, as needed.

∎

2F.
Representing families and moduli

Recall that Proposition
C
asserts that there is a smooth family whose fibers are precisely all minimal log resolutions
(X,D)
of del Pezzo surfaces of a fixed singularity type and small height; and Proposition
D
allows to choose such a family related to infinitesimal deformations of
(X,D)
. In this section, we introduce some technical language which will be convenient to inductively construct such families.

First, we explain our motivation. In the proof of Theorem
A
, we will construct vertical swaps to certain simple log surfaces, shown in Figure
2
. To prove Propositions
C
and
D
we will apply applying Observation
2.10
to each elementary vertical swap. The technical issue here is that the combinatorial data in
2.10
, i.e. the vertex or edge of the weighted graph where we need to blow up, is determined by the weighted graph of
D
only up to the action of a certain symmetry group
G
. Therefore, we will use
G
-equivariant blowups
and construct
G
-faithful families
. This approach requires to keep a consistent order of vertices of our graphs, which is possible due to the irreducibility assumption in Definition
1.9
(a)
. Roughly speaking, once we forget about this order, a
G
-faithful family will become
almost faithful
in the sense of Definition
1.9
(d)
.

Now we introduce precise definitions. A
combinatorial type
is a pair
(\Gamma,c)
, where
\Gamma
is a weighted graph as in Section
2A
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
. For a log surface
(X,D)
with smooth
X
and reduced boundary
D
its combinatorial type consists of the weighted graph of
D
(
without
the fixed identification of vertices and components of
D
) and the degree of the top Chern class of
X
. We will sometimes abuse the notation and use the same letter for a singularity type of a del Pezzo surface of rank one and the combinatorial type of its minimal log resolution.

Let
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq(\Gamma,c)
be a combinatorial type. We denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
the set of isomorphism classes of log surfaces of combinatorial type
\mathcal{S}
, modulo an isomorphism. We write
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})\coloneqq\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)
.

To study families of log smooth surfaces we need slightly more information than the combinatorial type, namely, a consistent way to enumerate boundary components. To this end, we define
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
as the set of triples
(X,D,\gamma)
such that
(X,D)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
and
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(D)
is an isomorphism of weighted graphs, modulo the following equivalence relation: we say that
(X_{1},D_{1},\gamma_{1})
is
equivalent to

(X_{2},D_{2},\gamma_{2})
if there is an isomorphism
(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
of log surfaces such that the induced isomorphism of weighted graphs, see Section
2A
, equals
\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}^{-1}
. In other words, the additional datum
\gamma
enumerates the components (and singular points) of
D
, and in
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
we identify two log surfaces if some isomorphism between them preserves that fixed order.

Given a triple
(X,D,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
, a vertex
v
and an edge
e
of
\Gamma
, we denote by
D^{v}
the component of
D
corresponding to the vertex
\gamma(v)
, and by
D^{e}
the singular point of
D
corresponding to the edge
\gamma(e)
.

Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family whose fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
are log smooth surfaces. For a component
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{D}
write
C_{b}=\mathcal{C}|_{X_{b}}
. By Definition
1.9
(c)
,
C_{b}
is an irreducible component of
D_{b}
.

As in Section
2A
, we define the weighted graph
\Gamma(f)
together with a bijection
v\mapsto\mathcal{D}^{v}
from the set of its vertices to the set of components of
\mathcal{D}
, such that the weight of
\mathcal{D}^{v}
equals
((D^{v}_{b})^{2},p_{a}(D^{v}_{b}))
, with two vertices
v,w
being connected by
(D^{v}_{b}\cdot D^{w}_{b})
edges of weight
1
, for some
b\in B
. Put
\mathcal{S}(f)=(\Gamma(f),c_{2}(X_{b}))
.

The combinatorial type
\mathcal{S}(f)
is well defined, i.e. does not depend on
b\in B
. Indeed, for any two components
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{D}
, and any
b\in B
, we have
X_{b}\cdot\mathcal{C}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=C_{b}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=C_{b}\cdot C_{b}^{\prime}
, where the second equality follows from the projection formula for the embedding
X_{b}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
. Since
f
is flat, the fibers
X_{b}
are numerically equivalent, so the number
C_{b}\cdot C_{b}^{\prime}
does not depend on
b\in B
. Since the restriction
f|_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow B
is flat, the arithmetic genus of
C_{b}
does not depend on
b\in B
, either
[
Har77
, Corollary III.9.10]
. Hence
\Gamma(f)
. Similarly, using adjunction we have
X_{b}\cdot K_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=K_{X_{b}}\cdot K_{\mathcal{X}}=K_{X_{b}}^{2}
, so
K_{X_{b}}^{2}
does not depend on
b\in B
, and since
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X_{b}})
does not depend on
b\in B
, either, by Noether’s formula neither does the number
c_{2}(X_{b})
, as claimed.

We can now relate families of del Pezzo surfaces to families of their minimal log resolutions.

Lemma 2.15

(Fiberwise contractions give a global contraction, cf.
[
Wah76
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family. Assume that for every
b\in B
, we have a contraction
\pi_{b}\colon X_{b}\longrightarrow\overline{X}_{b}
such that
D_{b}=\operatorname{Exc}\pi_{b}
and
\overline{X}_{b}
is a del Pezzo surface.
Then there is a factorization
f=\overline{f}\circ\pi
, where
\pi|_{f^{-1}(b)}=\pi_{b}
for every
b\in B
.

Since
\Gamma(f)
is negative definite, for each vertex
v
of
\Gamma(f)
we have a rational number
\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)
such that
\pi_{b}^{*}K_{\overline{X}_{b}}=K_{X_{b}}+\sum_{v}\operatorname{cf}_{\Gamma}(v)\,D_{b}^{v}
for all
b\in B
, see Section
2B
. Put
\mathcal{H}=-K_{\mathcal{X}}-\sum_{v}\operatorname{cf}(v)\,\mathcal{D}^{v}
. By adjunction
K_{X_{b}}=K_{\mathcal{X}}|_{X_{b}}
, see
[
Har77
, Proposition II.8.20]
, so
\pi_{b}^{*}K_{\overline{X}_{b}}=-\mathcal{H}|_{X_{b}}
. Since
\overline{X}_{b}
is del Pezzo, it follows that
\mathcal{H}|_{X_{b}}
is
\pi_{b}
-semi-ample. Thus for every
b\in B
, the linear system
|m_{b}\mathcal{H}|_{X_{b}}|
is base point free for some
m_{b}\geqslant 1
. Since
B
is Noetherian,
|m\mathcal{H}|
is base point free for some
m\geqslant 1
. Let
\Phi_{|m\mathcal{H}|}\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}
be the induced morphism, let
\pi=(\Phi_{|m\mathcal{H}|},f)\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}\times B
, and let
\overline{f}
be the restriction of the second projection to the image of
\pi
. Replacing
\pi
by its Stein factorization we can assume that
\pi
has connected fibers. Now the restriction
\pi|_{X_{b}}
contracts precisely the curves which intersect
m\mathcal{H}|_{X_{b}}=-m\pi_{b}^{*}K_{X_{b}}
trivially, so
\operatorname{Exc}\pi|_{X_{b}}=D_{b}
, as needed.

∎We remark that the morphism
\pi
from Lemma
2.15
above is a very weak simultaneous resolution of
\overline{f}
, see
[
Tei77
,
KSB88
]
. If the surfaces
\overline{X}_{b}
are log terminal then
\pi
can be obtained by an
f
-MMP run for
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})
.

We now continue with abstract definitions. Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family of log surfaces, and let
\mathcal{S}(f)=(\Gamma(f),c_{2})
be the combinatorial type of its fibers. For
b\in B
let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{b}\colon\Gamma(f)\longrightarrow\Gamma(D_{b})
be the isomorphism of weighted graphs mapping a vertex
v
to the vertex corresponding to
D^{v}_{b}
. Given an isomorphism of weighted graphs
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(f)
write
\gamma_{b}\coloneqq\widetilde{\gamma}_{b}\circ\gamma
for
b\in B
. We call the equivalence class
(X_{b},D_{b},\gamma_{b})\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
a
fiber of
f
over
b
with respect to
\gamma
, and we skip
\gamma
whenever it is clear from the context.

Note that the definition of a fiber
(X_{b},D_{b},\gamma_{b})
depends on the choice of
\gamma
. On the other hand, the equivalence of two such fibers does not. Indeed, the fibers of
f
over
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
with respect to
\gamma
are equivalent if and only if there is an isomorphism
(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
whose induced isomorphism of weighted graphs equals
\gamma_{b_{2}}\circ\gamma_{b_{1}}^{-1}=(\widetilde{\gamma}_{b_{2}}\circ\gamma)\circ(\widetilde{\gamma}_{b_{1}}\circ\gamma)^{-1}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{b_{2}}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{b_{1}}^{-1}
, and the latter expression does not depend on
\gamma
.

Let
\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f,\gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
be the set of equivalence classes of fibers
(X_{b},D_{b},\gamma_{b})
,
b\in B
. We say that the family
f

represents
a subset
\mathcal{R}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
if
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f,\gamma)
for some isomorphism
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(f)
. We say that
f
is
faithful
if its fibers over distinct points of
B
are non-equivalent.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the image of
\mathcal{R}_{+}
by the forgetful map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
. Note that if
f
represents
\mathcal{R}_{+}
then it represents
\mathcal{R}
in the sense of Definition
1.9
(c)
. We say that
f
is
h^{1}
-stratified
if it is
h^{1}
-stratified as a family representing
\mathcal{R}
, see Definition
1.10
(a)
, and the fibers over each stratum are pairwise equivalent. In this case, the numbers
\#\mathcal{R}_{+}
and
\#\mathcal{R}
are both equal to the dimension of the base. We say that a faithful family
f
is
universal
if at every point of the base its formal germ is semiuniversal.

The following example illustrates the difference between
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
; all non-trivial examples in this article will be constructed in a similar way.

Example 2.16

(Varying the fourth fiber on
\mathbb{F}_{0}
)
.

Denote by
H_{z}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{z\}
,
V_{z}\coloneqq\{z\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
z\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
the horizontal and vertical lines on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Put
B=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
. For any
b\in B
, the log surface
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},H_{0}+H_{\infty}+V_{0}+V_{1}+V_{\infty}+V_{b})
has combinatorial type
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq(\Gamma,4)
, where
\Gamma
is a graph with six vertices
h_{0},h_{\infty},v_{0},v_{1},v_{\infty},v_{*}
of weight
0
, and eight edges connecting
h_{0}
,
h_{\infty}
with
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
,
v_{\infty}
,
v_{*}
, see Figure
7
.

V_{0}
V_{1}
V_{\infty}
V_{\star}\!\leftarrow\!
moves
H_{0}
H_{\infty}
0
1
\infty
\star\!\leftarrow\!
moves
\mathbb{P}^{1}
h_{0}
h_{\infty}
v_{0}
v_{1}
v_{\infty}
v_{\star}
\Gamma

Figure 7
.
Example
2.16
.

Put
\mathcal{X}=B\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{\infty}+\mathcal{V}_{0}+\mathcal{V}_{1}+\mathcal{V}_{\infty}+\mathcal{V}_{\star}
, where
\mathcal{H}=B\times H_{z}
,
\mathcal{V}_{z}=B\times V_{z}
, and
\mathcal{V}_{\star}=\{(z,z)\in B\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The projection
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
is a faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
. Indeed, define
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(f)
so that
\gamma(v_{z})
,
\gamma(h_{z})
correspond to
\mathcal{V}_{z}
,
\mathcal{H}_{z}
. Fix
(X,D,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
. Let
V_{z}=D^{v_{z}}
,
z\in\{0,1,\infty,\star\}
,
H_{w}=D^{h_{w}}
,
w\in\{0,\infty\}
. Then
|V_{0}|
and
|H_{0}|
induce two
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of
X
, so
X
is a rational surface of rank
c_{2}(X)-2=2
, and since
X
admits two
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations, we have
X\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
\operatorname{pr}_{1},\operatorname{pr}_{2}
be the two projections
X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We can choose coordinates on each factor of
X
such that
\operatorname{pr}_{1}(V_{z})=z
for
z\in\{0,1,\infty\}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{2}(H_{w})=w
for
w\in\{0,\infty\}
. This way,
(X,D,\gamma)
gets identified with the fiber of
f
over
\operatorname{pr}_{1}(V_{\star})\in B
.

We will see in Example
2.17
that
f
, viewed as a family representing
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, is almost faithful, see Definition
1.9
(d)
. In fact, two fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
and
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
are isomorphic if and only if
b
and
b^{\prime}
lie in the same orbit of
\operatorname{Aut}(B)\cong S_{3}
. As a consequence, the restriction of
f
over
B\setminus\{b\}
for any
b\in B
still represents
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
in the sense of Definition
1.9
(c)
: indeed, the missing fiber
(X_{b},D_{b})
is isomorphic to
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
for any
b^{\prime}\neq b
in the same
S_{3}
-orbit; such
b^{\prime}
exists since the
S_{3}
-action on
B
has no fixed point. However, since
f
is faithful, the fiber
(X_{b},D_{b},\gamma_{b})
is not equivalent to
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}},\gamma_{b^{\prime}})
for any
b^{\prime}\neq b
, so the restriction of
f
over
B\setminus\{b\}
does not represent
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
in the sense of definitions above.

Eventually, we note that
f
is universal. By Lemma
2.14
(e)
for each
b\in B
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))=1=\dim B
, so it is enough to prove that
f
is formally versal at
b
. Let
\widecheck{f}\colon(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow T
be a formal deformation of
(X_{b},D_{b})
. Write
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}=\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{0}+\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{\infty}+\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{0}+\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{1}+\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{\infty}+\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{\star}
so that
\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{w}|_{X_{b}}=H_{w}
for
w\in\{0,\infty\}
and
\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{z}|_{X_{b}}=V_{z}
for
z\in\{0,1,\infty\}
. Then
\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{\star}|_{X_{b}}=V_{b}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is rigid, cf.
[
Har77
, Exercise III.9.10]
, we have an isomorphism
\psi\colon\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}\longrightarrow T\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}\times_{T}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}
. As before, we choose coordinates on each factor
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}
so that
\operatorname{pr}_{1}(\psi(\widecheck{\mathcal{V}}_{z}))=z
for
z=0,1,\infty
and
\operatorname{pr}_{2}(\psi(\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{w}))=w
for
w=0,\infty
: note that now we change coordinates over
T
, i.e. using matrices whose entries are regular functions on
T
. The point
\operatorname{pr}_{1}(\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\star}))\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
is given by a smooth morphism
\alpha\colon T\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}=B
,
and
\widecheck{f}
is the pullback of
f
through
\alpha
, as claimed.

The notions of an inner and outer blowup, reviewed in Section
2E
, extend naturally to combinatorial types. More precisely, fix a combinatorial type
\mathcal{S}=(\Gamma,c_{2})
and a vertex
v
(respectively, an edge
e
joining
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
) of
\Gamma
. We define an
outer
(respectively,
inner
) blowup
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}

at
v
(respectively,
at
e
) by putting
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},c_{2}+1)
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by reducing the weight of
v
(respectively, of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
) by
1
, adding a new vertex
w
of weight
-1
, and adding an edge between
w
and
v
(respectively, replacing
e
by edges joining
w
with
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
).
We call the inverse operation an
outer
(respectively,
inner
)
blowdown
of the vertex
w
.

A blowup
\beta\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
as above yields a surjection
\beta_{+}\colon\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
given by
\beta_{+}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})=(X,D,\gamma)
, where
(X,D)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
is a log surface obtained from
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
by contracting the
(-1)
-curve
(D^{\prime})^{w}
, and the isomorphism
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(D)
is given (on vertices) by the restriction of
\gamma^{\prime}
. If
\beta
is an inner blowup at an edge
e
then
\beta_{+}
is bijective: indeed,
\beta_{+}^{-1}
associates to each
(X,D,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
the log surface
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
obtained by blowing up the point
D^{e}
; together with the unique isomorphism
\gamma^{\prime}
extending
\gamma
. If
\beta
is an outer blowup at a vertex
v
, then the elements of the fiber
\beta_{+}^{-1}(X,D,\gamma)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the orbits of the action on
D^{v}\setminus(D-D^{v})
of the group of those automorphisms of
X
which fix
D
componentwise. Indeed, fix
(X_{i}^{\prime},D_{i}^{\prime},\gamma_{i}^{\prime})\in\beta_{+}^{-1}(X,D,\gamma)
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\beta_{i}\colon(X_{i},D_{i})\longrightarrow(X,D)
be the contraction of
(D^{\prime}_{i})^{w}
and let
p_{i}=\beta((D^{\prime}_{i})^{w})\in D^{v}\setminus(D-D^{v})
. Since
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
restricts to
\gamma
, we have
\beta_{i}((D_{i}^{\prime})^{q})=D^{q}
for every vertex
q
of
\Gamma
. If
(X^{\prime}_{i},D^{\prime}_{i},\gamma_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
are equivalent then there is an isomorphism
\alpha^{\prime}\colon(X_{1}^{\prime},D_{1}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X_{2}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime})
such that
\alpha^{\prime}((D_{1}^{\prime})^{w})=(D_{2}^{\prime})^{w}
and
\alpha^{\prime}((D_{1}^{\prime})^{q})=(D_{2}^{\prime})^{q}
for each
q
, so
\alpha^{\prime}
descends to
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
such that
\alpha(p_{1})=p_{2}
and
\alpha(D^{q})=D^{q}
for each
q
. Conversely, any such
\alpha
lifts to an equivalence between
(X_{i},D_{i},\gamma_{i}^{\prime})
, as claimed.

Let
G
be a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
. A
G
-equivariant blowup
at an edge
e
(respectively, at a vertex
v
) is a composition
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
of blowups at
ge
(respectively, at
gv
) for all
g\in G
. Note that each element of
G
uniquely extends to an automorphism of the new graph, so we can identify
G
with a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
and unambiguously speak about
G
-equivariant morphisms or birational maps.

Given two elements
(X_{i},D_{i},\gamma_{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
and an isomorphism of log surfaces
\varphi\colon(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
we put
\varphi_{+}\coloneqq\gamma_{2}^{-1}\circ\Gamma(\varphi)\circ\gamma_{1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
, where
\Gamma(\varphi)\colon\Gamma(D_{1})\longrightarrow\Gamma(D_{2})
is the induced isomorphism of weighted graphs, see Section
2A
. Then
\varphi(D_{1}^{v})=D_{2}^{\varphi_{+}(v)}
for each vertex
v
.

Fix
\mathcal{R}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
, and let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be a family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}
, i.e.
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f,\gamma)
for some isomorphism
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(f)
. We denote by
\operatorname{Aut}(f)\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})
the group of automorphisms mapping fibers of
f
to fibers of
f
. Any
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(f)
induces an automorphism of
B
, denoted by the same letter; and an element
\alpha_{+}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
given by
\alpha(\mathcal{D}^{\gamma(v)})=\mathcal{D}^{\gamma(\alpha_{+}(v))}
for each vertex
v
of
\Gamma
. Note that for every
b\in B
,
\alpha_{+}
is equal to the element
(\alpha|_{X_{b}})_{+}
of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
associated to the isomorphism
\alpha|_{X_{b}}\colon(X_{b},D_{b})\longrightarrow(X_{\alpha(b)},D_{\alpha(b)})
.

We say that
f
is
G
-faithful
if there is a finite subgroup
G_{f}\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(f)
whose image in
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
equals
G
, such that for every isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
with
\varphi_{+}\in G
there is
\alpha\in G_{f}
such that
\alpha_{+}=\varphi_{+}
and
\alpha(b_{1})=b_{2}
. As before, a
G
-faithful family is
universal
if it is formally semiuniversal at each
b\in B
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is the image of
\mathcal{R}_{+}
by the forgetful map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, then an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
-faithful family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}
is an almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{R}
in the sense of Definition
1.9
(d)
.

Example 2.17

(Varying the fourth fiber on
\mathbb{F}_{0}
, continued)
.

As in Example
2.16
, let
\mathcal{S}
be the combinatorial type of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
with two horizontal and four vertical lines. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})=\mathbb{Z}/2\times S_{4}
. Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be the faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
constructed in Example
2.16
. We claim that
f
is
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
-faithful, and therefore
f
is an almost faithful as a family representing
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
in the sense of Definition
1.9
(d)
.

To see this, we define actions of
\mathbb{Z}/2
and
S_{4}
on
B=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
and
\mathcal{X}=B\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, as follows. For a generator
\tau
of
\mathbb{Z}/2
, we put
\tau(b)=b
for
b\in B
and
\tau(b,x,y)=(b,x,y^{-1})
for
(b,x,y)\in\mathcal{X}
. To define the
S_{4}
-action, we use the following notation. Put
x_{1}=\infty
,
x_{2}=0
,
x_{3}=1
, and for
b\in B
put
x_{i}^{b}=x_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
x_{4}^{b}=b
, so
b
is the cross-ratio of
(x_{1}^{b},x_{2}^{b},x_{3}^{b},x_{4}^{b})
. Now for
\sigma\in S_{4}
,
b\in B
define
\sigma_{b}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
by
\sigma_{b}(x_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}^{b})=x_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and let
\sigma(b)\coloneqq\sigma_{b}(x_{\sigma^{-1}(4)}^{b})
. Then
\sigma_{b}(x_{i}^{b})=x_{\sigma(i)}^{\sigma(b)}
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, so
\sigma(b)
is the cross-ratio of
(x_{\sigma(1)}^{\sigma(b)},x_{\sigma(2)}^{\sigma(b)},x_{\sigma(3)}^{\sigma(b)},x_{\sigma(4)}^{\sigma(b)})
. Hence the formula
\sigma(b,x,y)=(\sigma(b),\sigma_{b}(x),y)
defines the required element of
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
.

For an isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
, the induced element
\varphi_{+}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
is given by the permutation
\tau
of
\{0,\infty\}
and
\sigma
of
\{0,1,\infty,\star\}
such that
\varphi(H_{z})=H_{\tau(z)}
and
\varphi(\mathcal{V}_{z}|_{X_{b_{1}}})=\mathcal{V}_{\sigma(z)}|_{X_{b_{2}}}
. In this case, the cross-ratios of
(x_{1}^{b_{1}},x_{2}^{b_{1}},x_{3}^{b_{1}},x_{4}^{b_{1}})
and
(x_{\sigma(1)}^{b_{2}},x_{\sigma(2)}^{b_{2}},x_{\sigma(3)}^{b_{2}},x_{\sigma(4)}^{b_{2}})
are equal, so
b_{2}=\sigma(b_{1})
, as needed.

We now explain how to lift a family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
by inner and outer blowups
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
, thus making Observation
2.10
precise. For most applications we will take
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
, so
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.18

(Inner blowups)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a combinatorial type, let
G
be a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
, and let
\beta\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a
G
-equivariant inner blowup.
Fix
\mathcal{R}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
and let
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}=\beta_{+}^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{+})
. Then
\#\mathcal{R}_{+}=\#\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{+}
is represented by a (
G
-faithful, universal, or (
h^{1}
)-stratified) family if and only if the same holds for
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
. Moreover, both families can be chosen having the same base, and the same stratification in the stratified case.

Assume first that
\mathcal{R}_{+}
is represented by a family
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
, i.e.
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f,\gamma)
for some
\gamma
. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a blowup at
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma(e)}
for each blown up edge
e
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\pi^{*}\mathcal{D})_{\mathrm{red}}
, let
\gamma^{\prime}
be the unique isomorphism extending
\gamma
, and let
f^{\prime}\coloneqq f\circ\pi\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
, so
f^{\prime}
is a family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
, and if
f
is
G
-faithful then so is
f^{\prime}
. Conversely, let
f^{\prime}\colon(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow B
be a (
G
-faithful) family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
, so
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
, and let
\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\sum_{v}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})^{\gamma^{\prime}(v)}
, where the sum runs over the new vertices. Then
\mathcal{E}
is a disjoint union of families of
(-1)
-curves, so it can be contracted by a composition of
K_{\mathcal{X}/B}
-extremal contractions
\pi\colon(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})
. The induced morphism
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
is a (
G
-faithful) family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}
, more precisely,
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f,\gamma)
, where
\gamma
is the restriction of
\gamma^{\prime}
.

Similarly, every small deformation of
(X_{b},D_{b})
lifts to one of
(X_{b}^{\prime},D_{b}^{\prime})
, and conversely, every small deformation of
(X_{b}^{\prime},D_{b}^{\prime})
blows down to one of
(X_{b},D_{b})
, cf.
[
FZ94
, Proposition 1.6(1)]
. This way, we get a morphism of functors from deformations of
(X_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
to those of
(X_{b},D_{b})
, mapping
f^{\prime}
to
f
. Since
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b^{\prime}}}(-\log,)D_{b^{\prime}})=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))
by Lemma
2.11
(b)
, this morphism is smooth, see
[
Ser06
, Proposition 2.3.6]
. It follows that
f^{\prime}
is versal at
b\in B
if and only if so is
f
. By Lemma
2.11
(c)
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}_{b}}(-\log D^{\prime}_{b}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))
, so
f^{\prime}
is
h^{1}
-stratified (or universal) if and only if so is
f
, with the same stratification of
B
.

∎

Lemma 2.19

(Outer blowups)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a combinatorial type, and let
\beta\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
be an outer blowup at a vertex
a
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
and let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{+}=\beta_{+}^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{+})
. Assume that
\mathcal{R}_{+}
is represented by a family
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{a}
be the component of
\mathcal{D}
corresponding to the blown up vertex,
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}\setminus(\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{A})
. For each
b\in B
let
A^{\circ}_{b}=\mathcal{A}^{\circ}|_{X_{b}}
and let
H_{b}\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(A_{b}^{\circ})
be the restriction of
\operatorname{Aut}(X_{b},D_{b},A_{b})
.
Then the following hold.

(
1
)

Assume that
f
is
G
-faithful for some group
G
fixing the blown-up vertex
a
. Let
G_{f}
be the corresponding subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
. Assume that each group
H_{b}
is the restriction of some
H\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(f)
such that the subgroup
G^{\prime}_{f}\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(f)
generated by
G_{f}
and
H
is finite. Let
G^{\prime}
be the image of
G^{\prime}_{f}
in
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
is represented by an
G^{\prime}
-faithful family
f^{\prime}\colon(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Moreover, if
f
is universal then
f^{\prime}
is universal, too.

(
2
)

Assume that
f
is stratified by
B=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{k}B_{j}
, and write
B_{k}=\{\overline{b}\}
.
Then the following hold.

(a)

Assume that for each
b\neq\overline{b}
the group
H_{b}
acts transitively on
A^{\circ}_{b}
; whereas
H_{\overline{b}}
has a fixed point
\overline{z}\in A^{\circ}_{\overline{b}}
and an open orbit
A^{\circ}_{\overline{b}}\setminus\{\overline{z}\}
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
is represented by family
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
, stratified by
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{j}
, where
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{k+1}=\{\overline{z}\}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{k}=A^{\circ}_{\overline{b}}\setminus\{\overline{z}\}
, and
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{j}=f^{-1}(B_{j})\cap\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
for
j<k
, see Figure
8
.

(b)

Assume that for every
b\in B
the group
H_{b}
acts transitively on
A^{\circ}_{b}
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
is represented by stratified family
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\widetilde{B}
over an open neighborhood of
\overline{b}\in B
, with the stratification restricted from
B
.

(c)

Put
h_{j}\coloneqq h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))
for
b\in B_{j}
. Assume that the conditions of
(2.a)
or
(2.b)
hold, and put
h_{j}^{\prime}\coloneqq h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{z}^{\prime}}(-\log D_{z}^{\prime}))
for
z\in\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\circ}
in case
(2.a)
and for
z\in\widetilde{B}_{j}
in case
(2.b)
. Then the following hold.

(i)

If for some
b\in B_{j}
,
z\in A_{b}^{\circ}
the sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b})
has a global section which does not vanish at
z
then
h_{j}^{\prime}=h_{j}
. This holds for instance if the derivative at
g=\mathrm{id}
of the morphism
H_{b}\ni g\mapsto g(z)\in X_{b}
(which we call simply
the derivative of the
H_{b}
-action
), is nonzero.

(ii)

Assume that
f
is
h^{1}
-stratified, and for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
we have
h_{j}=h_{j}^{\prime}
. In case
(2.b)
, assume furthermore that the group
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
acts transitively on
A_{b}^{\circ}
for each
b\in B
. Then
f^{\prime}
is
h^{1}
-stratified.

(iii)

If for some
b\in B_{j}
all sections of
\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b})
vanish along
A_{b}
then
h_{j}^{\prime}=h_{j}+1
.

\dots
\dots
D_{b}
\overline{z}
\star
z
moves in
(2.a)
fixed point in
(2.a)
,
b=\overline{b}
A_{b}
\dots
\dots
D_{z}^{\prime}
-1

(a)

fibers
(X_{b},D_{b})
and
(X_{z}^{\prime},D_{z}^{\prime})

B
\overline{b}
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
A_{\overline{b}}^{\circ}
\overline{z}

(b)

bases
B
and
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}

Figure 8
.
Lemma
2.19
(1)
,
(2.a)
: outer blowup increases the dimension of a representing family.

Write
\mathcal{S}=(\Gamma,c)
. We have
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f,\gamma)
for some isomorphism
\gamma\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma(f)
. Consider cases
(1)
and
(2.a)
. The pullback
\widehat{f}\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\times_{B}\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\longrightarrow B
of
f
along
f|_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\longrightarrow B
is another family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}
, in fact,
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(\widehat{f},\widehat{\gamma})
, where
(\mathcal{D}\times_{B}\mathcal{A}^{\circ})^{\widehat{\gamma}(v)}=\mathcal{D}^{\gamma(v)}\times_{B}\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times_{B}\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
be a blowup of the diagonal, and let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{D}\times_{B}\mathcal{A}^{\circ})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then
f^{\prime}\coloneqq\widehat{f}\circ\pi\colon(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
satisfies
\mathcal{R}_{+}=\operatorname{Fib}_{+}(f^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
for the unique extension
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\widehat{\gamma}
, so
f^{\prime}
represents
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
. We will show that it has properties required by
(1)
and
(2.a)
.

Consider case
(1)
. We claim that
f^{\prime}
is
G^{\prime}
-faithful.
Suppose that for some
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
we have an isomorphism
\alpha^{\prime}\colon(X^{\prime}_{z_{1}},D^{\prime}_{z_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{z_{2}}^{\prime},D_{z_{2}}^{\prime})
such that
\alpha^{\prime}_{+}\in G^{\prime}
. Let
b_{i}=f(z_{i})\in B
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. Since
\alpha^{\prime}_{+}\in G^{\prime}
,
\alpha^{\prime}_{+}
fixes
a
and the new vertex, so by the universal property of blowing up there is an isomorphism
\alpha\colon(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
between the corresponding fibers of
f
such that
\alpha(z_{1})=z_{2}
. Since
f
is
G
-faithful, we have
b_{2}=g(b_{1})
for some
g\in G_{f}
such that
g_{+}=\alpha_{+}
. Since
g^{-1}\circ\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(X_{b_{1}})
restricts to an element of
H_{b_{1}}
, by assumption
g^{-1}\circ\alpha=h|_{X_{b}}
for some
h\in H
. We have
g\circ h|_{X_{b}}=\alpha
, so
g\circ h(z_{1})=\alpha(z_{1})=z_{2}
and
(g\circ h)_{+}=\alpha_{+}
, so
g\circ h
lifts to the required element of
\operatorname{Aut}(f^{\prime})
. Thus
f^{\prime}
is
G^{\prime}
-faithful. The assertion about universality will be proved below.

Consider case
(2.a)
.
Fix
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
. For any two points
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{j}
the fibers
(X_{z_{i}}^{\prime},D_{z_{i}}^{\prime},\gamma_{z_{i}}^{\prime})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
are equivalent. Indeed, the fibers of
f
over
f(z_{i})
are equivalent, say by an isomorphism
\alpha\colon(X_{f(z_{1})},D_{f(z_{1})})\longrightarrow(X_{f(z_{2})},D_{f(z_{2})})
with
\alpha_{+}=\mathrm{id}
. Composing
\alpha
with an element of the connected component of
H_{f(z_{2})}
we can assume
\alpha(z_{1})=z_{2}
, so
\alpha
lifts to an isomorphism
\alpha^{\prime}\colon(X_{z_{1}}^{\prime},D_{z_{1}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X_{z_{2}}^{\prime},D_{z_{2}}^{\prime})
with
\alpha^{\prime}_{+}=\mathrm{id}
, which yields the required equivalence. Conversely, if for some
z\in\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\circ}
,
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j^{\prime}}^{\circ}
the fibers
(X^{\prime}_{z},D^{\prime}_{z})
and
(X^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}},D^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}})
are isomorphic then
j=j^{\prime}
. Indeed, by the universal property of blowing up such an isomorphism descends to an isomorphism between
(X_{f(z)},D_{f(z)})
and
(X_{f(z^{\prime})},D_{f(z^{\prime})})
, so if
j\neq j^{\prime}
then
\{j,j^{\prime}\}=\{k,k+1\}
and we get an automorphism of
(X_{\overline{b}},D_{\overline{b}})
which maps
\overline{z}
to a point of
A_{\overline{b}}\setminus\{\overline{z}\}
, contrary to our assumptions.

Consider now case
(2.b)
. Shrinking
B
around
\overline{b}
if needed, we can assume that
f|_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}
has a section
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Blowing up
\mathcal{H}
we obtain a family
f^{\prime}\colon(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\longrightarrow B
representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\prime}
. For every two points
b_{1},b_{2}\in B_{j}
the fibers
(X_{b_{i}},D_{b_{i}},\gamma_{b_{i}})
are equivalent, and there is an isomorphism
(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
mapping the point
D_{b_{1}}\cap\mathcal{H}
to
D_{b_{2}}\cap\mathcal{H}
, so the fibers
(X_{b_{i}}^{\prime},D_{b_{i}}^{\prime},\gamma_{b_{i}}^{\prime})
are equivalent, too. Conversely, if
(X^{\prime}_{b},D^{\prime}_{b})
and
(X^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}},D_{b^{\prime}})
are isomorphic then as before we get
b,b^{\prime}\in B_{j}
for some
j
.

Parts
(2.c.i)
and
(2.c.iii)
follow from Lemma
2.11
(d)
. It remains to prove part
(2.c.ii)
and the assertion about universality in
(1)
. In case
(1)
fix any
z\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
and let
b=f(z)
, in case
(2.a)
put
z\coloneqq\overline{z}
,
b\coloneqq\overline{b}
, and in case
(2.b)
put
z=b=\overline{b}
. Then by assumption
f
is semiuniversal at
b
, in particular
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{b}}(-\log D_{b}))=\dim B
.

We claim that
f^{\prime}
is versal at
z
. We apply the argument from
[
FZ94
, Proposition 1.6(2)]
, which can be outlined as follows. Consider a small deformation
\widecheck{f}^{\prime}\colon(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow T
of
(X^{\prime}_{z},D^{\prime}_{z})
. Let
\widecheck{\pi}\colon(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})
be the contraction of the component
\widecheck{\mathcal{E}}
of
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
corresponding to the new vertex: it exists since
\widecheck{\mathcal{E}}
is a family of
(-1)
-curves, so it spans a
K_{\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}/T}
-negative extremal ray. We get a small deformation
\widecheck{f}\colon(\widecheck{\mathcal{X}},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow T
of
(X_{b},D_{b})
. Since
f
is versal at
b
,
\widecheck{f}
is a pullback of
f
. Let
\psi\colon\widecheck{\mathcal{X}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
be the induced morphism. For
t\in T
let
p_{t}\coloneqq\widecheck{\pi}(\widecheck{E}_{t})\in\widecheck{D}_{t}
be the center of the blowup
\widecheck{\pi}|_{\widecheck{X}_{t}^{\prime}}
. Now
\widecheck{f}^{\prime}
is a pullback of
f^{\prime}
along the morphism
\overline{\psi}\colon T\ni t\mapsto\psi(p_{t})\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. This proves the claim in cases
(1)
and
(2.a)
. In case
(2.b)
, by assumption we can compose
\overline{\psi}
with an element of
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
so that its image is the section
\mathcal{H}
, and then
\widecheck{f}^{\prime}
is a pullback of
f^{\prime}
along the composition
f\circ\overline{\psi}
, as needed.

To conclude, by
[
Ser06
, Proposition 2.5.8]
it is enough to prove that the dimension of the base of
f^{\prime}
equals
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}_{z}}(-\log D^{\prime}_{z}))
. In case
(2.b)
it follows from our assumptions. In the remaining cases, the base of
f^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
, and by Lemma
2.11
(d)
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}_{z}}(-\log D^{\prime}_{z}))\leqslant\dim\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Suppose the inequality is strict. Let
\widehat{f}^{\prime}
be the formal deformation of
(X_{z}^{\prime},D_{z}^{\prime})
associated with
f^{\prime}
, it is a pullback of a semiuniversal formal deformation by some morphism
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\widehat{U}
. Since
\dim\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\circ}>\dim\widehat{U}
, this morphism contracts a curve
\widehat{C}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\circ}
, so
\widehat{f}^{\prime}
restricts to a trivial formal deformation along this curve. By Artin approximation theorem
[
Art69
, Theorem 1.7]
, the restriction of
f^{\prime}
along some curve through
z
becomes trivial after an étale base change. This is impossible because the central fiber
(X_{z}^{\prime},D^{\prime}_{z})
is not isomorphic to
(X^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}},D^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}})
for any
z^{\prime}\neq z
near
z
, a contradiction.

∎

In the proof of Propositions
C
and
D
we will deduce properties of certain subsets of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
from the properties of
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
, where
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
is a combinatorial type obtained from
\mathcal{S}
by adding vertices corresponding to some vertical
(-1)
-curves. This will be done using the following elementary observations.

Lemma 2.20

(Adding
(-1)
-curves)
.

Let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}=(\widecheck{\Gamma},c_{2})
be a combinatorial type, let
\Gamma_{E}
be a subgraph of
\widecheck{\Gamma}
whose all vertices have weight
-1
, and let
\mathcal{S}=(\widecheck{\Gamma}-\Gamma_{E},c_{2})
. Fix
\widecheck{\mathcal{R}}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
, let
\mathcal{R}_{+}
be the image of
\widecheck{\mathcal{R}}_{+}
by the restriction map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})
; and let
\mathcal{R}
be the image of
\mathcal{R}_{+}
by the forgetful map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
.

Let
\widecheck{f}\colon(\mathcal{X},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow B
be a smooth family representing
\mathcal{R}_{+}
. Let
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}
be a subdivisor corresponding to
\Gamma_{E}
. Write
\mathcal{D}=\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}-\mathcal{E}
and let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be the restriction of
\widecheck{f}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The formal germ of
\widecheck{f}
at
b\in B
is semiuniversal if and only if so is the germ of
f
.

(b)

If
\widecheck{f}
is an (
h^{1}
-)stratified family representing
\widecheck{\mathcal{R}}_{+}
then
f
is an (
h^{1}
-)stratified family representing
\mathcal{R}
, with the same stratification. In particular,
\#\mathcal{R}=\#\widecheck{\mathcal{R}}_{+}=\dim B+1
, see Definition
1.9
(e)
.

(c)

Assume that
\widecheck{f}
is an
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-faithful family representing
\widecheck{\mathcal{R}}_{+}
such that

(6)

\mbox{for every }b_{1},b_{2}\in B,\mbox{ every isomorphism }(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})\mbox{ maps }E_{b_{1}}\mbox{ to }E_{b_{2}}.

Then

f
is an almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{R}
. Moreover, if
\widecheck{f}
is a universal family representing
\widecheck{\mathcal{R}}_{+}
then
f
is an almost universal family representing
\mathcal{R}
, see Definition
1.10
(b)
.

By
[
FZ94
, Proposition 1.7(3)]
, a semiuniversal deformation of a log smooth surface remains such after removing a
(-1)
-curve from the boundary, which proves
(a)
. For the remaining parts, note that
f
represents
\mathcal{R}_{+}
and
\mathcal{R}
. Part
(b)
now follows directly from
(a)
and definitions; and to prove part
(c)
, it remains to prove that
f
is almost faithful. Let
\varphi\colon(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
be an isomorphism between fibers of
f
. By condition (
6
),
\varphi
extends to an isomorphism
\widecheck{\varphi}\colon(X_{b_{1}},\widecheck{D}_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},\widecheck{D}_{b_{2}})
; so since
f
is
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-faithful,
the points
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
lie in the same orbit of the
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})_{f}
-action, as needed.

∎

Lemma 2.21

(Checking condition (
6
))
.

We keep the notation and assumptions from Lemma
2.20
. Assume further that for each
b\in B
the following conditions
(a)
,
(b)
hold. Then condition (
6
) holds, too.

(a)

The Néron-Severi group
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X_{b})
is generated by
K_{X_{b}}
and the components of
D_{b}
(for instance,
(X_{b},D_{b})
is a log resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one).

(b)

The surface
X_{b}
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
E_{b}
is the sum of all vertical
(-1)
-curves, and
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
maps
k
-sections to
k
-sections, that is, the set of vertices of
\Gamma
admits an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
-invariant decomposition
\bigsqcup_{j\geqslant 0}\Gamma_{j}
such that the fiber
F_{b}
satisfies
F_{b}\cdot D_{b}^{v}=j
for every
j\geqslant 0
and every
v\in\Gamma_{j}
.

Fix an isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{b_{1}},D_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},D_{b_{2}})
and let
\psi=\varphi_{+}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
. Then
D_{b_{2}}^{v}=\varphi(D_{b_{1}}^{\psi(v)})
for every vertex
v
of
\Gamma(f)
.
By assumption
(b)
, for every
v\in\Gamma_{j}
we have

\psi(v)\in\Gamma_{j}
and

F_{b_{2}}\cdot D_{b_{2}}^{v}=j=F_{b_{1}}\cdot D_{b_{1}}^{v}=F_{b_{1}}\cdot D_{b_{1}}^{\psi(v)}=\varphi(F_{b_{1}})\cdot\varphi(D_{b_{1}}^{\psi(v)})=\varphi(F_{b_{1}})\cdot D_{b_{2}}^{v},

and by adjunction
F_{b_{2}}\cdot K_{X_{b_{2}}}=-2=F_{b_{1}}\cdot K_{X_{b_{1}}}
. Hence by assumption
(a)
we get a numerical equivalence
F_{b_{2}}\equiv\varphi(F_{b_{1}})
. It follows that the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations induced by
|F_{b_{2}}|
and
|\varphi(F_{b_{1}})|
are the same up to an automorphism of the base. In particular, they have the same vertical
(-1)
-curves, so
E_{b_{2}}=\varphi(E_{b_{1}})
, as needed.

∎

2G.
Summary of notation

We now introduce notation which will allow us to write down those weighted graphs which appear in all the lemmas cited in Theorem
A
. First, we recall the notation for rational log canonical singularity types. They are disjoint sums of rational chains, forks, and benches, see Section
2B
. We denote them as follows:

•

[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
is a chain with components of subsequent self-intersection numbers
-a_{1},\dots,-a_{n}
,

•

\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
is a fork with a branching
(-b)
-curve, and maximal twigs of types
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
,

•

[\![T]\!]
is a bench with central chain
T
.

We also use the following simplifying conventions:

•

(m)_{k}
stands for an integer
m
repeated
k
times, and
[(m)_{0}]
stands for an empty chain,

•

[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]*[b_{1},\dots,b_{l}]\coloneqq[a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}+b_{k}-1,b_{2},\dots,b_{l}]
, see formula (
1
),

•

(\mbox{empty chain})*[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]\coloneqq[a_{2},\dots,a_{k}]
,

•

[(2)_{-1}]*[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]\coloneqq[a_{2}+1,\dots,a_{k}]
, see formula (
2
),

•

[(2)_{-1},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]\coloneqq[a_{2},\dots,a_{k}]
, see formula (
2
),

•

T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}
is the transpose of the chain
T
, i.e.
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}=[a_{n},\dots,a_{1}]
,

•

T^{*}
is the chain adjoint to the chain
T
, given by the formula (
3
).

We now introduce additional notation, which allows to encode both the singularity type of a surface, and a structure of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration on its minimal resolution.

Notation 2.22

(Decorated types, see Example
2.24
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a normal surface, and let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
be its minimal log resolution. Assume that
D
is a sum of rational chains, forks and benches. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, and let
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
be all components of its degenerate fibers which are not contained in
D
. Assume that every
A_{j}
is a
(-1)
-curve, meeting every component of
D
at most once (this will always hold in our case). We describe the weighted graph of
\widecheck{D}\coloneqq D+\sum_{j}A_{j}
, together with the decomposition
\widecheck{D}=\widecheck{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}+\widecheck{D}_{\mathrm{vert}}
, as follows. First, we write the type of each chain, fork or bench in
D
using the conventions summarized above; so each number corresponds to a component of
D
. Next, we decorate it as follows.

(a)

We put in boldface those numbers which correspond to horizontal components of
D
.

•

If a symbol
T
stands for a chain
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
, we write
\boldsymbol{T^{+}}\coloneqq[\boldsymbol{a_{1}},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}]
,
\boldsymbol{T^{-}}\coloneqq[a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},\boldsymbol{a_{n}}]
.

(b)

We decorate by
|j|
the number corresponding to each component of
D
meeting
A_{j}
.

•

If
T
stands for a chain
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
, we write
\prescript{|j|}{}{T}\coloneqq[a_{1}^{|j|},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}]
,
T^{|j|}\coloneqq[a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n}^{|j|}]
.

•

For a bench
[\![T]\!]=[2,a_{1},2]+[a_{2},\dots,a_{n-1}]+[2,a_{n},2]
with central chain
T=[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
, we write

\begin{split}\prescript{|j|}{}{[\![}T]\!]&\coloneqq[2^{|j|},a_{1},2]+[a_{2},\dots,a_{n-1}]+[2,a_{n},2],\quad\prescript{|i|}{|j|}{[\![}T]\!]\coloneqq[2^{|i|},a_{1},2^{|j|}]+[a_{2},\dots,a_{n-1}]+[2,a_{n},2]\\
[\![T]\!]^{|j|}&\coloneqq[2,a_{1},2]+[a_{2},\dots,a_{n-1}]+[2^{|j|},a_{n},2],\quad[\![T]\!]^{|i|}_{|j|}\coloneqq[2,a_{1},2]+[a_{2},\dots,a_{n-1}]+[2^{|i|},a_{n},2^{|j|}].\end{split}

Note that if
\widecheck{D}
is snc then by assumption every two components of
\widecheck{D}
meet in at most one point, so decorations introduced in
(b)
unambiguously encode the weighted graph of
\widecheck{D}
. In some rare cases our
\widecheck{D}
will not be snc, because some
(-1)
-curve
A_{j}
will pass through a common point of two adjacent components of
D
. Although our notation does not capture such a degeneracy, it will usually be clear from the constructions when it occurs.

Notation 2.23

(Figures)
.

Unless stated otherwise, we use the following conventions to draw
\widecheck{D}=D+\sum_{j}A_{j}
.

(a)

A solid line annotated by
“
k
”
denotes an
k
-curve in
D
. Weight
k=-2
is skipped.

(b)

A dashed line denotes a
(-1)
-curve in
\widecheck{D}-D
.

Example 2.24

(Figure
3
expressed using Notation
2.22
)
.

The weighted graphs of
\widecheck{D}
in Figure
3
are
as follows:

(a)

\langle 2;\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],[2,2],[\boldsymbol{2},3]\rangle
,

(b)

\langle 2;\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2,2,2,2],[2],[\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
,

(c)

\langle\boldsymbol{2},[(2)_{r-1},\boldsymbol{2},2],\prescript{|2|}{}{[}2,2],[2]\rangle+[r]^{|1|}
,

(d)

\langle 2;\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2,\boldsymbol{2}],\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{m}],\prescript{|3|}{}{[}2]\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2,\boldsymbol{m}]^{|2|}
,

(e)

\langle 2;\prescript{|1|}{}{[}3,\boldsymbol{2}],\prescript{|2|}{}{[}2,2],\prescript{|3|}{}{[}2]\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2,2,\boldsymbol{2}]^{|2|}
.

Notation 2.25

(Symbol
\mathcal{M}^{d}
)
.

In Tables
1
,
2
and
10
–
15
we list numbers
\#\mathcal{C}
for some sets
\mathcal{C}
of isomorphism classes of log surfaces. In those tables, symbol
\mathcal{M}^{d}
for
d\geqslant 1
means that we have
\#\mathcal{C}=\infty
, and
\mathcal{C}
is represented by an almost faithful family of dimension
d
, see Definition
1.9
(d)
.

3.
Non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces

In this section, we study non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one. Looking at minimal models of their log resolutions, described in
[
PP24
, Proposition 3.2]
, we prove Proposition
3.1
below, which is a more detailed version of Proposition
B
. In Section
3B
we illustrate each of its cases by non–log canonical examples.

Proposition 3.1

(Non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a non–log terminal del Pezzo surface of rank one, and let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution. Write
D=D_{0}+D_{\mathrm{lt}}
, where
D_{\mathrm{lt}}
is the preimage of all log terminal singularities of
\overline{X}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The divisor
D_{0}
is a tree with at most one component
B
such that
p_{a}(B)>0
or
\beta_{D}(B)\geqslant 4
. In particular,
\overline{X}
has exactly one non–log terminal singularity.

(b)

If
0pt(\overline{X})=1
then every connected component of
D_{\mathrm{lt}}
is an admissible chain.

(c)

Assume
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 2
. Then
\overline{X}
is rational, has only rational singularities, and every component
C
of
D_{0}
satisfies
p_{a}(C)=0
and
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 4
.

(d)

Assume
0pt(\overline{X})=2
. Then one of the following holds.

(i)

Every connected component of
D_{\mathrm{lt}}
is an admissible chain. In this case,
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
from Examples
5.2
,
5.3
, see Fig.
2(b)
–
2(d)
,
2(f)
.

(ii)

The surface
\overline{X}
is constructed from a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space as in Example
3.5
, see Figure
11
. In particular,
D_{\mathrm{lt}}
is a disjoint union of one admissible fork and at most one admissible chain;
X
admits a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two branching components of
D
, and
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
or
2\mathrm{D}_{4}
from Example
5.2
(b)
,
(d)
, see Fig.
2(c)
,
2(e)
.

(iii)

We have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface from Example
5.5
, see Figure
2(i)
.

(e)

Assume
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
. Then
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
, and
\overline{X}
satisfies condition
(b)
or
(c)
of Proposition
B
. Moreover, every connected component of
D_{\mathrm{lt}}
is an admissible chain or a
(-2)
-fork.

3A.
Proof of Proposition
3.1
: structure of non–log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one

The proof is based on
[
PP24
, Proposition 3.2]
, which describes possible almost minimal models of
(X,D)
. To describe the process of almost minimalization, we will need the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 3.2

(Birational morphism with exactly one exceptional
(-1)
-curve)
.

Let
(Y,B)
be a log smooth surface. Let
\tau\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y
be a birational morphism such that
\operatorname{Exc}\tau
contains exactly one
(-1)
-curve, say
A
, and the point
\tau(A)
lies on
B
. Put
E=\operatorname{Exc}\tau-A
,
B^{\prime}=\tau^{-1}_{*}B^{\prime}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

Every component of
E
has branching number at most
3
in
B^{\prime}+E
.

(b)

The divisor
E
has at most two connected components.

(c)

A connected component of
E
which does not meet
B^{\prime}
is an admissible chain.

Straightforward by induction on the number of blowups in the decomposition of
\tau
.
∎

Assume first that
0pt(\overline{X})=1
. Fix a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
(X,D)
.
By Lemma
2.8
every degenerate fiber has a unique
(-1)
-curve. It follows from Lemma
2.3
(b)
, see also Lemma
4.1
below, that each fiber meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in a tip, and each connected component of
D
not containing
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is an admissible chain. This proves
(b)
and case
0pt=1
of
(a)
.

Thus by Remark
2.5
we can assume
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 2
. Let
\psi_{\min}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\min},D_{\min})
be an MMP run. Since
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 2
, the minimal model
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
is a dlt log del Pezzo surface of rank one, see
[
PP24
, Lemma 4.1(a)]
. We have
\#\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{\min}-\#D=\#\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{\min}-\rho(X)+\rho(\overline{X})=-\rho(X_{\min})+\rho(\overline{X})=0
, so
\#\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{\min}=\#D
. Since
X_{\min}
is log terminal, we have
\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{\min}\neq D
. Hence
D_{\min}\neq 0
. Put
n=\#D_{\min}
. The morphism
\psi_{\min}
contracts precisely
n
curves not contained in
D
; they are
(-1)
-curves by Lemma
2.7
(d)
. Since
(X,D)
is log smooth,
\psi_{\min}
factors as
\psi_{\min}=\alpha\circ\psi
, where
\alpha\colon(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})\longrightarrow(X_{\min},D_{\min})
is the minimal log resolution. This way, the analysis of non-lt del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, with no boundary, reduces to the analysis of dlt log del Pezzo surfaces
with
nonzero boundary, so-called
open del Pezzo
.

These surfaces are described in
[
PP24
, Proposition 3.2]
, which implies that
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
is as in (1) or (3)–(6) loc. cit: we refer to each of these cases simply by its number.
In any case, the surface
X_{\textnormal{am}}
, hence
\overline{X}
, is rational, which by
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.7]
is equivalent to
\overline{X}
having only rational singularities. This proves the first three statements of
(c)
, and shows that every connected component of
D
is a rational tree.

Case

(1).
In this case
X_{\textnormal{am}}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\deg D_{\textnormal{am}}\leqslant 2
. Suppose
n=1
. Then
D_{\textnormal{am}}
is a line or a conic. Since
D
is negative definite, and
\psi^{-1}
has exactly one base point, we have
\psi=\beta\circ\psi_{1}
, where
\beta
is a composition of
D_{\textnormal{am}}^{2}
blowups over a point of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
, on the proper transforms of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
. Now
|\beta^{-1}_{*}D_{\textnormal{am}}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height one with respect to
D
, contrary to our assumptions.

Thus
n=2
, so
D_{\textnormal{am}}
is a sum of two lines, say
{l}_{1}
and
{l}_{2}
. Since
{l}_{i}^{2}>0
, each
{l}_{i}
contains a base point of
\psi^{-1}
. If the unique base point of
\psi^{-1}
on
{l}_{i}
is
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}
, then the proper transform of
{l}_{i}
after a blowup at that point is a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
1
with respect to
D
, which is impossible. Thus
\psi^{-1}
has two base points, say
p_{i}\in{l}_{i}
,
p_{i}\not\in{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}
, and each
\psi^{-1}(p_{i})
contains exactly one
(-1)
-curve. By Lemma
3.2
(c)
all connected components of
D
, except possibly the one containing
\psi^{-1}_{*}D_{\textnormal{am}}
, are admissible chains; and by
3.2
(a)
all components of
D
have
\beta_{D}\leqslant 3
, so conditions
(a)
,
(c)
and
(d)(d.i)
hold. The proper transform of
{l}_{1}
after a blowup at
p_{1}
is a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose pullback to
(X,D)
has height
2
, as needed.

Case

(3.1)–(3.3).
We have
2=0pt(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})\geqslant 0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 2
by assumption, so
0pt(\overline{X})=2
. By
[
PP24
, Remark 3.3]
, we can choose the minimal model
(X_{\min},D_{\min})
so that
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
is as in Figure 2 loc. cit. We have
n\in\{1,2\}
. We claim that conditions
(a)
,
(c)
and
(d)(d.i)
hold.

Subcase

n=1
.
Let
R\coloneqq\alpha^{-1}_{*}D_{\min}
be the component of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
with nonnegative self-intersection number. Since
D
has no such component, the unique base point
p
of
\psi^{-1}
lies on
R
. Since
D_{\textnormal{am}}
is a chain, Lemma
3.2
(c)
,
(a)
implies that all connected components of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
, except possibly the one containing
\psi^{-1}_{*}R
are admissible chains, and
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 3
for every component
C
of
D
.
It remains to prove that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to one of the surfaces listed there. This will follow from a more general Lemma
5.9
, let us briefly sketch the argument here.

If
\#D_{\textnormal{am}}\geqslant 3
and
p\in D_{\textnormal{am}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, see
[
PP24
, Figure 2(c)]
, then the fact that
(\psi^{-1}_{*}R)^{2}\leqslant-2
, see Lemma
2.7
(c)
, easily implies that
(X,D)
swaps vertically to the surface of type
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
from Example
5.3
, see Figure
2(f)
. Assume that
\#D_{\textnormal{am}}=2
or
p\not\in D_{\textnormal{am}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and write
\psi=\beta\circ\psi_{1}
, where
\beta
is a composition of
R^{2}
blowups at
p
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
R
. Then
|\beta^{-1}_{*}R|
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
which is either of height one with respect to
D
, which is impossible, or has exactly two degenerate fibers, and
(X,D)
with this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration swaps vertically to the surface of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
from Example
5.2
(a)
; as needed.

Subcase

n=2
.
Now
D_{\textnormal{am}}
is a chain with a subchain
C=[0,1-m]
for some
m\geqslant 1
. If the node of
C
is a base point of
\psi^{-1}
let
\beta
be a blowup at this point; otherwise put
\beta=\mathrm{id}
. Then
\psi
factors as
\psi=\beta\circ\psi_{1}
and
(\beta^{*}C)_{\mathrm{red}}
is a chain whose both tips have self-intersection at least
-1
, so they contain base points of
\psi_{1}^{-1}
, because
D
has no
(-1)
-curves by Lemma
2.7
(c)
. By construction those two base points are different, so the preimage of each contains at most one
(-1)
-curve. Now Lemma
3.2
implies, as before, that all components of
D
have
\beta_{D}\leqslant 3
, and all but one connected components of
D
are admissible chains. Moreover, if
\psi
has a base point off
(D_{\textnormal{am}})_{\mathrm{vert}}
then
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to the surface from Example
5.2
(a)
or
(b)
, see Figures
2(b)
,
2(b)
; otherwise it swaps vertically to the one from Example
5.2
(c)
, see Figure
2(d)
. For more details see Lemma
5.9
below.

Case

(3.4).
We have
n=1
. Like in cases (3.1)–(3.3) above,
0pt(\overline{X})=2
. Let
H_{\textnormal{am}}\subseteq D_{\textnormal{am}}
be the branching component of the non-admissible fork, and let
H=\psi^{-1}_{*}H_{\textnormal{am}}
. Since
H^{2}\leqslant-2
by Lemma
2.7
(c)
, the base point of
\psi^{-1}
lies on
H
, and
\psi
factors through
H_{\textnormal{am}}^{2}+2
blowups over that point, as described in Example
3.5
. Thus
\overline{X}
is as in
(d)(d.ii)
. Lemma
3.2
implies that
D
all components of
D
except the proper transform of
B
have
\beta_{D}\leqslant 3
, and the latter has
\beta_{D}\leqslant 4
, so
(c)
holds. Moreover,
\psi^{-1}
is an isomorphism near the admissible fork in
D_{\textnormal{am}}
; and
D\wedge\operatorname{Exc}\psi
consists of an admissible chain and a connected component meeting
B
, so
(a)
holds, too, as needed.

In the remaining cases (4)–(6),
n=1
and
D_{\textnormal{am}}
has a fork with a branching
(-1)
-curve
A
. Since by Lemma
2.7
(c)

D
has no
(-1)
-curve, the base point of
\psi^{-1}
lies on
A
. Put
B=\psi^{-1}_{*}A
. Lemma
3.2
implies that for every connected component
C
of
D
one of the following holds: either
\psi
is an isomorphism near
C
, or
C\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\psi
is an admissible chain, or
C
is a tree containing
B
. Hence
D_{0}
is a rational tree. Moreover, since every component of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
has
\beta_{D_{\textnormal{am}}}\leqslant 3
, every component of
D-B
has
\beta_{D}\leqslant 3
, and
\beta_{D}(B)\leqslant 4
. This proves
(a)
and
(c)
.

Case

(4).
In this case, we have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and the surface
X_{\min}
swaps vertically to the one from Example
5.5
. Since the base point of
\psi^{-1}
lies on some vertical
(-1)
-curve, decomposing
\psi
into elementary vertical swaps shows that
\overline{X}
swaps to
\overline{X}_{\min}
, and therefore
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X}_{\min})\leqslant 2
and
(d)(d.iii)
holds.

Case

(5).
In this case, we have
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and the log surface
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
is described in
[
PP24
, Example 3.9]
, see Figures 4(b), 4(c) loc. cit. These figures are repeated in the leftmost and rightmost part of Figure
14
, explained in Remark
3.10
. In particular, we have
3=0pt(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})\geqslant 0pt(\overline{X})
.

We claim that Proposition
B
(c)
holds, i.e.
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface
\overline{Y}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{3}+4\mathrm{A}_{1}+[3]
, with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
3
constructed in Example
3.9
, see the middle of Figure
14
. For this we need another
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X_{\textnormal{am}}
, used in
[
PP24
, Lemmas 4.8, 4.9]
. It is shown in Figure
14
, too, and is constructed as follows. Let
A+T+T_{2}+T_{3}
be the fork in
D_{\textnormal{am}}
, with branching component
A=[1]
and twigs
T
,
T_{2}=[2]
,
T_{3}=[3]
. In case (5.1) of
[
PP24
, Proposition 3.2]
, we consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|T_{2}+2A+\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)|
, and in case (5.2) loc. cit., the one with a degenerate fiber supported on
A^{\prime}+T+A+T_{2}
, where
A^{\prime}
is a
(-1)
-curve denoted in loc. cit. by
|1|
. The horizontal part of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
consists of a
2
-section
T_{3}
and a
1
-section
H
; and degenerate fibers are supported on:
[2,1,2]
,
[2,1,2]
,
\langle 2;[1,3],[1],[2]\rangle
in case (5.1) and
[1,2,3,1,2]
,
[2,1,2]
,
\langle 2;[1],[2],[2]\rangle
in case (5.2). They meet
T_{3}
in the
(-1)
-curve of multiplicity
2
; and
H
either in a
(-1)
-curve of multiplicity
1
(if such occurs) or in a
(-2)
-tip. With respect to this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, the surface
X_{\min}
swaps vertically to
\overline{Y}
. Furthermore, as in case (4), since the base point of
\psi^{-1}
lies on a vertical
(-1)
-curve, we see that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to
X_{\min}
.

Moreover, all connected components of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
not containing
A
are admissible chains, so all connected components of
D
not containing
\psi^{-1}_{*}A
are admissible chains, too, as claimed in
(e)
.

Case

(6).
Here the surface
\overline{X}_{\min}
admits a descendant with elliptic boundary as in
B
(b)
, hence so does
\overline{X}
. Moreover all connected components of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
not containing
A
are
(-2)
-chains and
(-2)
-forks, so by Lemma
3.2
(c)
all components of
D
not containing
\psi^{-1}_{*}A
are admissible chains or
(-2)
-forks, as claimed in
(e)
.
∎

3B.
Examples of non–log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one

We now construct some non–log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one falling in each case of Proposition
3.1
. Although they are all constructed from a simple
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations, their singularities can still be complicated.

We begin with an example where the minimal log resolution has a boundary component with an arbitrarily high branching number. Note that by Proposition
3.1
(c)
such examples are necessarily of height one.

Example 3.3

(Case
3.1
(b)
:
0pt=1
, high branching number, see Figure
9
)
.

We construct two examples:
(a)
with
\nu+1
singularities, of type
\langle m+\nu;\nu\cdot[2]\rangle+\nu\cdot\mathrm{A}_{1}
; and
(b)
with only one singularity, of type
\langle m+\nu;\nu\cdot[2,2,2]\rangle
. Here
\langle b,\nu\cdot T\rangle
denotes a tree with one branching component
[b]
and
\nu
maximal twigs of type
T
.

(a)

Let
\mathbb{F}_{m}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
be a Hirzebruch surface for some
m\geqslant 1
. Choose
\nu
points
p_{1},\dots,p_{\nu}
on the negative section
\Xi_{m}=[m]
, and denote by
F_{i}
the fiber passing through
p_{i}
. Blow up at each
p_{i}
and its infinitely near point on the proper transform of
F_{i}
. Denote this morphism by
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
, let
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
be the
(-1)
- and
(-2)
-curves in
\psi^{-1}(p_{i})
, and let
H=\psi^{-1}_{*}\Xi_{m}
,
C_{i}=\psi^{-1}_{*}F_{i}
,
D=H+\sum_{i}(B_{i}+C_{i})
. Then
C_{i}=[2]
is a connected component of
D
, and
H=[m+\nu]
meets each
B_{i}=[2]
. In particular,
\beta_{D}(H)=\nu
. Note that
(X,D)
is the log surface from Example
4.2
for
g=0
,
e=m+\nu
, see Figure
2(a)
.

The divisor
D_{0}\coloneqq H+\sum_{i}B_{i}
contracts to a rational singularity; with fundamental cycle
D_{0}
. Let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
D
. Then
\rho(\overline{X})=1
. We compute that
\operatorname{cf}(H)=1+\frac{\nu-4}{\nu+2m}<2
, so by Lemma
2.6
the surface
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo. It
is log terminal (respectively, log canonical but not log terminal) if and only if
\nu\leqslant 3
(resp.
\nu=4
): these surfaces will appear in cases
(1)
–
(3)
,

(7)
and
(25)
of Lemma
4.4
.

-m-\nu
\dots
\nu
fibers
-m-\nu
\dots
\nu
fibers

Figure 9
.
Example
3.3
: non–log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height one, with a highly branching component in the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution.

(b)

We keep the notation from
(a)
. Blow up once at
A_{i}\setminus(D-A_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
, denote this morphism by

and put

. As before, there is a morphism

contracting
D
to a rational singularity, with reduced fundamental cycle. We have
\operatorname{cf}(H^{\prime})=1+\frac{3\nu-8}{\nu+4m}

2.6
, the surface

is del Pezzo if and only if

. For

(4)
,

. For

We now construct a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one and height two whose non-lt singular point has a non-star-shaped resolution graph, see Figure

. Note that if
\operatorname{char}{k}\not\in\{2,3,5\}
then such a surface necessarily falls into case

of Proposition

, so its log terminal singularities are images of admissible chains.

Example 3.4

(Case

(d)(d.i)
: a non-star-shaped resolution, see Figure

)
.

Let

, such that

.

Artin’s criterion implies that

,

. The inequality (

We now consider case

Example 3.5

(Case
3.1
(d)(d.ii)
: blowing up over a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space)
.

First, we recall a construction of a Platonic
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
-fiber space from
[
MT84
]
and its log smooth completion
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
, see Figure
11(a)
.

Fix
m\geqslant 2
, and consider a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
. Denote by
H_{1}^{\prime\prime}=[m]
the negative section. Choose three fibers
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}
and a section
H_{0}^{\prime\prime}
disjoint from
H_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, so
H_{0}^{\prime\prime}=[-m]
.
Let
\tau\colon X_{\textnormal{am}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
be a composition of blowups over
F_{i}\cap H_{0}^{\prime\prime}
for each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
(\tau^{*}F_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}=[T_{i},1,T_{i}^{*}]
for some admissible chains
T_{i}
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}>1
. Put
H_{j}^{\prime}=\tau^{-1}_{*}H_{j}^{\prime\prime}
, and define
D_{\textnormal{am}}
as
\tau^{*}(H_{1}^{\prime\prime}+H_{2}^{\prime\prime}+F_{1}+F_{2}+F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}
without the
(-1)
-curves. Then
D_{\textnormal{am}}
has two connected components: a fork
D_{\textnormal{am}}^{0}=\langle 3-m;T_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T_{3}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}\rangle
which is not negative definite, and an admissible fork
D_{\textnormal{am}}^{1}=\langle m,T_{1}^{*},T_{2}^{*},T_{3}^{*}\rangle
.

We now construct a class of del Pezzo surfaces from Proposition
3.1
(d)(d.ii)
. Choose any point
p\in H_{0}^{\prime}
, and blow up
m-1
times at
p
and its infinitely near points on the proper transform of
H_{0}^{\prime}
. Denote this morphism by
Y\longrightarrow X_{\textnormal{am}}
, and let
D_{Y}
be the reduced total transform of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
minus the exceptional
(-1)
-curve
A
. Now
(Y,D_{Y})
is a minimal resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{Y}
of rank one and type
\langle 2;T_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T_{3}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}\rangle+\langle m;T_{1}^{*},T_{2}^{*},T_{3}^{*}\rangle+[(2)_{m-2}]
if
p\in D_{\textnormal{am}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
or
[T_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},2,T_{2}]+\langle m,T_{1}^{*},T_{2}^{*},T_{3}^{*}\rangle+[(2)_{m-2}]*T_{3}
if
p\not\in D_{\textnormal{am}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, cf. Lemma
5.10
(29)
or
(15)
. We define
(X,D)
by a vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
whose unique base point lies on
A
.

For
i\in\{0,1\}
let
H_{i}
be the proper transform of
H_{i}^{\prime}
,
i\in\{0,1\}
, and let
D_{i}
be the connected component of
D
containing
H_{i}
. Then
D=D_{0}+D_{1}+E
, where
D_{1}
is an admissible fork, and
E
is zero or an admissible chain. Note that if
p\in D_{\textnormal{am}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
then
(Y,D_{Y})
, hence
(X,D)
, swaps vertically to the surface of type
2\mathrm{D}_{4}
from Example
5.2
(d)
, otherwise it swaps vertically to the surface of type

.

We now give a concrete example of a non–log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one obtained by the construction of Example

.

We keep the notation from Example

. Take

for

. Choose

, fix

so that

. Now

,

Remark 3.10

(Additional restrictions in

(c)

)
.

Let

be a del Pezzo surface as in Proposition

. Its minimal log resolution
(X,D)
admits a morphism

onto a minimal resolution of the surface from Example
3.9
. In the proof of Proposition
3.1
,
Case (5)
, we have constructed this morphism as a composition of the almost minimalization
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
with some further contraction
\tau\colon X_{\textnormal{am}}\longrightarrow Y
. The possible log surfaces
(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
are described in
[

, Proposition 3.2(5)]
, and shown in the left and right of Figure

is the sum of solid lines and the

-curve

“
\circ
”
or two points
“
\bullet
”

More precisely, the morphism

is constructed as follows. We keep notation from Example
3.9

be the
(-1)
-curve over

, let

be the proper transform of
{c}
, and let

be the first exceptional curve over

, see Figure

is a blowup at either:

)

: this is case (5.1) in
[

, Proposition 3.2]
, shown in the left part of Figure

, or

(
\bullet
)

A_{2}\cap C
and
