: this is case (5.2) loc. cit., shown in the right part of Figure

.

The map
\psi^{-1}
has a unique base point, call it
p
. Since
D
contains no
(-1)
-curves, the point

lies on the
(-1)
-curve

. We remark that

can be either a smooth point of

, as in Example

, or a singular one, as in all cases of Proposition

.

In the following discussion it will be more convenient to use the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of

constructed in
[

, Proposition 3.2(5)]
.
It is induced by the pencil of conics tangent to

\circ
)

,

in case

\bullet
)
. Its degenerate fibers are shown in the leftmost and rightmost parts of Figure

.

Example 3.11

(Non–log canonical surface in case

)
.

Let

(
\circ
)

,
[2]

[3]

Consider the pullback to

. Using

, which substituted to the inequality (
5
) shows that

.

We have

. We claim that the equality holds. Suppose

. Since

has 4 connected components, by Lemma

the witnessing

-fibration has at least 3 degenerate fibers. By Lemma

. The degenerate fiber containing the twig
[3]

(-1)

[3,1,2,2]
, which is impossible as
D
has no connected component of type
[2,2]
. The fact that

can be shown directly, too, but one can also use Theorem
A
. Indeed, since

is the unique branching component of

and
\beta_{D}(H)=4
, we see that
\overline{X}
does not swap vertically to any surface from Examples

,

; and since it has only one canonical singularity, of type
[2]
, it cannot swap vertically to the one from Example
5.5
, either.

We also note that
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Indeed, otherwise by Theorem
E
at least
6
components of
D
would lie in connected components of

which are
(-2)
-chains or forks, while there is only one such. As a consequence, the surface
\overline{X}
is covered only by case
(c)
of Proposition
B
.

Example

We end this section with the classification of log canonical surfaces as in Proposition
B
(c)
. We list their singularity types together with the structure of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration obtained by pulling back the one from Remark
3.10
. We use Notation
2.22
, and furthermore underline the numbers corresponding to the
2
-sections.

Proposition 3.12

(Log canonical surfaces in
B
(c)
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one which is log canonical, but not log terminal. Assume that
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
, and
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary. Then
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
,
0pt(\overline{X})=3
, and the surface
\overline{X}
, together with some witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of the minimal log resolution, is of one of the following types, for some
k\geqslant 2
and some admissible chain
V
.

(1)

\langle\boldsymbol{k};\prescript{|0|}{}{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]^{|2|},[3]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|0|}{}{V}^{*}*[(2)_{k-1},3,2^{|1|},2]+[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|1,2|},2^{|3|},2]+[2]^{|2|}
,
d(V)=6
,

(2)

\langle\boldsymbol{k};\prescript{|0|}{}{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]^{|2|},[3]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|0|}{}{V}^{*}*[(2)_{k-1},4,2]^{|1|}+[2,3^{|1|},\uline{\boldsymbol{2}}^{|2|},2^{|3|},2]+[2]^{|2|}
,
d(V)=6
,

(3)

\langle\boldsymbol{k};\prescript{|0|}{}{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]^{|2|},[2,2^{|3|},2]\rangle+\prescript{|0|}{}{V}^{*}*[(2)_{k-1},4]^{|1|}+[\uline{\boldsymbol{3}}^{|2,3|},2^{|1|},2]+[2]^{|2|}
,
d(V)=4
.

Moreover, each of the above singularity types is realized by exactly one surface
\overline{X}
, up to an isomorphism. Its minimal log resolution
(X,D)
satisfies
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
for
i=0,1
and
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=1
.

Since
\overline{X}
is non–log terminal,
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
and
\overline{X}
has no descendant with elliptic boundary, it is as in Proposition
B
(c)
. Remark
3.10
gives a morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\textnormal{am}},D_{\textnormal{am}})
onto one of the two log surfaces from
[
PP24
, Proposition 3.2(5)]
, see Figure
14
; such that
\psi^{-1}
has a unique base point, lying on the
(-1)
-curve
A\subseteq D_{\textnormal{am}}
. Let
D_{0}
be the connected component of
D
which contracts to the non-lt singularity
r_{0}\in\overline{X}
. The above description of
D_{\textnormal{am}}
shows that the only branching component of
D_{0}
is the proper transform of
A
, and it meets at least two twigs of
D_{0}
which are not of type
[2]
. Since
r_{0}
is log canonical, but not log terminal, it follows that
D_{0}
is a log canonical fork. Two of its maximal twigs are among
\{[2],[3],[2,2,2]\}
in case
(
\circ
)
and among
\{[2],[3],[2,3]\}
in case
(
\bullet
)
. By Lemma
2.1
all three maximal twigs of
D_{0}
are either
\{[2],[3],V\}
with
d(V)=6
, or, in case
(
\circ
)
, they are
\{[2],[2,2,2],V\}
with
d(V)=4
. Together with the pullback of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration from
[
PP24
, Proposition 3.2(5)]
we get the above list.

We claim that each of the resulting surfaces
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo.
Let
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
be the
1
- and
2
-section in
D
. Since
H_{1}
is the branching component of the log canonical fork, we have
\operatorname{cf}(H_{1})=1
. An easy computation, see
[
Kol92
, 3.2.1]
, shows that
\operatorname{cf}(H_{2})=\frac{3}{7}
. Now the claim follows from the inequality (
5
).

Next, we claim that each of the singularity types
\mathcal{S}
in
(1)
–
(3)
is realized by exactly one del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
, up to an isomorphism. From the classification in Theorems
A
and
E
, it follows that every such
\overline{X}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
3.12
, i.e. has
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
and no descendant with elliptic boundary. Indeed, if
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
then by Theorem
A
(3)
the singularity type
\mathcal{S}
should be listed in Tables
9
–
12
, but we check directly that it is not the case. Similarly, if
\overline{X}
had a descendant with elliptic boundary then by Theorem
E
(b)
we would have
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{can}}+\mathcal{T}
, for some canonical singularity type
\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{can}}
with
\#\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{can}}\geqslant 6
, which is false.

Therefore, the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}
has a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration as in
(1)
–
(3)
above. Let
\widecheck{D}
be the sum of
D
and vertical
(-1)
-curves. Contracting
(-1)
-curves in
\widecheck{D}
and its images we get an inner birational morphism
(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z})
onto the minimal log resolution of the configuration
{p}\coloneqq{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{3}+{c}
from Example
3.9
.
Since
{p}
is unique up to a projective equivalence, the log surface
(Z,D_{Z})
is unique up to an isomorphism, see Lemma
5.16
(a)
, case
\nu=3
. Hence by Lemma
2.18
, the same is true for
(X,D)
. This proves the uniqueness of
\overline{X}
. Moreover, by Lemma
2.11
(b)
,
(c)
we have
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
, which equals
0
for
i=0,1
and
1
for
i=2
by Lemma
5.16
(a)
,
(d)
, case
\nu=3
. This proves the last statement.

∎

4.
Del Pezzo surfaces of height one

We now move on to the proof of Theorem
A
. In this section, we settle the height one case. While it is relatively easy to classify the possible singularity types, see Section
4A
, computing the number of isomorphism classes of surfaces of each type (or moduli dimensions) takes more effort, see Section
4B
. We begin with an elementary lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(X,D)
be a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one, with a fixed
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height one with respect to
D
.
Let
H\coloneqq D_{\mathrm{hor}}
be the
1
-section.

(a)

Every degenerate fiber
F
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, say
L_{F}
; and
F_{\mathrm{red}}-L_{F}=F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}
.

(b)

Every degenerate fiber meets
H
in a tip of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. In particular, there are exactly
\beta_{D}(H)
degenerate fibers.

(c)

All connected components of
D
not containing
H
are admissible chains. In particular,
\overline{X}
has at most one non–log terminal singularity.

(d)

The log surface
(X,D)
swaps vertically to a log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
from Example
4.2
below, see Figure
2(a)
.

(a)
This follows from Lemma
2.8
(a)
,
(b)
.

(b)
Let
C_{F}
be the component of
F
meeting
H
, so
C_{F}
has multiplicity one in
F
. Since by
(a)
the fiber
F
has only one
(-1)
-curve,
L_{F}
, Lemma
2.3
(b)
implies that
C_{F}
is a tip of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
and
C_{F}\neq L_{F}
. By
(a)
we get that
C_{F}\subseteq D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. It follows that the branching number
\beta_{D}(H)=H\cdot D_{\mathrm{vert}}
equals the number of degenerate fibers.

(c)
Let
V
be a connected component of
D
not containing
H
. Then
V
is contained in some fiber
F
. Part
(a)
shows that
F
has a unique
(-1)
-curve
L_{F}
. Since
L_{F}
is non-branching in
F
, the divisor
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}=F_{\mathrm{red}}-L_{F}
has two connected components, namely
V
and
V^{\prime}\coloneqq F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}-V
. Since
V^{\prime}
meets
H
, it contains a component of multiplicity one in
F
. Thus
V
is a chain by Lemma
2.3
(b)(b.ii)
.

(d)
Put
g=p_{a}(H)
,
e=-H^{2}
. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
be all degenerate fibers. By
(a)
each
F_{i}
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, call it
L_{i}
. By
(b)
we have
\nu=\beta_{D}(H)
, so
e>2g-2+\nu
by Lemma
2.8
(b)
. For each
i
, contract
L_{i}
and
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent images of
D
until the image of
F_{i}
is supported on a chain
[2,1,2]
. Denote the resulting morphism by
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow Y
, and define
D_{Y}
as
\varphi_{*}D
minus all
(-1)
-curves. Part
(b)
implies that
\varphi
is an isomorphism near
H
. It follows that
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
is a vertical swap, see Definition
1.3
(a)
.

The surface
Y
is a minimal ruled surface over a smooth curve
B
, with a section
H_{Y}
. Since
H_{Y}\cong H
has genus
g
, so does
B
. By
[
Har77
, Proposition V.2.2]
, we have
Y=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
for some rank two vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
B
. Since
H_{Y}^{2}<0
, by Proposition V.2.9. loc. cit.
-\deg\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{E}=e
. Since
e>2g-2+\nu\geqslant 2g-2
, Theorem V.2.12(b) loc. cit. implies that
\mathcal{E}
is decomposable. Thus we can take
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{*}
for some line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
B
of degree
e
. Therefore,
(Y,D_{Y})
is the log surface constructed in Example
4.2
, as claimed.

∎

Example 4.2

(Primitive log surfaces of height one, see Figure
2(a)
)
.

Fix an integer
\nu\geqslant 0
. Let
B
be a smooth curve of genus
g
, and let
\mathcal{L}
be an ample line bundle on
B
, of degree
e>2g-2+\nu
. Let
S=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{*})
be a minimal ruled surface over
B
. Let
D_{S}
be the section corresponding to the surjection
\mathcal{O}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}
, see
[
Har77
, V.2.11.3]
. Then
D_{S}
is a smooth curve of genus
g
and
D_{S}^{2}=-e
. The log surface
(S,D_{S})
is the minimal log resolution of the cone
\overline{S}\coloneqq\operatorname{Proj}(\bigoplus_{k\geqslant 0}H^{0}(B,\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k}))
over
(B,\mathcal{L})
, see
[
Băd01
, §14.38]
. If
g=0
then
S=\mathbb{F}_{e}
and
\overline{S}
is the cone over a rational normal curve. If
g=1
we call
\overline{S}
an
elliptic cone
.

Choose
\nu
fibers
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
and a point
p_{i}\in F_{i}\setminus D_{S}
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
. Blow up at each
p_{i}
and its infinitely near point on the proper transform of
F_{i}
. Denote the resulting morphism by
\tau\colon Y\longrightarrow S
. Let
A_{i}
be the
(-1)
-curve over
p_{i}
and let
D_{Y}=\tau^{*}(D_{S}+\sum_{i}F_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}-\sum_{i}A_{i}
.

Remark 4.3
.

The log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
constructed in Example
4.2
is primitive, in the sense of Definition
1.3
(e)
.

Suppose the contrary. Then
Y
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
meeting
D_{Y}
normally, in exactly one
(-2)
-curve, say
C
. In particular,
Y
is not minimal, so
\nu\geqslant 1
. Suppose
C
lies in some fiber
F
. Let
L
be the
(-1)
-curve in
F
and let
\mu=A\cdot L
. We have
A\cdot F=2\mu+1
. If
F^{\prime}
is another degenerate fiber then
A
meets
F^{\prime}
only in the
(-1)
-curve, so
A\cdot F^{\prime}
is even, which is impossible. Thus
\nu=1
. Let
\psi\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
be the contraction of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-C
, and let
H=\psi((D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}})
,
V=\psi(C)
,
\overline{A}=\psi(A)
. Since
\operatorname{NS}(Z)
is generated by the classes of
V
and
H
, the equality
((2\mu+1)H-\overline{A})\cdot V=0
implies that
((2\mu+1)H-\overline{A})^{2}=0
. Since
H^{2}\leqslant 1-e\leqslant-1
,
\overline{A}\cdot H\geqslant 0
and
\overline{A}^{2}=-1+2\mu^{2}
, we get
0=((2\mu+1)H-\overline{A})^{2}\leqslant-(2\mu+1)^{2}-1+2\mu^{2}=-2\mu^{2}-4\mu-2\leqslant-2
, a contradiction.

Thus
C
is horizontal, so it is the proper transform of the section
D_{S}
. It follows that
(e,g)=(2,0)
. Since
A
is disjoint from
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}
, we have
A\cdot F=2\mu
for a fiber
F
, where
\mu
is the intersection number of
A
with each vertical
(-1)
-curve. Let
\psi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
be the contraction of all vertical curves not meeting
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Then
\psi(A)^{2}=-1+2\nu\mu^{2}
, so
\psi(A)
is a curve of odd self-intersection number on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
; a contradiction.

∎

4A.
Singularity types

We now list all singularity types of rational log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of height one, together with the structure of some witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration. Together with Lemma
4.1
(d)
above, this implies Theorem
A
(3.a)
. This way, we get a recipe to reconstruct such surfaces from
\mathbb{F}_{m}
. Case
0pt=1
of Proposition
C
, which we prove in Corollary
4.8
below, asserts that this process of reconstruction is unique, with exceptions listed in Table
13
.

To describe the witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of the minimal resolution we use decorations introduced in Notation
2.22
. The notation for singularity types is summarized in Section
2G
.

Lemma 4.4

(Case
0pt=1
: list of types, see Table
9
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank
1
and height
1
. Then either
\overline{X}
is an elliptic cone, or
\overline{X}
is rational and the singularity type of
\overline{X}
, together with the structure of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
(X,D)
of height one, is one of the following.

(1)

[\boldsymbol{m}]
,

(2)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[}T^{*},\boldsymbol{m}]+T^{|1|}
,

(3)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[}T_{1}^{*},\boldsymbol{m},T_{2}^{*}]^{|2|}+T_{1}^{|1|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{T}_{2}
,

(4)

[\boldsymbol{m},T^{*},r^{|1|},T]+[(2)_{r-2}]^{|1|}
,

(5)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[}T_{1}^{*},\boldsymbol{m},T_{2}^{*},r^{|2|},T_{2}]+T_{1}^{|1|}+[(2)_{r-2}]^{|2|}
,

(6)

[T_{1},r_{1}^{|1|},T_{1}^{*},\boldsymbol{m},T_{2}^{*},r_{2}^{|2|},T_{2}]+[(2)_{r_{1}-2}]^{|1|}+[(2)_{r_{2}-2}]^{|2|}
,

(7)

\langle\boldsymbol{m},\prescript{|1|}{}{T}_{1},\prescript{|2|}{}{T}_{2},\prescript{|3|}{}{T}_{3}\rangle
+
(T_{1}^{*})^{|1|}+(T_{2}^{*})^{|2|}+(T_{3}^{*})^{|3|}
, where
the fork is admissible or log canonical,

(8)

\langle\boldsymbol{m};\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],\prescript{|2|}{}{[}2],[T^{*},r^{|3|},T]\rangle+[2]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}+[(2)_{r-2}]^{|3|}
,

(9)

\langle\boldsymbol{m};\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],\prescript{|2|}{}{T},[2,2^{|3|},2]\rangle+[2]^{|1|}+(T^{*})^{|2|}
, where
T\in\{[3],[2,2],[2,2,2]\}
, and if
T=[2,2,2]
then
m\geqslant 3
,

(10)

\langle\boldsymbol{m};\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],[2,2^{|2|},2],[2,2^{|3|},2]\rangle+[2]^{|1|}
,
m\geqslant 3
,

(11)

\langle b;\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],T^{*},[\boldsymbol{2},T]\rangle+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|1|}
,
T\in\{[3],[2,2]\}

(12)

\langle b;\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],[2],[\boldsymbol{m},2]\rangle+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|1|}

(13)

\langle b;\prescript{|1|}{}{T},[2],[\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle+([(2)_{b-2}]*T^{*})^{|1|}
,
T\in\{[3],[2,2]\}

(14)

\langle b;\prescript{|1|}{}{T},[2],[\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+([(2)_{b-2}]*T^{*})^{|1|}
, where
d(T)\in\{3,4,5,6\}
and
b\geqslant 3
if
T=[(2)_{5}]

(15)

\langle 3;[2,2]^{|1|},[2],[\boldsymbol{3},2]\rangle
,

(16)

\langle b;[(2)_{b-1}]^{|1|},[2],[\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
, where
b\in\{3,4,5,6\}
,

(17)

\langle 3;[2,3]^{|1|},[2],[\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[2]^{|1|}
,

(18)

\langle b;[2],[2]^{|1|},\prescript{|2|}{}{[}T,\boldsymbol{m},2]\rangle+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|1|}+(T^{*})^{|2|}

(19)

\langle b;[2],[2]^{|1|},[T,r^{|2|},T^{*},\boldsymbol{m},2]\rangle+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|1|}+[(2)_{r-2}]^{|2|}
,

(20)

\langle b;[2],[3]^{|1|},[2,2^{|2|},2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[(2)_{b-3},3,2]^{|1|}
,

(21)

\langle 3,[2],[2,2]^{|1|},[2,2^{|2|},2,\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle
,

(22)

\langle b;[3],\prescript{|1|}{}{[}2],\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{r-1},\boldsymbol{2},2,2]\rangle+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|1|}+[r]^{|2|}
,

r\in\{2,3\}
,

(23)

\langle b;[2],\prescript{|1|}{}{T},\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{r-1},\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+([(2)_{b-2}]*T^{*})^{|1|}+[r]^{|2|}
,
r\leqslant 4
,
T\in\{[3],[2,2]\}
, and
b\geqslant 3
if
T=[2,2]
,
r=4
,

(24)

\langle 3;[2],[2,2]^{|1|},\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{r-1},\boldsymbol{2},2]\rangle+[r]^{|2|}
,
r\in\{2,3,4\}
,

(25)

\prescript{|1|}{|2|}{[\![}\boldsymbol{m}]\!]^{|3|}_{|4|}+[2]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}+[2]^{|4|}
,
m\geqslant 3
,

(26)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[\![}b,2,\boldsymbol{m}]\!]^{|2|}_{|3|}+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|1|}+[2]^{|2|}+[2]^{|3|}
where
b\geqslant 3
if
m=2
,

(27)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[\![}a,2,\boldsymbol{m},2,b]\!]^{|2|}+[(2)_{a-3},3]^{|1|}+[(2)_{b-3},3]^{|2|}
, where
b\geqslant a
and
b\geqslant 3
if
m=2
,

for some
m,r,r_{1},r_{2},a,b\geqslant 2
and admissible chains
T,T_{1},T_{2}
. Recall that we use a symbol
“
(2)_{-1}
”
, defined in formula (
2
), and as a result some chains in the above list become empty (and as such should be ignored). A list without this simplifying convention is given in Table
9
.

Let
D_{H}
be the connected component of
D
containing
H\coloneqq D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Assume first that
H
is non-rational. By the classification of log canonical singularities, see Section
2B
,
H
is a smooth curve of genus one, and
H=D_{H}
. Lemma
4.1
(b)
implies that there are no degenerate fibers, so by Lemma
4.1
(d)
,
\overline{X}
is an elliptic cone.

Thus we can assume that
H
is rational, so
D_{H}
is a rational tree. Since
\overline{X}
is log canonical,
D_{H}
can be a chain, a fork or a bench. We consider each of those cases separately.

Case 1:

D_{H}
is a chain.
Let
F
be a degenerate fiber. By Lemma
2.3
(b)
either
F
is columnar, or
F
becomes columnar after the contraction of some twig
[(2)_{r-2},1]
of
D+L_{F}
,
r\geqslant 2
, whose last tip is
L_{F}
. In the latter case
F_{\mathrm{red}}=\langle r;[(2)_{r-2},1],T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T^{*}\rangle
, hence
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}=[(2)_{r-2}]+[T,r,T^{*}]
. Therefore,
D
is as in

(1)
–
(6)
.

Case 2:

D_{H}
is a fork.
Let
B
be the branching component of
D_{H}
.

Assume first that
B=H
. Then the connected components of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
meeting
H
are chains. Lemma
2.3
implies that in fact all connected components of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
are chains. As in case
1
, we infer that every fiber
F
is either columnar, or satisfies
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}=[T,r,T^{*}]+[(2)_{r-2}]
. Let
c
be the number of columnar fibers. By Lemma
2.1
we have
c\leqslant 2
. If
c=0
then
D
is as in
(7)
. If
c=1
then either
D_{H}=\langle\boldsymbol{m};[T^{*},r,T],[2],[2]\rangle
, so
D
is as in
(8)
, or by Lemma
2.1
(e)
we have
[T^{*},r,T]=[2,2,2]
, so
D
is as in
(9)
. Eventually, if
c=2
then
D_{H}
has two twigs of length at least three, so these twigs are of type
[2,2,2]
, and
D
is as in
(10)
.

Assume now that
B\neq H
, so
B
is contained in some fiber
F
. Let
T_{H}
be the twig of
D_{H}
containing
H
, so
T_{H}=[T_{1},\boldsymbol{m},T_{2}]
for some
T_{1},T_{2}
, possibly empty. Contract
L_{F}
and subsequent
(-1)
-curves in the images of
F
until it becomes a chain, and denote this morphism by
\sigma
. The exceptional curves of
\sigma
have multiplicities at least
2
in
F
, so
\sigma
is an isomorphism near
H
. We infer that
T_{2}\neq 0
and
\sigma_{*}F_{\mathrm{red}}=[T_{2},1,T_{2}^{*}]
. Thus
D_{H}=\langle b;T,(T_{2}^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T_{H}\rangle
for some chain
T\subseteq F
meeting
L_{F}
.

Claim
.

Put
V=F_{\mathrm{red}}-L_{F}-(F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{H})
. Then
V
is a chain, and the following hold.

(a)

If
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
then
[V,L_{F},T]=[(2)_{b-2}]*[T^{*},1,T]
and
d(T)\leqslant 6
;

(b)

If
L_{F}
does not meet
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
then
\#T\geqslant 2
,
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)
, and either
[T,L_{F},V]=[2,3,1,2]
,
b=3
or
[T,L_{F},V]=[(2)_{b-1},1]
,
b\in\{3,4,5,6\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
(c)
,
V
is a chain. Since
\#T_{H}\geqslant\#(H+T_{2})\geqslant 2
, Lemma
2.1
gives
d(T)\leqslant 6
.

(a)
Assume that
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
. Then the chain
[T,L_{F},V]
contracts to a smooth point, so
V=T^{*}*[(2)_{k}]
for some
k\geqslant-1
. After this contraction,
B
becomes a
(-1)
-curve, so
-1=-b+k+1
, i.e.
k=b-2
, as claimed.

(b)
Assume that
L_{F}
does not meet
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
. Since
L_{F}
meets
T
, we have
\#T\geqslant 2
. Since
d(T)\leqslant 6
, we have
T=[2,3],[3,2]
, or
[(2)_{k}]
for some
k\in\{2,3,4,5\}
, see Lemma
2.1
(c)
. Because
L_{F}+T+V
contracts to a smooth point, we see that
L_{F}
meets the last tip of
T
and either
T=[2,3]
,
V=[2]
or
T=[(2)_{k}]
,
V=0
. After this contraction
B
becomes a
(-1)
-curve, so
b=3
or
k+1
; respectively.

∎

Subcase 2.1:

T_{1}=0
.
Then
F
is the unique degenerate fiber.
Assume
T_{2}\neq[2]
, so
d(T_{H})>d(T_{2})\geqslant 3
. Since
d(T_{2}^{*})=d(T_{2})\geqslant 3
and
d(T)^{-1}+d(T_{2}^{*})^{-1}+d(T_{H})^{-1}\geqslant 1
, we get
T=[2]
and
d(T_{2}^{*})=3
, so
T_{2}^{*}=[2,2]
or
[3]
. Claim
(a)
implies that
D
is as in
(11)
.

Next, assume that
T_{2}=[2]
,
T=[2]
. Then Claim
(a)
implies that
V=[(2)_{b-3},3]
, so
D
is as in
(12)
.

Eventually, assume
T_{2}=[2]
,
T\neq[2]
. Since
T_{H}=[\boldsymbol{m},2]
, Lemma
2.1
gives
m\in\{2,3\}
.

Consider the case
m=3
. Then
d(T)=3
, so
T=[3]
or
[2,2]
. If
T=[3]
then by Claim
(a)
we have
V=[(2)_{b-3},3,2]
, so
D
is as in
(13)
. If
T=[2,2]
then by Claim
(a)
and
(b)

D
is as in
(13)
if
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
and as in
(15)
if
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)
.

Consider the case
m=2
. Then
d(T)\leqslant 6
. If
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
, by Claim
(a)
,
D
is as in
(14)
. If
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)
, Claim
(b)
implies that either
T=[(2)_{b-1}]
,
b\leqslant 6
, and
D
is as in
(16)
; or
T=[2,3]
and
D
is as in
(17)
.

Subcase 2.2:

T_{1}\neq 0
.
Let
F^{\prime}\neq F
be the degenerate fiber containing
T_{1}
. The
1
-section
H
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T_{1})
, which therefore has multiplicity one in
F^{\prime}
. Hence by Lemma
2.3
either
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}=[T_{1}^{*},1,T_{1}]
, i.e.
F^{\prime}
is columnar, or
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}=\langle r;[(2)_{r-2},1],U^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},U^{*}\rangle
, where
T_{1}=[U^{*},r,U]
for some
r\geqslant 2
.

If
T_{2}^{*}=[2]
,
T=[2]
then by Claim
(a)

D
is as in
(18)
if
F^{\prime}
is columnar and as in
(19)
if it is not.
Assume
T_{2}^{*}\neq[2]
or
T\neq[2]
. Since
d(T_{H})^{-1}+d(T_{2}^{*})+d(T)\geqslant 1
, we have
d(T_{H})\leqslant 6
. Since
\#T_{H}=\#T_{1}+1+\#T_{2}\geqslant 3
we get
T_{H}=[(2)_{k}]
for some
k\in\{3,4,5\}
. Moreover, since
d(T_{H})\geqslant 4
, we have
\{d(T_{2}),d(T)\}=\{2,3\}
.

Consider the case when
F^{\prime}
is not columnar. Then
k=5
,
T_{2}=[2]
, so since by assumption
T\neq[2]
, we get
d(T)=3
. If
T=[3]
then by Claim
(a)

D
is as in
(20)
. If
T=[2,2]
then since
D_{H}
cannot consist of
(-2)
-curves, we have
b\geqslant 3
, so the Claim shows that
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T)
and
D
is as in
(21)
.

Consider now the case when
F^{\prime}
is columnar, so
F^{\prime}=[r,1,(2)_{r}]
, where
r=\#T_{1}=k-1-\#T_{2}
. Recall that
d(T_{2})\leqslant 3
, so
T_{2}=[2,2]
or
[2]
. If
T_{2}=[2,2]
then
T=[2]
and Claim
(a)
implies that
D
is as in
(22)
. Assume
T_{2}=[2]
. Then
d(T)=3
, so
T=[3]
or
[2,2]
. If
L_{F}
meets
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T)
then by Claim
(a)

D
is as in
(23)
. Otherwise,
T=[2,2]
, so by Claim
(b)

b=3
and
D
is as in
(24)
.

Case 3:

D_{H}
is a bench.
Assume first that
D_{H}-H
has no branching components. Then
D_{H}=[\![\boldsymbol{m}]\!]
, and
H
meets four disjoint
(-2)
-curves. Lemmas
4.1
(a)
and
2.3
(b)
imply that each of those
(-2)
-curves lies in a fiber
[2,1,2]
, and
D
is as in
(25)
.

Let now
V
be a connected component of
D_{H}-H
with a branching component. By Lemma
2.3
(b)(b.ii)
,
V
meets
H
in a tip. Hence
V
is a fork of type
\langle b,[2],[2],T_{V}\rangle
and meets
H
in
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T_{V})
. Let
F
be a fiber containing
V
. Contract
L_{F}
and subsequent
(-1)
-curves in
F
until the image of
V
contains a
(-1)
-curve. Denote by
H^{\prime}
,
V^{\prime}
,
F^{\prime}
the images of
H
,
V
and
F
, respectively, and let
L^{\prime}
be the
(-1)
-curve in
V^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
L^{\prime}
is not a tip of
V^{\prime}
. Since
L^{\prime}
is non-branching in
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}
, we get
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}=V^{\prime}
. Now
V^{\prime}-L^{\prime}
has two connected components: one of them is not a chain, and the other meets the
1
-section
H^{\prime}
, so contains a component of multiplicity one. This is a contradiction with Lemma
2.3
(b)(b.ii)
.

Thus
L^{\prime}
is a tip of
V^{\prime}
. Since
L^{\prime}
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F^{\prime}
, it has multiplicity at least two in
F^{\prime}
, so it does not meet
H^{\prime}
. It follows that
L^{\prime}
is the image of one of the short
(-2)
-twigs of
V
. Thus
F_{\mathrm{red}}=\langle b,[U,1,2],[2],T_{V}\rangle
, where
U
is a chain such that
[U,1,2]
contracts to a smooth point, so
U=[(2)_{k-1},3]
for some
k\geqslant 0
. After the contraction of
[U,1,2]
, the image of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
is a chain
[T_{V},b-k-1,2]
, so
T_{V}=[2]^{*}=[2]
and
b-k-1=1
, i.e.
k=b-2
. We conclude that if
D_{H}-H
has two branching components then
D
is as in
(27)
. Otherwise,
H
meets one fiber of the above type, and two twigs of type
[2]
. Like before, using Lemmas
4.1
(a)
and
2.3
(b)
we conclude that those twigs lie in fibers
[2,1,2]
, so
D
is as in
(26)
.
∎

Remark 4.5

(Canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height
1
, see Figure
16
)
.

For future reference we point out all canonical types on the list in Lemma
4.4
. Since for a canonical type all components have self intersection number
-2
, to get one we need to fix the value of all parameters as
2
; and types of all admissible chains as
[2,\dots,2]
. Note that if
T=[2,\dots,2]
then the same holds for
T^{*}
if and only if
T=[2]
. As a consequence, the canonical types in Lemma
4.4
are precisely those in Figure
16
. The vertically primitive ones are those of types
\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
and
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
, see Figures
16(a)
,
16(b)
,
16(c)
and
16(g)
. Their minimal log resolutions are constructed in Example
4.2
for
g=0
,
e=2
and
\nu\leqslant 3
; so they are primitive by Remark
4.3
.

4B.
Computation of moduli

Lemma
4.4
allows to construct each rational log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one and height one (o rather its minimal log resolution) by a specific sequence of blowups over some Hirzebruch surface. In this section we analyze to which extent this process is unique, thus proving Proposition
C
for surfaces of height one, see Corollary
4.8
. Once this is done, the proof of case
0pt(\overline{X})=1
of Theorem
A
will be complete.

We begin with a brief sketch of the proof. Our goal is to put all log surfaces
(X,D)
from Lemma
4.4
with a fixed graph of
D
in a smooth family, and to count the number of its non-isomorphic fibers.

Let
\widecheck{D}
be the sum of
D
and
(-1)
-curves provided by Lemma
4.4
. Lemma
2.20
allows to replace
D
by
\widecheck{D}
and to study the set
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
, where
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
is the combinatorial type of
(X,\widecheck{D})
, see Section
2F
.

Now, we simplify
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
by inner blowdowns. More precisely, let
(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z})
be a vertical inner snc-minimalization, i.e. the contraction of all
(-1)
-curves in
\widecheck{D}_{\mathrm{vert}}
and its images which are not tips of the images of
\widecheck{D}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the combinatorial type of
(Z,D_{Z})
: it is as in Lemma
4.6
. The center of each blowdown is uniquely determined by the combinatorial type
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
, so it is enough to study
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
, cf. Lemma
2.18
.

To further simplify
(Z,D_{Z})
, we need to perform an outer blowdown
(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
, centered somewhere on the curve
G^{\circ}\coloneqq G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)
, where
G
is some component of
D_{Y}
. At this point we need to understand the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}
, see Lemma
2.19
. This is done case by case in Lemma
4.7
.

We note that the proof of Propositions
C
in case
0pt(\overline{X})=2
is based on the same strategy, but requires substantially less computations, as surfaces of bigger height turn out to be more rigid.

We now proceed with the proof, following the strategy outlined above. First, we list possible combinatorial types
\mathcal{Z}
, together with some additional ones which will be useful either for induction or in the next section.

Lemma 4.6

(Vertical inner snc-minimalization, see Table
1
and Figure
17
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a rational log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one. Let
(X,D)
be its minimal log resolution, equipped with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration as in Lemma
4.4
. Let
\widecheck{D}
be the sum of
D
and all vertical
(-1)
-curves. Let
(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z})
be a vertical inner snc-minimalization, i.e. a contraction of a maximal number of vertical
(-1)
-curves which are not tips of the subsequent images of
\widecheck{D}
. Then the weighted graph of
D_{Z}
is as in one of the cases
(1)
–
(14)
described below and shown in Figure
17
, with additional restrictions listed in Table
1
.

In each of these cases, we have
D_{Z}=V_{Z}+\sum_{j=1}^{v}F_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{h}H_{j}
, where, unless stated otherwise:

•

V_{Z}
is the support of all degenerate fibers,

•

F_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,v\}
is a nondegenerate fiber,

•

h\in\{1,2\}
, and for each
j\in\{1,\dots,h\}
,
H_{j}
is a
1
-section,

•

if
h=2
then the
1
-sections
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
are disjoint and meet distinct components of
V_{Z}
,

•

H_{1}=[m]
for some
m\geqslant 0
; in fact
m=\widetilde{m}+v
, where
\widetilde{m}
is the self-intersection number of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
.

and, depending on the type of
(X,\widecheck{D})
as specified in Table
1
, one of the following holds, see Figure
17
:

(1)

V_{Z}=0
; and
v\leqslant 4

(2)

, or

where each

; and

(3)

; and

,

(4)

for some
k\geqslant 3
and admissible
T
, where
[1]
meets the underlined curve; or

for some

, where each

meets the
(-k)
-curve; and
v\leqslant 1
,

(5)

,

.

and admissible
T
,

,

,

.

\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2

Table 2
.
Lemma
4.7
(c)
: cases when the family is not
h^{1}
-stratified or universal.

We now state the main technical lemma, which is a counterpart of Proposition
C
for log surfaces
(Z,D_{Z})
. We prove it at the end of this section. For the relevant definitions see Section
2F
.

Lemma 4.7

(Moduli computation)
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the combinatorial type of a log surface
(Z,D_{Z})
from Lemma
4.6
.

(a)

If
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})<\infty
then
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a stratified family; and
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is listed in Table
1
.

(b)

If
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=\infty
then
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful family, whose base
B
and symmetry group is listed in Table
3
. The corresponding entry in Table
1
is
\mathcal{M}^{\dim B}
, see Notation
2.25
.

(c)

The family from
(a)
or
(b)
can be chosen
h^{1}
-stratified or universal, respectively, unless
\mathcal{Z}
is as in Table
2
.

Corollary 4.8

(Case
0pt=1
of Propositions
C
,
D
)
.

The set of isomorphism classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank 1, height 1 and fixed singularity type is represented by a family with properties listed in Propositions
C
,
D
.

Fix a singularity type
\mathcal{S}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
be the set of isomorphism classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, height one and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
be the set of minimal log resolutions of surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
. On each
(X,D)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
, consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration given by Lemma
4.4
. Let
\widecheck{D}
be the sum of
D
and all vertical
(-1)
-curves, and let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
be the combinatorial type of
(X,\widecheck{D})
. Since type
\mathcal{S}
appears exactly once in the list of Lemma
4.4
, it uniquely determines the extended type
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
is the image of
\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
by the natural restriction map
\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the corresponding type from Lemma
4.7
, see Table
1
.

By Lemma
2.18
we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
. Assume
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})<\infty
. By Lemma
4.7
(a)

\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a stratified family, which is
h^{1}
-stratified unless
\mathcal{Z}
is as in Table
2
. By Lemma
2.18
the same is true for
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
, with the corresponding exceptional types
\mathcal{S}
listed in Table
14
. By Lemma
2.20
(b)
, the same holds for
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
, and
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
. If
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
, this means that
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})=1
, and
(X,D)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
has
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
(or
1
for the exceptions). Otherwise, the number
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})=\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
(and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
in the exceptional case
\mathcal{S}=\mathrm{E}_{8}
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
) are listed in Tables
13
and
14
, as needed.

Assume that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is infinite. By Lemma
4.7
(b)

\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful family whose base and symmetry group is listed in Table
3
. This family is universal unless
\mathcal{Z}
is as in Table
2
.

Looking at the list in Lemma
4.4
we check directly that

(7)

\mbox{every automorphism of the weighted graph of }D\mbox{ preserves }D_{\mathrm{hor}}.

Observation (
7
) implies that
is
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-equivariant. To see this, write
\psi=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}
for some morphisms
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
, let
L
be a
(-1)
-curve in
\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{2}
, and let
L^{\prime}
be a component of
(\psi_{1})_{*}\widecheck{D}
corresponding to a vertex in the same
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-orbit. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}
are vertical
(-1)
-curves with of branching number
2
in
(\psi_{1})_{*}\widecheck{D}
. Since our
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration has height one,
L
and
L^{\prime}
are disjoint, so
\psi_{2}
contracts them both, as needed.

Thus
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
gets identified with a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
, see Section
2F
.
Hence by Lemma
2.18
the set
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
-faithful family with the same base and symmetry group; universal unless
\mathcal{Z}
is as in Table
2
. Observation (
7
) and Lemma
2.21
imply that this family satisfies condition (
6
), so by Lemma
2.20
(c)
it restricts to an almost faithful family representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
, as needed.

By Lemma
2.11
(c)
we have
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
. Let
\tau\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{m},B)
be the contraction of all vertical curves which are disjoint from
H_{1}
. Then
B
is a sum of the negative section and
\nu
fibers, where
\nu
is the number of degenerate fibers in
\widecheck{D}
. Put
\overline{h}^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{F}_{m}}(-\log B))
,
\overline{\chi}=\chi(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{F}_{m}}(-\log B))
. A direct computation as in
[
FZ94
, Lemma 2.5(2)]
gives
\overline{h}^{2}=0
and
\overline{\chi}=5+m-\nu
. By Lemma
2.11
,
h^{2}=\overline{h}^{2}=0
and
\chi=\overline{\chi}-\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon
is the number of outer blowups in the decomposition of
\tau
. Computing
\varepsilon
shows that
\chi
is as in Table
9
.

Remark 4.9

(Comparison of Lemma
4.7
with some results in the literature)
.

To give a self-contained treatment, we present a complete proof of Lemma
4.7
below. However, explicit computations in various cases were already made in literature. For example,
[
FZ94
, Example 4.16]
treats the case
4.6
(5)
; and a straightforward generalization of the computations from loc. cit. allows one to treat cases
4.6
(2)
and
4.6
(3)
, too.

Case
4.6
(13)
essentially classifies del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and type
\mathrm{E}_{k+4}
(together with a specific
(-1)
-curve on the minimal resolution, whose existence is known, see
[
Ye02
, Theorem 3.4]
). Thus Lemma
4.7
in this case can be inferred e.g. from explicit equations in weighted projective spaces given in
[
Ye02
,
KN20
]
.

Recall that the proof of Lemma
4.7
relies on describing the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}
, where
(Y,D_{Y})
is obtained from
(Z,D_{Z})
by an inner blowdown, and the curve
G^{\circ}
is the locus of its possible centers. Thus we study specific automorphisms of the affine surface
S_{Y}=Y\setminus(D_{Y}-G)
. The entire group
\operatorname{Aut}(S_{Y})
is much more complicated than
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
, but in some cases it is well described in the literature. For example, in case
4.6
(9)
with
v=1
, see Figure
17(i)
, the log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in Figure
17(b)
(with
h=v=1
,
T=[d]
), and
S_{Y}
is a Gizatullin surface. If
\operatorname{char}{k}|d
,
[
GD74
]
shows that
\operatorname{Aut}(S_{Y})
has a fixed point, which is the reason why in this case we get
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=2
, cf. Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
,
\operatorname{Aut}(S_{Y})
is usually transitive
[
Kov15
]
, and indeed we get
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
, cf. Lemma
2.19
(2.b)
. Of course, in the proof below we do not use the description of
\operatorname{Aut}(S_{Y})
, but work directly with its well-behaved subgroup
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
.

We study each case of Lemma
4.6
separately.

Case

4.6
(1)
.
In this case,
Z
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{m}
for some
m\geqslant 0
, and
D_{Z}
is a union of
v\leqslant 4
fibers, a negative section
H_{1}=[m]
, and possibly a section
H_{2}=[-m]
disjoint from
H_{1}
. By Lemma
2.14
(e)
we have

h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=0
if
v\leqslant 3
, and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=1
if
v=4
.

Assume
h=2
. Performing elementary transformations on a fiber in
D_{Z}
and applying Lemma
2.18
we can assume
m=0
. If
v=3
then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
, so
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family over a point, as claimed in
(a)
. If
v=4
, Examples
2.16
and
2.17
show that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a universal
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful family, with one-dimensional base parametrizing the choice of the fourth fiber, as claimed in
(b)
and
(c)
.

Assume
h=1
. If
v\leqslant 3
, case
h=2
above implies that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
, as needed. Assume
v=4
. Choose a section
H\not\subseteq D_{Z}
disjoint from
H_{1}
, and let
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
be the combinatorial type of
(Z,D_{Z}+H)
. An explicit description of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{m})
in
[
Bla12
, §6.1]
shows that
\operatorname{Aut}(Z,D_{Z})
acts transitively on the linear system
|H|
(and on
|H|\setminus\{H_{1}\}
in case
m=0
), so the restriction map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is bijective. Thus the universal,
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
-faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
constructed in case
h=2
above restricts to a faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
, this family is
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful. The fact that it is universal follows from
[
FZ94
, Proposition 1.7(1)]
and from the fact that the dimensions
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log(D_{Z}+H)))
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
are equal.

Case

4.6
(2)
.

Let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Z}
, and let
G\subseteq D_{Y}
be the component containing its image. Put
G^{\circ}\coloneqq G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)
.
Assume
h=2
,
v\geqslant 1
. By Lemma
2.18
performing elementary transformations on
F_{1}
we can further assume that
m=1
. Contracting all vertical curves in
D_{Y}
which do not meet the image of
H_{1}
we get an inner birational morphism
\tau\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{1},\widehat{{p}})
where
(\mathbb{F}_{1},\widehat{{p}})
is as in case
4.6
(1)
above, with
v\leqslant 3
; so
(\mathbb{F}_{1},\widehat{{p}})
is unique up to an isomorphism and has
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}(-\log\widehat{{p}}))=0
. In other words, if
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{F}
are the combinatorial types of
(Y,D_{Y})
and
(\mathbb{F}_{1},\widehat{{p}})
, then
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{F})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family over a point. By Lemma
2.18
the same is true for
\mathcal{Y}
.

We claim that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
. Let
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be a composition of
\tau
with the contraction of the negative section
\Xi_{1}
. Then
{p}
is a sum of lines
{l}_{0}
,
{l}_{j}\coloneqq\varphi(\sigma(F_{j}))
,
j\in\{1,\dots,v\}
which pass through the image of
H_{1}
; and a line
{l}\coloneqq\varphi(\sigma(H_{2}))
which does not. Fix coordinates
[x:y:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{l}=\{x=0\}
,
{l}_{0}=\{y=0\}
,
{l}_{1}=\{z=0\}
, and consider a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
given by
\alpha_{\lambda}\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[\lambda x:y:z]:\lambda\in{k}^{*}
. Put
{c}_{\lambda}=\{\lambda x^{d}=y^{e}\}
, where
d=d(T)
,
e=d(T^{*}-\mathrm{tip}^{+}(T^{*}))
. Then
{k}^{*}\ni\lambda\mapsto G^{\circ}\cap\tau^{-1}_{*}{c}_{\lambda}\in G^{\circ}
parametrizes
G^{\circ}
. Hence the transitive action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
on
\{{c}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{k}^{*}}
, given by
\alpha_{\lambda}({c}_{1})={c}_{\lambda^{d}}
lifts to a transitive action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}
; as needed.

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
is trivial if
v\leqslant 1
, and if
v=2
it is generated by the involution interchanging the fibers. The latter is realized by some
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(Z,D_{Z})
: to see this, choose the coordinates above so that
{l}_{2}=\{y=z\}
and note that an automorphism of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
given by
[x:y:z]\mapsto[\zeta x:y:y-z]
with
\zeta^{d}=(-1)^{d-e}
maps
({l}_{1},{l}_{2})
to
({l}_{2},{l}_{1})
and acts trivially on
G^{\circ}
, hence lifts to the required
\alpha
. Therefore, Lemma
2.19
(2.b)
implies that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful family over a point, in particular
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
.

If
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
then a direct computation shows that the derivative of the above
\mathbb{G}_{m}
action on
G^{\circ}
is nonzero, hence by Lemma
2.19
(2.c)
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=0
, as needed. If
v\leqslant 1
we have another
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
which lifts to a transitive action on
G^{\circ}
, namely
[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:y:\lambda z]
,
\lambda\in{k}^{*}
. Its derivative is nonzero if
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid e
. Since
\gcd(d,e)=1
, we conclude as before that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=0
.

Thus we have shown that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=0
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
or
v\leqslant 1
. The same holds if
h=1
: indeed, after removing
H_{2}
the numbers
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
can only decrease, see Lemma
2.14
(b)
for the latter. It remains to show that in case
\operatorname{char}{k}|d
,
v=2
, see Table
2
, we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=1
. By Lemma
2.19
(2.c.iii)
we need to check that every vector field
\xi
on
Y
tangent to
D_{Y}
vanishes along
G^{\circ}
.

As before, let
\tau\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
be the contraction of vertical curves which do not meet the image of
H_{1}
. The image
\overline{\xi}
of
\xi
is a vector field on
\mathbb{F}_{m}
which is tangent to three fibers, hence it is vertical. Choose a local coordinate system
(x,y)
around the center
p
of
\tau
such that
x
is the restriction of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
\mathbb{F}_{m}
. Since
\overline{\xi}
is vertical and vanishes at
p
, we can assume that
\xi=x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
or
-y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
. After a blowup
(x,y)\mapsto(xy,y)
, vector fields
x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
and
ax\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-by\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
,
a,b\in{k}
lift to
x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
(a+b)x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-by\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
. Thus by induction on the number of blowups, we conclude that, in some local coordinates
(x,y)
around
G\cap\sigma_{*}T
where
\sigma_{*}T=\{x=0\}
,
G=\{y=0\}
, we have
\xi=x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
or
dx\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-ey\frac{\partial}{\partial y}=-ey\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
. In any case,
\xi
vanishes along
G
, as needed.

Case

4.6
(3)
.
Assume
h=2
. Then
H_{2}=[2-m]
. Indeed, denoting by
\varphi\colon Z\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
the contraction of all vertical curves which are disjoint from
H_{1}
, we see that
\varphi(H_{1})
is the negative section and
\varphi(H_{2})\cap\varphi(H_{1})=\emptyset
, so
\varphi(H_{2})=[-m]
. Thus
H_{2}=[2-m]
, as needed.

We blow up
m-2\geqslant 0
times at some point of
H_{2}\cap V_{Z}
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
H_{2}
, so that the latter becomes a
0
-curve, and perform elementary transformations on it until the total transform of
D_{Z}
contains a chain
[0,0]
. Via further elementary transformations, we
“move
[0,0]
along
D_{Z}
”
to get an inner birational map
(Z,D_{Z})\dashrightarrow(Z^{\prime},D^{\prime})
such that a
(-1)
-tip of
D^{\prime}
meets a
0
-curve, cf.
[
FKZ07
, Examples 2.11]
. We get
D^{\prime}=L_{1}+L_{2}+R
, where
R=[\uline{0}]+[0,2,T_{1}]*[(2)_{m-1}]*[T_{2},\uline{2},T_{2}^{*},m,T_{1}^{*}]
is circular,
L_{j}=[1]
,
L_{j}\cdot R=1
,
j=1,2
. The underlined numbers refer to the components
G_{1}^{\prime}=[0]
,
G_{2}^{\prime}=[2]
meeting
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, respectively (in the degenerate case when some
V_{j}
is a chain, the corresponding chain
T_{j}
is empty). Let
U\neq G_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq R
be the other
0
-curve, and let
H
be the component of
D^{\prime}-G_{1}^{\prime}
meeting it. Then
|U|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Z^{\prime}
, whose degenerate fibers are supported on
D^{\prime}-(G_{1}^{\prime}+U+H)
and, by Lemma
2.4
, on
L_{1}^{\prime}+W=[1,1]
for a unique
(-1)
-curve
W
such that
W\cdot D^{\prime}=W\cdot H=1
. Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{W}
be the combinatorial types of
(Z^{\prime},D^{\prime}_{Z})
and
(Z^{\prime},D_{Z}^{\prime}+W)
. By Lemma
2.18
we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
. Since on each log surface
(Z^{\prime},D_{Z}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
the curve
W
is unique, we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{W})
. Similarly,
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z^{\prime}}(-\log(D^{\prime}+W)))
by Lemmas
2.11
(c)
and
2.14
(c)
. Contraction of
W
is an inner blowdown onto a log surface as in case
4.6
(2)
above, so by Lemma
2.18
we get
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{W})=1
, and by Lemma
2.11
(c)
we get
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z^{\prime}}(-\log(D^{\prime}+W)))=0
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d\coloneqq d([T_{1}^{*},m,T_{2}^{*}])
and
1
otherwise. This ends the proof in case
h=2
.

Assume
h=1
. From case
h=2
we conclude that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
and, using Lemma
2.14
(b)
,
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=0
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
, and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))\leqslant 1
if
\operatorname{char}{k}|d
. It remains to check that the latter inequality is an equality. Let
(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of a
(-1)
-tip of the first fiber, let
G\subseteq D_{Y}
be the new
(-1)
-curve. By Lemma
2.11
(d)
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))\geqslant h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
, with strict inequality if every vector field on
Y
tangent to
D_{Y}
vanishes along
G
. Thus if
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=1
then the result follows. Assume
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=0
. Case
4.6
(2)
above gives
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d(T_{1})
, so by symmetry
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d(T_{2})
. Let
(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Y}
, and let
(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{m},B)
be the inner morphism contracting both degenerate fibers to
0
-curves. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d(T_{1}),d(T_{2})
, the local computation at the end of case
4.6
(2)
shows that the lift of every vertical vector field on
\mathbb{F}_{m}
either does not vanish on some
(-1)
-curve in
D_{\overline{Y}}
, hence does not lift to
Z
(case
-y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
); or lifts to one vanishing on
G
(case
x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
). Since every vector field on
\mathbb{F}_{m}
tangent to
B
is a linear combination of a vertical one and one tangent to some
H_{2}
, cf.
[
FZ94
, Lemma 2.5(2)]
, case
h=2
above shows that all vector fields on
(Y,D_{Y})
vanish along
G
, as needed.

Case

4.6
(4)
.
We can assume
h=2
,
v=1
. Write
V_{Z}=L+B+T_{0}+T_{1}+T_{2}
, where
L=[1]
,
\beta_{V_{Z}}(B)=3
, and for
j\in\{1,2\}
,
T_{j}
is the maximal twig of
V_{Z}
meeting
H_{j}
. As in case
4.6
(2)
, after a sequence of elementary transformations on
F_{1}
we can assume
H_{1}=[0]
. Like in case
4.6
(3)
, we
“move”
the subchain
F_{1}+H_{1}=[0,0]
to
B+\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T_{1})
, that is, we perform elementary transformations
(Z,D_{Z})\dashrightarrow(Z^{\prime},D^{\prime})
such that the proper transforms
B^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime}
of
B
,
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(T_{1})
are
0
-curves. Now
|T^{\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{hor}}=B^{\prime}+H
consists of two disjoint
1
-sections; and
D^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}}=T^{\prime}+F^{\prime}+R
, where
T^{\prime},F^{\prime}
are fibers, and
R
is the image of
L+T_{0}
. By Lemma
2.4
, the fiber
F_{R}
containing
R
has exactly one component off
D^{\prime}
, say
W
, and
F^{\prime},F_{R}
are the only degenerate fibers. Hence
(F_{R})_{\mathrm{red}}=R+W
and
W\cdot D^{\prime}=W\cdot H=1
. We have
R=[1,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 3
, and
R^{(2)}
meets
B^{\prime}
, so it has multiplicity one in
F_{R}
. Therefore,
F_{R}=[1,(2)_{k-1},1]
since
k\geqslant 3
.

As in case
4.6
(3)
above, it is enough to study the log surface
(Z^{\prime},D^{\prime}+W)
. An inner morphism contracting a subchain
[1,(2)_{k-2}]
of
F_{R}
containing
W
reduces the proof to case
4.6
(2)
(with
T=0
), settled above.

Case

4.6
(5)
.
Let
A_{1},A_{2}
be the
(-1)
-curves in
V_{Z}
, and let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of
A_{2}
. Let
A_{1}^{\prime}=\sigma(A_{1})
, let
G_{2}
be the component of
D_{Y}
containing the point
\sigma(A_{2})
, and let
G_{2}^{\circ}=G_{2}\setminus(D_{Y}-G_{2})
. Case
4.6
(2)
shows that the combinatorial type
\mathcal{Y}
of
(Y,D_{Y})
satisfies
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
equals
0
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
and
1
otherwise. Moreover, in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
all vector fields on
Y
tangent to
D_{Y}
vanish along
G^{\circ}_{2}
: to see this, contract the fiber containing
A_{1}^{\prime}
to a
0
-curve and apply the computation from the end of
4.6
(2)
.

Thus
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by a faithful family over a point, universal if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. Lemma
2.19
(1)
shows that to construct a faithful family over
G_{2}^{\circ}
representing
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
it is enough to show that the group
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts trivially on
G_{2}^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
. Moreover, the resulting family will be universal if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
; and in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
we will get
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=1
. Since
G_{2}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
fixes each of the two points
G_{2}\setminus G_{2}^{\circ}
it is enough to show that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
has a fixed point on
G_{2}^{\circ}
.

Let
\upsilon\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(Z^{\prime},D_{Z}^{\prime})
be the contraction of
\sigma(A_{1})
, let
V_{i}\subseteq D^{\prime}_{Z}
be the vertical
(-1)
-curve and let
V_{i}^{\circ}=V_{i}\setminus(D_{Z}^{\prime}-V_{i})
. Let
\varphi\colon Z^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of the images of
H_{1}
and all vertical curves which are disjoint from it. Let
p,p_{1},p_{2}
be the images of
H_{1},A_{1},A_{2}
, let
{l}_{12}
be the line joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
, and for
i\in\{1,2\}
let
{l}_{i}
be the line joining
p_{i}
with
p
, so
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}
is the image of
V_{Z}
. Let
\{{c}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{P}^{1}}
be the pencil of conics tangent to
{l}_{i}
at
p_{1},p_{2}
, with degenerate members
{c}_{0}\coloneqq{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}
and
{c}_{\infty}=2{l}_{12}
. Write
\{q^{z}_{i}\}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}_{z}\cap V_{i}
, so
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}\ni z\mapsto q^{z}_{i}\in V_{i}^{\circ}
is a parametrization of
V_{i}^{\circ}
. Say that
q_{1}^{1}=\upsilon(\sigma(A_{1}))
. Then
(\varphi\circ\upsilon)^{-1}_{*}{c}_{1}
is fixed by
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
; so its common point with
G_{2}^{\circ}
, namely,
\upsilon^{-1}(q_{1}^{2})
, is fixed by
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
, as needed.

It remains to show that the above faithful family is
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful. The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
interchanges the degenerate fibers, see Figure
17(e)
. We need to endow our family with a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action such that the fibers
(Z_{b},D_{Z,b})
obtained from
(Y,D_{Y})
by blowing up points
b\in G_{2}^{\circ}
which lie in the same orbit are isomorphic.

It is easy to see that
(Z^{\prime},D_{Z}^{\prime})
admits a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action interchanging the degenerate fibers; and a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action which is transitive on
V_{1}^{\circ}
: indeed, otherwise we would have
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})>1
by Lemma
2.19
, which is false. Write the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action as
\lambda\cdot q_{z}^{1}=q_{\lambda z}^{1}
. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(Z^{\prime},D_{Z}^{\prime})
fixes the pencil
\{\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}_{z}\}
, we have
\lambda\cdot q_{z}^{2}=q_{\lambda z}^{2}
, too.

Parametrize
G_{2}^{\circ}\cong V_{2}^{\circ}
in such a way that
(Z_{b},D_{Z,b})
is obtained from
(Z^{\prime},D_{Z}^{\prime})
by blowing up
q_{1}^{1}
and
q_{b}^{2}
. Applying the above
\mathbb{Z}/2
- and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-actions, we see that
(Z_{b},D_{Z,b})
is isomorphic to the log surface obtained by blowing up
q_{b}^{1}
and
q_{1}^{2}
; and to the one obtained by blowing up
q_{1}^{1}
and
q_{1/b}^{2}
. Thus
(Z_{b},D_{Z,b})\cong(Z_{1/b},D_{Z,1/b})
, and the required
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action lifts the automorphism of
Z^{\prime}
mapping
(q_{1}^{1},q^{2}_{b})
to
(q^{2}_{1},q^{1}_{1/b})
. In particular, the symmetry group of our family is
\mathbb{Z}/2
, acting on the base
G_{2}^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
is by
b\mapsto\frac{1}{b}
.

Case

4.6
(6)
.

Let
F_{1,Z},F_{2,Z}
be the fibers in
D_{Z}
, where
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}=\langle 2;[2],[2],[1]\rangle
, see Figure
17(f)
. Let
T_{i,Z}
be the
(-2)
-tip of multiplicity
1
of
F_{i,Z}
which does not meet
H_{1}
, and let
U_{i,Z}
be the
(-1)
-curve in
F_{i,Z}
. Let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of
U_{2,Z}
, let
F_{i}=\sigma_{*}F_{i,Z}
,
T_{i}=\sigma(T_{i,Z})
,
U_{1}=\sigma_{*}(U_{1},Z)
; let
G
be the
(-1)
-curve in
F_{2}
and let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
.

The contraction of
F_{2}-T_{2}
is an inner morphism from
(Y,D_{Y})
onto a log surface
(Y^{\prime},D_{Y}^{\prime})
as in case
4.6
(4)
. We have shown that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y^{\prime}}(-\log D_{Y}^{\prime}))=0
and the combinatorial type
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
of
(Y^{\prime},D_{Y}^{\prime})
has
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})=1
, so
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})
is represented by a universal faithful family over a point. By Lemma
2.18
, the same is true for
(Y,D_{Y})
. Note that the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
is trivial, see Figure
17(f)
. Thus to conclude that
4.7
(b)
holds in this case it is enough, by Lemma
2.19
(1)
, to prove that the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
is trivial.

Let
A
be the restriction of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
to
G^{\circ}
, so
A\leqslant\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The action of
A
on
T_{1}\setminus(D_{Y}-T_{1})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
has a fixed point, say
p_{1}
. Let
\tau\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the contraction of
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}-T_{1}-T_{2}
. Let
H^{\prime}
be the horizontal line through
\tau(p_{1})
. The action of
A
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
fixes
H^{\prime}
, so
A\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y}+H)
, where
H=\tau^{-1}_{*}H^{\prime}=[0]
.

Now, we argue like in case
4.6
(4)
. Let
B
be the branching component of
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}-U_{1}
. Perform two elementary transformations on
H
so that the proper transform of
T_{1}
, say
\widehat{T}_{1}
, becomes a
0
-curve, and denote this map by
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\dashrightarrow(\widehat{Y},\widehat{D})
. Consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration induced by
|\widehat{T}_{1}|
. Then
\widehat{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, namely
\varphi_{*}B
and a
0
-curve, say
E
. The divisor
\widehat{D}_{\mathrm{vert}}
has three connected components, namely: a smooth fiber
\widehat{T}_{1}
, a chain
C^{\prime}=[2,2,2,1,4]
supporting a degenerate fiber, and a chain
C=[1,2,2]\subseteq\varphi_{*}F_{1}
, such that
\varphi^{-1}
is an isomorphism near
C
. Lemma
2.4
implies that the unique degenerate fiber not contained in
\varphi_{*}D_{Y}
is the one containing
C
, which is a chain
C+L=[1,2,2,1]
. We have
L\cdot\widehat{D}=2
, and
L
meets
E
and the
(-2)
-tip of
\widehat{D}
.

Perform elementary transformations on
\widehat{T}_{1}
in such a way that the image of
B
becomes a
(+1)
-curve, and contract all components of the image of
\widehat{D}+L
which are disjoint from it. Denote the resulting map by
\psi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
. Then
{p}
is the sum of four lines: the image of
B
, call it
{l}
, and three others, call them
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{3}
, which meet at one point away from
{l}
: these contain the images of
C
,
C^{\prime}
and
\widehat{T}_{1}
, respectively. The image of
U_{1}
is a point
q\in{l}_{1}\setminus({p}-{l}_{1})
. We have an injective homomorphism
A\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p},q)
whose image fixes each component of
{p}
, so
A=\{\mathrm{id}\}
, as needed.

Case

4.6
(7)
.
We claim that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=2
. Let
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
be the
(-1)
- and
(-2)
-tip of
D_{Z}
, and let
\varphi\colon Z\longrightarrow Z^{\prime}
be the contraction of
D_{Z}-T_{1}-T_{2}-F_{1}=[1,2,2,2,2]
, see Figure
17(g)
. Then
\rho(Z^{\prime})=2
and
\varphi(T_{2})
is a
(+1)
-curve, so
Z^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\varphi(T_{2})
is disjoint from the negative section
\Xi_{1}=[1]
. We have
\varphi(T_{1})=[0]
, so
\varphi(T_{1})
is a fiber, and
\varphi(F_{1})
is a
(+5)
-curve meeting
\varphi(T_{1})
and
\varphi(T_{2})
only at their unique common point, with multiplicities
1
and
3
, respectively; so
\varphi(F_{1})\equiv 2\varphi(T_{1})+\varphi(T_{2})
. Thus
\Xi_{1}\cdot\varphi(F_{1})=2
,
\Xi_{1}\cdot\varphi(T_{1})=1
, and
\Xi_{1}\cdot\varphi(T_{2})=0
. Let
\tau\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be a composition of
\varphi
with the contraction of
\Xi_{1}
. Then
\tau(F_{1})
is a rational cubic,
\tau(T_{2})
is its inflectional tangent line, and
\tau(T_{1})
is a line joining
\tau(F_{1})\cap\tau(T_{2})
with
\operatorname{Sing}\tau(F_{1})
. Up to an isomorphism there are exactly two such pairs
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
, one with
\tau(F_{1})
nodal and the other with
\tau(F_{1})
cuspidal, see
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.5]
. Since each blowup in a decomposition of
\tau
is uniquely determined by
{p}
, by the universal property of blowing up we get exactly two log surfaces
(Z,D_{Z})
, cf. Lemma
2.18
and its proof. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
is trivial, this means that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=2
, as needed.

We claim that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a stratified family. Let
(Z,D_{Z})\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\longrightarrow}(Y_{1},D_{1})\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\longrightarrow}(Y_{2},D_{2})
be the contractions of the
(-1)
-tips of
D_{Z}
and
D_{1}
; let
G_{i}
be the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{i}
, let
G^{\circ}_{i}=G_{i}\setminus(D_{i}-G_{i})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and let
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
be the combinatorial type of
(Y_{i},D_{i})
. Now
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
is as in case
4.6
(2)
above, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})=1
.

Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})<\infty
, Lemma
2.19
(1)
implies that for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{i},D_{i})
on
G_{i}^{\circ}
has an open orbit; so applying Lemma
2.19
(2)
twice we get that
\mathcal{Z}
is represented by a stratified family, as needed.

We remark that, since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=2=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})+1
, Lemma
2.19
(2)
implies that the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{i},D_{i})
on
G_{i}^{\circ}
is transitive for one
i\in\{1,2\}
, and has a fixed point for the other. The proof in case
4.6
(9)
below will show that this action is transitive for
i=2
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, and for
i=1
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.

To conclude that this family is
h^{1}
-stratified, by Lemma
2.19
(2.c.ii)
it is enough to show that for
(Z,D_{Z})
lying over the open stratum, i.e. in case when
\tau(F_{1})
is nodal, we have
h^{1}\coloneqq h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=0
. Let
E=[1]
be the proper transform of the negative section of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
, and let
L\subseteq Z
be the proper transform of a line tangent to
\operatorname{Sing}\tau(F_{1})
. Since
\tau(F_{1})
is nodal, the sum
D_{Z}+E
is snc, so Lemma
2.14
(c)
gives
h^{1}=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log(D_{Z}+E)))
. Let
\upsilon\colon W\longrightarrow Z
be a blowup at
E\cap L
, and let
D_{W}=(\upsilon^{*}D_{Z})_{\mathrm{red}}
,
E_{W}=\upsilon^{-1}_{*}E=[2]
,
L_{W}=\upsilon^{-1}_{*}L=[1]
. By Lemmas
2.11
(c)
and
2.14
(c)
we have
h^{1}=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{W}(-\log(D_{W}+E_{W})))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{W}(-\log(D_{W}+E_{W}+L_{W}))
. Contracting non-branching
(-1)
-curves in
D_{W}+E_{W}+L_{W}
and its images yields an inner morphism
(W,D_{W}+L_{W})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
, where
{p}
is a sum of four general lines, so
h^{1}=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-\log{p}))=0
by Lemma
2.14
(d)(d.i)
, as claimed.

Case

4.6
(8)
.

Let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Z}
, let
G
be the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Y}
, let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and let
\mathcal{Y}
be the combinatorial type of
(Y,D_{Y})
. Case
4.6
(2)
,
v=1
shows that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family over a point. By Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
,
(2.c)
,
it is enough to show that the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
has two orbits, and the derivative of this action is nonzero.

We argue like in
4.6
(3)
above. We assume that
V_{Z}
is a fork, the degenerate case
V_{Z}=[1,2,1]
is analogous.

Say that
H_{1}
meets
T^{*}
. Note that
H_{2}=[1-m]
: indeed, after the contraction of all vertical curves disjoint from
H_{1}
, the image of
H_{2}
is a member of
|\sigma_{m}|
. Replacing
H_{1}
with
H_{2}
if needed, we can assume
m\geqslant 1
.

Let
\varphi_{1}\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y
be the composition of blowups over
\sigma(H_{2})\cap\sigma(F_{1})
such that

(\varphi_{1}^{*}D_{Y})_{\mathrm{red}}=[1,2]+[T,\uline{1},T^{*},\boldsymbol{1},T]*[(2)_{m-1},\uline{1},\uline{m},T^{*}].

Here, the first chain is the proper transform of
G+B
, where
\beta_{D_{Y}}(B)=3
; the second chain meets
(\varphi_{1})^{-1}_{*}B
; and the subsequent underlined components are the proper transforms of
H_{2}
,
F_{1}
and
H_{1}
, see Figure
18
.

.
The map
\varphi\colon Y\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
constructed in case
4.6

.

Let
L_{1}
,

be the components of
T^{*}
and

meeting the exceptional
(-1)
-curve of
\varphi_{1}
, denoted above by
“

. Let
\varphi_{2}\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(\varphi_{1}^{*}D_{Y})_{\mathrm{red}}-(\varphi_{1}^{-1})_{*}B-L_{1}-L_{2}
. Put
\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}^{-1}
. Then
{l}_{j}\coloneqq\varphi_{2}(L_{j})
,

and
