are non-concurrent lines. Write
\{q\}={l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}
,
\{q_{j}\}={l}_{j}\cap{l}
,
\{q_{0}\}=\varphi(G)\subseteq{l}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
, let
{l}_{0}
be the line joining
q_{0}
with
q
; and
{p}={l}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}{l}_{j}
. Fix coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{l}=\{x=0\}
,
{l}_{1}=\{y=0\}
,
{l}_{2}=\{z=0\}
,
{l}_{0}=\{y=z\}
. Now
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\{[x:y:z]\mapsto[\lambda x:y:z]:\lambda\in{k}^{*}\}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
has two orbits on
G^{\circ}
, namely
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{0}\cap G^{\circ}
and the open one, and the vector field
x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
lifts to a vector field on
(Y,D_{Y})
which does not vanish along that open orbit, as needed.

Case

4.6
(9)
.
As in case
4.6
(8)
above, let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
V_{Z}
, let
G\subseteq D_{Y}
be the
(-1)
-tip of
V_{Y}\coloneqq\sigma_{*}V_{Z}
, and let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The combinatorial type
\mathcal{Y}
of
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in case
4.6
(2)
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, and in case
v\leqslant 1
or
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=0
.

Let
T=[d]
be the maximal twig of
V_{Y}
which does not meet
H_{Y}\coloneqq\sigma_{*}H_{1}
. Put
F=\sigma_{*}F_{1}
if
v\geqslant 1
, otherwise let
F\subseteq Y
be some non-degenerate fiber. Fix a section
H\subseteq Y
whose image under the contraction
Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m-1}
of
V_{Y}-T
is disjoint from the negative section. Then
H\cdot(V+H_{Y})=H\cdot T=1
and
H=[1-m]
.

Subcase

\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
.
By Lemma
2.19
(2.b)
,
(2.c.i)
it is enough to check that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
, and the derivative of this action is nonzero.

For this we can assume
v=2
. If
m=1
blow up at
F_{1}\cap H_{Y}
, otherwise blow up
m-2
times at
H\cap F_{1}
and its infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
H
. Denote this morphism by
\tau_{1}\colon Y^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow Y
. Let
U
be the exceptional
(-1)
-curve of
\tau_{1}
; and let
\tau_{2}\colon Y^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
(\tau_{1}^{*}D_{Y})_{\mathrm{red}}-(\tau_{1})^{-1}_{*}(T+F_{2})-U
: if
m=1
, this divisor is a sum of disjoint chains
[1]+[(2)_{d+1},1]
; otherwise it is a chain
[(2)_{m-3},1,m,(2)_{d},1]
. Put
\tau=\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}^{-1}
,
{l}_{j}=\tau_{*}F_{j}
,
{l}_{T}=\tau_{*}T
,
{p}=\tau_{*}D_{Y}={l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{l}_{T}
. Then
{l}_{1},{l}_{2},{l}_{T}
are lines meeting at a point
q
. Choose coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{l}_{1}=\{x=0\}
,
{l}_{T}=\{y=0\}
and put
{c}=\{x^{d+1}=yz^{d}\}
. Then
q=[0:0:1]
is smooth point of
{c}
, and we have
({c}\cdot{l}_{T})_{q}=d+1
. Let
B\subseteq D_{Y}
be the
(-2)
-curve meeting
G
. Write
\tau=\upsilon\circ\upsilon^{\prime}
, where
\upsilon^{\prime}
is the contraction of
G
. Then
\upsilon^{-1}_{*}{c}
meets
(\upsilon^{*}{l}_{T})_{\mathrm{red}}
only in
\upsilon^{\prime}(B)
, once. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, we can assume that the point
\upsilon^{\prime}(G)
lies on
\upsilon^{-1}_{*}{c}
, so
\tau^{-1}_{*}{c}
meets
G^{\circ}
.

For
\lambda\in{k}
define
\alpha_{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
by
\alpha_{\lambda}[x:y:z]=[x:y:z+\lambda x]
. For
\lambda\neq 0
we have
(\alpha_{\lambda}({c})\cdot{c})_{q}=d+2
. To see this, parametrize
\alpha_{\lambda}({c})
near
q
by
t\mapsto[t:t^{d+1}:1+\lambda t]
. Substituting this parametrization to the equation
x^{d+1}-yz^{d}
of
{c}
we get

t^{d+1}-t^{d+1}(1+\lambda t)^{d}=d\lambda t^{d+2}+\textnormal{h.o.t.},

where
“
h.o.t.
”
stands for
“higher order terms”
. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
, the lowest exponent is
d+2
, as claimed.

In particular,
(\alpha_{\lambda}({c})\cdot{c})_{q}>d+1=({l}_{T}\cdot{c})_{q}
. Hence by our construction
\alpha_{\lambda}
lifts to an element of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
. In turn, since
(\alpha_{\lambda}({c})\cdot{c})_{q}=d+2
, this lift does not fix
\tau^{-1}_{*}{c}\cap G^{\circ}
. We conclude that
\{\alpha_{\lambda}:\lambda\in{k}\}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
lifts to a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acting transitively on
G^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, as needed.

A direct computation shows that the derivative of this action is nonzero. Indeed, in local coordinates
(\widecheck{x},\widecheck{y})=(\frac{x}{z},\frac{y}{z})
, the
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action is given by
\lambda\cdot(\frac{\widecheck{x}}{1+\lambda\widecheck{x}},\frac{\widecheck{y}}{1+\lambda\widecheck{x}})
, so its derivative at
\lambda=0
maps the unit vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}
to
\xi\coloneqq-\widecheck{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}-\widecheck{x}\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
. At a point of
G^{\circ}
choose local coordinates
(x,y)
on
Y
, with
G^{\circ}=\{x=0\}
, and write
\tau\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
as a composition of an outer blowup
(x,y)\mapsto(xy+1)
and
d+1
blowups
(x,y)\mapsto(x,xy)
, so
\tau(x,y)=(x,x^{d+1}(xy+1))
. Multiplying
\xi
by the inverse of its jacobian matrix, we conclude that
\xi
lifts to
-x^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(d+1)xy\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+d\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, which does not vanish along
G^{\circ}=\{x=0\}
because
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
.

Subcase

\operatorname{char}{k}|d
.
We claim that the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}
has a fixed point.

Put
\widetilde{D}_{Y}=V_{Y}+H_{Y}+H+F
. Then
(Y,\widetilde{D}_{Y})
is the same as the log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
considered in case
4.6
(8)
above. Let
\varphi\colon(Y,\widetilde{D}_{Y})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be the map constructed there, see Figure
18
. The curve
L_{0}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{0}
has a unique cusp at
H\cap F
. Its minimal log resolution is
\varphi_{1}\colon(Y^{\prime},L_{0}^{\prime}+R)\longrightarrow(Y,L_{0})
, where
R=[(2)_{d-1},1,d+1,(2)_{m-2}]\subseteq D^{\prime}
. Fix
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
and put
\overline{L}_{0}=\alpha(L_{0})
. Then
\overline{L}_{0}
has a cusp of the same type. The curve
\overline{L}_{0}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\varphi_{1}^{-1})_{*}\overline{L}_{0}
meets
R
in at most one point, and if it does then either
\overline{L}_{0}^{\prime}\cdot R=\overline{L}_{0}^{\prime}\cdot R^{(d)}=1
, or
\overline{L}_{0}^{\prime}\cdot R=\overline{L}_{0}^{\prime}\cdot R^{(i)}=d
for some
i>d
; where
R^{(j)}
is the
j
-th component of the chain
R
. In any case, it follows that at the point
{l}_{1}\cap{l}_{2}
, the lines
{l}_{1}
and
{l}_{2}
meet every branch of
\overline{{l}}_{0}\coloneqq\varphi_{*}\overline{L}_{0}
with multiplicity divisible by
d
. As in case
4.6
(8)
above, we choose coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{l}_{1}=\{y=0\}
,
{l}_{2}=\{z=0\}
,
{l}_{0}=\{y=z\}
. Then the curve
\overline{{l}}_{0}
can be parametrized by
[s:t]\mapsto[f:g_{1}^{d}:g_{2}^{d}]
for some
f,g_{1},g_{2}\in{k}[s,t]
. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}|d
, we get
\operatorname{char}{k}|({l}_{0}\cdot\overline{{l}}_{0})_{r}
for every
r\in{l}_{0}\cap\overline{{l}}_{0}
. In particular,
\operatorname{char}{k}
divides the intersection multiplicity of
{l}_{0}
and
\overline{{l}}_{0}
at the point
q_{0}=\varphi(G)
, so
(\overline{{l}}_{0}\cdot{l}_{0})_{q_{0}}\geqslant 2
. Hence
\overline{L}_{0}
meets
L_{0}
at
\{p\}\coloneqq L_{0}\cap G
, which is the required fixed point.

Consider the case
v\leqslant 1
. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=0
, Lemma
2.19
and the above claim imply that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified or universal family over
G^{\circ}
. On the other hand, for some section
H^{\prime}\subseteq Z
the combinatorial type
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
of
(Z,D_{Z}+H^{\prime})
is as in case
4.6
(8)
above, so we have a surjective restriction map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
. Thus
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})\leqslant\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})=2
, so our family if
h^{1}
-stratified, as claimed.

Consider the case
v=2
. We claim that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful family over
G^{\circ}
. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
and
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Y})
, by Lemma
2.19
(1)
it is enough to prove that the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
has finite orbits. Suppose the contrary. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
has a fixed point on
G^{\circ}
, it has a subgroup
A\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
with an open orbit on
G^{\circ}
. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
fixes three fibers in
D_{Y}
, it acts trivially on the base, i.e. sends every fiber to itself. Thus
A\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
has a fixed point on
T\setminus D_{Y}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and on
F^{\prime}\setminus H_{1}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
for any nondegenerate fiber
F^{\prime}
. It follows that
A
fixes some section
H
as above, so the map
\alpha\mapsto\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\varphi^{-1}
defines an embedding
\iota\colon A\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
. In the above coordinates we have
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})=\{[x:y:z]=[x:y:\lambda z]:\lambda\in{k}^{*}\}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
. On the other hand,
\iota(A)
fixes
\varphi(F_{2})
, which is a curve of degree
d
, satisfying
\varphi(F_{2})\cap{l}_{j}={l}\cap{l}_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Thus
\varphi(F_{2})=\{zy^{d-1}=cx^{d}\}
for some
c\in{k}^{*}
. The only element of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
fixing this curve is the identity, so
A=\{\mathrm{id}\}
, a contradiction. Thus
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful family over
G^{\circ}
.

The symmetry group of the above family is the image of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
in
\operatorname{Aut}(G^{\circ})
. A direct computation shows that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
is generated by the automorphisms interchanging the non-degenerate fibers in
D_{Y}
constructed in case
4.6
(2)
, which act on
G^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
as multiplication by
\zeta
, where
\zeta^{d}=-1
; as needed.

To conclude, we need to show that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
equals
2
for the central fiber of this family, and
1
for the remaining ones. By Lemma
2.11
(d)
, it is enough to prove that there is a unique point
p\in G^{\circ}
such that every vector field
\xi\in H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
vanishes at
p
. Let
\tau\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m-1}
be the contraction of
V_{Y}-T
. It can be written as
\tau(x,y)=((xy+1)y^{d},y)
, where
(x,y)
are local coordinates at some
q\in G^{\circ}
, with
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
, and at the target we choose local coordinates
(\widecheck{x},\widecheck{y})
such that
\widecheck{x}
is the restriction of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
\mathbb{F}_{m-1}
, and
\{\widecheck{y}=0\}
is the equation of the negative section. The vector field
\tau_{*}\xi
is tangent to three fibers of
\mathbb{F}_{m-1}
, so it is vertical, and therefore a multiple of
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
or
\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
. Multiplying these vector fields by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\tau
we see that in the first case
\xi
vanishes along
G^{\circ}
, and in the second case it vanishes at
q
. Furthermore, if
\tau_{*}\xi=\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
then
\xi=-x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
does not vanish along
G^{\circ}\setminus\{q\}
, as needed.

Case

4.6
(10)
.
Let
V_{Z,1}=\langle 2;[(2)_{d-1}],[d],[1,2]\rangle
,
V_{Z,2}=\langle 2;T^{*},T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[1]\rangle
be the connected components of
V_{Z}
, they both meet
H_{1}=[m]
in the first tip of the first twig. Let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
V_{Z,1}
, let
H=\sigma_{*}H_{1}
,
V_{i}=\sigma_{*}V_{Z,i}
, let
B_{i}
be the branching component of
V_{i}
, let
G
be the
(-1)
-tip of
V_{1}
and let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, see Figure
17(j)
. The log surface
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in case
4.6
(3)
.

Let
\upsilon\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(Y_{1},D_{1})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
V_{2}
, and let
\tau\colon(Y_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(Y_{2},D_{2})
be the contraction of all components of
\upsilon_{*}V_{2}
except for the tip of
D_{1}
. The log surface
(Y_{2},D_{2})
is as in case
4.6
(2)
,
v=1
, see Figure
17(b)
. In case
4.6
(9)
above we have shown that the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{2},D_{2})
on the image of
G^{\circ}
has a fixed point if
\operatorname{char}{k}|d
and is transitive with nonvanishing derivative if
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
. Since the morphism
\tau
is inner and
\operatorname{Bs}\tau^{-1}
is fixed by
\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{2},D_{2})
, every automorphism of
(Y_{2},D_{2})
lifts to an automorphism of
(Y_{1},D_{1})
.

Consider the case
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid d
. Since
\upsilon(G^{\circ})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, there is a subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{a}\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{1},D_{1})
acting transitively with nonvanishing derivative on
\upsilon(G^{\circ})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
. This subgroup fixes the two points
\upsilon(B_{2})\cap(D_{1}-\upsilon(B_{2}))
, so it fixes
\upsilon(B_{2})
pointwise, so its action lifts to a transitive action on
G^{\circ}
, with nonvanishing derivative. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, by Lemma
2.19
(2.b)
we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=1
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
, as needed.

Consider the case
\operatorname{char}{k}|d
. Put
c=d([(2)_{d-1},m,T^{*}])
. To prove that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a faithful family over
G^{\circ}
, by Lemma
2.19
(1)
it is enough to prove that the restriction
A
of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
to
G^{\circ}
is finite (the group
A
will then be the symmetry group of the resulting family). Since
A
has a fixed point on
G^{\circ}
, we have
A\leqslant\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Any subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acting on
(Y,D_{Y})
fixes the proper transform of some section
H_{2}\subseteq Y
as in
4.6
(3)
. Let
(Y,D_{Y}+H_{2})\dashrightarrow(Z^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the birational map constructed in
4.6
(3)
, and let
(Z^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
be the contraction of all vertical curves not meeting the section
G_{1}^{\prime}=[0]
of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration constructed there. Now
B
consists of two horizontal and three vertical lines, say
\overline{H}_{0},\overline{H}_{\infty}
and
\overline{V}_{0},\overline{V}_{1},\overline{V}_{\infty}
, where
\overline{V}_{1}
is the proper transform of
G^{\circ}
, and
\{p\}\coloneqq\overline{H}_{0}\cap\overline{V}_{0}
is the image of
L_{2}
. Fix coordinates
(x,y)
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that
\overline{H}_{z}=\{x=z\}
,
\overline{V}_{z}=\{y=z\}
. Every automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
preserving
B
componentwise is of the form
\alpha_{\lambda}\colon(x,y)\mapsto(\lambda x,y)
, for
\lambda\in{k}^{*}
. Such
\alpha_{\lambda}
lifts to an element of
\in\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{1},D_{1})
, and a direct computation shows that the latter acts on the
(-1)
-curve
L
in the image of
V_{2}
as a multiplication by
\lambda^{c}
. Thus
\alpha_{\lambda}
lifts to an element of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if
\lambda^{c}=1
, and the latter acts on
G^{\circ}
as multiplication by
\lambda
. Therefore,
A
is the finite group of
c
-th roots of unity, as needed.

Case
4.6
(3)
gives
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, so the above claim and Lemma
2.19
(1)
imply that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a faithful family over
G^{\circ}
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid c
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=0
, so this family is universal.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}|c
, so
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=1
. We have
c=d\cdot d([m,T^{*}])-(d-1)\cdot d(T)
, so
\operatorname{char}{k}|d(T)
. Let
G_{2}^{\circ}
be the image of
G^{\circ}
on
Y_{2}
.
At the end of case
4.6
(9)
we have shown that all vertical vector fields on
(Y_{2},D_{2})
vanish at some
q\in G^{\circ}
, and there is one nonvanishing along
G_{2}^{\circ}\setminus\{q\}
. Moreover, since
\operatorname{char}{k}|d(T)
, the computation in case
4.6
(2)
shows that these vector fields lift to
(Y,D_{Y})
. We claim that every
\xi\in H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
is vertical, so vanishes at
q
. Indeed, the image of
\xi
by the contraction
Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m-2}
is a linear combination of a vertical vector field and
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}
, where as in case
4.6
(9)

\widecheck{x}
denotes a parameter on the base of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\mathbb{F}_{m-2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, such that the images
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
lie over
\{\widecheck{x}=0\}
and
\{\widecheck{x}=\infty\}
, see
[
FZ94
, Lemma 2.5]
. A direct computation as in
4.6
(9)
shows that
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}
does not lift to
Y
, which proves the claim.

Now Lemma
2.11
(d)
shows that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))+\varepsilon=1+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon=1
if the blowup
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
is centered at
q
and
0
otherwise; as needed.

Case

4.6
(11)
.
As usual, let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Z}
, let
G
be the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Y}
, and let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The combinatorial type
\mathcal{Y}
of
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in case
4.6
(2)
,
v=0
, see Figure
17(b)
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
and
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family (over a point). In case
4.6
(8)
, we have seen that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts on
G^{\circ}
with an open orbit, and the derivative of this action along the open orbit does not vanish. By Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
,
(2.c.i)
it remains to prove that it has a fixed point.

Let
T
be the
(-2)
-tip of
\sigma(V_{Z})
, and let
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l})
be the contraction of
D_{Y}-T=\langle 2;[3,2],[2],[1]\rangle
, and let
\{q\}=\operatorname{Bs}\varphi^{-1}
. Choose coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{l}=\{x=0\}
and
q=[0:0:1]
. Put
{c}=\{y^{5}=x^{2}z^{3}\}
. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, we can assume that
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}
meets
G^{\circ}
. We have an injective group homomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})\ni\alpha\mapsto\varphi\circ\alpha\circ\varphi^{-1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
, whose image consists of those automorphisms
\upsilon\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
such that
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\upsilon({c})
meets
G^{\circ}
, too, i.e.
(\upsilon({c})\cdot{c})_{q}\geqslant 11
. Take any
\upsilon\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
, so
\upsilon[x:y:z]=[x:ax+by:cx+dy+ez]
for some
b,e\in{k}^{*}
,
a,c,d\in{k}
. We can parametrize
\upsilon({c})
near
q
by
t\mapsto[t^{5}:at^{5}+bt^{2}:ct^{5}+dt^{2}+e]
. Substituting this to the equation of
{c}
gives
(at^{5}+bt^{2})^{5}-t^{10}(ct^{5}+dt^{2}+e)^{3}
, which has coefficient zero near
t^{11}
. Therefore, whenever
(\upsilon({c})\cdot{c})_{q}\geqslant 11
, we have in fact
(\upsilon({c})\cdot{c})_{q}\geqslant 12
, that is,
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}\cap G^{\circ}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\upsilon({c})\cap G^{\circ}
. Hence
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{c}\cap G^{\circ}
is the required fixed point of the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}
.

Case

4.6
(12)
.
Let
(Z,D_{Z})\overset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})
be the contractions of the
(-1)
-tips of
D_{Z}
and
D_{Y}
. Let
G,\overline{G}
be the
(-1)
-tips of
D_{Y}
,
\overline{D}_{Y}
and let
G^{\circ}=D_{Y}\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\overline{G}^{\circ}=\overline{G}\setminus(D_{\overline{Y}}-\overline{G})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
be the combinatorial types of
(Y,D_{Y})
and
(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})
, respectively: they are as in cases
4.6
(9)
and
4.6
(2)
,
v=0
. Thus
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})=1
, and
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family, say
f
, over a point if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, and over
\overline{G}^{\circ}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. By Lemma
2.19
(2)
it remains to show that
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
acts on
G^{\circ}
transitively if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 5
, with exactly two orbits if
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
, and the derivative of this action along the open orbit does not vanish.

Let
T
be the
(-2)
-tip of
D_{Y}
, and let
\varphi\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{2},{p})
be the contraction of
\sigma(V_{Z})-T
. Then
{p}
is the sum of the negative section
\Xi_{2}
and a fiber
F_{T}\coloneqq\varphi(T)
. Let
\chi
be a smooth germ at
\{q\}\coloneqq\Xi_{2}\cap F_{T}
such that
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\chi
meets
G^{\circ}
. Fix coordinates on
\mathbb{F}_{2}=\{([x:y:z];[u:v])\in\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}:yv^{2}=zu^{2}\}
so that
\Xi_{2}=\{y=z=0\}
;
F_{T}=\{u=0\}
. At
q
, we have local coordinates
(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})\coloneqq(\frac{z}{x},\frac{u}{v})
. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})=1
, we can assume that
\chi
is parametrized in those coordinates by
t\mapsto(t,t^{2}+\lambda t^{3})
. The number
\lambda\in{k}
specifies the point where the proper transform of
\chi
meets
\overline{G}^{\circ}
. Moreover, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, too, so we can assume
\lambda=0
.

We have an injective group homomorphism
\iota\colon\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})\ni\beta\mapsto\varphi\circ\beta\circ\varphi^{-1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},{p})
, and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},{p})
lies in its image if and only if
(\alpha(\chi)\cdot\chi)_{q}\geqslant 4
. In this case,
\iota^{-1}(\alpha)
fixes the point
\{p\}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}_{*}\chi\cap G^{\circ}
if and only if
(\alpha(\chi)\cdot\chi)_{q}\geqslant 5
. Near
F_{T}
we can express any
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},{p})
as

\alpha([x:y:z],[u:1])=([x+z(a_{0}+a_{1}u+a_{2}u^{2}):z(au)^{2}:z(cu+d)^{2}],[au:cu+d]),

for some
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},c\in{k}
,
a,d\in{k}^{*}
, see
[
Bla12
, §6.1]
. In local coordinates
(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})
at
q
, the germ
\alpha(\chi)
is given by

t\mapsto\left(\frac{t(d+c(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3}))^{2}}{1+t(a_{0}+a_{1}(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3})+a_{2}(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3})^{2})},\ \frac{a(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3})}{d+c(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3})}\right).

Substituting this to the equation
\widecheck{u}-\widecheck{z}^{2}-\lambda\widecheck{z}^{3}
of
\chi
and clearing denominators, we get, modulo
t^{5}
:

\begin{split}&a(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3})(1+a_{0}t)^{3}-t^{2}(d+c(t^{2}+\lambda t^{3}))^{5}\cdot(1+a_{0}t+\lambda d^{2}t)=\\
=&(a-d^{5})t^{2}+(3aa_{0}+a\lambda-a_{0}d^{5}-\lambda d^{7})t^{3}+(3aa_{0}^{2}+3a\lambda a_{0}-5cd^{4})t^{4}\end{split}

hence
\alpha=\iota(\widetilde{\alpha})
for some
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if
a=d^{5}
and
a_{0}(3a-d^{5})=\lambda(d^{7}-a)
, and under these conditions,
\widetilde{\alpha}(p)=p
if and only if
3aa_{0}(a_{0}+\lambda)=5cd^{4}
.

Consider the case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 5
. Taking
a,d=1
,
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}=0
we get a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},{p})
consisting of

([x:y:z],[u:v])\mapsto([xv^{2}:zu^{2}:z(cu+v)^{2}],[u:cu+v]),\quad c\in{k},

which lifts to
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
, does not fix
p
, and
acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
.
Moreover, it does not depend on
\lambda
, so it comes from the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
, as needed.

To compute the derivative, we first write this action in local coordinates
(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})
as
c\cdot(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})=(\widecheck{z}(c\widecheck{u}+1)^{2},\frac{\widecheck{u}}{c\widecheck{u}+1})
. Its derivative at
c=0
maps
\frac{\partial}{\partial c}
to
\xi\coloneqq 2\widecheck{z}\widecheck{u}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{z}}-\widecheck{u}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{u}}
. We need to compute the lift of
\xi
to
Y
. To this end, we first perform two blowups at
(0,0)
, and write each one as
(z,u)\mapsto(z,zu)
. Next, we perform an outer blowup
(z,zu+1)
which yields
\overline{Y}
, and to get
Y
we write
(z,u)\mapsto(z,zu)
once more. We conclude that in local coordinates
(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})
on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
and some
(z,u)
on
Y
, with
G^{\circ}=\{z=0\}
we have
\varphi(z,u)=(z,z^{2}(uz^{2}+1))
. Multiplying
\xi=2z^{3}(uz^{2}+1)\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{z}}-z^{2}(uz^{2}+1)\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{u}}
by the inverse of its jacobian matrix, we conclude that
\xi
lifts to
(uz^{2}+1)\cdot(2z^{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}-(9uz^{2}+5)\frac{\partial}{\partial u})
, which does not vanish along
G^{\circ}=\{z=0\}
because
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 5
.

Consider the case
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
. Then
\lambda=0
by assumption. Now
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{2},{p})
lifts to
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if
a=d^{5}
and
a_{0}=0
, so the condition
3aa_{0}(a_{0}+\lambda)=5cd^{4}
holds automatically, i.e.
p
is a fixed point of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
. On the other hand, taking
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},c=0
and
a=d^{5}
we get a subgroup consisting of

\alpha_{d}\colon([x:y:z],[u:v])\mapsto([xv^{2}:z(d^{5}u)^{2}:z(dv)^{2}],[d^{4}u:v]),\quad d\in{k}^{*},

which lifts to
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
and acts transitively on
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
. To see the last assertion, take a germ
\eta
at
q
such that
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\eta
meets
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
, say
t\mapsto(t,t^{2}+t^{4})
in coordinates
(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})
. In these coordinates,
\alpha_{d}(\eta)
is parametrized by
t\mapsto(d^{2}t,d^{4}(t^{2}+t^{4}))
, which substituted to the equation of
\eta
gives
d^{4}(t^{2}+t^{4})-d^{4}t^{2}-d^{8}t^{4}=d^{4}(1-d^{4})t^{4}
, so
(\alpha_{b}(\eta)\cdot\eta)_{q}=4
unless
d^{4}=1
. It follows that the above subgroup acts transitively on
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
, as needed.

A direct computation as before shows that the derivative of this action does not vanish along
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
. Indeed, in local coordinates on
\mathbb{F}_{2}
this action is given by
d\cdot(\widecheck{z},\widecheck{u})=(d^{2}\widecheck{z},d^{4}\widecheck{u})
, so its derivative at
d=1
is
\xi\coloneqq 2\widecheck{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{z}}+4\widecheck{u}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{u}}
. As before, writing
\varphi(z,u)=(z,z^{2}(uz^{2}+1))
in some local coordinates
(z,u)
with
G^{\circ}=\{z=0\}
, we conclude that
\xi
lifts to
2z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}-4u\frac{\partial}{\partial u}
, so does not vanish along
G^{\circ}
, as claimed.

Case

4.6
(13)
.
As before, let
(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})
be the contraction of the
(-1)
-tips of
D_{Z}
and
D_{Y}
, let
G,\overline{G}
be the
(-1)
-tips of
D_{Y}
and
D_{\overline{Y}}
, let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)
,
\overline{G}^{\circ}=D_{\overline{Y}}\setminus(\overline{G}-D_{\overline{Y}})
, and let
\mathcal{Y}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
be the combinatorial types of
(Y,D_{Y})
and
(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})
.

Let
T
be the
(-2)
-tip of
D_{Z}
contained in
V_{Z}
, and let
\varphi\colon Z\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
D_{Z}-T
. Then
{l}\coloneqq\varphi(T)
is a line, and
\{q\}\coloneqq(\operatorname{Bs}\varphi^{-1})_{\mathrm{red}}\subseteq{l}
. Fix coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
{l}=\{x=0\}
,
q=[0:0:1]
. Let
\chi_{p}
be a germ at
p\in G^{\circ}
meeting
G^{\circ}
normally. In local coordinates
(\widecheck{x},\widecheck{y})\coloneqq(\frac{x}{z},\frac{y}{z})
at
q
, we write
\chi\coloneqq\varphi_{*}\chi_{p}
as
(t^{3}+\textnormal{h.o.t.},t)
, with higher order terms chosen for each particular case. Any
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
can be written as

(8)

\alpha\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:ax+by:cx+dy+ez],\quad a,c,d\in{k},\ b,e\in{k}^{*}.

As in the previous cases, we say that
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)

lifts to
(Y,D_{Y})
if it is in the image of the injective homomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})\ni\upsilon\mapsto\varphi\circ\upsilon\circ\varphi^{-1}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
. This happens if and only if
(\alpha(\chi)\cdot\chi)_{q}\geqslant k+3
. The induced automorphism of
(Y,D_{Y})
, denoted by the same letter, fixes
p
if and only if
(\alpha(\chi)\cdot\chi)_{q}\geqslant k+4
.

The proof in subsequent cases
k=2,3,4
boils down to a computation analogous to the one in case
4.6
(12)
.

Subcase

k=2
.

In this case, type
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
is as in case
4.6
(2)
,
v=0
, see Figure
17(b)
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})=1
. Therefore, we can take
\chi=(t^{3}+\lambda t^{4},t)
, where
\lambda\in{k}
parametrizes
\overline{G}^{\circ}
. Since
\mathcal{Y}
is as in case
4.6
(9)
with
v=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family
f
. Moreover, if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})=1
, we can take
\lambda=0
.

By Lemma
2.19
(2)
it remains to prove that
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
, with exactly two orbits if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, and in each case the derivative of this action does not vanish along the open orbit.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
. Then the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
consisting of

(9)

\alpha_{a}\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:y+ax:z],\quad a\in{k}

lifts to a subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{a}\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acting transitively on
G^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
, with nonvanishing derivative. Indeed, substituting
\alpha_{a}(\chi)=(t^{3}+\lambda t^{4},t+at^{3}+a\lambda t^{4})
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}(1+\lambda\widecheck{y})-\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
, we get:

t^{3}(1+at^{2}+a\lambda t^{3})^{3}(1+\lambda t+a\lambda t^{3}+a\lambda^{2}t^{4})-(t^{3}+\lambda t^{4})=3at^{5}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

so
(\alpha_{a}(\chi)\cdot\chi)_{q}=5
, and
a\mapsto\alpha_{a}(p)
parametrizes
G^{\circ}
, so the subgroup (
9
) acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
. In fact, since (
9
) does not depend on
\lambda
, it lifts to a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
, as needed.

Nonvanishing of the derivative follows similarly. The derivative of (
9
) at
a=0
maps
\frac{\partial}{\partial a}
to
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
. Decomposing
\varphi
as two outer blowups
(x,y)\mapsto(xy+\lambda,y)
,
(x,y)\mapsto(xy+1,y)
and three inner ones
(x,y)\mapsto(xy,y)
, we get
(\widecheck{x},\widecheck{y})=\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{5}+\lambda y^{4}+y^{3},y)
, where
(x,y)
are some local coordinates on
Y
with
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
. To compute the required derivative, multiply
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi
. The resulting vector field
-(xy^{2}+\lambda y+1)(5xy^{2}+4\lambda y+3)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(xy^{5}+\lambda y^{4}+y^{3})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
does not vanish on
G^{\circ}
since
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Then
\lambda=0
. Fix
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
; so
\alpha
is given by formula (
8
). We now substitute
\alpha(\chi)=(t^{3}(ct^{3}+dt+e)^{-1},(at^{3}+bt)(ct^{3}+dt+e)^{-1})
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}-\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
. Clearing denominators, we get

t^{3}((at^{2}+b)^{3}-(ct^{3}+dt+e)^{2})=(b^{3}-e^{2})t^{3}-2det^{4}-d^{2}t^{5}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

Recall that
\alpha
lifts to
(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if the coefficients near
t^{0},\dots,t^{4}
above vanish, i.e. if
b^{3}=e^{2}
and
d=0
. But under these conditions, the coefficient near
t^{5}
vanishes, too, so
\alpha
fixes
p
. We conclude that
p
is a fixed point of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
. On the other hand, the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
consisting of

(10)

\varepsilon_{a}\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[a^{3}x:ay:z],\quad a\in{k}^{*}.

lifts to a subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{m}\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acting transitively on
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{*}
. To see this, take a germ
\eta^{\prime}
meeting
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
normally, say
\eta^{\prime}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}_{*}\eta
for
\eta=(t^{3}+t^{5},t)
. Substituting
\varepsilon_{a}(\eta)=(a^{3}t^{3}+a^{3}t^{5},at)
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}+\widecheck{y}^{5}-\widecheck{x}
of
\eta
, we get

(a^{5}-a^{3})t^{5}
,
so
{k}^{*}\ni a\mapsto\varepsilon_{a}(\eta^{\prime})\cap G^{\circ}\in G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
is surjective, as claimed.

Again, a direct computation shows that the derivative of this action does not vanish. Indeed, the derivative of (
10
) at
a=1
is
\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
, and since
\lambda=0
, the local expression of
\varphi
chosen above is
\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{5}+y^{3})
, where
(x,y)
are some local coordinates at
p
, with
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
. Multiplying
\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi
we conclude that our derivative is
(xy^{2}+1)x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, which does not vanish on
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
.

Subcase

k=3
.
Now
\mathcal{Y}
is as in Subcase
k=2
above; and
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
is as in case
4.6
(9)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})=1
,
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by a family
f
over
\overline{G}^{\circ}
, so we can take
\chi=(t^{3}+\lambda t^{5},t)
for some
\lambda\in{k}
. By the previous Subcase and Lemma
2.19
(2)
, it remains to prove that
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
acts transitively on each
G^{\circ}
, with nonzero derivative. The subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
consisting of

(11)

\beta_{a}\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:y:z+ax],\quad a\in{k}

lifts to a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(f)
acting transitively on
G^{\circ}
. Indeed, substituting
\beta_{a}(\chi)=((t^{3}+\lambda t^{5})(1+at^{3}+a\lambda t^{5})^{-1},t(1+at^{3}+a\lambda t^{5})^{-1})
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}(1+\lambda\widecheck{y}^{2})-\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
we get

t^{3}((1+at^{3}+a\lambda t^{5})^{2}+\lambda t^{2})-t^{3}(1+\lambda t^{2})(1+at^{3}+a\lambda t^{5})^{4}=-2at^{6}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

so
a\mapsto\beta_{a}(p)
parametrizes
G^{\circ}
. As before, we check directly that the derivative of this action does not vanish. To compute it, we multiply the derivative of (
11
), namely
-\widecheck{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}-\widecheck{x}\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
, by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{6}+\lambda y^{5}+y^{3},y)
, where
(x,y)
are some local coordinates on
Y
with
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
. Substituting
y=0
to the resulting vector field gives
2\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
, which does not vanish along
G^{\circ}
, as needed.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. In the previous Subcase, we have inferred from case
4.6
(9)
that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})
is parametrized by germs
\chi=(t^{3}+\lambda t^{4},t)
and we have shown that the same is true for
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
. Thus we can take
\chi=(t^{3}+\lambda t^{4},t)
.

We now study two particular values:
\lambda=1
and
\lambda=0
. We claim that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
in the first case, and has one fixed point in the second case; moreover, the derivative of either action does not vanish along the open orbit. Once this is shown, we will see that
\lambda=1,0
correspond to the general and special fiber of the
h^{1}
-stratified family representing
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
, and our result will follow from Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
.

Consider the case
\lambda=1
. Then a transitive
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on
G^{\circ}
is given by

(12)

\gamma_{a}\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:y+a(1+a)x:z+ay],\quad a\in{k}.

Indeed, substituting
\gamma_{a}(\chi)=(\frac{t^{3}(1+t)}{1+at},\frac{t+a(1+a)t^{3}(1+t)}{1+at})
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}+\widecheck{y}^{4}+\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
, we get

t^{3}(1+a(1+a)t^{2}(1+t))^{3}(1+at+t+a(1+a)t^{3}(1+t))+t^{3}(1+t)(1+at)^{3}=(a+a^{2})t^{6}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

so
a\mapsto\gamma_{a}(p)
is surjective, as needed. To compute the derivative, we multiply the derivative of (
12
), namely
\widecheck{x}\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}+(\widecheck{x}+\widecheck{y}^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{6}+y^{4}+y^{3},y)
, and restrict it to
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
. The resulting vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
does not vanish, as needed.

Consider the case
\lambda=0
. Then for any
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
as in (
8
), substituting
\alpha(\chi)=(\frac{t^{3}}{ct^{3}+dt+e},\frac{at^{3}+bt}{ct^{3}+dt+e})
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}+\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
gives

(at^{3}+bt)^{3}+t^{3}(ct^{3}+dt+e)^{2}=(b^{3}+e^{2})t^{3}+(ab^{2}+d^{2})t^{5}+a^{2}bt^{7}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

Therefore,
\alpha
lifts to
(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if
b^{3}=e^{2}
and
ab^{2}=d^{2}
; and under these conditions
\alpha
fixes
\{p\}
. The subgroup (
10
) acts transitively on
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
. To see this, fix a germ
\eta
such that
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\eta
meets
G^{\circ}
transversally off
p
, say
\eta=(t^{3}+t^{6},t)
. Then substituting
\varepsilon_{a}(\eta)=(a^{3}t^{3}(1+t^{3}),at)
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}(1+\widecheck{y}^{3})+\widecheck{x}
of
\eta
gives

a^{3}t^{3}(1+a^{3}t^{3})+a^{3}t^{3}(1+t^{3})=(a^{3}+a^{6})t^{6},

so
{k}^{*}\ni a\mapsto\varphi^{-1}_{*}\varepsilon_{a}(\eta)\cap G^{\circ}\in G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
is indeed surjective. Its derivative is obtained by multiplying the derivative of (
10
), namely
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}+\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
, by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{6}+y^{4},y^{3})
. Restricted to
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
, the resulting vector field is
x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
, so it does not vanish on
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
, as needed.

Subcase

k=4
.
Now
\mathcal{Y}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
are as in Subcases
k=3
and
k=2
above. Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family whose base is parametrized by some family of germs
\chi
. Let
(Y_{\chi},D_{Y,\chi})\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
be the fiber corresponding to the germ
\chi
, and as usual let
G^{\circ}_{\chi}=G_{\chi}\setminus(D_{Y,\chi}-G_{\chi})
, where
G_{\chi}
is the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Y,\chi}
. Let
\mathcal{A}(\chi)
be the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{\chi},D_{Y,\chi})
on
G^{\circ}_{\chi}
. We claim that it is enough to prove the following.

(i)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
then
\mathcal{A}(t^{3},t)
has exactly two orbits.

(ii)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then
\mathcal{A}(t^{3}+t^{5},t)
is transitive, and
\mathcal{A}(t^{3},t)
has exactly two orbits.

(iii)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
then
\mathcal{A}(t^{3},t)
,
\mathcal{A}(t^{3}+t^{4},t)
, and
\mathcal{A}(t^{3}+t^{6},t)
are transitive.

(iv)

The derivative of each of the above actions does not vanish along the open orbit, unless
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\chi=(t^{3},t)
. In the latter case, all vector fields on
Y_{\chi}
tangent to
D_{Y_{\chi}}
vanish along
G_{\chi}^{\circ}
.

Indeed, assume that
(i)
–
(iv)
hold. If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
, so
4.7
(a)
follows from
(i)
,
(iv)
and Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
then
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=2
, and
(ii)
shows that the germs
(t^{3}+t^{5},t)
and
(t^{3},t)
correspond to, respectively, the general and special fibers of our
h^{1}
-stratified family; so again
4.7
(a)
follows from
(ii)
,
(iv)
and Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
. Eventually, assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. In Subcase
k=1
we have shown that the log surfaces corresponding to
(t^{3},t)
and
(t^{3}+t^{4},t)
are non-isomorphic; and in Subcase
k=2
we have shown that the one corresponding to
(t^{3}+t^{6},t)
is not isomorphic to either of them; in fact it is the special fiber of our family. Thus in this case
4.7
(a)
follows from
(iii)
,
(iv)
and Lemma
2.19
(2.b)
,
(2.c)
.

Thus it remains to show
(i)
–
(iv)
. We begin with the case
\chi=(t^{3},t)
. Fix any
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{l},q)
as in (
8
). Substituting
\alpha(\chi)
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}-\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
, we get

(at^{3}+bt)^{3}-t^{3}(ct^{3}+dt+e)^{2}=(b^{3}-e^{2})t^{3}-2det^{4}+(3ab^{2}-d^{2})t^{5}-2cet^{6}+(3a^{2}b-2cd)t^{7}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}.

Such
\alpha
lifts to
(Y_{\chi},D_{Y,\chi})
if and only if coefficients near
t^{0},\dots,t^{6}
vanish.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(Y_{\chi},D_{Y,\chi})
is generated by (
10
) and, in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, by (
9
). In any case, the coefficient near
t^{7}
above vanishes if
\alpha
lifts to
(Y_{\chi},D_{Y,\chi})
, so
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
fixes
\{p\}
. The map
a\mapsto\varphi^{-1}_{*}\varepsilon_{a}(\eta)\cap G_{\chi}^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
for
\eta=(t^{3}+t^{7},t)
is surjective, so (
10
) acts transitively on
G_{\chi}^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
, as claimed in
(i)
and
(ii)
. Note that this ends the proof of
(i)
in case
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, we will treat case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
at the end.

To prove the corresponding claim in
(iv)
we multiply, as before, the derivative
3\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}+\widecheck{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
of (
10
) by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{7}+y^{3},y)
, where
(x,y)
are coordinates at
p
with
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
. The resulting vector field
-4x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
does not vanish along
G^{\circ}\setminus\{p\}
, as needed.

Assume now that
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, consider
\chi=(t^{3}+t^{5},t)
and the subgroup (
9
). Substituting
\alpha_{a}(\chi)=(t^{3}(1+t^{2}),t(1+at^{2}(1+t^{2})))
to the equation
\widecheck{y}^{3}(1+\widecheck{y}^{2})-\widecheck{x}
of
\chi
, we get

t^{3}(1+a^{3}t^{6}(1+t^{6}))(1+t^{2}(1+at^{2}(1+t^{2}))^{2})-t^{3}(1+t^{2})=2at^{7}+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

so
a\mapsto\alpha_{a}(p)
parametrizes
G_{\chi}^{\circ}
, hence (
9
) acts transitively on
G_{\chi}^{\circ}
, which ends the proof of
(ii)
. Again, we compute directly that the derivative of this action does not vanish, as claimed in
(iv)
. To this end, we multiply the derivative
\widecheck{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
of (
9
) by the inverse of the jacobian of
\varphi=(xy^{7}+y^{5}+y^{3},y)
: the resulting vector field, restricted to
G^{\circ}=\{y=0\}
equals
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
, so does not vanish along
G^{\circ}
, as needed.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Like before we compute that (
11
) gives a transitive action of
\mathcal{A}(t^{3}+t^{4},t)
, and

(13)

\delta_{d}\colon[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:y+d^{2}x:z+dy],\quad d\in{k},

gives a transitive action of
\mathcal{A}(t^{3}+t^{6},t)
; moreover, their derivatives do not vanish along
G^{\circ}
, as needed. In the remaining case
\chi=(t,t^{3})
, the group (
13
) acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
, too, which ends the proof of
(i)
–
(iii)
.

To end the proof of
(iv)
, we need to show that for
\chi=(t,t^{3})
, every vector field
\xi
on
Y
tangent to
D_{Y}
vanishes along
G^{\circ}
. The image
\widecheck{\xi}
of
\xi
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
vanishes at
(0,0)
and is tangent to
\{x=0\}
, so in coordinates
(\widecheck{x},\widecheck{y})
it can be written as
\widecheck{x}(e+c\widecheck{x}+d\widecheck{y})\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}}+(a\widecheck{x}+\widecheck{y}(b+c\widecheck{x}+d\widecheck{y}))\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{y}}
for some
a,b,c,d,e\in{k}
.

To compute
\xi
, we multiply
\widecheck{\xi}
by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\varphi(x,y)=(xy^{7}+y^{3},y)
, where as before
(x,y)
are some local coordinates on
Y
with
\{y=0\}
. The coefficient near
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
of the resulting vector field is
(x+y^{-4})(b+e+ay^{2}+axy^{7})
. Since
\widecheck{\xi}
lifts to a vector field
\xi
on
Y
, we have
b+e=0
and
a=0
, so the coefficient near
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
is zero. Thus
\xi
vanishes along
G^{\circ}
, as claimed.

Case

4.6
(14)
.
Let
F_{1}^{\prime}
,
F_{2}^{\prime}
be the fibers contained in
D_{Z}
, and for
i\in\{1,2\}
let
A_{i}
and
T_{i}
be the
(-1)
- and
(-2)
-tip of
(F_{i}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Let
\sigma\colon(Z,D_{Z})\longrightarrow(Y,D_{Y})
be the contraction of
A_{2}
, let
G
be the
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Y}
containing
\sigma(A_{2})
, and let
G^{\circ}=G\setminus(D_{Y}-G)\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Moreover, let
(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},D_{\overline{Y}})
be the contraction of a
(-1)
-tip of
D_{Y}
other than
G
, let
\overline{G}
be the new
(-1)
-tip of
D_{\overline{Y}}
, and let
\overline{G}^{\circ}=\overline{G}\setminus(D_{\overline{Y}}-\overline{G})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

The combinatorial type
\mathcal{Y}
of
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in case
4.6
(10)
with
d=2
,
T=[2]
. We claim that
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts on
G^{\circ}
trivially if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and transitively with nonvanishing derivative if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
.

Put
n=m-2\geqslant 1
. Let
\tau\colon(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{n},{p})
be the contraction of
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}-T_{1}-T_{2}
. Then
{p}=\Xi_{n}+F_{1}+F_{2}
, where
F_{i}=\tau_{*}T_{i}
is a fiber, and
\Xi_{n}=[n]
is the negative section. Fix coordinates on
\mathbb{F}_{n}=\{([x:x_{1}:x_{2}],[u_{1}:u_{2}])\in\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}:x_{1}u_{1}^{n}=x_{2}u_{2}^{n}\}
so that
F_{i}=\{u_{i}=0\}
,
\Xi_{n}=\{x_{1}=x_{2}=0\}
.
It follows from
[
Bla12
, §6.1]
that
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{n},{p})=\{\alpha_{p,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}:\ p\in{k}[u_{1},u_{2}]_{n},\ \lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in{k}^{*}\}
, where
\alpha_{p,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}
is given on
\{u_{1}\neq 0\}
by

\alpha_{p,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\colon([x:x_{1}:x_{2}],[u_{1}:u_{2}])\mapsto([x+x_{2}\cdot p(1,\tfrac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}):\lambda_{1}^{n}x_{1}:\lambda_{2}^{n}x_{2}],[\lambda_{2}u_{1}:\lambda_{1}u_{2}]).

Write
\{q_{i}\}=F_{i}\cap\Xi_{n}
. Let
\chi_{i}
be a germ given in local coordinates
(\widecheck{x}_{i},\widecheck{u}_{i})\coloneqq(\frac{x_{i}}{x},\frac{u_{i}}{u_{2-i}})
at
q_{i}
by
t\mapsto(t,a_{i}t^{2}+b_{i}t^{3})
for some
a_{i}\in{k}^{*}
,
b_{i}\in{k}
. Fix
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2}
so that the proper transform
\tau^{-1}_{*}\chi_{i}
meets
D_{Y}
normally, in a
(-1)
-tip of
\sigma_{*}F_{i}^{\prime}
: then
{k}\ni b_{2}\mapsto\tau^{-1}_{*}\chi_{2}\cap G^{\circ}
is a parametrization of
G^{\circ}
.
An
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{n},{p})
lifts to
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if
(\alpha(\chi_{1})\cdot\chi_{1})_{q_{1}}\geqslant 4
,
(\alpha(\chi_{2})\cdot\chi_{2})_{q_{2}}\geqslant 3
; and it fixes the point
\tau^{-1}_{*}\chi_{2}\cap G^{\circ}
if and only if
(\alpha(\chi_{2})\cdot\chi_{2})_{q_{2}}\geqslant 4
. Let
s_{i}\coloneqq a_{i}t^{2}+b_{i}t^{3}
be the
\widecheck{u}_{i}
-th coordinate of
\chi_{i}
. Then in the local coordinates
(\widecheck{x}_{i},\widecheck{u}_{i})
at
q_{i}
we have

\alpha_{p,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(\chi_{1})=\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}^{n}\cdot t}{1+t\cdot p(s_{1},1)},\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\cdot s_{1},\right),\quad\alpha_{p,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(\chi_{2})=\left(\frac{\lambda_{2}^{n}\cdot t}{1+t\cdot p(1,s_{2})},\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}\cdot s_{2}\right).

Substituting these formulas to the equations
a_{i}\widecheck{x}_{i}^{2}+b_{i}\widecheck{x}_{i}^{3}-\widecheck{u}_{i}
of
\chi_{i}
, we compute that
(\alpha_{p,\lambda,\mu}(\chi_{i})\cdot\chi_{i})\geqslant 3
if and only if
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{2-i}^{2n+1}
, in which case
(\alpha_{p,\lambda}(\chi_{i})\cdot\chi_{i})\geqslant 4
if and only if
2a_{i}p_{i}=(\lambda_{i}^{n}-1)b_{i}
, where
p_{i}
is the coefficient of
p
near
u_{2-i}^{n}
. Thus
\alpha_{p,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}
lifts to
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
if and only if
\lambda_{1}^{n}=\lambda_{2}^{n}=1
and
2p_{1}=0
; and such a lift acts trivially on
G^{\circ}
if and only if
2p_{2}=0
.
Thus if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
then the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
on
G^{\circ}
is trivial.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. For
q\in{k}
put
\alpha_{q}=\alpha_{qu_{1}^{n},1,1}
. The subgroup
\{\alpha_{q}:q\in{k}\}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}
lifts to
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
and acts transitively on
G^{\circ}
. To compute its derivative, write
\alpha_{q}
in local coordinates as
\alpha_{q}(\widecheck{x}_{2},\widecheck{u}_{2})=(\frac{\widecheck{x}_{2}}{1+q\widecheck{x}_{2}},\widecheck{u}_{2})
. Its derivative at
q=0
maps
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}
to
-\widecheck{x}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widecheck{x}_{2}}
. Multiplying this vector field by the inverse of the jacobian matrix of
\tau(x,u)=(x,ux^{3}+x^{2})
, where like before
(x,u)
are some local coordinates on
Y
with
G^{\circ}=\{x=0\}
, we get
-x^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(3xu+2)\frac{\partial}{\partial u}
, which does not vanish along
G^{\circ}
since
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
, as claimed.

If
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
then the above claim and Lemma
2.19
(2.b)
show that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})=\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})=1
; and by Lemma
2.19
(2.c.i)
we get
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))
, which equals
0
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\nmid m-1
and
1
otherwise.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Lemma
2.19
(1)
implies that
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by a faithful family over
G^{\circ}\times\overline{G}^{\circ}
, which by symmetry of our construction is
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful for
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})=\mathbb{Z}/2
interchanging the factors of
G^{\circ}\times\overline{G}^{\circ}
.

Put
\overline{G}_{1}^{\circ}=\overline{G}^{\circ}
and let
\overline{G}_{2}^{\circ}
be the image of
G^{\circ}
on
\overline{Y}
. The computation in case
4.6
(9)
(repeated for both fibers) shows that there are points
\overline{q}_{i}\in\overline{G}_{i}^{\circ}
such that every vector field
\xi\in H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{\overline{Y}}(-\log D_{\overline{Y}}))
vanishes at
\overline{q}_{1},\overline{q}_{2}
, and there is a vector field
\xi_{1}
which does not vanish along
\overline{G}_{1}^{\circ}\setminus\{\overline{q}_{1}\}
. We check that any such
\xi_{1}
does not vanish along
\overline{G}_{2}^{\circ}\setminus\{\overline{q}_{2}\}
, either. Since
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{\overline{Y}}(-\log D_{\overline{Y}}))=1
, Lemma
2.11
(d)
implies that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=3
if
(Z,D_{Z})
is obtained by blowing up at
\overline{q}_{1}
,
\overline{q}_{2}
, and
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))=2
otherwise.
∎

5.
Del Pezzo surfaces of height two

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
A
by describing all del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height
2
. The proof is organized into the following steps.

Step 1.

We construct vertically primitive surfaces of height
2
from Theorem
A
, see Figures
2(b)
–
2(i)
.

Step 2.

We show that every del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank
1
and height
2
swaps vertically to one of the surfaces constructed in Step
1
. This is done in Lemmas
5.9
and
5.13
, by a suitable choice of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

Step 3.

We reconstruct all possible sequences of vertical swaps from Step
2
, thus classifying singularity types.

Step 4.

We classify surfaces
\overline{X}
of a given singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, that is, we check to which extent the sequence reconstructed in Step
3
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{S}
. This amounts to classifying vertically primitive surfaces from Step
1
, and describing outer blowups made in Step
3
, as outlined in Observation
2.10
.

We note that the proof in case
0pt=1
followed a similar path: we have constructed primitive surfaces in Example
4.2
, and classified singularity types in Lemma
4.4
. The main technical part was the final Step
4
.

Now, the situation is different. There are several classes of vertically primitive surfaces, including one specific to characteristic
2
. The latter is constructed from a pencil of lines tangent to a smooth planar conic, see
[
KM99
, §9]
or Example
5.5
: clearly, such a pencil exists if and only if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. The remaining surfaces are constructed from simpler planar configurations, and their combinatorics do not depend on
\operatorname{char}{k}
.

As a consequence, the reconstruction process in Step
3
gives a longer list of singularity types: for example, from the primitive surfaces specific to characteristic
2
we get complicated ones listed in Table
12
, see Theorem
A
(3.c.ii)
. Nonetheless, it turns out that this process involves very few outer blowups, so Step
4
is easier, and essentially boils down to the known classification of vertically primitive surfaces from Step
1
.

In Steps
2
–
4
, we first study the case
0pt=2
, i.e. when for some witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration the horizontal part of the boundary consists of two
1
-sections. This is done in Section
5B
. The remaining case
0pt=1
, which contains examples specific to characteristic
2
, is settled in Section
5C
.

5A.
Vertically primitive surfaces of height
2

We now construct vertically primitive surfaces
appearing in Theorem
A
(3.b)
,
(3.c)
. Their constructions
are known; see e.g.
[
MZ88
]
for the canonical ones (Fig.
2(b)
–
2(h)
) and
[
KM99
, §9]
for the additional ones in characteristic
2
(Fig.
2(i)
). Nonetheless, in our approach we need a self-contained presentation.

Notation 5.1

(A pencil of lines)
.

Fix a point
p_{0}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
, lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
, passing through
p_{0}
, and a curve
{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Put
{p}={c}+{l}_{1}+\dots+{l}_{\nu}
. For a birational morphism
\varphi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
p_{0}\in\operatorname{Bs}\varphi^{-1}
, we define
D_{Y}
as
(\varphi^{*}{p})_{\mathrm{red}}
with all
(-1)
-curves subtracted, and consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
induced by the pencil of lines through
p_{0}
. We denote the proper transforms of
{l}_{i},{c}
by
L_{i},C
, respectively. For a point
p_{i}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we denote by
A_{i}
the
(-1)
-curve in the preimage of
p_{i}
. The surface
\overline{Y}
from Theorem
A
is obtained from
Y
by contracting
D_{Y}
.

Example 5.2

(Del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and types
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
or
2\mathrm{D}_{4}
, see Figure
19
)
.

We keep Notation
5.1
. Let
{c}
be a line not passing through
p_{0}
. Write
\{p_{j}\}={l}_{j}\cap{c}
. We now define a morphism
\varphi\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
as in Notation
5.1
, such that
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in Figure
19(a)
–
19(d)
; and the del Pezzo surface
\overline{Y}
obtained by contracting
D_{Y}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
or
\mathrm{D}_{4}
.

(a)

For type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
: take
\nu=2
, and blow up three times at
p_{0},p_{1}
and their infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
{l}_{2}
and
{c}
, respectively, see Figure
19(a)
.

(b)

For type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
: take
\nu=3
, blow up at
p_{1}
; and at
p_{0}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
and their infinitely near points on the proper transforms of
{l}_{1}
,
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{3}
, respectively, see Figure
19(b)
.

(c)

For type

Example 5.5

(Additional class of primitive del Pezzo surfaces in case
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, cf.
[
KM99
, §9]
)
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Take
\nu\geqslant 3
, and let
{c}
be a conic such that
({l}_{j}\cdot{c})_{p_{j}}=2
for all
j
. Clearly, such a
strange
conic exists if and only if
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Blow up at
p_{0}
, and twice over each
p_{j}
, on the proper transforms of
{c}
, see Figure
20(c)
. The singularity type of
\overline{Y}
, written together with the resulting
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration using Notation
2.22
, is

(14)

[\boldsymbol{2\nu-4}]^{|1,\dots,\nu|}+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}([2]^{|j|}+[2]^{|j|}).

For
\nu=3
we get a canonical surface of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, unique up to an isomorphism. In general, the set of isomorphism classes of surfaces
\overline{Y}
has moduli dimension
\nu-3
, with the representing family parametrized by the choice of
{l}_{4},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
; see Lemma
5.16
(c)
for details.

We claim that
\overline{Y}
is primitive. We argue as in Remark
4.3
. Case
\nu=3
is covered by Remark
5.7
, so assume
\nu\geqslant 4
. Suppose
\overline{Y}
is not primitive. Then
Y
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
meeting
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
once.
Every degenerate fiber
F
is supported on a chain
[2,1,2]
, with a
(-1)
-curve of multiplicity
2
and tips of multiplicity
1
. Hence
A\cdot F
is odd for the fiber
F
containing the point
A\cap D_{\mathrm{vert}}
and even for all the other degenerate fibers, a contradiction.

Example 5.6

(See Figure
21
)
.

We now reconstruct remaining canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height
2
, and we show that the following hold, see Table
15
.

(a)

Surfaces of types
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
\mathrm{A}_{8}
,
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
swap vertically to the one of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, constructed in
5.2
(a)
.

(b)

Surfaces of types
\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5}

\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}
swap vertically to the one of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, constructed in
5.2
(b)
.

(c)

Surfaces of type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
swap vertically to the one of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, constructed in
5.5
for
\nu=3
.

Let
(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},0)
and
C,L_{j},A_{j}\subseteq Y
be as in one of the examples quoted above.

(a)
Take
\overline{Y}
of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, see Figure
19(a)
. Choose points
q\in L_{1}\setminus D_{Y}
,
r\in A_{0}\setminus D_{Y}
, and some
q^{\prime}
infinitely near to
q
, not on the proper transform of
L_{1}
. Define a vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
as a blowup either at
q
, or at
q
and
r
, or at
q
and
q^{\prime}
; and let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
D
. Then
\overline{X}
is either of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
, or
\mathrm{A}_{8}
, or
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
, respectively, see Figures
21(a)
,
21(b)
, or
21(c)
.
Lemma
4.7
in cases
4.6
(2)
and
(8)
implies that types
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
\mathrm{A}_{8}
are realized by a unique surface, and type
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
by exactly two; moreover, the set
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family.

One can also see this directly, as follows. Put
\overline{q}=\varphi(q)\in{l}_{1}
. Clearly,
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p},\overline{q})
is unique up to an isomorphism, hence so is
\overline{X}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
. Fix coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
p_{0}=[0:1:0]
,
p_{1}=[0:0:1]
,
p_{2}=[1:0:0]
,
\overline{q}=[0:1:1]
. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p},\overline{q})=\{[x:y:z]\mapsto[x:y:\alpha z]:\alpha\in{k}^{*}\}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts transitively on the set of irreducible members of the pencil
\{\lambda x^{3}=\mu yz^{2}\}_{[\lambda:\mu]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}}
: this action lifts to a transitive action on
A_{1}\setminus D_{Y}
. It follows that the surface
\overline{X}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{8}
is unique up to an isomorphism. On the other hand, the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p},\overline{q})
on the pencil of lines through
\overline{q}
other than
{l}_{1}
has two orbits: the open one, and the fixed line
\overline{{l}}
joining
\overline{q}
with
p_{2}
; and the derivative of this action does not vanish along the open orbit. Thus by Lemma
2.19
(2.a)
we have
\#\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6})=2
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6})
is represented by an
h^{1}
-stratified family, as claimed.

We remark that by
[
PP24
, Proposition 1.5(b)]
, see Table
15
, exactly one of the two surfaces of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
has a singular member
\overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{X}}|
in its smooth locus. We claim that it is the general one if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
, and the special one if
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Here the
general
and
special
one is, respectively, the one corresponding to the open and closed orbit, i.e. to the general and special fiber of the
h^{1}
-stratified family.

Indeed, let
T
be the proper transform of
\overline{T}
on
X
. We have
T\in|-K_{X}|
, so by adjunction
T
meets each
(-1)
-curve once. It follows that the image of
T
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, call it
{q}
, is a rational cubic such that
p_{0},p_{1},\overline{q}\in{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
;
({q}\cdot{l}_{2})_{p_{0}}=({q}\cdot{c})_{p_{1}}=3
, and
{q}
is tangent to
\overline{{l}}
at
\overline{q}
precisely in the special case. The Hurwitz formula or
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.5]
show that the latter holds exactly when
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, as claimed.

(b)
Take
\overline{Y}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Figure
20(b)
. Choose a point
q_{j}\in A_{j}^{\circ}\coloneqq A_{j}\setminus D_{Y}
,
j\in\{0,2\}
. A vertical swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
given by a blowup at
q_{0}
or
q_{2}
, followed by the contraction of
D
, gives a surface
\overline{X}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5}
or
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}
, respectively, see Figures
21(d)
,
21(e)
. Like before, Lemma
4.7
in case
4.6
(2)
implies that these surfaces are unique up to an isomorphism. To see this directly, note that the choice of
q_{0},q_{2}
is equivalent to a choice of a conic
{q}
tangent to
{l}_{1}
and
{c}
at
p_{0}
,
p_{2}
; and the configuration
{p}+{q}
is uniquely determined by four points
(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},{q}\cap{l}_{3}\setminus\{p_{0}\})
in a general position.

(c)
Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and take
\overline{Y}
of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Figure
20(c)
. Blowing up a point
q\in A_{1}\setminus D_{Y}
gives a surface of type
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Since the group
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,D_{Y})
acts on
A_{1}\setminus D_{Y}
with finite orbits, Lemma
2.19
(1)
implies that the set of isomorphism classes of such surfaces has moduli dimension
1
, with the representing family parametrized by the choice of
q
, cf.
[
PP24
, Example 8.1(b)]
or Lemma
5.17
.

(a)

.
Example
5.6
: canonical surfaces of height

which are not vertically primitive.

Remark 5.7

(Primitive canonical surfaces, see Table
15
)
.

Recall that all singular, canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one are those listed in Table
15
. For future reference, we note that the primitive ones are those of types:
\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
(see Remark
4.5
);
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
(see Figures
19
and
20
) and
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see
[
PP24
, Examples 7.1, 8.1(c)]
.

We have seen in Remark
4.5
and in the examples above that all other canonical surfaces are not primitive. It remains to prove that those listed in the statement are primitive. Suppose one of them, say
\overline{Y}
, is not. By Definition
1.3
(e)
, the minimal log resolution
(Y,D_{Y})
of
\overline{Y}
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
such that
A\cdot D_{Y}=1
.

To get a contradiction, one can use a description of all
(-1)
-curves on their minimal resolutions, given in
[
BBD84
, Table 1]
. Nonetheless, we provide an easy direct argument. By Remark
4.5
we can assume
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
.

Let
R
be the connected component of
D_{Y}
meeting
L
. Then
\#R\geqslant 4
, because
A+R
is not non-positive definite. Recall that we have assumed
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
, so looking at Table
15
we see that
\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
. In particular,
R
is a chain, so since
A+R
is not negative definite,
A
does not meet a tip of
R
.

Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
shown in Figure
19(c)
or
19(e)
.
In the first case,
R
contains
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
; in the second case we can assume that the same holds by symmetry.
Let
F
be a general fiber, let
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the contraction of
D_{Y}
, and let
\overline{F}=\pi(F)
,
\overline{A}=\pi(A)
. Since
\overline{Y}
is canonical, we have
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{Y}}=K_{Y}
, so by Noether’s formula
K_{\overline{Y}}^{2}=K_{Y}^{2}=10-\rho(Y)=1
, and by adjunction
\overline{F}\cdot K_{\overline{Y}}=F\cdot K_{Y}=-2
. Since
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, it follows that
\overline{F}=-2K_{\overline{Y}}
, so

\overline{A}\cdot\overline{F}=-2\overline{A}\cdot K_{\overline{Y}}=-2A\cdot K_{Y}=2
. We compute that
\pi^{*}\overline{F}=F+2R-\mathrm{tip}^{+}(R)-\mathrm{tip}^{-}(R)
, so
\overline{A}\cdot\overline{F}=A\cdot F+2
, hence
A\cdot F=0
, i.e.
A
is vertical, a contradiction, see
Figures
19(c)
,
19(e)
.

∎

5B.
Case
0pt(X,D)=0pt(X,D)=2

Throughout this section, we assume that

(15)

\overline{X}
is a del Pezzo surface of rank one and height two;

(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
is the minimal log resolution; and

p\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
2
-sections.

Lemma 5.8

(The structure of
p
)
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2}
be the
1
-sections in
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
be the degenerate fibers.

(a)

We have
H_{1}=[n]
,
H_{2}=[m]
for some
n,m\geqslant 2
.

(b)

If both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are branching in
D
then they lie in different connected components of
D
.

(c)

The fiber
F_{1}
has exactly two
(-1)
-curves, and each
F_{j}
for
j\geqslant 1
has exactly one.

(d)

Each fiber
F_{j}
for
j\geqslant 2
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in tips of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
. In particular,
\nu-1\leqslant\min\{\beta_{D}(H_{1}),\beta_{D}(H_{2})\}\leqslant 3
.

(e)

Let
\varphi
be the contraction of
(-1)
-curves in
F_{1}
and its images such that all
(-1)
-curves in
\varphi_{*}F_{1}
meet
\varphi_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, and
\varphi
is an isomorphism in some neighborhood of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Then
\varphi_{*}F_{1}=[1,(2)_{s},1]
for some
s\geqslant 0
.

(a)
The curves
H_{1},H_{2}
are rational by Lemma
2.7
(b)
. We have
H_{i}^{2}\leqslant-1
, because
D
is negative definite, and
H_{i}^{2}\neq-1
by Lemma
2.7
(c)
.

(b)
Suppose the contrary. Then
D
contains a rational chain
T
with
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
as tips, and four components
T_{i,j}\not\subseteq T
such that
T_{i,j}
meets
T
only in
H_{i}
, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. Put
B=T+\sum_{i,j}T_{i,j}
. By
[
Kol92
, 3.2.5, 3.1.3]
we have
1\leqslant\operatorname{cf}_{B}(H_{i})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(H_{i})
, so
2\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(H_{1})+\operatorname{cf}(H_{2})
, contrary to the inequality (
5
).

(c)
By Lemma
2.8
(b)
, the
(-1)
-curves in
F_{i}
are precisely those components of
F_{i}
which do not lie in
D
. Thus
(c)
follows from Lemma
2.8
(a)
.

(d)
Fix
j>1
. By
(c)
, the fiber
F_{j}
contains a unique
(-1)
-curve. This
(-1)
-curve has multiplicity at least
2
, so it does not meet
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. This proves the first inequality in
(d)
, cf. Lemma
4.1
(b)
. The second one is obvious if
\overline{X}
is log terminal; otherwise it follows from Proposition
3.1
(a)
.

(e)
If
\varphi_{*}F_{1}=[0]
then, since
\varphi
is an isomorphism near
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, the proper transform
\varphi^{-1}_{*}(\varphi^{*}F_{1})
meets both components of
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, which is impossible by Lemma
2.8
(c)
. Thus
\varphi_{*}F_{1}
contains a
(-1)
-curve. By the definition of
\varphi
, every such curve meets
\varphi_{*}D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so it has multiplicity
1
in
\varphi_{*}F_{1}
. Hence
\varphi_{*}F_{1}
has at least
2
such
(-1)
-curves, and part
(c)
shows that it has exactly two. The result follows, since
\varphi_{*}F_{1}
contracts to a
0
-curve.

∎

Recall from
[
Fuj82
, 7.2]
that a
rivet
is a connected component of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
meeting both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.

Lemma 5.9

(Vertical swaps)
.

Let
(X,D)
and
m,n,s,\nu,\varphi
be as in Lemma
5.8
. We can choose
p
so that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a canonical surface
\overline{Y}
constructed in Examples
5.2
–
5.3
; and one of the following holds.

(a)

H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=0
,

s=m+n-\nu
,
\nu=2,3
or
4
,
and
\overline{Y}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
or
2\mathrm{D}_{4}
, respectively,

(b)

H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=0
,
s=m+n-2
,
\nu=3
,
and
\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,

(c)

H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=1
,
s=m+n
,
\nu=2
,
and
\overline{Y}
is of type
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
.

Assume furthermore that
\overline{X}
is log canonical. Then we can choose
p
so that if case
(a)
holds and
R
is a rivet then
R\subseteq F_{2}
, and either
\nu=3

or
\nu=2
, and the connected components of
D-R
containing
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
meet the same
(-1)
-curve in
F_{1}
.

Let
R_{0}
be a component of
D
meeting both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
; put
R_{0}=0
if there is no such. Since
D
has no circular subdivisors,
R_{0}
is unique. By Lemma
2.8
(c)

R_{0}\not\subseteq F_{1}
, so we can assume
R_{0}\subseteq F_{2}
. Put
\varepsilon=\#R_{0}\in\{0,1\}
.

Let
\upsilon\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{n}
be the contraction of all vertical curves not meeting
H_{1}
. Then
\upsilon(H_{1})
is the negative section, and
\upsilon(H_{2})
is a
1
-section such that
\upsilon(H_{1})\cdot\upsilon(H_{2})=H_{1}\cdot H_{2}\in\{0,1\}
. Numerical properties of
\mathbb{F}_{n}
yield
0=(\upsilon(H_{2})-\upsilon(H_{1}))^{2}=\upsilon(H_{2})^{2}-2(H_{1}\cdot H_{2})-n
, so
\upsilon(H_{2})^{2}=n+2(H_{1}\cdot H_{2})
. Decomposing
\upsilon
as a sequence of blowups, we see that those blowups which are not isomorphisms near the image of
H_{2}
contract exactly
s+1
components of
F_{1}
and, if
\nu>1
, also
1-\varepsilon
components of
F_{2}
and one component of
F_{j}
for each
j>2
. We get
(s+1)+(1-\varepsilon)+(\nu-2)=\upsilon(H_{2})^{2}-H_{2}^{2}=n+2(H_{1}\cdot H_{2})+m
, so

(16)

s=m+n-\nu+\varepsilon+2H_{1}\cdot H_{2}.

In particular, since
\nu\leqslant 4
by Lemma
5.8
(d)
, we have
s\geqslant m+n-4
.

Let
\tau^{\prime}
be the contraction of
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
and of all components of
F_{2}+\dots+F_{\nu}
which do not meet
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Then
\tau^{\prime}_{*}H_{1}=[n]
,
\tau^{\prime}_{*}H_{2}=[m]
. Since
s\geqslant m+n-4
, we can further contract
(-1)
-curves in
F_{1}
and its images until the images of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are
(-2)
-curves. This way, we get a vertical swap
\tau\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
. Put

\overline{H}_{j}=\tau_{*}H_{j}
,

\overline{F}_{j}=\tau_{*}(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then
\overline{H}_{j}=[2]
,
\overline{F}_{j}=[2,1,2]
for
j>1
; and
\overline{F}_{1}=[1,(2)_{s^{\prime}},1]
for
s^{\prime}=s-m-n+4
. By (
16
)

(17)

s^{\prime}=4-\nu+\varepsilon+2H_{1}\cdot H_{2}.

Note that
(Y,D_{Y})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},0)
is a minimal log resolution of some canonical surface
\overline{Y}
. We have
\rho(\overline{Y})=\rho(\overline{X})=1
and
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})=2
, so
\overline{Y}
is del Pezzo by Lemma
2.6
.
We need to show that
\overline{Y}
is as in Examples
5.2
–
5.3
.

Assume
\nu\geqslant 3
. By Lemma
5.8
(b)
we have
\nu\leqslant 4
. If
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=1
or
F_{1}
contains a rivet then both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are branching in
D
, contrary to Lemma
5.8
(b)
. Hence
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=0
, and a rivet, if exists, is contained in
F_{2}
. This way, we get
(b)
if
\varepsilon=1
and
(a)
if
\varepsilon=0
.

Assume
\nu\leqslant 2
. Formula (
17
) gives
s^{\prime}\geqslant 2
. Put
\overline{F}=\overline{H}_{1}+2\overline{F}_{1}^{(1)}+\overline{F}_{1}^{(2)}
, so
\overline{F}_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
, and put
\widetilde{F}=\tau^{*}\overline{F}
. The linear system
|\widetilde{F}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\widetilde{p}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The horizontal part of
D
for
\widetilde{p}
, call it
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
, consists of disjoint
1
-sections.
Assume
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=1
. If
\nu=2
then
(c)
holds, so assume
\nu=1
. Now,
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two disjoint
1
-sections:
H_{2}
and
\tau^{-1}_{*}\overline{F}_{1}^{(3)}
. Thus replacing
p
by
\widetilde{p}
, we can assume
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=0
.

We have
\nu=\#\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 0pt(X,D)=2
, so
\nu=2
; and
\widetilde{D}_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections. Since
s^{\prime}\geqslant 2
, those sections are disjoint and do not meet the same component of
D
. Thus after replacing
p
by
\widetilde{p}
, we get case
(a)
.

To prove the last statement, assume that
D
has a rivet
R
. Since
D
has no circular subdivisors,
R
is unique. If
R\subseteq F_{1}
then the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\widetilde{p}
defined above admits a rivet, too. This rivet lies in the fiber
\widetilde{F}
, which has exactly one component off
D
. Thus replacing
p
by
\widetilde{p}
, we can assume
R\subseteq F_{2}
.

Since
D
has no circular subdivisors, the sections
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
lie in different connected components of
D-R
, call them
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Put
V=\tau^{-1}_{*}(\overline{F}_{1}\wedge D_{Y})
. If
V\not\subseteq D_{1}+D_{2}
then
\widetilde{p}
has no rivet, so we are done by replacing
p
with
\widetilde{p}
. Thus we can assume
V\subseteq D_{1}
. Let
\overline{A}
be the tip of
\overline{F}_{1}
which meets
\overline{H}_{2}
. Then since
F_{1}
contains no rivet the preimage
\tau^{*}\overline{A}
contains a
(-1)
-curve meeting both
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, as claimed.

∎

We are now ready to list singularity types of log canonical surfaces satisfying assumption (
15
).
We use notation summarized in Section
2G
. In particular, we encode our
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
(X,D)
using a decorated singularity type, introduced in Notation
2.22
. Singularity types without decorations are listed in Table
10
.

Remark 5.11

(Redundancies in Lemma
5.10
)
.

Interchanging the roles of chains
T,T_{1},T_{2}
in cases
(1)
–
(3)
or
(11)
can yield the same combinatorial type
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
, hence isomorphic del Pezzo surfaces
\overline{X}
. For example, in case
(1)
replacing
(T,T_{1},T_{2})
with
(T_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},(T_{2}^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})
or
(T_{1}^{*},T_{2}^{*},T)
we get the same
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
. In fact, in this case
D+\sum_{i}A_{i}
is circular, and the above substitution corresponds to a reflection or a rotation of its graph.

We also recall that by convention (
2
) type
[(2)_{0}]
refers to a smooth point, and should be ignored.

We keep notation from Lemma
5.8
and choose
p
as in Lemma
5.9
. Then
D_{\mathrm{hor}}=H_{1}+H_{2}
,
H_{1}=[n]
,
H_{2}=[m]
. Let
D_{j}
be the connected component of
D
containing
H_{j}
. First, we show that the structure of
p
(hence the singularity type of
\overline{X}
) is as in
(a)
–
(e)
. We study each case
5.9
(a)
–
5.9
(c)
separately.

Case

5.9
(a)
.
Assume first that
D_{1}\neq D_{2}
. Then for
j\geqslant 2
, the fiber
F_{j}
is columnar, say
(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}=[T_{j-1},1,T_{j-1}^{*}]
. Assume
F_{1}\cap H_{j}\not\subseteq D
for both
j\in\{1,2\}
. Then
F_{1}=[1,(2)_{m+n-\nu},1]
. If
\nu=2
then
D
is as in
(1)
for
\#T_{1}=\#T_{2}=1
. If
\nu=3
then
D
is as in
(11)
. If
\nu=4
then
D_{1},D_{2}
are forks, and if neither is admissible then
\operatorname{cf}(H_{1})+\operatorname{cf}(H_{2})=2
, contrary to inequality (
5
). Thus
D
is as in
(29)
.

Assume now that
F_{1}\cap H_{1}\subseteq D
. Then
\nu=\beta_{D}(H_{1})\leqslant 4
. Consider the case
\nu=4
. Then
D_{1}
is a bench, so
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}=[2]
. It follows that
\beta_{D}(H_{2})\geqslant 3
, and as before, inequality (
5
) implies that
D_{2}
is an admissible fork. In particular,
F_{1}
meets
D_{2}
in a
(-1)
-curve. Since
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{1}=[2]
, we get
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,3,(2)_{s-1},1]
. Lemma
5.9
(a)
gives
s=m+n-5
, so
D
is as in
(30)
.

Consider the case
\nu\leqslant 3
. Contract
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent images of
F_{1}
until it becomes a chain meeting the image of

in tips, and denote this morphism by

. Since

contracts to

,

Assume

. Then

. If

if

Case

(e)
.
The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is a subgroup of
S_{3}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, where
S_{3}
permutes the fibers
F_{2},F_{3},F_{4}
with one
(-1)
-curve each; and

. Put

. Let
\psi\colon(X,\widecheck{D})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},{p})
be the contraction of all vertical curves disjoint from

, followed by some elementary transformations on the image of

. Clearly,
\psi
is
G
-equivariant, and we check directly that
\psi
is inner. The image
{p}
of

is the sum of two horizontal and four vertical fibers. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the combinatorial type of
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},{p})
. We have seen in Examples
2.16
and
2.17
that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{Z})
is represented by an
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})
-faithful universal family over

, parametrized by the cross-ratio of the images of
F_{1},\dots,F_{4}
. By Lemma
2.18

\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
is represented by a universal
G
-faithful family over the same base, and
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log{p}))=1
for

.

It remains to prove that this family, viewed as one representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
, is almost faithful. Assume that we have an isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{b_{1}},\widecheck{D}_{b_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{b_{2}},\widecheck{D}_{b_{2}})
between two fibers. We check directly that any element of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
preserves the set of vertices corresponding to horizontal components, so
\varphi
maps the quadruple
\{F_{j,b_{1}}\cap H_{1,b_{1}}:j=1,2,3,4\}
to
\{F_{j,b_{2}}\cap H_{i,b_{2}}:j=1,2,3,4\}
for some
