. Hence their cross-ratios are equivalent by the
S_{3}
-action, which means that
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
lie in the same
G
-orbit, as needed.
∎

5C.
Case
0pt(X,D)=2
,
0pt(X,D)=1

To prove Theorem
A
, it remains to work in the following setting.

(18)

(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
is the minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one, such that
0pt(\overline{X})=2
, and for all witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations,
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is irreducible.

Fix a log surface
(X,D)
satisfying assumption (
18
), and a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
p
of
X
witnessing the height.

Lemma 5.12

(The structure of
p
)
.

Let
H
be the
2
-section in
D
. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
be the degenerate fibers.

(a)

The curve
H
is rational, and
\beta_{D}(H)\leqslant 3
.

(b)

Each
F_{j}
has exactly one unique
(-1)
-curve, say
L_{j}
, and
L_{j}=(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}-(F_{j})_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{\mathrm{vert}}
.

(c)

We have
\#F_{j}\cap H=1
for every
j\geqslant 2
, and either
\#F_{1}\cap H=1
or
F_{1}
is columnar.

(d)

Assume
\#F_{j}\cap H=1
. Contract those
(-1)
-curves in
F_{j}
and its images which do not meet the image of
H
, and let
F_{j}^{\prime}
be the image of
F_{j}
. Put
k_{j}=\#(F_{j}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}-1
. Then
(F_{j}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
or
\langle 2;[2],[2],[1,(2)_{k_{j}-3}]\rangle
.

(e)

There is a morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
{c}\coloneqq\psi(H)
is a conic and
{l}_{j}\coloneqq\psi_{*}F_{1}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
are concurrent lines, meeting away from
{c}
. The line
{l}_{j}
is tangent to
{c}
if and only if
\#F_{j}\cap H=1
.

(f)

Let
k_{1},\dots,k_{\nu}
be as in
(d)
, where we put
k_{1}=1
if
F_{1}
is columnar. Then
H^{2}=4-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}k_{j}
.

(a)
Since
0pt(\overline{X})=2
, rationality of
H
follows from Lemma
2.7
(b)
. If
\beta_{D}(H)\geqslant 4
then a direct computation shows that
\operatorname{cf}(H)\geqslant 1
, contrary to Lemma
2.6
.

(b)
This follows from Lemma
2.8
(a)
,
(b)
.

(c)
Assume
\#F_{1}\cap D=2
. Then
F_{1}
meets
D
in components of multiplicity one. By
(b)
, they lie in
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
, in fact in different connected components of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
since otherwise
D
would have a circular subdivisor. Lemma
2.3
implies that
F_{1}
is columnar. If
\#F_{2}\cap D=2
then
\beta_{D}(H)\geqslant 4
contrary to
(a)
. Hence
\#F_{j}\cap D=1
for
j\geqslant 2
.

(d)
Part
(b)
shows that
F_{j}^{\prime}
has a unique
(-1)
-curve, say
L_{j}^{\prime}
. By the definition of
\varphi^{j}
, the curve
L_{j}^{\prime}
meets
\varphi^{j}(H)
, so it has multiplicity
2
in
F_{j}^{\prime}
. If
L_{j}^{\prime}
is a tip of
(F_{j}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}
, contract a maximal twig
[1,(2)_{k}]
of
(F_{j}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}
containing it and denote by
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}
the image of
F_{j}^{\prime}
; otherwise put
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}=F_{j}^{\prime}
. Then the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F_{j}^{\prime\prime}
has multiplicity two and is not a tip of
(F_{j}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}
, so by Lemma
2.3
,
(F_{j}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
. This proves the claim.

(e)
By
(c)
and
(e)
, there is a morphism
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{n}
, for some
n\geqslant 0
, such that
\varphi_{*}H\cong H
is a
2
-section, so
\varphi_{*}H\in|bf+2\sigma_{n}|
for some
b\geqslant 0
. Moreover,
0=p_{a}(H)=p_{a}(\varphi_{*}H)=n+b-1
, so
n=1-b\leqslant 1
. We can choose the last blowdown in such a way that
n=1
, so
b=\Xi_{1}\cdot\varphi_{*}H=0
,
(\varphi_{*}H)^{2}=4
, and the composition of
\varphi
with the contraction of the negative section
\Xi_{1}
is the required morphism
\psi
.

(f)
The morphism
\psi
from
(e)
factors as
\beta\circ\alpha
, where
\alpha
is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
H
, and
\beta
is a composition of exactly
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}k_{j}
blowups, whose exceptional curves meet the images of
H
once each. Part
(f)
follows since
\psi(H)^{2}=4
.

∎

Lemma 5.13

(Vertical swaps)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be as in (
18
). Then
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
5.4
or
5.5
. With notation from Lemma
5.12
, one of the following holds.

(a)

\nu=2
,
\#F_{1}\cap H=2
,
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}\neq[2,1,2]
,
k_{2}=3-H^{2}\geqslant 5
; and
\overline{Y}
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5}
, see Example
5.4
(a)
,

(b)

\nu=3
,
\#F_{1}\cap H=2
,
k_{2}+k_{3}=3-H^{2}\geqslant 5
;
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
; and
\overline{Y}
of type
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
, see Example
5.4
(b)
,

(c)

\nu\geqslant 3
,
\#F_{1}\cap H=1
;
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, and
\overline{Y}
is of type (
14
), see Example
5.5
.

Let
\psi
be as in Lemma
5.12
(e)
. Then
(\operatorname{Bs}\psi^{-1})_{\mathrm{red}}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{\nu}\}
, where
p_{j}\in{l}_{j}\cap{c}
. In fact,
\{p_{j}\}={l}_{j}\cap{c}
if and only if
\#F_{j}\cap H=1
, i.e.
j\geqslant 2
or
j=1
and
F_{1}
is not columnar.

If
\nu=1
then the pencil of lines through
p_{1}
pulls back to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant 2
, and
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
1
-sections, contrary to assumption (
18
). Thus
\nu\geqslant 2
.

Consider the case
\nu=2
. If
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
or
\#F_{1}\cap H=1
, then using the pencil of conics tangent to
{l}_{1},{l}_{2}
at
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
we get a contradiction with (
18
) as before; see Example
5.4
(a)
for a particular case with
k_{2}=5
. Lemma
5.12
(f)
gives
k_{2}=3-H^{2}\geqslant 5
, and we conclude that

(a)
holds.

Consider the case
\nu\geqslant 3
. Assume first that
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. Then at most two lines through
p_{0}
are tangent to
{c}
, so
\nu=3
and
\#F_{1}\cap D=2
. As before, Lemma
5.12
(f)
gives
k_{2}+k_{3}=3-H^{2}\geqslant 5
, so, say,
k_{2}\geqslant 3
and

(b)
holds.

Assume

\operatorname{char}{k}=2
. Since
p_{0}
lies on two lines
{l}_{2}
,
{l}_{3}
tangent to
{c}
, it lies on all of them, so
\#F_{j}\cap H=1
for all
j
. Contracting all degenerate fibers to chains
[2,1,2]
we get a vertical swap as required in
(c)
.

∎

We now list all singularity types of log canonical surfaces
\overline{X}
satisfying assumption (
18
), and describe the witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations.
In the list, we will frequently encounter fibers supported on rational forks with two twigs of type
[2]
, cf. Lemma
2.3
(b)(b.ii)
. Thus to simplify our list, we introduce the following notation (we recall that all the remaining conventions used in our statements are summarized in Section
2G
).

Notation 5.14

(Forks and chains with two twigs
[2]
)
.

Let
T
be an admissible chain, and let
r\geqslant 2
. We put

\!\langle\!\langle[T,r]\rangle\!\rangle\coloneqq\langle r;T,[2],[2]\rangle,\qquad\!\langle\!\langle[r]\rangle\!\rangle=[2,r,2].

We extend Notation
2.22
by putting
\prescript{|i|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[T,r]\rangle\!\rangle=\langle r,\prescript{|i|}{}{T},[2],[2]\rangle
, and
\prescript{|i|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[r]\rangle\!\rangle=[2,r^{|i|},2]
. Moreover, we extend the notation for Dynkin type
\mathrm{D}_{k}
by putting
\mathrm{D}_{3}=\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{D}_{2}=2\mathrm{A}_{1}
, and extend Notation
2.22
to such types by

\mathrm{D}_{k}^{|i|}=\prescript{|i|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[(2)_{k-2}]\rangle\!\rangle\mbox{ for }k\geqslant 3\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{D}_{2}^{|i|}=[2]^{|i|}+[2]^{|i|}.

First, we consider those cases in Lemma
5.9
which can occur if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
; thus completing the proof of Theorem
A
and Propositions
C
,
D
in this case.

Lemma 5.15

(Classification in cases
5.13
(a)
,
(b)
, see Tables
10
–
11
.)
.

Let
(X,D)
be as in (

), i.e.

,

(b)

We have

(3)

,

(6)

,

for some integers

and an admissible chain
T
.

We keep notation from Lemma
5.12
. Let
D_{H}
be the connected component of
D
containing

. Since

by Lemma
5.13
, Lemma
5.12
(c)
implies that
F_{1}
is columnar, so
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}=[T,1,T^{*}]
.

If
L_{j}
meets
H
for all

then by Lemma
5.12
(d)
,
(f)

D
is as in
(1)
if
\nu=2
and
(3)
if
\nu=3
. Assume
F_{2}

in some component of

. Then
\beta_{D}(H)\geqslant 3
, so
\beta_{D}(H)=3
by Lemma
5.12
(a)
. Suppose

is not a fork. Then

contains a chain whose tips are branching in

, and

, 3.1.3, 3.2.5]

, a contradiction with Lemma

. Thus

for some admissible chain

; and we have either
\nu=2
, or
\nu=3
and
L_{3}
meets
H
. Note that the fork

is admissible, since otherwise
\operatorname{cf}(H)\geqslant 1
, contrary to Lemma

.

Consider the case
k_{2}=2
. Then
\nu=3
, since otherwise

by Lemma
5.13
(a)
. The morphism from Lemma

(d)
maps
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
to a chain

or

and

.

Consider the case

. Then

. Assume

. Then interchanging

,

and as in

if

.

To see the uniqueness result, we argue, once again, as in the proof of Corollary
4.8
. Let
\widecheck{D}
be the sum of
D
, the vertical
(-1)
-curves, and the
(-1)
-curve over the common point of point

of the lines in Example

. Let
\psi\colon(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
be the morphism onto the surface from Example

, and let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
,
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
be the combinatorial types of
(X,\widecheck{D})
and

. Applying Lemma

(b)
to

we see that it is enough to prove that

and

. We check that the morphism

is inner, so by Lemmas

and

(c)
it is enough to prove the corresponding statements for

.

Since each planar configuration used to construct

in Example

is unique up to a projective equivalence (even with a fixed order of components and singular points), we have
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{0})=1
. It remains to prove that

. We keep the notation from Example

.

In case

put

, where

is the proper transform of a line joining

with

. By Lemma

we have

. The contraction of

, where

is the fork in

minus its branching component, is an inner morphism

, where

is a sum of four general lines. Thus

for all

by Lemmas

and

.

In case

, let

be the contraction of

, and let

be the image of

, where

is the line joining

with
p_{i}
. Then

by Lemmas

(c)
and
2.14
(c)
. Contracting the image of

we get an inner morphism

, where

is the sum of three horizontal and three vertical lines, so

by Lemmas

and

.

To prove Theorem

,

Lemma 5.16

(Moduli in Example

)
.

With the above notation, the following hold.

(a)

The set
\mathcal{R}
is represented by a
(\mathbb{Z}/2\rtimes S_{\nu})
-faithful universal family
f\colon(\mathcal{Y},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow M
, with
\dim M=\nu-3
.

(b)

Fix
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
. If the pairs
(C_{m_{j}},\{A_{i,m_{j}}\cap C_{m_{j}}\}_{i=1}^{\nu})
,
j\in\{1,2\}
are isomorphic, then
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
lie in the same orbit of the above
S_{\nu}
-action.

(c)

The set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{\ref{ex:ht=2_twisted_cha=2}}}(\nu)
has moduli dimension
\nu-3
.

(d)

Every
(Y,D_{Y})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\textnormal{\ref{ex:ht=2_twisted_cha=2}}}(\nu)
satisfies
h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=0
and
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=\nu-2
.

We will use the open part of the moduli space of genus zero curves with
\nu
marked points. It is constructed in
[
Kap93
]
as follows, cf.
[
DO88
, §III.2]
. Fix a
\nu
-tuple
\uline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu})
of points in
\mathbb{P}^{\nu-2}
in general position. Let
H\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{\nu-2}
be the union of hyperplanes spanned by
(\nu-1)
-tuples of points in
\uline{x}
, let
U
be the blowup of
\mathbb{P}^{\nu-2}
in
\uline{x}
with the proper transform of
H
removed, and let
E_{i}\subseteq U
be the exceptional divisor over
x_{i}
. There is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
u\colon U\longrightarrow M
whose fibers are proper transforms of rational normal curves passing through
\uline{x}
. For
m\in M
let
U_{m}
be the fiber over
m
, and write
\{x_{i,m}\}=E_{i}\cap U_{m}
. It is shown in
[
Kap93
, p. 243]
that for every ordered
\nu
-tuple of points
\uline{p}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the pair
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\uline{p})
is isomorphic to
(U_{m},\uline{x}_{m})
for a unique
m\in M
. The
S_{\nu}
-action on
\mathbb{P}^{\nu-2}
permuting the points in
\uline{x}
induces an
S_{\nu}
-action on
U
and
M
which makes
u
equivariant.

For any
m\in M
, the germ of
u
at
m\in M
is a semiuniversal deformation of
(U_{m},Z_{m})
, where
Z_{m}
is the divisor
\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}x_{i,m}
. To see this, we note first that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log Z_{m}))=\nu-3
, so it is enough to show that
u
is versal at
m
. For any infinitesimal deformation
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z})\longrightarrow T
of
(U_{m},Z_{m})
we perform the above construction in a parametric way. More precisely, we argue as follows. Since
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is rigid, we have
\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times T
, and
\mathcal{Z}
consists of
\nu
sections of the second projection. Let
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times T\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{\nu-2}\times T
be a family of Veronese embeddings, mapping
\mathcal{Z}
to
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu}\}\times T
such that at the central fiber over
t_{0}\in T
, the point
(x_{i,m},t_{0})\in U_{m}\times\{t_{0}\}
is mapped to
(x_{i},t_{0})
. This embedding lifts to an embedding
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times T\longrightarrow U\times T
, whose image lies in a neighborhood of
U_{m}\times T
. Composing it with the first projection and with the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
u\colon U\longrightarrow M
we get a morphism
\alpha\colon T\longrightarrow U
such that our deformation is a pullback of
u
along
\alpha
, as needed.

We go back to the proof of the lemma. Let
\overline{\Gamma}^{\circ}
be a graph consisting of
\nu
vertices of weight
0
and no edges; let
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}=(4,\overline{\Gamma}^{\circ})
and let
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{\circ}
be a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ})
consisting of those triples whose underlying surface is
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In other words,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{\circ}
is the set of isomorphism classes of
\nu
ordered vertical lines on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}=U\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=E_{i}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, and let
\overline{f}\colon\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow M
be the composition of the first projection and the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
u
. Then
\overline{f}\colon(\overline{\mathcal{Y}},\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ})\longrightarrow M
is a universal,
S_{\nu}
-faithful family representing
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{\circ}
.

We now introduce a hypersurface
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
corresponding to the family of conics, as follows. Write
\{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\}=\{0,1,\infty\}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\coloneqq U\times\{z_{i}\}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. For
m\in M
, let
\Delta_{m}\subseteq\overline{Y}_{m}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the unique curve of type
(1,1)
passing through
\overline{V}_{i,m}\cap\overline{H}_{i,m}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. We claim that there is a hypersurface
\Delta
in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
such that
\overline{f}|_{\Delta}
is a smooth family with fibers
\Delta_{m}
. If
\nu=3
then the claim is clear. Otherwise let
\eta\colon\mathbb{P}^{\nu-2}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the linear projection from the hyperplane spanned by
x_{4},\dots,x_{\nu}
, and choose the coordinates on the target
\mathbb{P}^{1}
so that for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
we have
\eta(x_{i})=z_{i}
. Then
\eta
extends to a morphism
U\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
whose graph is the required hypersurface
\Delta
. Let now
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq(\mathrm{id}_{U},s)^{-1}(\Delta)
, where
s\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is given by
s(z)=z^{2}
. Write
\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. The restriction
\overline{f}|_{\overline{\mathcal{C}}}
is a smooth morphism whose fiber
\overline{C}_{m}
is a curve of type
(2,1)
on
\overline{Y}_{m}
, and since
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, it is tangent to
\overline{V}_{i,m}
at
\{q_{i,m}\}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}_{i}\cap\overline{Y}_{m}
. For
i\in\{1,2,3\}
we have
s(z_{i})=z_{i}
, so
\{q_{i,m}\}=\overline{V}_{i,m}\cap\overline{H}_{i,m}
. Note that these properties determine the curve
\overline{C}_{m}\subseteq\overline{Y}_{m}
uniquely; and for every curve
\overline{C}_{m}^{\prime}
of type
(2,1)
on
\overline{Y}_{m}
which is tangent to every vertical line there is an automorphism of
\overline{Y}_{m}
mapping
\overline{C}^{\prime}_{m}
to
\overline{C}_{m}
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{D}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}+\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. For every
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
blow up at
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
and at the intersection of the exceptional divisor with the proper transform of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, and denote the resulting morphism by
\Phi\colon\mathcal{Y}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
. Let
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\Phi^{-1}_{*}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
,
\mathcal{H}_{i}=\Phi^{-1}_{*}\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}=\Phi^{-1}_{*}\overline{\mathcal{C}}
; let
\mathcal{W}_{i}
,
\mathcal{A}_{i}
be the first and second exceptional divisor over
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, and let
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}=(\Phi^{*}\overline{\mathcal{D}})_{\mathrm{red}}
,
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}=(\Phi^{*}\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Let
f\coloneqq\overline{f}\circ\Phi\colon(\mathcal{Y},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow M
and let
f^{\circ}
be the restriction of
f
to
(\mathcal{Y},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ})
. By the definition of
\mathcal{R}
every log surface
(X,\widecheck{D})
in
\mathcal{R}
has a morphism
\varphi
mapping
(X,\widecheck{D}-C)
to a fiber
(\overline{Y}_{m},\overline{D}^{\circ}_{m})
of
\overline{f}^{\circ}
, and the above uniqueness property of
\overline{C}_{m}
shows that
\varphi(C)=\overline{C}_{m}
. Hence
f
is a family representing
\mathcal{R}
. It is
S_{\nu}
-faithful since
\overline{f}
is.

We now claim that
f
has property
(b)
. To this end, assume that for two fibers
(Y_{m_{j}},\widecheck{D}_{m_{j}})
,
j=1,2
there is an isomorphism
\tau\colon C_{m_{1}}\longrightarrow C_{m_{2}}
preserving the distinguished
\nu
-tuples. Applying the
S_{\nu}
-action we can assume that
\tau(A_{i,m_{1}}\cap C_{m_{1}})=A_{i,m_{2}}\cap C_{m_{2}}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
. Hence
\tau
descends to an isomorphism
\overline{C}_{m_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{C}_{m_{2}}
mapping
q_{i,m_{1}}
to
q_{i,m_{2}}
. This in turn yields an isomorphism
\Delta_{m_{1}}\longrightarrow\Delta_{m_{2}}
which after projecting to the
U
-coordinate yields an isomorphism
U_{m_{1}}\longrightarrow U_{m_{2}}
, mapping
\uline{x}_{m_{1}}
to
\uline{x}_{m_{2}}
. Hence
m_{1}=m_{2}
, which proves
(b)
.

To prove that
f
is
(\mathbb{Z}/2\rtimes S_{\nu})
-faithful, we need to construct an involution of
(\mathcal{Y},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})
which maps fibers of
f
to fibers of
f
, and on every such fiber it maps the
(-2)
-tips
V_{1,m},V_{2,m}
to
W_{1,m},W_{2,m}
and fixes the remaining components of
\widecheck{D}_{m}
. To this end, let
\Phi^{\prime}\colon(\mathcal{Y},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime},\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
be the contraction of
\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\mathcal{A}_{i}+\mathcal{V}_{i})+\sum_{i\geqslant 2}^{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{i}+\mathcal{W}_{i})
.
Then for each
m\in M
, the weighted graphs of
\overline{D}_{m}^{\prime}
and
\overline{D}_{m}
are the same, in particular
\overline{Y}_{m}^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
(\overline{Y}_{m}^{\prime},\overline{D}_{m}^{\prime})
can be identified with some fiber
(\overline{Y}_{m^{\prime}},\overline{D}_{m^{\prime}})
of
\overline{f}
. The curves
\Phi^{\prime}(C_{m})
and
\overline{C}_{m}^{\prime}
with distinguished
\nu
-tuples of points are isomorphic, so using property
(b)
we can assume that
m=m^{\prime}
. This way, we can identify the family
(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime},\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
with
(\overline{\mathcal{Y}},\overline{\mathcal{D}})
. The birational map
\Phi^{-1}\circ\Phi^{\prime}
extends to the required involution.

To end the proof of
(a)
we need to check that
f
is semiuniversal at every
m\in M
. Let
h\colon(\mathcal{Y}_{T},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}_{T})\longrightarrow T
be a small deformation of
(Y_{m},\widecheck{D}_{m})
, let
\mathcal{C}_{T}
be the component of
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
corresponding to the vertex
c
, and let
\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}_{T}=\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}_{T}-\mathcal{C}_{T}
. As in the proof of Lemma
2.18
, we have a blowdown
\Phi_{T}\colon(\mathcal{Y}_{T},\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}_{T}^{\circ})\longrightarrow(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{T},\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}_{T})
to a small deformation
\overline{h}^{\circ}\colon(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{T},\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}_{T})\longrightarrow T
of
(\overline{Y}_{m},\overline{D}_{m}^{\circ})
. Since
\overline{f}^{\circ}
is semiuniversal at
m
, we get a morphism
\alpha\colon T\longrightarrow M
such that
\overline{h}^{\circ}=\alpha^{*}\overline{f}^{\circ}
, with differential uniquely determined by
h
. The image of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
on
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{T}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}\times_{T}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}
is a curve of type
(2,1)
tangent to every vertical line, so composing
\Phi_{T}
with an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}\times_{T}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}
we can further assume that
\Phi_{T}(\mathcal{C}_{T})=\alpha^{*}\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. By the universal property of blowing up we obtain that
h=\alpha^{*}f
, as required.

We have thus proved
(a)
and
(b)
. Now we prove
(c)
.
Recall that the image of
\mathcal{R}
under the forgetful map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
equals
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\textnormal{\ref{ex:ht=2_twisted_cha=2}}}(\nu)
, so the restriction of
f
to
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{D}_{Y})
, where
\mathcal{D}_{Y}=\widecheck{\mathcal{D}}-\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, represents
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\textnormal{\ref{ex:ht=2_twisted_cha=2}}}(\nu)
.

We need to show that this restriction, call it
f_{Y}
, is almost universal. The fibers of
f_{Y}
differ from those of
f
by removing some
(-1)
-curves from the boundary. Since
f
is semiuniversal at each
m\in M
, by
[
FZ94
, Proposition 1.7(1)]
so is
f_{Y}
, cf. Lemma
2.14
(c)
. By Definitions
1.9
(d)
and
1.10
(b)
it remains to prove that whenever two fibers of
f_{Y}
are isomorphic (as log surfaces), they are equivalent under the
S_{\nu}
-action constructed above.

If
\nu=3
then
M
has dimension
0
, so
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\textnormal{\ref{ex:ht=2_twisted_cha=2}}}(\nu)=1
and there is nothing to prove. Assume
\nu\geqslant 4
, and let
\tau\colon(X_{m_{1}},D_{Y,m_{1}})\longrightarrow(X_{m_{2}},D_{Y,m_{2}})
be an isomorphism between fibers of
f_{Y}
. The horizontal part of
D_{Y,m_{i}}
, namely the curve
C_{m_{i}}
, is the unique
(4-2\nu)
-curve in
D_{Y,m_{i}}
, hence
\tau(C_{m,1})=C_{m,2}
. Lemma
2.21
implies that
\tau(A_{i,m_{1}})=A_{\sigma(i),m_{2}}
for some
\sigma\in S_{\nu}
. The claim now follows from property
(b)
.

It remains to prove
(d)
. Fix
(Y,D_{Y})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\textnormal{\ref{ex:ht=2_twisted_cha=2}}}(\nu)
. Part
(c)
implies that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}))=\nu-3
. The normal bundle
\mathcal{N}_{C}
to
C=[2\nu-4]
in
Y
satisfies
h^{i}(\mathcal{N}_{C})=h^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(4-2\nu))=2\nu-5
for
i=1
and
0
otherwise. Hence by Lemma
2.14
(a)
it is enough to show that
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log(D_{Y}-C)))
for
i=0,1,2
equals
0,2\nu-6,0
, respectively.

Put
B_{Y}=D_{Y}-C+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}(A_{i}+H_{i})
, where
H_{i}
is the proper transform of the horizontal curve passing through
\{q_{i}\}=C\cap V_{i}
. Since
A_{i}
and
H_{i}
are
(-1)
-curves, by Lemma
2.14
(c)

h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log D_{Y}-C))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log B_{Y}))
. By Lemma
2.11
(c)
,
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}(-\log B_{Y}))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log B))
, where
B\coloneqq\varphi_{*}B_{Y}
consists of
\nu
horizontal and
\nu
vertical lines. We compute directly that
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log B))
equals
0,2\nu-6,0
for
i=0,1,2
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 5.17

(Classification in case
5.13
(c)
,
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, see Table
12
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be as in (
18
), i.e.
(X,D)
is a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one, height
2
and width
1
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
as in Lemma
5.13
, and assume that
\overline{X}
swaps vertically to a surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
5.5
for some
\nu\geqslant 3
.

Assume
\overline{X}
is log canonical. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the singularity type of
\overline{X}
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
be the set of isomorphism classes of surfaces
\overline{X}
of type
\mathcal{S}
and above properties. Let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
be the combinatorial type of the sum of
D
and all vertical
(-1)
-curves. Then
h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
,
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=\nu-2
, and one of the following holds.

(a)

The set
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
\nu-3
, and
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
is one of the following (see Notations
2.22
,
5.14
):

(1)

\prescript{|1,\dots,\nu|}{}{[}\boldsymbol{k_{1}^{\geqslant}-4}]+\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(2)

\prescript{|2,\dots,\nu|}{}{[}\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}-2},l^{|1|},2]+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{l-2},3]+\sum_{j=2}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(3)

{}^{|2,\dots,\nu|}[\boldsymbol{k_{1}^{\geqslant}-3},T]^{|1|}+\prescript{|1|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T^{*}*[(2)_{k_{1}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=2}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(4)

[2,l_{1}^{|1|},\prescript{|3,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{3}^{\geqslant}},l_{2}^{|2|},2]+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{l_{1}-2},3]+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{l_{2}-2},3]+\sum_{j=3}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(5)

[2,l^{|1|},\prescript{|3,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}-1},T]^{|2|}+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{l-2},3]+\prescript{|2|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T^{*}*[(2)_{k_{2}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=3}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(6)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[}T_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},\prescript{|3,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{1}^{\geqslant}-2},T_{2}]^{|2|}+\prescript{|1|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T_{1}^{*}*[(2)_{k_{1}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+\prescript{|2|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T_{2}^{*}*[(2)_{k_{2}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=3}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(7)

\langle\prescript{|4,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{3}^{\geqslant}+1};[2,2]^{|1|},[2,2]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|3|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[3,(2)_{k_{3}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+[3]^{|1|}+[3]^{|2|}+\sum_{j=4}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(8)

\langle\prescript{|4,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{3}^{\geqslant}+1};[2,3]^{|1|},[2,2]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|3|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[3,(2)_{k_{3}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+[3,2]^{|1|}+[3]^{|2|}+\sum_{j=4}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(9)

\langle\prescript{|4,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}};[2,l]^{|1|},[2]^{|2|},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\sum_{j=2}^{3}\prescript{|j|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[3,(2)_{k_{j}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+\prescript{|1|}{}{[}(2)_{l-2},3]+\sum_{j=4}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(10)

\langle\prescript{|4,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}};[2,3]^{|1|},\prescript{|2|}{}{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|2|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T^{*}*[(2)_{k_{2}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+[3,2]^{|1|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[3,(2)_{k_{3}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=4}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,
d(T)=3
,

(11)

\langle\prescript{|4,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}};[2,2]^{|1|},\prescript{|2|}{}{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\prescript{|2|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T^{*}*[(2)_{k_{2}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+[3]^{|1|}+\prescript{|3|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[3,(2)_{k_{3}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=4}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,
d(T)\in\{3,4,5\}
,

(12)

\langle\prescript{|4,\dots,\nu|}{}{\,}\!\boldsymbol{k_{1}^{\geqslant}-1};\prescript{|1|}{}{T}_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},\prescript{|2|}{}{T}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[2]^{|3|}\rangle+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\!\!\prescript{|j|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T_{j}^{*}*[(2)_{k_{j}-1}]\rangle\!\rangle+\prescript{|3|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle[3,(2)_{k_{3}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=4}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,
\frac{1}{d(T_{1})}+\frac{1}{d(T_{2})}>\tfrac{1}{2}
,

(13)

\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[2],[\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}-2}]^{|2,\dots,\nu|}\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]*[2]^{|1|}+\sum_{j=2}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(14)

\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[2],[2,l^{|2|},\boldsymbol{k_{3}^{\geqslant}}]^{|3,\dots,\nu|}\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]*[2]^{|1|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{[}(2)_{l-2},3]+\sum_{j=3}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(15)

\langle k;[2]^{|1|},[2],\prescript{|2|}{}{[}T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},\boldsymbol{k_{2}^{\geqslant}-1}]^{|3,\dots,\nu|}\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]*[2]^{|1|}+\prescript{|2|}{}{\!}\langle\!\langle T^{*}*[(2)_{k_{2}-2}]\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=3}^{\nu}\mathrm{D}_{k_{j}}^{|j|}
,

(16)

\langle k;[2],\prescript{|1|}{}{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},[\boldsymbol{6}]^{|2,3,4,5|}\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]*(T^{*})^{|1|}+\sum_{j=2}^{5}([2]^{|j|}+[2]^{|j|})
,
d(T)=3
,
k\geqslant 3
,

(17)

,

; here

Thus we got the list of singularity types

. The equalities

and

. It remains to construct the almost universal families representing each

.

We argue as in the proof of Corollary
4.8
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
be the set of minimal log resolutions of surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
. For
(X,D)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
let
\widecheck{D}
be the sum of
D
and the vertical
(-1)
-curves found above, so
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
is the combinatorial type of
(X,\widecheck{D})
. As usual, since type
\mathcal{S}
appears exactly once on our list, it determines
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
uniquely, i.e.
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
is the image of
\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
. Let
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
be the morphism onto the surface from Example
5.5
. As in Figure
20(c)
we denote by
L_{j}
the proper transform of
{l}_{j}
, and by
A_{j}
the
(-1)
-curve over
{l}_{j}\cap{c}
. Let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
be the combinatorial type of
(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
, and let
\mathcal{R}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}_{0})
be the subset from Lemma
5.16
(a)
, distinguished by choosing a
(-2)
-tip of each degenerate fiber in
\widecheck{D}_{Y}
. By Lemma
5.16
,
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is represented by a universal
(\mathbb{Z}/2\rtimes S_{\nu})
-faithful family over a base

of dimension
\nu-3
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be the preimage of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
, and let

be the maximal subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}/2\rtimes S_{\nu}
such that
\psi
is
G
-invariant.

Write
\psi=\psi_{\textnormal{out}}\circ\psi_{\textnormal{in}}
, where
\psi_{\textnormal{in}}
is a vertical inner minimalization. In case
(a)
we have
\psi_{\textnormal{out}}=\mathrm{id}
, so by Lemma

\mathcal{R}
is represented by a universal

-faithful family over the same base

as

. Consider case

. Then
\psi_{\textnormal{out}}
is a single outer blowup on
A_{1}^{\circ}\coloneqq A_{1}\setminus(\widecheck{D}_{Y}-A_{1})
. We note that the only elements of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
which fix

are the identity and the involution switching the two points

, which is the restriction of the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action from Lemma

. Thus by Lemmas
2.18
and
2.19
(1)

\mathcal{R}
is represented by a universal
G
-faithful family
f
whose base

admits a fibration

whose fibers are identifies with curves

as above.

Thus we get a universal
G
-faithful family representing
\mathcal{R}
, of dimension
\nu-3
in case
(a)
and
\nu-2
in case
(b)
.

Claim
.

The restriction of the forgetful map
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
to
\mathcal{R}
is surjective.

For
(X,\widecheck{D})\in\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
we need to find
(X,\widecheck{\mathcal{D}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
such that the triple
(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y},\gamma_{0})
, with
\gamma_{0}
induced from
\gamma
by the morphism
\psi
, is equivalent to some
(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y},\widetilde{\gamma}_{0})\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, see Section
2F
for definitions.

Let
V_{j}^{1},V_{j}^{2}
be the two tips of multiplicity one in the fiber
F_{j}
. The isomorphism
\gamma
is unique up to reordering those fibers
F_{j}
which have the same weighted graph, and reordering
\{V_{j}^{1},V_{j}^{2}\}
whenever the weighted graph of
F_{j}
has an involution interchanging the corresponding vertices. In the latter case, call
F_{j}

symmetric
. We check directly that there is at least one symmetric fiber.

We need to show that, after possibly replacing
V_{j}^{1}
with
V_{j}^{2}
in some symmetric fibers, there is
\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(Y,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
such that
\psi_{*}V_{j}^{1}=\alpha(L_{j})
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
.
Let
J_{\alpha}^{i}=\{j:\psi_{*}V_{j}^{i}=\alpha(L_{j})\}
, clearly
J_{\alpha}^{1}\sqcup J_{\alpha}^{2}=\{1,\dots,\nu\}
. Applying
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action from Lemma
5.16
we see that for every pair of distinct indices
j,j^{\prime}
we can change
\alpha
and move both
j
and
j^{\prime}
between
J_{\alpha}^{2}
and
J_{\alpha}^{1}
. Thus we can choose
\alpha
so that either
J_{\alpha}^{2}=\emptyset
, as needed, or
J_{\alpha}^{2}=\{j\}
and
F_{j}
is symmetric, in which case we are done after replacing
V_{j}^{1}
with
V_{j}^{2}
.

∎

The above Claim implies that

f
represents
\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
. Recall that the image of
\mathcal{P}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
equals
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
, so
f
restricts to a family representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
. If
d=0
this means that
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})=1
, as needed, so we can assume
d\geqslant 1
. We need to prove that
f
, viewed as a family representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
, is almost faithful.

Let
(X_{i},\widecheck{D}_{i},\gamma_{i})\in\mathcal{R}
be a fiber of
f
over some
b_{i}\in B
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
E_{i}
be the sum of vertical
(-1)
-curves in
\widecheck{D}_{i}
, let
D_{i}=\widecheck{D}_{i}-E_{i}
,
H_{i}=(D_{i})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, let
F_{j,i}
be the fibers in
\widecheck{D}_{i}
, ordered by
\gamma_{i}
; and let
F_{i}\subseteq X_{i}
be the general fiber. Fix an isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
.

Case 1:

\varphi(H_{1})=H_{2}
.
We argue as in the last part of the proof of Lemma
5.16
. By Lemma
2.21
we have
\varphi(\widecheck{D}_{1})=\widecheck{D}_{2}
. Replacing
b_{2}
by another element in its
G
-orbit we can reorder the fibers
F_{2,j}
in such a way that
\varphi(H_{1}\cap F_{1,j})=H_{2}\cap F_{2,j}
. Now
\varphi
descends to an isomorphism between fibers of
f_{0}
which maps the image of
H_{1}
to the image of
H_{2}
, and preserves their
\nu
marked points. The property of
f_{0}
stated in Lemma
5.16
shows that the images of
b_{1},b_{2}
in
M
are the same. In case
(a)
we have
B=M
, so the result follows. In case
(b)
we get that
b_{1},b_{2}
lie in the same fiber of
B\longrightarrow M
, see Lemma
2.19
(1)
. This fiber is identified with
A_{1}^{\circ}
, and this identification maps
b_{i}
to the cross-ratio of the four common points of the proper transform of
A_{1}
with the remaining part of
\widecheck{D}_{i}
. These points are ordered by
\gamma^{i}
, and lie, respectively: on
H_{i}
, on the twig with a
(-1)
-curve, and on two twigs
[2]
which are interchanged by an involution in
G
coming from the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action in Lemma
5.16
. Thus the cross-ratios
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
lie in the same orbit of the
G
-action, as needed.

Case 2:

\varphi(H_{1})\neq H_{2}
.
Now
\varphi(H_{1})
is vertical, so
\varphi(F_{1})
is horizontal. Since
\varphi(H_{1})
is vertical, we have
\varphi(F_{1})\cdot F_{2}\geqslant\varphi(F_{1})\cdot\varphi(H_{1})=F_{1}\cdot H_{1}=2
. For every component
C
of
D_{2}-H_{2}-\varphi(H_{1})
we have
F_{2}\cdot C=0=\varphi(F_{1})\cdot C
because
\varphi^{-1}(C)
is vertical. Since
K_{X_{2}}
and components of
D_{2}
generate the group
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X_{2})
, we conclude that
\varphi(F_{1})-F_{2}\equiv a\cdot(H_{2}-H_{1})
for some
a\in\mathbb{Q}
. Intersecting the above numerical equivalence with
F_{2}
and
\varphi(H_{1})
we get
2\leqslant\varphi(F_{1})\cdot F_{2}=2a
, so
a\geqslant 1
, and
2=a(H_{2}\cdot\varphi(H_{1})-H_{1}^{2})\geqslant H_{2}\cdot\varphi(H_{1})-H_{1}^{2}\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}k_{j}-4\geqslant 2\nu-4
by Lemma
5.12
(f)
. Thus
\nu=3
(so we are in case
(b)
by assumption
d\geqslant 1
),
k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}=2
; and the weighted graph of
D_{1}
has an automorphism which does not preserve
H_{1}
.

We check directly that these conditions can hold only in cases
(24)
and
(27)
.
In these cases, the morphism
\psi\colon(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(X,\widecheck{D}_{Y})
has only one exceptional
(-1)
-curve, call it
U
.

Recall that, since we are in case
(b)
and
\nu=3
, the base
B
of our family
f
is the curve
A_{1}^{\circ}=A_{1}\setminus D_{Y}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{**}
, see Figure
20(c)
.
Put
\widehat{D}=D+U
,
\widehat{D}_{Y}=\psi_{*}\widehat{D}=D_{Y}+A_{1}
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
be the combinatorial types of
(X,\widehat{D})
and
(Y,\widehat{D}_{Y})
; and let
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
be the images of
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
and
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{0})
, respectively. Case
\nu=3
of Lemma
5.16
, or the proof above, shows that
\#\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}=1
.
The restriction of
\operatorname{Aut}(Y,\widehat{D}_{Y})
to
\operatorname{Aut}(A_{1}^{\circ})\cong S_{3}
is surjective, see
[
PP24
, Lemma 8.2(e)]
. Applying Lemma
2.19
(1)
to a family representing
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
(whose base is a point), we get an
S_{3}
-faithful family over
A_{1}^{\circ}
representing
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
(which is in fact the restriction of
f
).

Let
(X_{i},\widehat{D}_{i})
be its fiber over
b_{i}\in A_{1}^{\circ}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
U_{i}
be the
(-1)
-curve in
\widehat{D}_{i}
, let
D_{i}=\widehat{D}_{i}-U_{i}
, and as before let
A_{1,i}
be the proper transform of
A_{1}
in
\widehat{D}_{i}
. The point
b_{i}
is determined by the cross-ratio of the quadruple
A_{1,i}\cap(\widehat{D}_{i}-A_{1,i})
. Since
A_{1,i}
is the unique component of
\widehat{D}_{i}
of branching number
4
, any isomorphism
(X_{1},\widehat{D}_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{1},\widehat{D}_{2})
maps
A_{1,1}
to
A_{1,2}
, so the corresponding cross-ratios are equivalent by the
S_{3}
-action. Hence our
S_{3}
-faithful family representing
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
is an almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}(\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
.

To prove that it is almost faithful as a family representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
, which is the image of
\mathcal{P}(\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, we need to prove that every isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
satisfies
\varphi(U_{1})=U_{2}
.

Let
R_{2}
be the connected component of
D_{2}
containing
H_{2}
. The
(-1)
-curve
\varphi(U_{1})
meets
D_{2}
only in
R_{2}
and in the last tip of a connected component
T_{2}
of
D_{2}
, where
T_{2}=[(2)_{k-2}]
in case
(24)
and
T_{2}=[(2)_{k-3},3]
in case
(27)
. The fork
T_{2}+\varphi(U_{1})+(R_{2}-H_{2})
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X_{2}
, such that
(D_{2})_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists only of the
2
-section
H_{2}
. Since the components of
D_{2}
and
K_{X_{2}}
generate
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X_{2})
, we conclude that this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X_{2}
is the same as the original one (up to an automorphism of the base). Since
U_{2}
is the unique vertical
(-1)
-curve which is disjoint from
D_{2}-R_{2}-T_{2}
, we have
U_{2}=\varphi(U_{1})
, as claimed.
∎

The proof of Theorem
A
and Propositions
C
,
D
is now complete. For the readers’ convenience, we gather here the references to lemmas covering each case of those results.

The assertions in Theorem
A
about vertical swaps are proved in Lemma
4.1
(d)
in case
0pt=1
, Lemma
5.9
in case
0pt=2
,
0pt=2
and Lemma
5.13
in case
0pt=2
,
0pt=1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a log canonical singularity type. If
\mathcal{S}
is non-rational it is a type of an elliptic cone. In this case, by Proposition
B
every surface in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
is of height one, hence by Lemma
4.1
(d)
swaps vertically to an elliptic cone and so, in fact, is an elliptic cone, as claimed. Assume that
\mathcal{S}
is rational. Let
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
,
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{0pt=2}(\mathcal{S})
,
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{sep}}(\mathcal{S})
and
\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
be the subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
described in Lemmas
4.4
,
5.10
,
5.15
and
5.17
, respectively; so
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
is their union. We check directly that the lists of singularity types in those lemmas are disjoint, so for each
\mathcal{S}
exactly one of the above sets is nonempty, and therefore equal to
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
.

In case
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{P}_{0pt=1}(\mathcal{S})
, the list of possible types
\mathcal{S}
is given in Lemma
4.4
, and the required properties of
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
– namely the number
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
and the existence of representing families – are proved in Corollary
4.8
. In the remaining cases they are proved in Lemmas
5.10
,
5.15
and
5.17
, respectively.

The numbers
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
for minimal log resolutions
(X,D)
of surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
are computed in the quoted results, too. By Lemma
2.14
(g)
the numbers
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
are zero in all cases except when
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{P}_{0pt=2}^{\textnormal{insep}}(\mathcal{S})
: in the latter case, they are computed in Lemma
5.17
.

∎

Remark 5.18

(Bases and symmetry groups of almost faithful families in case
0pt=2
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a singularity type of a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one and height
2
such that
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
is infinite. Then Lemmas
5.10
,
5.15
,
5.17
imply that
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
\nu-3+\varepsilon
, where
\nu\geqslant 3
is the number of degenerate fibers of the witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration constructed in the above lemmas; we put
\varepsilon=1
in case
5.17
(b)
and
\varepsilon=0
otherwise. Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be the almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
constructed above. We now discuss its base
B
and symmetry group
G\leqslant\operatorname{Aut}(B)
.

In each case, the family
f
is constructed from a family
U_{\nu}\longrightarrow M_{\nu}
representing the combinatorial type of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
with
\nu
fibers, see Example
2.16
for the case
\nu=4
. The base
M_{\nu}
is the (open part of the) moduli space of genus
0
curves with
\nu
marked points: explicitly,
M_{\nu}
can be realized as the complement in
\mathbb{P}^{\nu-3}
of the union of hyperplanes spanned by all
(\nu-2)
-tuples of fixed
(\nu-3)
points in a general position, see
[
Kap93
]
for details. The symmetry group
H_{\nu}
of the family
U_{\nu}\longrightarrow M_{\nu}
is the image in
\operatorname{Aut}(M_{\nu})
of the group
S_{\nu}
permuting the fibers: we have
H_{\nu}\cong S_{\nu}
if
\nu\geqslant 5
;
H_{4}\cong S_{3}
,
M_{4}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, cf. Example
2.17
; and
H_{3}=\{\mathrm{id}\}
,
M_{3}=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
.

In case
\varepsilon=0
the base
B
of
f
is still
M_{\nu}
, and its symmetry group
G
is the image in
H_{\nu}
of the product
\prod_{i}S_{k_{i}}\leqslant S_{\nu}
, where each factor permutes degenerate fibers of the same type (i.e. such that there is an automorphism of
\mathcal{S}
mapping one fiber to another). For instance, assume
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
. Then
\mathcal{S}
is as in Lemma
5.10
(e)
, so
\varepsilon=0
,
\nu=4
. Thus in this case
B=M_{4}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, and
G
is isomorphic to
S_{3}
in cases
5.10
(30)
and
5.10
(29)
with
T_{1}=T_{2}=[2]
,
\mathbb{Z}/2
in case
5.10
(29)
with
T_{1}=T_{2}\neq[2]
or
T_{1}\neq T_{2}=[2]
, and trivial otherwise.

Assume
\varepsilon=1
. Then
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
swaps vertically to the surface
\overline{Y}
from Example
5.5
, and this vertical swap factors through one outer blowup, centered at a point
p
on a vertical
(-1)
-curve, say
p\in A_{1}^{\circ}\coloneqq A_{1}\setminus D_{Y}\cong M_{4}
, see Figure
20(c)
. If
\mathcal{S}=4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
then
\nu=3
,
B=A_{1}^{\circ}
and
G=\operatorname{Aut}(A_{1}^{\circ})\cong S_{3}
, see Example
5.6
(c)
. In all other cases we have
B=M_{\nu}\times A_{1}^{\circ}
and
G\cong\Sigma\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. The first factor
\Sigma\leqslant S_{\nu}
is a product of symmetric groups, acting on
M_{\nu}
as in case
\varepsilon=0
: note that
\Sigma
fixes the first degenerate fiber, so in fact
\Sigma\leqslant S_{\nu-1}
. The second factor
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts on
A_{1}^{\circ}
as the automorphism of
(Y,D_{Y})
switching the two points
A_{1}\cap(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}
, cf. Figure
22
.

6.
Del Pezzo surfaces admitting descendants with elliptic boundary

In this section we prove Theorem
E
, that is, we describe log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height at least
3
admitting descendants with elliptic boundary. Recall from Definition
1.11
that a normal projective surface
\overline{X}

has a descendant with elliptic boundary
if there is a morphism
\varphi
from the minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}
onto a log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
where
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a curve of arithmetic genus one. If
\overline{X}
is a del Pezzo surface of rank one, further properties of
\varphi
are stated in Lemma
1.12
. We summarize these properties, and subsequent Notation
6.1
, in the following diagram.

(19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,D_{Y}+D_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,\sigma_{*}D_{Y}+\sigma_{*}T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{Y},\overline{T})}
del Pezzo of rank 1
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle{\overline{Y}\ -\ \textnormal{canonical del Pezzo of rank 1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle{\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}},\ p_{a}(\overline{T})=1,\ \overline{T}\in|-K_{\overline{Y}}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Exc}\sigma=D_{T}+L-T,\ L=[1],\ L\not\subseteq D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}T\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}\\
D_{T}\coloneqq(\varphi^{*}\overline{T})_{\mathrm{red}}-L\end{subarray}}
min. res.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}\textnormal{min. res. of }\overline{Y}\\[2.45836pt]
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha\ =\ \sigma_{*}D_{Y}\end{subarray}}

Assume that
\overline{T}
is smooth and
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo. Then
T
is a component of
D
of genus
1
, so by Proposition
B
we have
0pt(\overline{X})=1
. Thus
X
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
X\longrightarrow B
such that
T
is a
1
-section, so
B\cong T
is a curve of genus
1
. If all fibers are non-degenerate then
\overline{X}
is an elliptic cone, see Example
4.2
, as needed. Suppose there is a degenerate fiber
F
. Then by Lemma
4.1
(a)
,
(b)

F
meets
T
in a connected component
C
of
D_{\mathrm{vert}}
which contains no
(-1)
-curves, hence does not blow down to a smooth point. However,
\varphi(C)
is a point of
\overline{T}
, hence a smooth point of
\overline{Y}
. Thus
\varphi^{-1}(\varphi(C))
contains a horizontal
(-1)
-curve. This is a contradiction, as the genus of each horizontal curve equals at least the genus of
B
, which is
1
.

Assume now that
\overline{T}
has a singular point
q
. Since
T
is smooth,
q
is a base point of
\varphi^{-1}
, and since
q
is a smooth point of
\overline{Y}
, its preimage contains a
(-1)
-curve
L
. Since
D
contains no
(-1)
-curves,
L
is a component of
L^{\prime}\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi-(D-T)
. We have
\#L^{\prime}=(\rho(X)-\rho(\overline{Y}))-(\#D-1)=\rho(\overline{X})-\rho(\overline{Y})+1=2-\rho(\overline{Y})\leqslant 1
, so we get
L^{\prime}=L
, which proves
(b)
, and
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, which together with
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.11(b)]
proves
(a)
.

Consider the case
\overline{Y}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Part
(a)
shows that
\overline{T}
is a cubic. Part
(b)
shows that
\varphi
factors through the blowup at
q
, so the pencil of lines through
q
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D=F\cdot T=2
. Thus
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
, as claimed in
(c)
.

Consider the case
\overline{Y}\not\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Write
\varphi=\alpha\circ\sigma
, where
\alpha\colon(Y,D_{Y}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},0)
is a minimal log resolution. Fix a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
(Y,D_{Y}^{\prime})
. Let
F_{Y}
be its non-degenerate fiber which does not pass through the singular point of
T_{Y}\coloneqq\alpha^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}
. By
(a)
,
F_{Y}\cdot T_{Y}=-F_{Y}\cdot K_{Y}=2
. Part
(b)
shows that
\sigma
is an isomorphism near
F\coloneqq\sigma^{*}F_{Y}
, so
F=[0]
,
F\cdot T=2
and
F
meets
D-T
only in
D_{Y}\coloneqq\tau^{-1}_{*}D_{Y}^{\prime}
. Thus
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant F\cdot D\leqslant F_{Y}\cdot(T_{Y}+D_{Y}^{\prime})=2+0pt(\overline{Y})
. Table
15
shows that
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 4
, and
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 2
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
, which ends the proof of
(c)
.

In any case, we have constructed a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
2
-section
T
and components with
\operatorname{cf}=0
. Lemma
2.6
shows that
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo if and only if
\operatorname{cf}(T)<1
, as claimed in
(d)
.

∎

Notation 6.1
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one and singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, admitting a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary such that
\overline{T}
is singular. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D+L)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be the morphism from Definition
1.11
, where
L
is the
(-1)
-curve from Lemma
1.12
(b)
. We call the curve
L
an
elliptic tie
.

Write
\varphi=\alpha\circ\sigma
, where
\alpha\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
is the minimal resolution, see diagram (
19
). Write
D=D_{Y}+D_{T}
, where
D_{Y}=\sigma^{-1}_{*}(\operatorname{Exc}\alpha)
: note that
\sigma
is an isomorphism near
D_{Y}
. Let
T\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}\subseteq D_{T}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
and
\mathcal{T}
be the weighted graphs of
D_{Y}
and
D_{T}
, respectively. Hence
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
is the singularity type of
\overline{Y}
. Put
s\coloneqq\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}
. By Noether’s formula we have

(20)

\overline{T}^{2}=K_{Y}^{2}=10-\rho(Y)=9-s.

6A.
Singularity types

We now determine the possible singularity types
\mathcal{S}
.
Lemma
6.2
describes part
\mathcal{T}
,
obtained by blowing up over the singular point of
\overline{T}
. Assumption
s\geqslant 6
is justified by subsequent Lemma
6.3
.

Lemma 6.2

(Possible types
\mathcal{T}
)
.

Let the notation be as in
6.1
. Assume
s\geqslant 6
.
Then the following hold.

(a)

If
\overline{T}
is nodal or
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
then
\mathcal{T}
is one of the following:

(1)

[s-5]^{\langle 1,1\rangle}
if
s\geqslant 7
,

(2)

\prescript{|1|}{}{[}V,s-5]*(V^{*})^{\langle 1\rangle}
.

(b)

If
\overline{T}
is cuspidal and
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
then
\mathcal{T}
is one of the following:

(3)

[s-3]^{\langle 1\rangle}+[3]^{\langle 1\rangle}+[2]^{\langle 1\rangle}
,

(4)

[s-3,k^{\langle 1\rangle},3]+[3,(2)_{k-2}]^{\langle 1\rangle}
,

(5)

[s-3,k^{\langle 1\rangle},2]+[4,(2)_{k-2}]^{\langle 1\rangle}
,

(6)

[3,k^{\langle 1\rangle},2]+[s-2,(2)_{k-2}]^{\langle 1\rangle}
,

(7)

\langle k;[s-3],[3],V^{\langle 1\rangle}\rangle+[3,(2)_{k-2}]*(V^{*})^{\langle 1\rangle}
, where either
V=[2]
or
s=6
and
d(V)\leqslant 3
,

(8)

\langle k;\prescript{|1|}{}{V},[s-3],[2]\rangle+[4,(2)_{k-2}]*(V^{*})^{\langle 1\rangle}
, where
d(V)\leqslant 6,4,3
if
s=6,7,8
, respectively,

(9)

\langle k;\prescript{|1|}{}{V},[3],[2]\rangle+[s-2,(2)_{k-2}]*(V^{*})^{\langle 1\rangle}
, where
d(V)\leqslant 6
,

(10)

\langle k^{\langle 1\rangle};[2],[3],[s-3]\rangle+[(2)_{k-2}]^{\langle 1\rangle}
, or
\langle 2^{\langle 1\rangle};[2],[3],[s-3]\rangle
if
k=2
,

where
k\geqslant 2
,
V
is an admissible chain;
V^{*}
is its adjoint, see formula (
3
); and the symbol
*
is introduced in formula (
1
). The numbers decorated by
⟨1⟩
(respectively,
⟨1,1⟩
) refer to the components meeting the elliptic tie
L
once (resp. twice). Decoration at an end of a chain refers to the corresponding tip, cf. Notation
2.22
.

(a)
Since
\operatorname{cf}(T)<1
by Lemma
1.12
(d)
, the connected component of
D_{T}
containing
T
is not circular. It follows that
D_{T}+L
is circular, and
(a)
holds.

(b)

Let
\vartheta
be the minimal embedded resolution of the cusp of
\alpha^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}
. Then
(\vartheta^{*}\overline{T})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a fork
F
with a branching
(-1)
-curve
B
and twigs
T_{0}=[s-3]
,
T_{2}=[2]
,
T_{3}=[3]
. If
\#\mathcal{T}=3
then
\sigma=\vartheta
and
(3)
holds. Assume
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 4
, so
\sigma
is a composition of
\vartheta
with a sequence of blowups over some
p\in B
.
If all these blowups are centered on the proper transforms of
B
then
\mathcal{T}
is as in
(4)
,
(5)
,
(6)
if
p\in T_{2},T_{3},T_{0}
; respectively, or as in
(10)
if
p\in F^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Assume now that one of these blowups is not centered on a proper transform of
B
. If
p\in F^{\mathrm{reg}}
then the proper transform
B^{\prime}
of
B
satisfies
\beta_{D_{T}}(B^{\prime})\geqslant 4
and meets a
(-3)
-curve, which is impossible. Hence
p\in T_{0},T_{2}
or
T_{3}
, and
\beta_{D_{T}}(B^{\prime})=3
. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}
equals
\langle k;T_{x},T_{y},V\rangle+T_{z}*[(2)_{k-1}]*V^{*}
or
\langle k;T_{x},T_{y},T_{z}*[(2)_{k-1}]*[V^{*},l,V]\rangle+[(2)_{l-2}]
, where
V
is an admissible chain,
k,l\geqslant 2
and
\{x,y,z\}=\{0,2,3\}
. In the second case, at most one twig of the fork in
D_{T}
is of type
[2]
, and the third one has length at least
3
and is not a
(-2)
-twig, contrary to Lemma
2.1
. Hence the first case holds. If
z=2
then
T_{x},T_{y}\neq[2]
, so by Lemma
2.1

V=[2]
and
\mathcal{T}
is as in
(7)
. If
z=3,0
then Lemma
2.1
shows that
\mathcal{T}
is as in
(8)
,
(9)
, respectively.

∎

We now focus on the part
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
, that is, the possible singularity types of the canonical surface
\overline{Y}
. Recall that in Theorem
E
we restrict our attention to the case
0pt\geqslant 3
, not covered by Theorem
A
.

Lemma 6.3

(
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3\implies\#\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})\geqslant 6
, and exceptions to the converse)
.

We keep Notation
6.1
. If
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
then
s\geqslant 6
. Conversely, if
s\geqslant 6
then either
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
, or one of the following holds.

(a)

\overline{X}
is canonical, of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
or
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
.

(b)

\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{E}_{s}
and
\overline{T}
is cuspidal or
\mathcal{T}=[(2)_{l},s+l-5]
for some
l\in\{0,1,2\}
.

(c)

\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6},\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5},\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{E}_{7},2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6},\mathrm{D}_{8}\}
and
\mathcal{T}=[3]
.

(d)

\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5},\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6},\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{E}_{7}\}
,
\overline{T}
is cuspidal and
\mathcal{T}=[s-3]+[3]+[2]
.

Moreover, if
\overline{X}
is of one of the above types then
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
.

By Lemma
1.12
(a)
we have
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, so
\overline{T}^{2}>0
. Suppose
\overline{T}^{2}\geqslant 4
. After
\overline{T}^{2}-3
blowups over
\operatorname{Sing}\overline{T}
, the proper transform of
\overline{T}
is a
0
-curve meeting the exceptional divisor at most twice. Its pullback on
(X,D)
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most 2. Thus if
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
then
\overline{T}^{2}\leqslant 3
, so by formula (
20
)
s=9-\overline{T}^{2}\geqslant 6
.

Assume now that
s\geqslant 6
. Recall that types
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
and
\mathcal{T}
are listed in Table
15
and Lemma
6.2
. Comparing them with Tables
9
–
12
we conclude that the inequality
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
can occur only in cases
(a)
–
(d)
above. We now construct a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
\leqslant 2
in each case.
Let
T_{Y}\in|-K_{Y}|
be the proper transform of
\overline{T}
on the minimal resolution
Y
of
\overline{Y}
. By formula (
20
) we have
T_{Y}^{2}=9-s
and by adjunction
T_{Y}\cdot A=1
for every
(-1)
-curve
A
on
Y
. It follows from Lemma
1.12
(b)
that the map
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is an isomorphism near
\sigma^{-1}_{*}A
.

(a)
Here we have
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
by
[
PP24
, Proposition 6.1]
, see Table
15
.

(b)
By
[
PP24
, Proposition 6.1]
we have
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, and
\overline{Y}
is as in Remark
4.5
, see Figure
16
. Thus
Y
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
meeting
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
once, in the last tip of its long
(-2)
-twig
G=[(2)_{s-4}]
. If
D_{T}=[(2)_{l},s+l-5]
for some
l\in\{0,1,2\}
then a subchain of
T+\sigma^{-1}_{*}(A+G)=[s+l-5,1,(2)_{s-4}]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most two, as needed. In fact, if
l=0
then
0pt(\overline{X})=1
and
\overline{X}
as in Lemma
4.4
(23)
; and if
l=1,2
then
0pt(\overline{X})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
5.10
(9)
,
(15)
, respectively.

Assume that
\overline{T}
is cuspidal. If
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
then
D_{T}
is as in the previous case, so we can assume
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
. Then
\sigma=\gamma\circ\vartheta
for some morphism
\vartheta
such that
\#\operatorname{Exc}\vartheta=3
, so
T^{\prime}\coloneqq\vartheta^{-1}_{*}T_{Y}=[s-3]
. Since
\overline{T}
is cuspidal, we have
\beta_{D}((\gamma^{*}T^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}})\leqslant 1
, cf. Lemma
6.2
(b)
. Now the pullback of
\vartheta^{-1}_{*}(T_{Y}+A+G)=[s-3,1,(2)_{s-4}]
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most two, as needed. More precisely, if
\#\mathcal{T}=3
then we get
0pt(\overline{X})=1
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
4.4
(7)
, and if
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 4
then
0pt(\overline{X})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
5.10
(15)
,
(16)
–
(20)
, or
(29)
.

(c)
Consider the case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
. Figure
21(c)
shows that
Y
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
meeting
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
once, in the maximal twig
G=[2]
. Let
B
be the branching component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. Now
T+\sigma^{-1}_{*}(A+G+B)=[3,1,2,2]
supports a fiber of a fibration of height
2
. In fact,
0pt(\overline{X})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
5.10
(24)
.

In the remaining cases, we see from Figure
21(d)
or
16
that
Y
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
meeting
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
once, in some tip of a twig
G=[2,2]
. Now
T+\sigma^{-1}_{*}(A+G)=[3,1,2,2]
supports a fiber of a fibration of height
1
or
2
. In fact, if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{E}_{7}
then
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})=1
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemmas
4.4
(9)
, otherwise
0pt(\overline{X})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
5.10
(13)
,
5.15
(4)
or
5.17
(25)
if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
equals
\mathrm{D}_{8},\mathrm{A}_{3}+\mathrm{D}_{5}
or
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6}
respectively.

(d)
Like before, we see from Figure
16
that there is a
(-1)
-curve
A\subseteq Y
such that
A+\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
contains a subchain
R=[(2)_{s-4},1]
meeting the remaining part of
A+\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
only in the second component, once. Now
\sigma^{-1}_{*}R+T=[(2)_{s-4},1,s-3]
supports a fiber of the fibration of height
2
, as needed. In fact,
0pt(\overline{X})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
5.17
(4)
,
(14)
,
(22)
for
s=6,7,8
, respectively.

∎

6B.
Computation of the height

As in the previous section, we consider a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
of rank one having a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary. The singularity type of
\overline{X}
is
\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
is the singularity type of
\overline{Y}
;
\mathcal{T}
is described as in Lemma
6.2
. Lemma
1.12
(c)
shows that
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
: in fact, pulling back a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration from the minimal log resolution of
\overline{Y}
we get a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height
0pt(\overline{Y})+2
with respect to
D
. In this section we prove Theorem
E
(f)
, which asserts that this inequality is in fact an equality, up to some minor exceptions. More precisely, we prove the following statement. We keep Notation
6.1
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Assume
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
. If
0pt(\overline{X})\neq 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
then one of the following holds.

(a)

\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5},\mathrm{A}_{8},\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}\}
and
\#\mathcal{T}=1
,

(b)

\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{3\mathrm{A}_{2},\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5},\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}\}
and
\mathcal{T}=[s-3]+[3]+[2]
. In this case, we have
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
.

Conversely, if
\overline{X}
is of type
\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}
listed above then
0pt(\overline{X})=3
and
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
.

Within the proof we keep Notation
6.1
. First, we construct the exceptional
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of height
3
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume that the singularity type
\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}
of
\overline{X}
is as in Proposition
6.4
. Then
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})+1=3
.

By
[
PP24
, Proposition 6.1]
we have
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, and the minimal resolution
Y
of
\overline{Y}
is constructed in Section
5A
, cf. Table
15
. Since
-K_{\overline{Y}}=\overline{T}
, the adjunction formula shows that each
(-1)
-curve on
Y
meets the image of
T
once, so it pulls back to a
(-1)
-curve on
X
meeting
T
once and disjoint from the elliptic tie
L
.
Lemma
6.3
gives
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
, so it is enough to find
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of height
3
in each case.

(a)

Consider the case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
. Looking at Figure
19(c)
, we see that there is a
(-1)
-curve
A_{0}
on
X
meeting
T
once and
D_{Y}
twice, once in a tip of a connected component
V=[2,2]
of
D_{Y}
, and once off
V
. Now
T+A_{0}+V=[3,1,2,2]
supports a fiber of the required
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

Consider the case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{8}
. Looking at Figure
21(b)
we see that there are disjoint
(-1)
-curves
A_{1},A_{2}
on
X
such that
A_{i}\cdot T=A_{i}\cdot D_{Y}=1
,
i=1,2
, and
A_{1},A_{2}
meet different components of
D_{Y}
. Let
V
be the component of
D_{Y}
meeting
A_{2}
, so
\beta_{D}(V)=2
. Now
A_{1}+T+A_{2}+V=[1,3,1,2]
supports a fiber of the required
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

Eventually, consider the case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{7}
. Looking at Figure
21(e)
we see that there are
(-1)
-curves
A_{1},A_{2}
on
X
such that
A_{1}\cdot T=A_{2}\cdot T=1
,
A_{1}\cdot D_{Y}=1
,
A_{2}\cdot D_{Y}=2
and
A_{2}
meets a connected component
V=[2]
of
D+A_{1}
. As before,
A_{1}+T+A_{2}+V=[1,3,1,2]
supports the required fiber.

(b)
In this case
\overline{T}
is cuspidal, so Table
15
shows that
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
. Looking at Figures
19(a)
,
21(a)
and
21(c)
we see that there is a
(-1)
-curve
A
on
X
such that
D_{Y}+A
has a subchain
V=[(2)_{s-4},1]
meeting
D_{Y}
once, in the third component. Now
V+T=[(2)_{s-4},1,s-3]
supports the fiber of the required
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.

∎

We now verify the completeness of the list in Proposition
6.4
. We treat separately the cases when the graph of
D_{T}+L
is circular (i.e.
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
or
\overline{T}
is nodal) and when it is not, which lead to
6.4
(a)
and
6.4
(b)
, respectively. In each case we argue by induction on
\#\mathcal{T}
. We begin with a basic observation in the former case.

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume that
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
or
\overline{T}
is nodal. Let
A\neq L
be a
(-1)
-curve on
X
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
-K_{X}=D_{T}+L
. In particular,
A\cdot(D_{T}+L)=1
.

(b)

If
F
is a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
then
F\cdot(D_{T}+L)=2
.

(c)

Assume
A\cdot T=1
. Then
D_{Y}+A
is not negative (semi)-definite. In particular, the connected component of
D_{Y}+A
containing
A
is neither a chain
[1,2,\dots,2]
nor a fork
\langle 2;[2],[2],[1,2,\dots,2]\rangle
.

(d)

Assume
\#\mathcal{T}=1
. If
A
meets
L
then
A\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
.

(a)
,
(b)

Pulling back the linear equivalence
-K_{\overline{Y}}=\overline{T}
we get the linear equivalence
-K_{X}=D_{T}+L
from
(a)
. The remaining part of
(a)
and
(b)
follow by the adjunction formula.

(c)
Since
\rho(\overline{Y})=1
, we have
0<\varphi(A)^{2}=(\varphi^{*}\varphi(A))^{2}
. Part
(a)
implies that
A
is disjoint from
D_{T}-T+L
, so
\varphi^{*}\varphi(A)
is supported on
D_{Y}+A
, hence the latter is not negative (semi)-definite.

(d)
We have
A\cdot D_{Y}=\sigma(A)\cdot\sigma_{*}D_{Y}
and
\sigma(A)=[0]
, so
|\sigma(A)|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
of height
A\cdot D_{Y}
with respect to
\sigma_{*}D_{Y}=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. Thus
A\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
, as needed.

∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Assume that
\#\mathcal{T}=1
,
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
and
0pt(\overline{X})\neq 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
. Then the following hold.

(a)

For every
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
of height at most
0pt(\overline{Y})+1
with respect to
D
the elliptic tie
L
is horizontal.

(b)

We have
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})=2
and
\overline{X}
is as in Proposition
6.4
(a)
or as in Lemma
6.3
.

Lemma
1.12
(c)
and the assumption
0pt(\overline{X})\neq 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
give
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
.

To prove
(a)
suppose
F\cdot L=0
. Then
F\cdot T=2
by Lemma
6.6
(b)
and
\sigma_{*}F
is a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
, so
F\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
. We get
F\cdot D=F\cdot D_{Y}+F\cdot T\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+2
, a contradiction.

It remains to prove part
(b)
. By Lemma
6.3
we can assume
F\cdot D\geqslant 0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
.
If
\overline{X}
is canonical then
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
or
0pt(\overline{X})=4=0pt(\overline{Y})+2
, see Table
15
, contrary to the assumptions. Thus
-3\geqslant T^{2}=5-\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}
by (
20
), so
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}=8
. Part
(a)
and Lemma
6.6
(b)
show that
L
is a
1
- or a
2
-section. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\nu}
be all degenerate fibers, and let

k_{i}=\#(F_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}-1
.

Consider the case
F\cdot L=1
. Then
F\cdot T=1
by Lemma
6.6
(b)
. By Lemma
6.6
(a)
, every vertical
(-1)
-curve meets
T+L
once, so it has multiplicity
1
in
F_{i}
. Lemma
2.8
(b)
implies that each
F_{i}
consists of
(-1)
- and
(-2)
-curves, so
F_{i}=[1,(2)_{k_{i}-1},1]
. Let
L_{i},T_{i}
be the tips of
F
meeting
L
and
T
, respectively, and let
G_{i}=F_{i}-L_{i}-T_{i}
. We have
L_{i}\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
and
(T_{i}+G_{i})\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 1
by Lemma
6.6
(d)
,
(c)
, so
0pt(\overline{Y})+1\geqslant F_{i}\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=L_{i}\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}+(T_{i}+G_{i})\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}+F_{i}\cdot T\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})-L_{i}\cdot G_{i}+2
. We conclude that
L_{i}\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=0pt(\overline{Y})-1
,
G_{i}\neq 0
and
(T_{i}+G_{i})\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
, so
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets
G_{i}+T_{i}
exactly once. We order the chain
F_{i}
so that
L_{i}
is its first tip. Then
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets the
(l_{i}+1)
-th component of
F_{i}
for a unique
l_{i}\in\{1,\dots,k_{i}\}
. We have
\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-1=\nu
by Lemma
2.8
(a)
. Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
L+\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}(G_{i}+T_{i})
.

Suppose
0pt(\overline{X})>3
. Then
0pt(\overline{Y})>2
, so
0pt(\overline{Y})=4
and
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{8\mathrm{A}_{1},4\mathrm{A}_{2}\}
, see Table
15
. By Remark
5.7
the surface
\overline{Y}
is primitive, so each
(-1)
-curve
T_{i}
meets
D_{Y}
at least twice. Thus
T_{i}
meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, i.e.
l_{i}=k_{i}
, and therefore
G_{i}
is a connected component of
D_{Y}
. If
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}
then
8=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\#G_{i}+\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=3\nu
, so
\nu=\frac{8}{3}
, which is impossible. Thus
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, and
8=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\#G_{i}+\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=2\nu
, so
\nu=4
. Thus
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of four
1
-sections. Each of the four curves
T_{i}
meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
exactly once, so there is a component of
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, call it
H
, which meets at most one
T_{i}
. Then
\tau(H)^{2}\leqslant-2+2=0
, a contradiction.

Hence
0pt(\overline{X})=3
. We have
\nu=\#(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\leqslant 2
. If
\nu=1
then
\tau((D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}})
is a conic, so
4=\tau((D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}})^{2}=-2+l_{1}
. Thus
l_{1}=6
, and we get
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{D}_{8}
, so Table
15
shows that
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, a contradiction. Hence
\nu=2
. Now
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=H_{1}+H_{2}
, where each
H_{i}
is a
1
-section, so
\tau(H_{i})
is a line. If
H_{1}
meets
L_{i}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
then
\tau(H_{i})^{2}=-2
, which is impossible. Thus, say,
H_{i}
meets
G_{i}+T_{i}
for
i=1,2
, and
1=\tau(H_{i})^{2}=-2+l_{i}
, so
l_{i}=3
. We have
l_{i}\leqslant k_{i}
and
k_{1}+k_{2}=\#D_{Y}=8
, so either
k_{1}=k_{2}=4
, or, say,
k_{1}=5
,
k_{2}=3
. Moreover, the map
\tau^{-1}
is an isomorphism near the common point of the lines
\tau(H_{i})
, so
H_{1}\cdot H_{2}=1
. It follows that either
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{8}
, as needed, or
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
, in which case Lemma
6.3
gives
0pt(\overline{X})=2
, a contradiction.

Consider the case
F\cdot L=2
. By Lemma
6.6
(b)

T
is vertical, so, say,
T\subseteq F_{1}
. For
i\geqslant 2
, the fiber
F_{i}
consists of
(-1)
- and
(-2)
-curves, so
(F_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a chain
[1,(2)_{k_{i}-1},1]
,
[2,1,2]
or on a fork
\langle 2;[1,(2)_{k_{i}-3}],[2],[2]\rangle
.

Since
T
meets the
2
-section
L
twice, it has multiplicity
1
in
F_{1}
. Hence there is a birational morphism
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{m}
such that
\tau(T)
is a fiber. Lemma
6.8
(a)
implies that the exceptional
(-1)
-curve of every blowup in the decomposition of
\tau
either lies in the image of
F_{1}
and is disjoint from the image of
L
, or lies in the image of
F_{i}
for some
i\geqslant 2
and meets the image of
L
once. Thus
\tau(L)
is a smooth, rational
2
-section and
\tau(L)^{2}=L^{2}-\sum_{i=2}^{\nu}k_{i}
. Numerical properties of
\mathbb{F}_{m}
imply that
\tau(L)^{2}=4
, so
\sum_{i=2}^{\nu}k_{i}=\tau(L)^{2}-L^{2}=5
.

Suppose the Lemma fails. Then
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{2\mathrm{A}_{4},4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4},4\mathrm{A}_{2},8\mathrm{A}_{1}\}
, see Table
15
. Suppose that each
F_{i}
for
i\geqslant 2
has exactly one
(-1)
-curve; in particular
k_{i}\geqslant 2
. If
\nu=2
then
k_{2}=5
, so
D_{Y}
has a subdivisor of type
\langle 2,[2],[2],[2,2]\rangle
, which is false for the above types
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
. Hence
\nu=3
and, say,
(k_{2},k_{3})=(2,3)
. Now
D_{Y}
has a subchain
[2,2,2]
, so the above list gives
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{2\mathrm{A}_{4},4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}\}
. In particular,
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, see Table
15
. By Lemma
6.6
(d)
, the
(-1)
-curve
A
in
F_{3}
satisfies
A\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, so it meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Since
A
has multiplicity
2
in
F_{3}
, and
F_{3}\cdot D\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1=3
, we see that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of a
2
-section meeting
A
, and a
1
-section
H
which meets
F_{i}
in a
(-2)
-tip for
i=2,3
. Thus
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}+H\subseteq D_{Y}
contains a subchain
[(2)_{5}]
, a contradiction.

Therefore, say,
F_{2}
has two
(-1)
-curves, call them
A_{1},A_{2}
. Then
F_{2}=[1,(2)_{k_{2}-1},1]
. Since
T
is vertical, we have
A_{i}\cdot L=1
and
A_{i}\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
by Lemma
6.6
(a)
,
(d)
. If
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{4\mathrm{A}_{2},8\mathrm{A}_{1}\}
then
0pt(\overline{Y})=4
, so
5\geqslant F_{2}\cdot D\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{2}(A_{i}\cdot D_{Y}-1)\geqslant 6
, a contradiction. Hence
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{2\mathrm{A}_{4},4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}\}
and
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
. If
k_{2}=1
then as before
3\geqslant F_{2}\cdot D=\sum_{i=1}^{2}A_{i}\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 4
, which is false, so
k_{2}\geqslant 2
. By Lemma
2.8
(a)
, the number of
(-1)
-curves in
F_{1}
is at most
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}-1\leqslant 2
, in particular
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2
. Since
F_{1}
consists of
(-1)
-curves off
D
,
(-2)
-curves in
D_{Y}
and the
(-3)
-curve
T
, it is supported on a chain
[1,3,1,2]
or
[3,1,2,2]
; and we have
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=3
or
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2
, respectively. We conclude that
F_{1}
contains a
(-1)
-curve
A
of multiplicity
2
or
3
, respectively, and
A\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=0
. Thus
A\cdot D_{Y}=1
. Since
A\cdot L=0
, we have
\sigma(A)=[1]
, so
\overline{Y}
is not primitive. By Remark
5.7
we have
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\neq 2\mathrm{A}_{4}
, so
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Let
G
be the connected component of
D_{Y}
of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
.

Suppose
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]
. The subchain
[2,2]
of
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}
is contained in
G
, so it meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
once. Since the components of this subchain have multiplicities
1
and
2
in
F_{1}
, it follows that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets
T
or
A
, a contradiction.

Thus
(F_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}=[1,3,1,2]
, so Lemma
2.8
(a)
shows that
\#D_{\mathrm{hor}}=3
, and each
F_{i}
for
i\geqslant 2
contains exactly one
(-1)
-curve. Since
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of
1
-sections, it meets
F_{1}
only in tips. By Lemma
6.8
(c)
, the
(-1)
-tip of
F_{1}
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so the
(-2)
-tip of
F_{1}
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
at most twice. Thus the
(-2)
-tip of
F_{1}
is either is a tip of
G
, or a connected component of
D_{Y}
. Hence the sum of
A
and the connected component of
D_{Y}
meeting it is a chain
[1,2]
or a fork
\langle 2;[1,2],[2],[2]\rangle
, contrary to Lemma
6.6
(c)
.

∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Assume that
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
, and either
\#\mathcal{T}=2
or
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
and
\overline{T}
is nodal. Then
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})+2
.

Suppose the contrary. Then
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
by Lemma
1.12
(c)
. Assume that the number
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 2
is minimal possible. Table
15
shows that
\overline{X}
is not canonical, so the elliptic tie
L
meets exactly one
(-2)
-curve in
D
, call it
C
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
.

Claim 1
.

The elliptic tie
L
is horizontal.

Suppose the contrary. Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the contraction of
L
, and let
D^{\prime}=\tau_{*}(D-C)
,
F^{\prime}=\tau_{*}F
. Then
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a swap onto a minimal resolution of another del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
of rank one, with the same descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime},L^{\prime}
etc. be as in Notation
6.1
for the surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
, so
L^{\prime}=\tau(C)
. The linear system
|F^{\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
F^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=F\cdot D-F^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1-F^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}
. In particular,
0pt(\overline{X}^{\prime})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
, so
\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=1
by the minimality of
\#\mathcal{T}
. By Lemma
6.7
, the elliptic tie
L^{\prime}
is horizontal and
0pt(\overline{X}^{\prime})\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, so the above inequality
F^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1-F^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}
gives
F^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}=1
and
F^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=2
. By Lemma
6.6
(b)
we have
F^{\prime}\cdot T^{\prime}=1
. Let
F^{\prime\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
be a degenerate fiber. By Lemma
2.8
(b)

F^{\prime\prime}
consists of
(-2)
-curves in
D^{\prime}
and
(-1)
-curves off
D^{\prime}
. By Lemma
6.6
(a)
, every such
(-1)
-curve meets
T^{\prime}
or
L^{\prime}
, which are
1
-sections, so it has multiplicity
1
in
F^{\prime\prime}
. Thus
F^{\prime\prime}=[1,2,\dots,2,1]
,
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F^{\prime\prime})
meets
L^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(F^{\prime\prime})
meets
T^{\prime}
. By Lemma
6.6
(d)

\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F^{\prime\prime})
meets
(D^{\prime}_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, which is a
1
-section because
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=2
. Hence
F^{\prime\prime}-\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F^{\prime\prime})=[2,\dots,2,1]
meets
D-F^{\prime\prime}\wedge D
only in
T
, a contradiction with Lemma
6.6
(c)
.

∎

Claim 2
.

Assume that
A
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
L+D_{T}-T
. Then
A\cdot D_{Y}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})
.

By Lemma
6.6
(a)
we have
A\cdot(D_{T}+L)=1
.
Contracting
L
and
(-1)
-curves in subsequent images of
D_{T}
until the image of
A
meets a
(-1)
-curve
L^{\prime}
in the image of
L+D_{T}
, we get a swap
(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
onto a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
with the same descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
. If
\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=1
then the claim follows from Lemma
6.6
(d)
. Assume
\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\geqslant 2
. The linear system
|L^{\prime}+A^{\prime}|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X^{\prime}
such that the elliptic tie
L^{\prime}
is vertical, so by Claim
1
we have
0pt(\overline{Y})+2\leqslant(A^{\prime}+L^{\prime})\cdot D^{\prime}=A\cdot D_{Y}+2
, as needed.

∎

Claim 3
.

We have
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, and for any fiber
F
containing a component of
D_{T}
, one of the following holds.

(a)

F=U+G_{1}+\dots G_{r}
, where
U=[V,T,V^{*}]
or
U=T
, and each
G_{i}
is a chain
[1,2,\dots,2]
, meeting
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, whose first tip meets
T
and the remaining components are contained in
D_{Y}
.

(b)

F_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
,
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge(D_{T})_{\mathrm{vert}}=C
,
(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
1
-section meeting
C
, and
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
2
-section meeting the
(-1)
-curve in
F
, once.

Since
D_{Y}
consists of
(-2)
-curves, every
(-1)
-curve in
F
meets
D_{T}
. Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{k}
be the
(-1)
-curves in
F
which do not meet
T
. Let
\mu_{i}
be the multiplicity of
A_{i}
in
F
, and let
\beta_{i}\coloneqq\beta_{F_{\mathrm{red}}}(A_{i})-1\in\{0,1\}
.
Put
G\coloneqq F_{\mathrm{red}}-D_{T}\wedge F_{\mathrm{red}}
. Let
r\geqslant 0
be the number of connected components of
G
meeting
T
. Since
D_{Y}
consists of
(-2)
-curves, every such connected component is of type
[1,2,\dots,2]
, so it meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
by Lemma
6.6
(c)
.

Assume
k=0
. Then after the contraction of
G
the image of
F
is either a
0
-curve, or a chain meeting the image of
(L+D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}
in tips. Since
F\cdot(L+D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}=2
, those tips have multiplicity one, so the image of
F
is of type
[0]
or
[V,1,V^{*}]
for some admissible chain
V
. Since the above contraction is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
D_{T}-T
, we conclude that
F
is as in
(a)
. Suppose
0pt(\overline{Y})\neq 2
. Since by assumption
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
, Table
15
shows that
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{4\mathrm{A}_{2},8\mathrm{A}_{1}\}
, so every
(-1)
-curve in
F
meets
T
and a tip of a connected component of
D_{Y}
which is a chain. This contradicts Lemma
6.6
(c)
. Hence
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, as needed.

Assume
k\geqslant 1
. We have
F\cdot D\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}A_{i}\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}+F\cdot(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}+r
. Claim
2
shows that for each
i=1,\dots,k
we have
A_{i}\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}=A_{i}\cdot D_{Y}-\beta_{i}\geqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})-\beta_{i}
, so the inequality
