F\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
gives

(21)

0pt(\overline{Y})+1\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}(0pt(\overline{Y})-\beta_{i})+F\cdot(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}+r.

Suppose
\beta_{1}=0
, i.e.
A_{1}
is a tip of
F
. Since
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
, inequality (
21
) implies that
\mu_{1}=1
, so
F
contains another
(-1)
-curve, call it
A^{\prime}
. If
A^{\prime}
does not meet
T
, say
A^{\prime}=A_{2}
, then inequality (
21
) implies that
\mu_{2}=1
and
\beta_{2}=1
, which is impossible. Thus
A^{\prime}
meets
T
, so
r\geqslant 1
. Inequality (
21
) gives
r=1
, i.e.
A^{\prime}
is unique, and
F\cdot(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}=0
, i.e.
F
contains
D_{T}
. We conclude that
F=[1,2,\dots,2,T,1,(2)_{-T^{2}-2}]
, and
F_{T}-A_{1}
meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
only in the last tip, call it
V
. The chain
L+F_{\mathrm{red}}-A=[1,T,1,(2)_{-T^{2}-2}]
supports a fiber
F^{\prime}
of another
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
. We have
F^{\prime}\cdot D=3
, because
F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}
meets
D-F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D
only once on
L
, once on
T
, and once on
V
; and
L,T,V
have multiplicity
1
in
F^{\prime}
. Thus
F^{\prime}\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
, and for this new
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration the elliptic tie
L
is vertical, a contradiction with Claim
1
.

We conclude that for all
i
we have
\beta_{i}=1
, i.e. the
(-1)
-curve
A_{i}
is not a tip of
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, and therefore
\mu_{i}\geqslant 2
. Inequality (
21
) implies that
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\leqslant 3
, so
k=1
and
\mu_{1}\leqslant 3
.

Suppose
F\cdot(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}=0
. Then
D_{T}\subseteq F
. If
r=0
then
A_{1}
is the unique
(-1)
-curve in
F
, so the conditions
\beta_{1}=1
,
\mu_{1}\leqslant 3
give
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[3,1,2,2]
or
[2,1,2]
, contrary to the assumption
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 2
. Thus
r\geqslant 1
, and inequality (
21
) gives
r=1
and
\mu_{1}=2
. Moreover, the connected component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}-D_{T}
meeting
T
, which is a chain
[1,2,\dots,2]
, meets
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in a component of multiplicity
1
. We conclude that
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,3,2,\dots,2]*[T,1,2,\dots,2]
, so the tips of
D_{T}=[3,2,\dots,2]*T
are not
(-2)
-curves. This is a contradiction, as one of those tips is
C
.

Thus
F\cdot(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 1
, so by Lemma
6.6
(b)
and Claim
1
the curves
(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}
and
L
are
1
-sections. Inequality (
21
) gives
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
,
\mu_{1}=2
and
r=0
, so
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
and
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{T}
is a
(-2)
-curve meeting
L
, called
C
. Since
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets
A_{1}
, we conclude that
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
2
-section, so
(b)
holds, as needed.

∎

Claim 4
.

The curves
L
and
H_{T}\coloneqq(D_{T})_{\mathrm{hor}}
are
1
-sections.

By Lemma
6.6
(b)
any fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D_{T}=2-F\cdot L\leqslant 1
by Claim
1
. If
F\cdot D_{T}=1
then the claim follows, so assume
F\cdot D_{T}=0
, i.e.
D_{T}
is contained in some fiber
F_{T}
. Such
F_{T}
is as in Claim
3
(a)
. Since
\#D_{T}\geqslant 2
, we get
D_{T}=[V,T,V^{*}]
, so
D_{T}+L-T=[V^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},1,(V^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}]=[V^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},1,(V^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})^{*}]
, see
[
Fuj82
, §3.9]
, which blows down to a
0
-curve and not to a smooth point, a contradiction.

∎

Claim 5
.

Let
F
be a degenerate fiber which does not contain any component of
D_{T}
. Then
F=[1,2,\dots,2,1]
,
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F)
meets
L
and
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, and
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(F)
meets
H_{T}
and
T+(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
.

Let
A
be a
(-1)
-curve in
F
. By Lemma
6.6
(a)
,
A
meets
D_{T}+L
. Since
L
is horizontal and
F
is disjoint from
(D_{T})_{\mathrm{vert}}
, the curve
A
meets
(D_{T}+L)_{\mathrm{hor}}
, which by Claim
4
consists of
1
-sections. Thus
A
has multiplicity
1
in
F
. Since
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D
consists of
(-2)
-curves, we get
F=[1,2,\dots,2,1]
. Since both
(-1)
-curves in
F
meet
D_{T}+L
, we can order
F
in such a way that
\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F)
meets
L
and
\mathrm{tip}^{-}(F)
meets
H_{T}
. By Claim
2
if a tip of
F
does not meet
T
then it meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, which ends the proof.

∎

Claim 6
.

The curve
T
is contained in a fiber
F_{T}
as in Claim
3
(a)
.

Suppose the contrary. Then
T
is horizontal, and
D_{T}-T
is contained in a fiber
F
as in Claim
3
(b)
, so
D_{T}=T+C
,
H_{2}\coloneqq(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
2
-section, and
F_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{Y}=[2]
. By Claim
5
any degenerate fiber
F^{\prime}\neq F
is a chain
[1,2,\dots,2,1]
, so by Lemma
2.8
(a)
there is exactly one such
F^{\prime}
. By Claim
5

A\coloneqq\mathrm{tip}^{+}(F^{\prime})
meets
L
and
H_{2}
. If
A\cdot H_{2}=2
then the chain
F^{\prime}-A=[1,2,\dots,2]
meets
T
and is disjoint from
H_{2}
, contrary to Lemma
6.6
(c)
. Thus
H_{2}
meets the first and
l
-th component of
F^{\prime}
for some
l\geqslant 2
. Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
L+(F_{\mathrm{red}}-C)+(F^{\prime}-A)
. Then
\tau(H_{2})
is a conic, so
4=\tau(H_{2})^{2}=-2+2+l
, hence
l=4
. We conclude that
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5},\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{6},\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{E}_{7}\}
, so
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, see Table
15
, a contradiction.

∎

Recall that by Claim
3
we have
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
, so
F\cdot D=3
. By Claim
4
,
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a sum of a
1
-section
H_{T}
and
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is either a
2
-section or two
1
-sections. We claim that every component of
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}
meeting
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
lies in the fiber
F_{T}
from Claim
6
. Indeed, suppose that a component
V
of
D_{Y}
meeting
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
lies in a fiber
F\neq F_{T}
. By Claim
3
,
F
does not contain any component of
D_{T}
. Since
T
is vertical, Claim
5
implies that both tips of
F
meet
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, so
2=F\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 2+V\cdot(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}\geqslant 3
, a contradiction.

Let
G_{i}=[2,\dots,2,1]
for
i=1,\dots,r
be the connected components of
(F_{T})_{\mathrm{red}}-D_{T}\wedge(F_{T})_{\mathrm{red}}
, see Claim
3
(a)
. Let
A_{i}
be the
(-1)
-curve in
G_{i}
. Note that
G_{i}^{(j)}
has multiplicity at least
j
in
F_{T}
.

Suppose
r\geqslant 2
. Since each
G_{i}
meets
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
, we conclude that
r=2
and
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets
G_{1}^{(1)},G_{2}^{(1)}
. Hence the sum
G_{1}+(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}+G_{2}-A_{2}
or
G_{1}+(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a chain
[1,2,\dots,2]
meeting the remaining part of
A_{1}+D
only in
A_{1}\cap T
. This is a contradiction with Lemma
6.6
(c)
.

Thus
r=1
. Since
D_{Y}
has no circular subdivisor, we see that either
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
2
-section meeting
G_{1}^{(1)}
or
G_{1}^{(2)}
, or two
1
-sections meeting
G_{1}^{(1)}
. Thus
G_{1}+(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a chain
[1,2,\dots,2]
or a fork
\langle 2;[1,2,\dots,2],[2],[2]\rangle
meeting the remaining part of
A_{1}+D
only in
A_{1}\cap T
, which again contradicts Lemma
6.6
(c)
.

∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Assume that
\#\mathcal{T}=3
,
\overline{T}
is cuspidal,
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
and
0pt(\overline{X})\neq 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
. Then the following hold.

(a)

For every
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
of height at most
0pt(\overline{Y})+1
with respect to
D
, the elliptic tie
L
is horizontal.

(b)

The surface
\overline{X}
is as Proposition
6.4
(b)
, in particular,
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})+1=3
.

The divisor
D_{T}
is a disjoint sum of
T=[s-3]
,
T_{3}=[3]
, and
T_{2}=[2]
, see
6.2
(3)
. By Lemmas
6.3
and
1.12
(c)
we have
s\geqslant 6
and
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
.

(a)
Suppose that
L
is vertical. Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the contraction of
L
and let
D^{\prime}=\tau_{*}(D-T_{2})
. Then
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
of rank one with the same descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, and the corresponding number
\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
equals
\#\mathcal{T}-1=2
. By Lemma
6.8
we have
0pt(\overline{X}^{\prime})=0pt(\overline{Y})+2
. On the other hand,
|\tau_{*}F|
induces a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X^{\prime}
such that
\tau_{*}F\cdot D^{\prime}=F\cdot(D-T_{2})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
, a contradiction.

(b)
Pulling back the linear equivalence
-K_{\overline{Y}}=\overline{T}
we get a linear equivalence
-K_{X}=T+T_{2}+T_{3}+2L
. The adjunction formula gives
2=F\cdot(T+T_{2}+T_{3}+2L)
, so since
F\cdot L\geqslant 1
by
(a)
, we infer that
L
is a
1
-section and each
T_{i}
is vertical. Since
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
meet the
1
-section
L
, they lie in different fibers, call them
F,F_{2},F_{3}
. Since
F_{3}
consists of some
(-1)
-curves off
D
, some
(-2)
-curves in
D_{Y}
, and a
(-3)
-curve
T_{3}
of multiplicity
1
, it is supported on a chain
[2,2,1,3]
or
[2,1,3,1]
, or on a fork
\langle 3;[1],[1],[1]\rangle
. Let
A
be a
(-1)
-curve in
F_{3}
. The image
\sigma(A)
of
A
on
Y
is a
0
-curve, so
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant\sigma(A)\cdot\operatorname{Exc}\alpha=A\cdot D_{Y}
. We get
F_{3}\cdot D\geqslant 3(0pt(\overline{Y})-1)
if
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,2,1,3]
,
F_{3}\cdot D\geqslant 2(0pt(\overline{Y})-1)+0pt(\overline{Y})
if
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,3,1]
, and
F_{3}\cdot D\geqslant 30pt(\overline{Y})
if
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=\langle 3;[1],[1],[1]\rangle
. Since
F_{3}\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
and
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
, we conclude that
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,2,1,3]
,
0pt(\overline{Y})=2
,
A\cdot D_{\mathrm{hor}}=1
and
(F_{3})_{\mathrm{red}}-A
is disjoint from
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Thus
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
is a
3
-section. By Lemma
2.8
(a)
every degenerate fiber has exactly one
(-1)
-curve, so
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[(2)_{s-2},1,s-3]
,
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}=[2,1,2]
. The
3
-section
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets exactly one component of
F
: indeed, otherwise the components of
F
meeting
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
have multiplicity at most
2
in
F
, so since
s-3\geqslant 3
, they lie in
D
and therefore
D
has a circular subdivisor, which is false. Thus
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets the unique component of multiplicity
3
in
F
, which is the third component of
F_{\mathrm{red}}=[(2)_{s-2},1,s-3]
. Similarly, we conclude that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
meets
F_{2}
in its
(-1)
-curve, with multiplicity
2
, and once in its
(-2)
-tip
(F_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}\wedge D_{Y}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=3\mathrm{A}_{2},\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5},\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
if
s=6,7,8
, respectively, as needed.

∎

Lemma 6.10
.

Assume that
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
,
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 4
and
\overline{T}
is cuspidal. Then
0pt(\overline{X})=0pt(\overline{Y})+2
.

Suppose the contrary. Then
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
by Lemma
1.12
(c)
. Fix a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
whose fiber
F
satisfies
F\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})+1
. We assume that the number
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 4
is minimal possible, and exactly as in the proof of Claim
1
of Lemma
6.8
, we conclude that the curve
L
is horizontal. Indeed, suppose
L
is vertical. Then a swap
\tau\colon(X,D)\rightsquigarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
of
L
for a
(-2)
-curve
C\subseteq D_{T}
meeting
L
gives a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}
of rank one, with the same descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, with
\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\#\mathcal{T}-1
, and with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose fiber
F^{\prime}=\tau_{*}F
satisfies
F^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}+F^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}=F\cdot D\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
. Thus
F^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
, so
\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=3
by minimality of
\#\mathcal{T}
, and Lemma
6.9
implies that
F^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=0pt(\overline{Y})+1
and
F^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, a contradiction.

Pulling back the linear equivalence
-K_{\overline{Y}}=\overline{T}
we get a linear equivalence
-K_{X}=T+E
, where
E
is an effective divisor supported on
D_{T}+L-T
. Since
L
is horizontal and
2=-K_{X}\cdot F=F\cdot(T+E)
, the multiplicity of
L
in
E
equals at most
2
. Writing
\varphi=\alpha^{\prime}\circ\sigma^{\prime}
, where
\alpha^{\prime}
is the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, we conclude that
\sigma^{\prime}
is a composition of outer blowups on the
(-1)
-curves in the subsequent total transforms of
\overline{T}
. Thus
\mathcal{T}
is as in Lemma
6.2
(10)
for
k=2
. Moreover, the multiplicity of
L
in
E
is exactly
2
, so the equality
2=F\cdot(T+E)
implies that
D_{T}
is vertical and
L
is a
1
-section. Since
D_{T}
is connected, it is contained in some fiber
F
. Let
B
be the branching
(-2)
-curve in
D_{T}
, see Lemma
6.2
(10)
. Clearly,
B
is branching in
F_{\mathrm{red}}
, too, and
B
has multiplicity
1
in
F
because it meets
L
. Hence
0=F\cdot B\geqslant\beta_{F_{\mathrm{red}}}(B)+B^{2}\geqslant 1
, a contradiction.

∎

Assume
3\leqslant 0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 0pt(\overline{Y})+1
. By Lemmas
6.8
,
6.10
we have
\#\mathcal{T}=1
or
\#\mathcal{T}=3
and
\overline{T}
is cuspidal; so Lemmas
6.7
,
6.9
imply that the singularity type of
\overline{X}
is as in
6.4
(a)
or
6.4
(b)
, respectively. Conversely, if
\overline{X}
has one of these singularity types then
0pt(\overline{X})=3
by Lemma
6.5
, as needed.

∎

6C.
Uniqueness of a descendant

Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
complete the proof of Theorem
E
(b)
,
(c)
, that is, they give a list of possible singularity types of
\overline{X}
. Theorem
E
(f)
, which computes
0pt(\overline{X})
, is proved in Proposition
6.4
. The remaining part of Theorem
E
addresses the question whether the singularity type determines
\overline{X}
uniquely, up to an isomorphism. As a first result in this direction we prove Theorem
E
(a)
, which asserts that
\overline{X}
determines its descendant uniquely, provided
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
. More precisely, we prove the following statement.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\overline{X}
be as in Notation
6.1
. Assume that
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The morphism
\varphi
from Definition
1.11
is unique up to an automorphism of the source and target.

(b)

The singularity types of
\overline{Y}
and
\overline{T}
are uniquely determined by the singularity type of
\overline{X}
.

(a)
Let
\varphi^{\prime}\colon(X,D+L^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y}^{\prime},\overline{T}^{\prime})
be another such morphism. As in Notation
6.1
write
T=(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}^{\prime}
and
D=D_{T}^{\prime}+D_{Y}^{\prime}
, where
D_{Y}^{\prime}=(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}(\operatorname{Sing}\overline{Y}^{\prime})
,
D_{T}^{\prime}+L^{\prime}=(\varphi^{\prime})^{*}\overline{T}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}
. To prove the lemma, it is enough to find an automorphism
\iota\in\operatorname{Aut}(X,D)
such that
\iota(L)=L^{\prime}
and
\iota(T)=T^{\prime}
.

Case 1:

T=T^{\prime}
.
Let
\pi_{T}\colon X\longrightarrow X_{T}
be the contraction of
D-T
, in other words,
X_{T}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
is the extraction of
T
, see
[
Kol13
, 1.39]
or Section
7A
.
Then
\rho(X_{T})=2
. The cone
\operatorname{NE}(X_{T})
has two extremal rays: one spanned by
\pi_{T}(T)
, which corresponds to the contraction
X_{T}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
, and the other spanned by
\pi_{T}(L)
, corresponding to the contraction
X_{T}\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
. But there is also another extremal contraction
X_{T}\longrightarrow\overline{Y}^{\prime}
, corresponding to
\pi_{T}(L^{\prime})
. Since
\pi_{T}(L^{\prime})
contracts to a smooth point and
\pi_{T}(T)
does not, we infer that
\pi_{T}(L^{\prime})
spans the same extremal ray as
\pi_{T}(L)
. Since
\pi_{T}(L)^{2}<0
we get
\pi_{T}(L)=\pi_{T}(L^{\prime})
, and as a consequence
L=L^{\prime}
, as needed.

Case 2:

L=L^{\prime}
.
Then
D_{T}=D_{T}^{\prime}
and
D_{Y}=D_{Y}^{\prime}
, in particular
\overline{Y}
and
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
have the same singularity type
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
. Write
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi_{0}
,
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\circ\varphi_{0}
such that
\#\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{0}
is maximal possible. If
\varphi_{0}=\varphi
then
T=T^{\prime}
, as needed, so we can assume
\varphi_{0}\neq\varphi
, hence
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\neq\mathrm{id}
. Let
E
and
E^{\prime}
be the unique
(-1)
-curve in
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{1}
, and
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
, respectively. Since
\#\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{0}
is maximal,
E
meets
E^{\prime}
and
E=\varphi_{0}(T^{\prime})
,
E^{\prime}=\varphi_{0}(T)
. In particular,
-1=\varphi_{0}(T)^{2}\leqslant\overline{T}^{2}-4\overset{\mbox{\tiny{\eqref{eq:Noether}}}}{=}5-s\leqslant-1
by Lemma
6.3
, so
\varphi_{1}
(respectively,
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
) is a single blowup at the singular point of
\overline{T}
(respectively,
\overline{T}^{\prime}
), and
s=6
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{E}_{6}
then
\overline{T}
is nodal by Lemma
6.3
(b)
. Thus by
[
PP24
, Proposition 1.5]
the singularity type
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
determines the log surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
uniquely, up to an isomorphism, see Table
15
. We conclude that there is an isomorphism
\alpha\colon(\overline{Y}^{\prime},\overline{T}^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
such that
\alpha\circ\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
; so
\iota\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}\circ\alpha\circ\varphi^{\prime}
extends to the required automorphism of
(X,D)
.

Case 3:

T\neq T^{\prime}
,
L\neq L^{\prime}
.
Suppose
D_{T}=D_{T^{\prime}}
. Then the description of
D_{T}+L
in Lemma
6.2
shows that
L
and
L^{\prime}
meet the same components of
D
. Since
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
is generated by
K_{X}
and the components of
D
, the curves
L
and
L^{\prime}
are numerically equivalent, hence equal because
L^{2}<0
; contrary to our assumptions. Thus
D_{T}\neq D_{T}^{\prime}
. Let
R
be the sum of common components of
D_{T}
and
D_{T}^{\prime}
, and let
B=D_{T}-R
. Then
B\subseteq D^{\prime}_{Y}
, so each connected component of
B
is a
(-2)
-chain or a
(-2)
-fork which is also a connected component of
D_{T}
.

Lemma
6.2
shows that such connected components occur only if
R=0
or if
D_{T}
is as in
6.2
(3)
or
6.2
(10)
. Consider the last two cases. Then
R=[s-3]+[3]
or
\langle k;[s-3],[3],[2]\rangle
. Since
T\neq T^{\prime}
, we get
s=6
and
T=[3]
,
T^{\prime}=[3]
. Now we check directly that after the contraction of
D-T
, the image of
L^{\prime}
can still be contracted; so as in case
1
above we get
L=L^{\prime}
, as needed.

Consider now the case
R=0
, so
\overline{X}
is canonical. Since
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
, by
[
PP24
, Proposition 6.1]

\overline{X}
is of type
4\mathrm{A}_{2}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
. Now
D_{T}
and
D_{T}^{\prime}
are different connected components of
D
, and the
(-1)
-curve
L
(respectively,
L^{\prime}
) meets
D
in both tips of
D_{T}
(respectively,
D_{T}^{\prime}
). This condition uniquely determines the numerical classes of
L
and
L^{\prime}
, hence it uniquely determines
L
and
L^{\prime}
. The result now follows from the fact that
\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{X})
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Sing}\overline{X}
by
[
PP24
, Lemmas 7.2(d), 8.4(d)]
, cf.
[
KN22
, Corollary 5.2 and Proposition 5.22(4)]
.

(b)
We claim that
\mathcal{S}
determines
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
uniquely. If
\overline{X}
is canonical, then since
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
we have
\mathcal{S}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, see Table
15
, so Lemma
6.2
yields
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=3\mathrm{A}_{2}
or
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, respectively, as needed. Suppose
\overline{X}
is not canonical and
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
is not uniquely determined by
\mathcal{S}
. We have two decompositions
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\neq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{Y}
are canonical singularity types, and
\mathcal{T}\neq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are as in Lemma
6.2
. It follows that, say,
\mathcal{T}
has a common connected component with
\mathcal{S}_{Y}^{\prime}
. As before, we infer from Lemma
6.2
that
\mathcal{T}=[s-3]+[3]+[2]
or
\langle k,[2],[3],[s-3]\rangle+[(2)_{k-2}]
for some
k\geqslant 3
; and applying Lemma
6.2
again we get
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

In turn,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
uniquely determines the singularity type of
\overline{T}
. Indeed, Lemma
6.3
implies that
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 6
and
\overline{T}
is nodal if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\in\{\mathrm{E}_{6},\mathrm{E}_{7},\mathrm{E}_{8}\}
, so the claim follows from the classification in Table
15
.

∎

6D.
Computation of moduli

Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
denotes the set of isomorphisms classes of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and type
\mathcal{S}
, admitting descendants with elliptic boundary; and
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{node}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
,
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
denotes the set of isomorphism classes of log surfaces
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, where
\overline{Y}
is a canonical surface of rank one and type
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
; and
\overline{T}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a nodal or cuspidal member of
|-K_{\overline{Y}}|
, respectively. We assume that type
\mathcal{S}
is such that
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
contains a surface
\overline{X}
with
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
. By Lemma
6.3
the same inequality holds for all surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
. Lemma
6.11
implies that we have a map from
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
to
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{node}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
or
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
, associating to a surface
\overline{X}
its descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
. In the first case the entry in Table
15
corresponding to
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
is
\mathrm{N}
; in the second it is
\mathrm{C}
or
\mathrm{C}_{d}
, where
d
is the moduli dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
, see
[
PP24
, Proposition 1.5]
or Lemma
6.14
below. In all the other cases we put
d=0
.

With this information at hand, we can now compute the numbers
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
and thus prove the remaining parts
(d)
,
(e)
of Theorem
E
. The proof is organized as follows. The case when
\overline{T}
is nodal is settled in Lemma
6.13
, where we use the fact that
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{node}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})=1
to prove that
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})=1
. If
\overline{T}
is cuspidal (so
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
, see Table
15
and Lemma
6.3
) then the proof splits in two cases, depending on whether
(X,D)
dominates the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
or not, i.e. whether
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
or
\#\mathcal{T}\in\{1,2\}
. In the first case we take the almost universal family of dimension
d
representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
and lift it to one representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
following Observation
2.10
: this is done in Lemma
6.15
(b)
. In case
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
we still get an almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
by blowing down the above one. Nonetheless, it can happen that
h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))>d
, so an almost
universal
family may no longer exist, see Proposition
6.17
.

First we make an elementary observation which will frequently allow us to assume that
\overline{Y}
is primitive.

Lemma 6.12

(Simplifying
\overline{Y}
)
.

Assume that
\overline{Y}
swaps to a canonical surface
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
of type
\mathcal{S}_{Y}^{\prime}
such that
w\coloneqq T^{2}+s-\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}^{\prime}\leqslant-2
. Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{Y}^{\prime}+\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, where type
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{T}
by replacing the weight of a vertex corresponding to
T
with
w
. Then
\overline{X}
swaps to a surface
\overline{X}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
. More precisely, there is an inner morphism
(X,D+L+A)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+L^{\prime}+A^{\prime})
where
A
and
A^{\prime}
are
(-1)
-curves,
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
, and
L^{\prime}
is an elliptic tie, see Notation
6.1
. In particular,
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))
.

We can assume that the swap
\overline{Y}\rightsquigarrow\overline{Y}^{\prime}
is elementary, i.e. it is a contraction of a
(-1)
-curve
A_{Y}
. Since
\sigma_{*}(D_{Y})
consists of
(-2)
-curves, by Definition
1.3
(a)
we have
A_{Y}\cdot\sigma_{*}(D_{Y})=1
. By adjunction, we have
A_{Y}\cdot\sigma_{*}(T_{Y})=A_{Y}\cdot(-K_{Y})=1
. Thus the proper transform
A
of
A_{Y}
on
X
satisfies
A\cdot(D+L)=2
, and meets
D+L
in
T
and in a
(-2)
-curve in
D_{Y}
. The required morphism is given by the contraction of
A
.

∎

Lemma 6.13

(Uniqueness in the nodal case, see Theorem
E
(d)
)
.

Assume that
\overline{T}
is nodal. Then
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})=1
and the minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of the unique surface
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
satisfies
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))=0
for all
i
.

Let
\overline{Y}\rightsquigarrow\overline{Y}^{\prime}
be a swap to a vertically primitive surface as in Remark
4.5
or Example
5.6
. Since
\overline{T}
is nodal, looking at Table
15
we see that
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
is of one of the types:
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2
;
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 3
;
2\mathrm{A}_{4}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 5
; or
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
. Let
\overline{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\prime}
be the image of
\overline{T}
, and let
(Y^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y}^{\prime},\overline{T}^{\prime})
. Consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations of
Y^{\prime}
and
X
induced by the ones in Figures
16
or
20
,
21
, and let
\widecheck{B}^{\prime}
be the sum of
B^{\prime}
and all vertical
(-1)
-curves. Those
(-1)
-curves are the proper transforms of those shown in Figures
16
,
20
,
21
, the elliptic tie
L^{\prime}
over the node of
\overline{T}^{\prime}
, and the proper transform of the fiber passing through the node, call it
V
. The divisor
\widecheck{B}^{\prime}
is snc: to see this, we need to check that
V
meets
\widecheck{B}-V
normally, which is obvious in all cases except
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=2\mathrm{A}_{4}
, when one needs to check that
V
does not pass through the common point of the horizontal
(-2)
-curves. This follows from a direct computation using the construction of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
in
[
PP24
, §9]
, relying on the fact that
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 5
.

Let
\widecheck{D}^{\prime}
be the reduced total transform of
\widecheck{B}^{\prime}
on
X
: again, it is a sum of
D
and
(-1)
-curves. We have an inner morphism
(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(Y^{\prime},\widecheck{B}^{\prime})
, so by Lemma
2.11
(c)
and
2.20
(c)
it is enough to prove that
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Y^{\prime}}(-\log\widecheck{B}^{\prime}))=0
and that the combinatorial type
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(Y^{\prime},\widecheck{B}^{\prime})
has
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})=1
.

Let
\varphi\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
be the contraction of
V
. Put
B_{Z}=\varphi_{*}\widecheck{B}^{\prime}
if
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
and
B_{Z}=\varphi_{*}(\widecheck{B}-L^{\prime})-H^{\prime}
, where
H^{\prime}
is one of the horizontal
(-1)
-curves in
\varphi_{*}B
. Furthermore, in case
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
replace
Z
with a blowup at one of the two common points of the proper transforms of
\overline{T}
and
L
, and
B_{Z}
by its reduced total transform. In any case, we conclude as before that it is enough to prove the required statements for
(Z,B_{Z})
.

Let
\tau\colon Z\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of the horizontal
(-1)
-curve in
B_{Z}
and all vertical curves disjoint from it. Then
\tau_{*}B_{Z}
consists of a conic
{c}
and lines
{l}_{1},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
for some
\nu\in\{2,3\}
meeting at a point off
{c}
, such that
{l}_{2},\dots,{l}_{\nu}
are tangent to
{c}
and
{l}_{1}
is not. Let
p
be the base point of
\tau^{-1}
in
{l}_{1}\cap{c}
, let
{l}
be the line joining
p
with
{l}_{2}\cap{c}
, and if
\nu=3
let
{l}^{\prime}
be the line joining
{l}_{2}\cap{c}
with
{l}_{3}\cap{c}
. The proper transforms of
{l}
and (in case
\nu=3
)
{l}^{\prime}
are
(-1)
-curves meeting
B_{Z}
normally, so as before we can add it to
B_{Z}
. In case
\nu=2
we have an inner morphism
(Z,B_{Z})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
, where
{p}
is a sum of four lines in a general position, so
(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
is unique up to an isomorphism and
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-\log{p}))=0
by Lemma
2.14
(d)(d.i)
, which ends the proof. In case
\nu=3
we have an inner morphism
(Z,B_{Z})\longrightarrow(Z^{\prime},B_{Z}^{\prime})
such that the proper transform
T^{\prime}
of
\overline{T}
on
Z^{\prime}
is a
(-1)
-curve, so we can replace
(Z,B_{Z})
by
(Z^{\prime},B_{Z}^{\prime}-T^{\prime})
. The latter admits an inner morphism onto
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
, where
B
is a sum of
3
vertical and
3
horizontal lines. Like before, the result follows since
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
is unique up to an isomorphism and
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log B))=0
for all
i
by Lemma
2.14
(d)(d.iii)
.

∎

From now on we assume that
\overline{T}
is cuspidal, so
\operatorname{char}{k}\in\{2,3,5\}
. The corresponding entry in Table
15
is
\mathrm{C}_{d}
or
\mathrm{C}
. In the latter case we put
d=0
. Moreover, we put
h^{i}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
for a minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of some fixed
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
. We begin with case
\#\mathcal{T}=3
, i.e. when
(X,D+L)
is the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
. We note that in case
d\geqslant 1
the result essentially follows from
[
PP24
, Proposition 1.5(c)]
, but since the argument in case
d=0
works similarly in case
d\geqslant 1
, too, we sketch a proof for any
d
.

Lemma 6.14

(Moduli in case
\overline{T}
cuspidal,
\#\mathcal{T}=3
)
.

Assume

\#\mathcal{T}=3
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
d
. Moreover, we have
(h^{0},h^{1},h^{2})=(0,d,d+1)
for any
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
.

By Lemma
6.12
we can assume that
\overline{Y}
is primitive. Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
, so
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3},3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4},7\mathrm{A}_{1}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, and
d=0,1,2
or
3
, respectively. Consider a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
which is a pullback of the one of
Y
shown in Figure
16(c)
,
16(g)
,
20(c)
or
[
PP24
, Figure 6(b)]
, see Figure
23(a)
for the latter. Let
V
be the proper transform of the fiber passing through the cusp of
\overline{Y}
, and let
\varphi
be the contraction of
V
. In cases
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=2\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{3}
and
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
we get this way an inner morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
onto a log surface as in Lemma
5.16
for
\nu=d+1
, so the result follows from Lemma
2.18
. Consider cases
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=7\mathrm{A}_{1}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
. Put
B=D-(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
. We have a composition of
d-1
outer blowdowns
(X,B)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
onto a log surface as in Lemma
5.16
for
\nu=4
, so
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=0,1,2
for
i=0,1,2
. By Lemma
2.11
(d)
we get
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log B))=0,d,2
for
i=0,1,2
. By Lemma
2.14
(a)
we get
h^{1}\leqslant d
and
h^{2}=h^{1}+1
. Moreover, by Lemma
2.19
(1)
we get an almost universal family representing the image of
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
in
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
, where
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
,
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
are the combinatorial types of
(X,D+L)
and
(X,B+L)
, respectively. By construction, this family can be extended (by adding boundary components) to an almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
. It follows that
h^{1}\geqslant d
. Therefore, we have
h^{1}=d
and the natural map between deformations of
(X,D)
and
(X,B)
is an isomorphism. Thus our family is almost universal, as needed. For an alternative construction of such families see
[
PP24
, Lemmas 8.2(a), 8.4(a)]
.

Consider the case
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=3\mathrm{A}_{2}
. We keep the notation from Example
5.2
(a)
, and denote the proper transforms on
X
of the curves in Figure
19(a)
by the same letters. The image of
T
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a cuspidal cubic
{q}
tangent to
{c},{l}_{2}
at
p_{1},p_{0}
with multiplicity
3
, so since
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, the line
{l}^{\prime}
joining
p_{2}
with the cusp
q\in{q}
is tangent to
{q}
at
q
, too, see
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.5(b)]
. For
i\in\{0,1\}
let
{l}_{i}^{\prime}
be the line joining
q
with
p_{i}
, and let
L^{\prime},L_{i}^{\prime}
be the proper transforms of
{l}^{\prime},{l}_{i}^{\prime}
on
X
, they are
(-1)
-curves. Let
D^{\prime}=D+L+L_{1}+L_{1}^{\prime}-C
. Let
G_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
be the connected components of
D_{Y}
meeting
L_{1}
and
A_{i}
. The contraction of
(L_{0}^{\prime}+G_{0})+(A_{1}+G_{1})+(L^{\prime}+L_{2})
is an inner morphism
(X,D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
, where
B
is the sum of three horizontal and three vertical lines. Lemmas
2.14
(d)(d.iii)
and
2.18
show that
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log(D-C)))=0
for all
i
, and the combinatorial type
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(X,D^{\prime})
satisfies
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=1
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})=1
. Since
C=[2]
we get
(h^{0},h^{1},h^{2})=(0,0,1)
by Lemma
2.14
(a)
.

In case
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}
the first statement is proved in
[
PP24
, Lemma 7.2(b)]
and the second is elementary. For a direct argument we proceed as follows. Recall that
\overline{Y}
has a descendant of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
with cuspidal elliptic boundary, and the corresponding
(-1)
-curve over the cusp, call it
L^{\prime}
, has
L^{\prime}\cdot D_{Y}=2
and meets
D_{Y}
in a node. By adjunction
L^{\prime}\cdot T=1
, so
L^{\prime}\cdot D=3
. Let
G,H
be the components of
D_{Y}
meeting
L^{\prime}
, and let
(X,D-H)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a contraction of
L^{\prime}+G
. It is a composition of an inner and outer blowup, and the resulting log surface
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface as in the previous paragraph, i.e. in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(3\mathrm{A}_{2}+[3]+[3]+[2])
. Thus
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=0,0,1
for
i=0,1,2
, so Lemma
2.11
implies that
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log(D-H)))=0,1,1
, so
h^{0}=0
and
h^{1}=h^{2}-1\geqslant 1
by Lemma
2.14
(a)
. Moreover, Lemma
2.19
(1)
implies that
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
is represented by an almost faithful family over a curve, so as in cases
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
above we conclude that
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
h^{1}=1
, as needed.

It remains to consider case
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=2\mathrm{A}_{4}
. Again, we denote the curves in Figure
19(e)
and their proper transforms on
X
by the same letters. For
i=0,2
let
L_{i}^{\prime}=[1]
be the proper transform of the line joining
p_{i}
with the cusp of the image of
T
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Write
D_{T}=T+U+V
, where
U=[3]
,
V=[2]
. Let
G_{T}=[(2)_{4}]
,
G_{U}=[2,2]
and
G_{V}=[2]
be the connected components of
D_{Y}-C
. Then
T+A_{1}+G_{T}=[5,1,(2)_{4}]
supports a fiber a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration such that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}=C
is a
5
-section, and
U+L_{0}^{\prime}+G_{U}
supports another degenerate fiber, see Figure
23(b)
. It follows from Lemma
2.4
that the remaining degenerate fiber is supported on
V+A+G_{V}=[2,1,2]
for some
(-1)
-curve
A
. Let
D^{\prime}=D+L+A_{2}+L_{2}^{\prime}-C
. The contraction of
(A_{1}+G_{T})+(L_{0}^{\prime}+G_{U})+(A+G_{V})
is an inner morphism
(X,D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
, where
B
is a sum of three horizontal and three vertical lines, so
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=0
for
i=0,1,2
. We conclude exactly as in the case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=3\mathrm{A}_{2}
.

∎

, cf.

(\overline{Y},\overline{T})

Lemma 6.15

(Moduli in the cuspidal case, see Theorem
E
(e)
)
.

Let
t
be as in Theorem
E
(e)(e.ii)
.

(a)

The set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
is represented by an almost faithful family of dimension
d+t
.

(b)

Assume

\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
d+t
.

Let
\widecheck{\mathcal{S}}
be the combinatorial type of
(X,D+L)
. By Lemmas
6.11
and
2.20
(c)
, it is enough to prove the above properties for
\#\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
. First, we prove part
(b)
. Since
\#\mathcal{T}\geqslant 3
, the morphism
\varphi\colon(X,D+L)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
factors through
\psi\colon(X,D+L)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+L^{\prime})
, where
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+L^{\prime})
is the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
, and
L^{\prime}=[1]
. If
t=0
then
\psi
is inner, so the claim follows from Lemmas
2.18
and
6.14
.

Assume
t=1
. Then
\psi=\gamma\circ\psi^{\prime}
, where
\psi^{\prime}\colon(X,D+L)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime}+L^{\prime\prime})
is inner, and
\gamma\colon(X^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime}+L^{\prime\prime})\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+L^{\prime})
is a single outer blowup, centered on
L^{\prime}
, which is the branching component of the fork
\psi_{*}(D_{T}+L)=\langle 1;[2],[3],[s-3]\rangle
, see Lemma
6.2
(10)
. Since every vector field on
X^{\prime}
tangent to
D^{\prime}+L^{\prime}
vanishes at three points of
L^{\prime}
, it vanishes identically along
L^{\prime}
. If
s\geqslant 7
then
\operatorname{Aut}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+L^{\prime})
fixes each of the three points in
L^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime}
, so it acts trivially on
L^{\prime}
. In this case, applying Lemma
2.19
(1)
we conclude that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
has moduli dimension
d+1
, as claimed. Assume
s=6
. Then
d=0
, see Table
15
, so
\#\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})=1
. Still, the restriction of
\operatorname{Aut}(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+L^{\prime})
to
L^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{3\mbox{ points}\}
is a finite group (in fact, it is
\mathbb{Z}/2
interchanging the common points of
L^{\prime}
with
(-3)
-curves in
D^{\prime}
), so applying Lemmas
2.19
(1)
and
2.18
as before we conclude that
\mathcal{P}_{+}(\widecheck{\mathcal{S}})
has moduli dimension
1
.

To prove
(a)
it remains to construct almost faithful families in case
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
. To do this, we take one constructed above for
\#\mathcal{T}=3
and blow down divisors corresponding to
L
and the
(-2)
-curve meeting it.

∎

Remark 6.16

(Bases and symmetry groups in Lemma

(a)
, see Table

)
.

Let
B_{0},G_{0}
be the base and symmetry group of an almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{cusp}}(\mathcal{S}_{Y})
: if
d>0
then

are listed in
[

, Table 2]
, which we repeat in Table

below. If
d=0
then
B_{0}=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
,
G_{0}=\{\mathrm{id}\}
. Let
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\longrightarrow B
be the almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
constructed in Lemma
6.15
(a)
, and let
G
be its symmetry group. The proof of Lemma
6.15
(a)
shows the following. If
t=0
then
B=B_{0}
and
G=G_{0}
. If
t=1
then
B\cong B_{0}\times L^{\circ}
, where
L^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{3\mbox{ points}\}
parametrizes the center of the outer blowup, and either
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 7
and
G=G_{0}
acts trivially on
L^{\circ}
, or we have
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}=6
,
B\cong L^{\circ}
and
G\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
.
