To prove Theorem
E
it remains to study the case when
\overline{T}
is cuspidal and
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
. The result is summarized in Proposition
6.17
below. Its proof relies on direct computations made in Lemmas
6.19
and
6.20
.

Proposition 6.17

(The special case
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
of Theorem
E
(e)
)
.

We keep Notation
6.1
and assume
0pt(\overline{X})\geqslant 3
. Assume furthermore that
\overline{T}
is cuspidal and
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 2
. Put
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log D))
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
h^{0}=0
and
h^{1}=h^{2}\in\{d,d+1\}
.

(b)

Assume that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
or
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
\#\mathcal{T}=1
. Then
h^{1}=d
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S})
has moduli dimension
d
.

(c)

Assume that
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
or
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
\#\mathcal{T}=2
and
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\neq 4\mathrm{A}_{2}
. Then
h^{1}=1=d+1
.

Remark 6.18
.

The only case missing from Proposition
6.17
(b)
,
(c)
is
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
,
\mathcal{S}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}+[4,2]
. In this case, by Lemma
6.15
(b)
the set
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(4\mathrm{A}_{2}+[4,2])
is represented by an almost faithful family of dimension
d=1
, so
h^{1}\in\{1,2\}
by Proposition
6.17
(a)
. We do not know which of these two possibilities holds.

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be as in Proposition
6.17
(b)
. Then
h^{0}=0
and
h^{2}\leqslant d
.

We note that by Lemma
2.11
for any birational morphism
(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
between log smooth surfaces we have
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{1}}(-\log D_{1}))\leqslant h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{2}}(-\log D_{2}))
for
i=0,2
,
so we can replace
(X,D)
by its images.

Assume first that
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
.
Then
\overline{Y}
swaps to a surface
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
,
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
. In the first case, by Lemma
6.3
we have
\#\mathcal{T}=2
, so by Lemma
6.12

\overline{X}
swaps to the surface of type
(\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{5})+[2,2]
, and
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
, where
(Z,D_{Z})
is its minimal log resolution, see Figure
19(c)
. We keep the notation from Example
5.2
(c)
. Let
V
be the proper transform of the line joining
q
with
p_{2}
, and let
\widecheck{D}_{Z}
be the sum of
D_{Z}+V
and all
(-1)
-curves in Figure
19(c)
. We have an inner morphism
(Z,\widecheck{D}_{Z})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},B)
, where
B
is a sum of three vertical and three horizontal lines, so
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-\log B))=0
, as needed.

In the remaining cases we argue as in the proof of Lemma
6.14
. By Lemma
6.12
we can assume that
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\in\{3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4},7\mathrm{A}_{1},8\mathrm{A}_{1}\}
. Consider a pullback to
X
of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration shown in Figures
16(g)
,
20(c)
(for
\nu=3
) or
[
PP24
, Figure 6(b)]
, and let
V
be the proper transform of the fiber passing through the cusp of
\sigma(T)
.

Assume
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Z,D_{Z}+H)
be the contraction of
V
, where
H=[1]
is the image of
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
. Then
h^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log(D_{Z}+H)))=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{Z}(-\log D_{Z}))
by Lemma
2.14
(c)
. Contracting the image of
D_{T}-T
we get a minimal log resolution of a surface of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, so
h^{0}=0
and
h^{2}=1
by Lemma
5.16
(d)
.

Assume
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=7\mathrm{A}_{1}
or
8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, so
d=2
or
3
, respectively. Let
B=D-(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{hor}}
,
\widetilde{h}^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log B))
. Since
D-B
is a sum of
d-1

(-2)
-curves, by Lemma
2.14
(a)
we have
h^{0}=\widetilde{h}^{0}
and
h^{2}\leqslant\widetilde{h}^{2}+d-1
. The contraction of
V+D_{T}-T
and, in case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=8\mathrm{A}_{1}
, of a vertical
(-1)
-curve disjoint from
(D_{Y})_{\mathrm{vert}}
, cf. Figure
23(a)
, maps
(X,B)
onto a minimal log resolution of the surface of type
7\mathrm{A}_{1}
, so
\widetilde{h}^{0}=0
,
\widetilde{h}^{2}=1
by Lemma
5.16
(d)
, as needed.

Assume now that
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, so
\#\mathcal{T}=1
by assumption
6.17
(b)
. In case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}
let
H
be a component of
D_{Y}
and let
B=D-H
; in other cases put
B=D
. By Lemma
2.14
(a)
it is enough to prove
h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log B))=0
for
i=0,2
. We have a birational morphism
(X,B)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, where
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is the minimal log resolution of a surface
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
blown up in a point away from the boundary. Indeed, if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\neq 4\mathrm{A}_{2}
then
\overline{Y}
swaps to
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
and we get the required morphism as in Lemma
6.12
. In turn, if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}
then, since
\overline{Y}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary, see
[
PP24
, Example 7.1]
, there is a
(-1)
-curve
L^{\prime}
meeting
D_{Y}
only in
H\cap(D_{Y}-H)
, so the required morphism is the contraction of
L^{\prime}
and the component of
D_{Y}-H
meeting it.

It remains to prove that
\widetilde{h}^{i}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X^{\prime}}(-\log D^{\prime}))=0
for
i=0,2
. Example
5.2
(a)
shows that there is an inner morphism
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{1},\Delta^{\prime})
, where
\Delta^{\prime}
is the sum of the negative section
\Xi_{1}
and three sections disjoint from
\Xi_{1}
meeting normally, so
\widetilde{h}^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}(-\log\Delta^{\prime}))
. The contraction of
\Xi_{1}
maps
\Delta^{\prime}
to a triangle
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
, so by Lemma
2.11
we have
\widetilde{h}^{i}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-\log\Delta))=0
for
i>0
and
\widetilde{h}^{0}=h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-\log\Delta))-2=0
, as needed.

∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be as in Proposition
6.17
(c)
. If
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
assume further that
\#\mathcal{T}=1
. Then
h^{2}\geqslant 1
.

Assume
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, so
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\neq 4\mathrm{A}_{2}
and
\#\mathcal{T}=2
. By Lemma
6.12
we can assume that

\mathcal{S}_{Y}=3\mathrm{A}_{2}
, so
\mathcal{S}=4\mathrm{A}_{2}
. Denote the proper transforms on
X
of the curves in Figure
19(a)
by the same letters. Let
\widecheck{D}=D+A_{0}+A_{1}+L_{1}
, so
h^{2}=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log\widecheck{D}))
. Let
G=D_{T}-T
. We have
2K_{X}+\widecheck{D}=G+L
, so by Lemma
2.14
(f)
,
h^{2}=h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log\widecheck{D})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(G+L))
. We will construct a nonzero global section of the latter sheaf.

Let
\tau\colon(X,\widecheck{D})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},{p})
be the composition of
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with the morphism from Example
5.2
(a)
. Then
{p}
is a sum of the triangle
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{c}
and a cuspidal cubic
{q}
tangent to
{l}_{2}
and
{c}
at their common points with
{l}_{1}
, with multiplicity
3
. Since
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
, the cubic
{q}
is tangent with multiplicity
3
to every line passing through
{l}_{2}\cap{c}
, see cf.
[
PP24
, Lemma 5.5(b)]
. Moreover, every vector field on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, which is tangent to those lines, is tangent to
{q}
, too. Let
\xi
be such a vector field which vanishes along
{l}_{1}
: explicitly, choosing coordinates such that
{q}=\{y^{3}=x^{2}z\}
,
{c}=\{z=0\}
,
{l}_{1}=\{z=y\}
,
{l}_{2}=\{z=x\}
we can take
\xi=(y-z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
. Write
\tau=\eta\circ\psi
, where
\eta
is a composition of two blowups over the cusp of
{q}
, so
E\coloneqq\operatorname{Exc}\eta=\psi_{*}(G+L)
. A direct computation shows that
\xi
lifts to a section of
\mathcal{T}_{\psi(X)}(-\log\psi_{*}\widecheck{D})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(E)
. Since the morphism
\psi
is inner and is an isomorphism near
G+L
,
\xi
further lifts to a global section of
\mathcal{T}_{X}(-\log\widecheck{D})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(G+L)
, as needed.

Assume now that
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
, so
\mathcal{S}=2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[3]
. We proceed as in the previous case, this time using a pencil of lines coming from the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration constructed in the proof of Lemma
6.14
, see Figure
23(b)
.

Let
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
be the minimal log resolution of
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
. We denote the proper transforms on
\widetilde{X}
of the curves on
X
and those in Figure
19(e)
by the same letters, so
\widetilde{D}=D+V+W+H
, where
H=[1]
meets
T=[5]
,
V=[3]
,
W=[2]
. The
(-1)
-curve
A_{1}
meets
D
in
T
, in
C
and in a tip of a connected component
G_{T}
of
D_{Y}
. The chain
T+A_{1}+G_{T}=[5,1,(2)_{4}]
supports a fiber of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
f\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We have seen in the proof of Lemma
6.14
(or in the proof of
[
PP24
, Corollary 1.4]
), that the horizontal part of
\widetilde{D}
consists of a
1
-section
H
and a
5
-section
C
, and the degenerate fibers are supported on:
T+A_{1}+G_{T}=[5,1,(2)_{4}]
,
V+A_{V}+G_{V}=[3,1,2,2]
and
W+A_{W}+G_{W}=[2,1,2]
for some
(-1)
-curves
A_{V},A_{W}
. The Hurwitz formula shows that
f|_{C}\colon C\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is totally ramified.
Let
\tau\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of
H+(A_{1}+G_{T})+(A_{V}+G_{V})+(A_{W}+G_{W})
. It maps fibers to concurrent lines, and
C
to a quintic tangent to each of those lines with multiplicity
5
. We can choose coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
so that
\tau(T)=\{x=0\}
,
\tau(V)=\{x=z\}
,
\tau(W)=\{z=0\}
,
\tau(C)=\{y^{5}=xz^{3}(2z-x)\}
. Let
\xi
be a vector field on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
which is tangent to those lines and vanishes along
\tau(W)
, say
\xi=z\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
. It is tangent to
\tau(C)
, too, and a direct computation shows that it lifts to a global section of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{X}}(-\log D^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}(H+A_{1}+G_{T}+A_{V}+G_{V}-A_{W})\cong\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{X}}(-\log D^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}(2K_{\widetilde{X}}+D^{\prime})
, where
D^{\prime}=D+A_{1}
. Hence
h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{X}}(-\log D^{\prime}))\neq 0
by Lemma
2.14
(f)
. We have
h^{2}=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{X}}(-\log D^{\prime}))
by Lemmas
2.14
(c)
, and
2.11
, so
h^{2}\neq 0
, as needed.

∎

For
j\in\{1,2,3\}
let
\mathcal{S}_{j}=\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}_{j}
be a type as in
6.17
with
\#\mathcal{T}_{j}=j
, i.e.
\mathcal{T}_{1}=[s-5]
,
\mathcal{T}_{2}=[2,s-4]
,
\mathcal{T}_{3}=[s-3]+[3]+[2]
, cf. Lemma
6.2
. For
j\in\{1,2,3\}
let
(X_{j},D_{j})
be the minimal log resolution of
\overline{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{j})
such that the morphism
(X_{3},D_{3})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
factors through
(X_{3},D_{3}+L_{3})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2}+L_{2})\longrightarrow(X_{1},D_{1}+L_{1})
, where
L_{j}
is the elliptic tie. Let
h^{i}_{j}\coloneqq h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{j}}(-\log D_{j}))
, let
T_{j}
be the proper transform of
\overline{T}
on
X_{j}
, and for
j\in\{2,3\}
let
C_{j}
for be the
(-2)
-curve in
D_{j}
meeting
L_{j}
.

By Lemma
6.14
we have
(h^{0}_{3},h^{1}_{3},h^{2}_{3})=(0,d,d+1)
. Moreover, we have
h^{0}_{1}=0
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{S}_{Y}\neq 2\mathrm{A}_{4}
then
h^{0}_{1}=0
by Lemma
6.19
. In case
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=2\mathrm{A}_{4}
we have
h^{0}_{1}\leqslant h^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{1}}(-\log(D_{1}-T_{1})))
by Lemma
2.14
(a)
, and we see from Example
5.3
that there is a birational morphism
X_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
mapping
D_{1}-T_{1}
to the sum of two vertical lines, one horizontal line and a diagonal, so
h^{0}_{1}=0
by Lemmas
2.11
and
2.14
(d)(d.iii)
, as claimed.

The contraction of
L_{3}
is an inner blowup
(X_{3},D_{3}-C_{3})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
, so
h^{i}_{2}=h^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{3}}(-\log D_{3}-C_{3}))
. By Lemmas
6.14
and
2.14
(a)
we get
h^{0}_{2}=0
and
h^{1}_{2}=h^{2}_{2}\in\{d,d+1\}
. Moreover, if
h^{1}_{2}=d
then the natural map between infinitesimal deformations of
(X_{2},D_{2})
and
(X_{2},D_{2}-C_{2})
is an isomorphism, so the almost faithful family representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{2})
obtained by blowing down one representing
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{3})
is in fact almost universal.

In turn, the contraction of
L_{2}
is an outer blowup
(X_{2},D_{2}-C_{2})\longrightarrow(X_{1},D_{1})
, so since
h^{0}_{1}=0
, Lemma
2.11
implies that
h^{1}_{1}=h^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{2}}(-\log D_{2}-C_{2}))-1
and
h^{2}_{1}=h^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{X_{2}}(-\log D_{2}-C_{2}))
. By Lemma
2.14
(a)
we get
h^{2}_{1}=h^{1}_{1}
and
h^{j}_{1}\leqslant h^{j}_{2}
for
j=1,2
. On the other hand, by Lemma
6.15
(b)
the class
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{1})
is represented by an almost faithful family of dimension
d
, which in particular is non-trivial along any germ of a curve, so
h^{1}_{1}\geqslant d
; and as before we see that if
h^{1}_{1}=d
then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{1})
the above almost faithful family is almost universal.

This proves
6.17
(a)
and shows that if
h^{2}_{j}=d
for all
\overline{X}_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{j})
then
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{deb}}(\mathcal{S}_{j})
has moduli dimension
d
. By Lemma
6.19
under the assumptions of
6.17
(b)
we have
h^{2}_{j}\leqslant d
, so by
6.17
(a)

h^{1}_{j}=d
and thus
6.17
(b)
holds. Under the assumptions of Lemma
6.20
we have
h^{2}_{j}\geqslant 1=d+1
, so
h^{1}_{j}=h^{2}_{j}=1
and
6.17
(c)
holds.

∎

Part
E
(a)
is proved in Lemma
6.11
(a)
. Part
E
(b)
follows from Lemma
6.3
. The list of singularity types in
E
(c)
can be read off from Table
15
(part
\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})
) and Lemma
6.2
(part
\mathcal{T}
). Part
E
(d)
is proved in Lemma
6.15
. Part
E
(e)
follows from Lemma
6.15
and Proposition
6.17
. Part
E
(f)
is proved in Proposition
6.4
. Eventually, Lemma
6.3
gives a list of exceptions where
\#\mathcal{S}(\overline{Y})\geqslant 6
but
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
.

∎

6E.
Primitive models

As a last result of this section, we prove an analogue of Theorem
A
(1)
announced in Remark
1.13
. That is, we show that del Pezzo surfaces of rank one admitting descendants with elliptic boundary swap to particularly simple primitive surfaces, which are either listed in Theorem
A
(1)
or in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.21

(Primitivity criterion)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a rational, non-canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, having a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be the morphism from Definition
1.11
,
and let
\eta\colon(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be the minimal log resolution. Then
\overline{X}
is primitive if the following hold.

(a)

The birational map
\varphi^{-1}\circ\eta\colon\widetilde{Y}\dashrightarrow X
is regular, i.e.
\#\mathcal{T}=1
if
\overline{T}
is nodal and
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 3
if
\overline{T}
is cuspidal.

(b)

The canonical surface
\overline{Y}
is primitive.

(c)

Either
0pt(\overline{Y})\geqslant 2
, or
\overline{X}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}+[3,2]
and
\overline{T}
is cuspidal.

As a consequence,
\overline{X}
is primitive if and only if its singularity type is one of the following:

(1)

2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[3]
;

(2)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}\not\in\{2,3\}
:
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}+[3]
;

(3)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
:
2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[4,2]
,
2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[5]+[3]+[2]
;

(4)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=3
:

3\mathrm{A}_{2}+[3]+[3]+[2]
,

4\mathrm{A}_{2}+[3]
,
4\mathrm{A}_{2}+[4,2]
,
4\mathrm{A}_{2}+[5]+[3]+[2]
;

(5)

If
\operatorname{char}{k}=2
:

3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}+[3,2]
,

7\mathrm{A}_{1}+[3,2]
,
7\mathrm{A}_{1}+[4]+[3]+[2]
,

8\mathrm{A}_{1}+[3]
,
8\mathrm{A}_{1}+[4,2]
,
8\mathrm{A}_{1}+[5]+[3]+[2]
.

It is enough to prove that
\overline{X}
satisfying conditions
(a)
–
(c)
is primitive; the subsequent list follows from the classification
in Table
15
. Suppose the contrary, and let
A\subseteq X
be a
(-1)
-curve as in Definition
1.3
(a)
.

As in Notation
6.1
let
L
be the elliptic tie, write
D=D_{T}+D_{Y}
where
D_{T}=\varphi^{*}\overline{T}_{\mathrm{red}}-L
and
D_{Y}=\varphi^{-1}(\operatorname{Sing}\overline{Y})
, let
T=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}
and let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be the morphism onto minimal resolution
Y
of the canonical surface
\overline{Y}
. Note that the Picard rank of
\sigma
equals
\#\mathcal{T}
.

By condition
(a)
we have
\#\mathcal{T}=1
if
\overline{T}
is nodal and
\#\mathcal{T}\leqslant 3
if
\overline{T}
is cuspidal. In particular,
-2\geqslant T^{2}\geqslant\overline{T}^{2}-6=3-\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}
by formula (
20
), so
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 5
. Pulling back the linear equivalence
K_{\overline{Y}}+\overline{T}=0
we get
-K_{X}=D_{T}+\varepsilon L
, where
\varepsilon=1
if
\#\mathcal{T}\in\{1,2\}
and
\varepsilon=2
if
\#\mathcal{T}=3
. Adjunction formula gives

(22)

1=A\cdot(-K_{X})=A\cdot D_{T}+\varepsilon\,A\cdot L.

Consider the case
A\cdot L>0
. Then formula (
22
) gives
A\cdot L=1
,
A\cdot D_{T}=0
and
\varepsilon=1
, i.e.
\#\mathcal{T}\in\{1,2\}
. Suppose
\#\mathcal{T}=1
. Then
\sigma(A)
is a
0
-curve. Since
A
meets
D_{Y}
once, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
induced by
|\sigma(A)|
has height one with respect to
\sigma_{*}D_{Y}
, hence
0pt(\overline{Y})\leqslant 1
. Since
\#\mathcal{T}=1
, this is a contradiction with
(c)
.

Thus
\#\mathcal{T}=2
. Since
\overline{X}
is not canonical, we have
T^{2}\leqslant-3
, so
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 7
by formula (
20
). Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow Z
be the contraction of
L
, and let
D_{Z}=\psi_{*}(D_{Y}+T)
.Then
(Z,D_{Z})
is a minimal log resolution of a normal surface
\overline{Z}
of rank one, again having
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
as a descendant with elliptic boundary. By Lemma
1.12
(d)
,
\overline{Z}
is del Pezzo. The curve
\psi(A)
is a
0
-curve meeting
D_{Z}
once in
\psi_{*}D_{Y}
and once in
\psi_{*}T
, so
0pt(\overline{Z})\leqslant 2
. Thus
\overline{Z}
is one of the exceptional surfaces in Lemma
6.3
. Since
\overline{Y}
is primitive by assumption
(b)
, its singularity type is listed in Remark
5.7
, see Table
15
. We conclude that
\overline{Z}
is of type
2\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
or
4\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Thus
0pt(\overline{Z})=2
, see Table
15
. Moreover, we have constructed a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
\overline{Z}
such that
(D_{Z})_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two
1
-sections, so
0pt(\overline{Z})=2
. This is a contradiction with Lemma
5.10
; cf. Theorem
A
(3.c)
.

Consider the case
A\cdot L=0
, so
A\cdot D_{T}=1
by formula (
22
). Then
\varphi(A)
is a smooth rational curve on
\varphi(Y)
. Since
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 5>1
,
[
PP24
, Lemma 2.11(a)]
implies that
\varphi(A)\not\subseteq\overline{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Thus
A
meets
D_{Y}
. It follows that the component of
D_{T}
meeting
A
is not a
(-2)
-curve. If
A
meets
T
then the image of
A
on
Y
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
\sigma_{*}D_{Y}
only once, contrary to
(b)
. Therefore,
\#\mathcal{T}=3
and
A
meets the
(-3)
-curve in
D_{T}
, so
A_{Y}
is a
0
-curve. It follows that
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, and since
\#\mathcal{T}=3
we get a contradiction with condition
(c)
.

∎

Proposition 6.22

(Primitive models)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one having a descendant with elliptic boundary. Then the following hold.

(a)

The surface
\overline{X}
swaps to a primitive del Pezzo surface
\overline{Z}
of rank one such that one of the following holds.

(i)

\overline{Z}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary, hence is one of the surfaces in Lemma
6.21
above,

(ii)

\overline{Z}
is canonical,

(iii)

\operatorname{char}{k}=2
and
\overline{Z}
is a surface of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}+[4]
from Example
5.5
, case
\nu=4
.

(b)

Assume
\overline{X}
is log canonical. If
\overline{X}
is non-primitive then it admits an elementary swap to a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one which either has a descendant with elliptic boundary, or is as in
(a)(a.ii)
–
(a)(a.iii)
.

(c)

Assume that
\overline{X}
is not canonical, and does not satisfy condition
6.21
(a)
. Then the contraction of the
(-1)
-curve
L\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
is an elementary swap. In particular, if
\overline{X}
is log canonical then it admits an elementary vertical swap to a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one with the same descendant as
\overline{X}
.

Clearly, any canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one swaps to a primitive canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one, so we can assume that
\overline{X}
is not canonical. Assume that
\overline{X}
does not satisfy the condition
6.21
(a)
. Then the elliptic tie
L
satisfies
L\cdot D\leqslant 2
and
L\cdot C=1
for some
(-2)
-curve
C\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}\varphi
. If
L
meets a
(-2)
-curve in
D-C
then
D_{T}
consists of
(-2)
-curves, so
\overline{X}
is canonical, contrary to our assumption. Thus we can assume that
L
does not meet any
(-2)
-curve in
D-C
. The contraction of
L
is an elementary swap, as claimed in
(c)
.

Contracting
L
and repeating this process we eventually get a minimal log resolution of a del Pezzo surface of rank one, with the same descendant as
\overline{X}
, satisfying
6.21
(a)
. Note that if
\overline{X}
is log canonical then by Lemma
2.9
(d)
all the intermediate log surfaces are minimal log resolutions of log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one, with the same descendant, as claimed in
(b)
and
(c)
. In general, we only claim that so is the last one: this is clear as the eventual
D_{T}
is a chain
[s-5]
,
[s-4,2]
or a disjoint sum
[s-3]+[3]+[2]
.

Therefore, we can assume that
\overline{X}
satisfies
6.21
(a)
. By Lemma
6.12
we can assume that either
\overline{Y}
is primitive, so
6.21
(b)
holds, or
T=[2]
, which since
\overline{X}
is not canonical implies that
\overline{T}
is cuspidal and
D_{T}=[2]+[2]+[3]
.

Assume that
\overline{X}
it does not satisfy
6.21
(c)
. Then
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
. Let
A_{Y}
be the fiber of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
Y
passing through the singular point of
T_{Y}
. By adjunction,
A_{Y}\cdot T_{Y}=A_{Y}\cdot(-K_{Y})=2
. It follows that
A\coloneqq\sigma^{-1}_{*}A_{Y}
satisfies
A\cdot(D+L)=2
, meets a
(-2)
-curve in
D_{Y}
and the first exceptional curve of
\sigma
, call it
B
. Condition
6.21
(a)
implies that
-2\geqslant T^{2}\geqslant\overline{T}^{2}-6=3-\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}
by formula (
20
), so
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 5
. The equality holds if and only if
D_{T}=[2]+[2]+[3]
. In this case
B=[3]
, and contracting
A
we get a swap to a canonical surface, as needed. Assume
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 6
, so
D_{T}\neq[2]+[2]+[3]
, and therefore by assumption
6.21
(b)
holds, i.e.
\overline{Y}
is primitive. Since
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
and
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 6
, Remark
4.5
shows that
\overline{Y}
is of type
3\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{4}
. Then
D_{T}=[2]
,
[2,3]
or
[2]+[3]+[4]
. The first two cases are excluded since
\overline{X}
is not canonical and does not satisfy
6.21
(c)
. Consider the latter case. Then
B=[3]
and
T=[4]
. Taking the witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration as in Figure
16(g)
, we see that
A
meets the branching
(-2)
-curve in
D_{Y}
. Contracting
A
we see that
\overline{X}
swaps to the surface of type
8\mathrm{A}_{1}+[4]
from Example
5.5
, as needed.

As a consequence, we can assume that
6.21
(c)
holds. If
D_{T}=[2]+[2]+[3]
then by formula (
20
) we have
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}=9-\overline{T}^{2}=3-T^{2}=5
, so looking at Table
15
we see that
0pt(\overline{Y})=1
, contrary to
6.21
(c)
. Thus
\overline{X}
satisfies all three conditions
6.21
(a)
–
(c)
, which ends the proof.

∎

Eventually, we note the following partial results towards the statement discussed in Remark
1.14
.

Remark 6.23
.

Let
\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}
be non-isomorphic del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and the same log canonical singularity type
\mathcal{S}
. Assume that for each
i=1,2
, the surface
\overline{X}_{i}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary or satisfies
0pt(\overline{X}_{i})\leqslant 2
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
0pt(\overline{X}_{1})=0pt(\overline{X}_{2})
.

(b)

Assume that
\overline{X}_{1}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary. Then the same holds for
\overline{X}_{2}
. Moreover, for
i=1,2
the descendant of
\overline{X}_{i}
with elliptic boundary, call it
(\overline{Y}_{i},\overline{T}_{i})
, is unique up to an isomorphism, and the surfaces
\overline{Y}_{1}
,
\overline{Y}_{2}
have the same singularity type.

(c)

Nonetheless, there exist
\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}
as in
(b)
such that
\overline{Y}_{1}\not\cong\overline{Y}_{2}
and
\overline{T}_{1}\not\cong\overline{T}_{2}
.

Assume that
0pt(\overline{X}_{1})\geqslant 3
, so
\overline{X}_{1}
has a descendant
(\overline{Y}_{1},\overline{T}_{1})
with elliptic boundary. Hence
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
is the singularity type of
\overline{Y}_{1}
. By Theorem
E
(b)
we have
\#\mathcal{S}_{Y}\geqslant 6
, and
\mathcal{S}
is not one of the types listed in Lemma
6.5
. We check directly that such
\mathcal{S}
is not listed in Tables
9
–
12
, so
0pt(\overline{X}_{2})\geqslant 3
. Hence, by assumption,
\overline{X}_{2}
has a descendant
(\overline{Y}_{2},\overline{T}_{2})
with elliptic boundary. By Theorem
E
(a)
, the descendants
(\overline{Y}_{1},\overline{T}_{1})
and
(\overline{Y}_{2},\overline{T}_{2})
are unique, and have the same singularity type, as claimed in
(b)
. In particular, we see from Table
15
that
0pt(\overline{Y}_{1})=0pt(\overline{Y}_{2})
, so
0pt(\overline{X}_{1})=0pt(\overline{X}_{2})
by Theorem
E
(f)
, as claimed in
(a)
.

We may now assume that
0pt(\overline{X}_{i})\leqslant 2
for
i=1,2
. Since
\#\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})\geqslant 2
, Proposition
C
shows that the singularity type
\mathcal{S}
is listed in Table
13
. Theorem
A
implies that all surfaces in
\mathcal{P}_{0pt\leqslant 2}(\mathcal{S})
are of the same height (listed in Table
13
, too), so
0pt(\overline{X}_{1})=0pt(\overline{X}_{2})
. This proves
(a)
.

For the proof of
(b)
, assume that
\overline{X}_{1}
has a descendant with elliptic boundary. To describe the singularity type
\mathcal{S}
, we need a version of Lemma
6.2
without the assumption
s\geqslant 6
. Here the same argument yields the same list, with appropriate conditions ensuring that all chains and forks which appear on the list are admissible or log canonical (so, for instance, in
6.2
(7)
–
(9)
we allow
s=4,5
, with weaker restrictions on
V
).

Comparing this list of singularity types
\mathcal{S}
with Table
13
we conclude that the decomposition
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{Y}+\mathcal{T}
as in Notation
6.1
is unique,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\mathrm{D}_{5},\mathrm{E}_{6},\mathrm{E}_{7},\mathrm{E}_{8}
, and
\overline{X}_{i}

is as in one of the following:

(i)

Lemma

4.4
(23)
with
b=2
,
r=s-4
,
T=[2,2]
, so
\mathcal{T}=[s-4]^{\langle 1,1\rangle}

(ii)

Lemma
5.10
(9)
with
m=s-4
, so
\mathcal{T}=[s-4^{\langle 1\rangle},2^{\langle 1\rangle}]

(iii)

Lemma

5.10
(29)
with
n=2
,
k=s-3
,
T_{2}=[2,2]
,
T_{3}=[(2)_{s+2}]
, so
\mathcal{T}=\langle m^{\langle 1\rangle},[s-3],[3],[2]\rangle+[(2)_{m-2}]^{\langle 1\rangle}
.

To prove
(b)
, it is enough to find an elliptic tie, that is, a
(-1)
-curve
L
on a minimal log resolution
(X,D)
of
\overline{X}_{i}
, meeting
D
in components decorated above by
⟨1⟩
, cf. Lemma
6.2
. We note that such
L
, if exists, is unique. Indeed, if
L^{\prime}
is another elliptic tie then the weighted graphs of
D+L
and
D+L^{\prime}
are the same, and since
K_{X}
and the components of
D
generate
\operatorname{NS}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X)
, we get
L\equiv L^{\prime}
, hence
L=L^{\prime}
, because
L^{2}<0
. It follows that the descendant
(\overline{Y}_{i},\overline{T}_{i})
of
\overline{X}_{i}
is unique, too. We now find
L
in each of the above cases. We denote by
A_{1},A_{2},\dots
the
(-1)
-curves on
X
corresponding to
{}^{|1|},^{|2|},\dots
in the lemmas cited above.

(i)
Let
\tau\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the contraction of the subchain
[1,(2)_{r+3}]
of
D+A_{1}
and
[1,2]
of
D+A_{2}
. Then
\tau_{*}D
is a sum of a line
{l}
and a conic
{c}
tangent to
{l}
at the point
\tau(A_{2})
. The required elliptic tie is the proper transform of a line
{l}^{\prime}\neq{l}
passing through
\tau(A_{1})
and its infinitely near point on the image of
A_{2}
.

(ii)
We have a vertical swap
\tau\colon(X_{2},D_{2})\rightsquigarrow(Y,D_{Y})
, where
(Y,D_{Y})
is as in Example
5.2
(a)
. Composing
\tau
with the morphism
\varphi
from
5.2
(a)
, we get a morphism
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
mapping
D
to a triangle
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}+{c}
. Put
L_{i}=\psi^{-1}_{*}{l}_{i}
,
C=\psi^{-1}_{*}{c}
. Then
L_{1}
is the branching component of
D
, and
C+L_{2}=[s-4,2]
is a connected component of
D
. Let
p=\psi(A_{1})\in{l}_{1}\setminus({l}_{2}\cup{c})
, and let
p^{\prime}
be the unique base point of
\psi^{-1}
which is infinitely near to
p
. Let
{l}
be the line passing through
p
and
p^{\prime}
. Then
\psi^{-1}_{*}{l}

is the required elliptic tie.

We note that for
s=6
, the pair of surfaces
\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}
of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{E}_{6}
was constructed in Example
5.6
(a)
.

(iii)
Here the required elliptic tie is
A_{2}
. In fact, in this case we get an almost universal family of del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and type
\mathcal{S}
, all with the same descendant of type
\mathrm{E}_{s}
and cuspidal boundary.

Eventually, we show how
(i)
and
(ii)
provide examples proving
(c)
. The elliptic boundary in
(i)
(respectively, in
(ii)
) is cuspidal if
{l}^{\prime}
is tangent to
{c}
(respectively, if
{l}
passes through
{l}_{1}\cap{c}
). This gives examples with
\overline{T}_{1}\not\cong\overline{T}_{2}
. In case
s=8
, we get
\overline{Y}_{1}\not\cong\overline{Y}_{2}
, too. Indeed, suppose
\overline{Y}
is a canonical surface of type
\mathrm{E}_{8}
whose smooth locus contains curves
\overline{T}_{i}
with
p_{a}(\overline{T}_{i})=1
,
i=1,2
, such that
\overline{T}_{1}
is cuspidal and
\overline{T}_{2}
is nodal. Let
\eta\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow\overline{Y}
be the minimal resolution of
\overline{Y}
, followed by the contraction of the
(-1)
-curve from Figure
16(l)
and a chain
[(2)_{7}]
meeting it. Then
(\eta^{*}\overline{T}_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}={l}+{q}_{i}
, where
{q}_{i}\cong\overline{T}_{i}
is a cubic,
{l}
is a line tangent to
{q}_{i}
with multiplicity
3
at an inflection point
p\in{q}_{i}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and
({q}_{1}\cdot{q}_{2})_{p}\geqslant 8
. If the equality holds then
{q}_{1}
meets
{q}_{2}
normally at some
q\neq p
, so
q=0
in the group law on
{q}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with
p=0
, which is false. Hence
({q}_{1}\cdot{l})_{p}=9
, so
{q}_{2}
is a member of a pencil of cubics generated by
{q}_{1}
and
{l}
. In some coordinates
[x:y:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
we have
{q}_{2}=\{\lambda y^{3}=x^{3}-yz^{2}\}
for some
\lambda\in{k}^{*}
, and a direct computation shows that
{q}_{2}
is smooth if
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
and cuspidal otherwise, a contradiction.

∎

7.
Comparison with other classification results

We now compare Theorem
A
with some other results partially classifying (closed) del Pezzo surfaces of rank one. For convenience, throughout this section we take
{k}=\mathbb{C}
.

7A.
Keel–McKernan’s approach and Lacini’s list

The main result of
[
KM99
]
asserts that the smooth part of a del Pezzo surface of rank one is dominated by images of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The proof becomes easier if a given del Pezzo surface
\overline{X}
admits a
tiger
, see Definition 1.13 loc. cit. Assume that
\overline{X}
is log terminal, otherwise it has a tiger by definition. Then a
tiger
is an exceptional divisor
T
such that
-(K_{X_{T}}+T)
has nonnegative Kodaira dimension; where
\pi_{T}\colon X_{T}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
is the extraction of
T
, see
[
KM99
, Lemma on p. 9]
. Note that log terminality guarantees that such an extraction exist, see
[
Kol13
, 1.38]
.

The major part of
[
KM99
]
concerns bounding the class of surfaces without tigers. Following this approach, in
[
Lac24
, §6.1]
, Lacini arranged all non-canonical del Pezzo surfaces in 24 series, assuming only
\operatorname{char}{k}\neq 2,3
. Most of these series consist of one or a few well described surfaces
without
tigers.

On the other hand, Remark
7.1
below shows that del Pezzo surfaces of height at most
2
, or admitting descendants with elliptic boundary, do have tigers. Hence from the point of view of
[
KM99
,
Lac24
]
, one does not need a thorough description of their geometry. In fact, various constructions of such surfaces, similar to reversing vertical swaps, are outlined in series LDP 20 – 24 of
[
Lac24
]
. The question of uniqueness of this process and the problem of classifying resulting singularity types are not addressed. In fact, addressing them requires an analysis along the lines of Lemmas
4.7
,
5.10
and
5.15
.

To make this comparison more precise, we note that the definitions in loc. cit. imply that all surfaces in series LDP 20, 22, 24 are of height at most
2
. Series LDP 21 consists of one surface of type
2\mathrm{A}_{4}+[2]+[3]+[5]
, which exists only if
\operatorname{char}{k}=5
and admits a descendant with elliptic boundary. Series LDP 23 is constructed from log surfaces
(S,C)
listed in §5 loc. cit, and unless the latter is as in Proposition 5.1(3)–(5) loc. cit, the resulting del Pezzo surface either has a descendant with elliptic boundary (this holds in cases 5.1(2), 5.10, 5.11(1),(4),(8) and 5.9(5) with
E\subseteq S^{\mathrm{reg}}
) or is of height at most two (this holds in all the remaining cases).

In our forthcoming articles we will see that all surfaces in LDP 1–19, and the ones in LDP 23 obtained from 5.1(3)–(5), are of height
3
or
4
; and in most cases they do not admit descendants with elliptic boundary.

Remark 7.1

(Surfaces in this article have tigers)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a del Pezzo surface of rank one. If
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
, or if
\overline{X}
admits a descendant with elliptic boundary, then
\overline{X}
has a tiger.

We can assume that
\overline{X}
is log terminal. Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a minimal resolution, and let
D=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
.

Assume first that
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
, and let
F
be a fiber of a witnessing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, so
F\cdot D\leqslant 2
. We have
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}+C
, where the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
C\coloneqq\sum_{E}\operatorname{cf}(E)E
is effective since
\pi
is minimal, see Lemma
2.7
(a)
. Since
\overline{X}
is del Pezzo of rank one, we have
K_{\overline{X}}=-\lambda\cdot\pi_{*}F
for some
\lambda>0
, so
-K_{X}=\lambda\pi^{*}\pi_{*}F+C=V+H
for some effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors
V
,
H
such that
V
is vertical and
\operatorname{Supp}H\subseteq D_{\mathrm{hor}}
. We have
F\cdot H_{\mathrm{red}}\leqslant F\cdot D\leqslant 2=-F\cdot K_{X}=F\cdot H
, so some component
H_{0}
of
H
has coefficient at least one in
H
. Therefore
-(K_{X}+H_{0})=V+(H-H_{0})
is effective, so
H_{0}
is a tiger.

Assume now that
\overline{X}
has a descendant
(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
with elliptic boundary. Let
\varphi\colon(X,D+L)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{T})
be the morphism from Definition
1.11
, and let
T_{X}=\varphi^{-1}_{*}\overline{T}\subseteq D
. We have factorizations
\pi=\pi_{T}\circ\rho_{T}
, where
\pi_{T}
is the extraction of
T\coloneqq\rho_{T}(T_{X})
, and
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\rho_{T}
, where
\varphi^{\prime}
is a morphism of relative Picard rank one, i.e. contracts only one curve, call it
T^{\vee}
. We have
\varphi^{\prime}_{*}(K_{X_{T}}+T)=K_{\overline{Y}}+\overline{T}=0
, so
K_{X_{T}}+T=\lambda T^{\vee}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
\lambda T^{\vee}\cdot T=(K_{X_{T}}+T)\cdot T=\pi_{T}^{*}(K_{\overline{X}})\cdot T+(1-\operatorname{cf}(T))T^{2}<0
, so
\lambda<0
. Therefore, the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
-(K_{X_{T}}+T)
is effective, so
T
is a tiger. We remark that
T^{\vee}=\rho_{T}(L)
.

∎

7B.
Miyanishi and Zhang’s approach: curves of minimal anti-canonical degree

Another interesting way to study log terminal del Pezzo surfaces was proposed by Zhang in
[
Zha88
]
, following the ideas of Miyanishi, Sugie and Tsunoda
[
MS80
]
,
[
MT84
]
. The key object in this approach is a curve
C\subseteq X
of minimal anti-canonical degree
-K_{\overline{X}}\cdot\overline{C}
, where
\overline{C}
is the image of
C
on
\overline{X}
. Now, the arguments split in two cases, depending on whether the linear system
|K_{X}+D+C|
is empty or not, the first case being substantially more difficult. This strategy led Gurjar and Zhang
[
GZ94
]
to the proof of finiteness of
\pi_{1}(\overline{X}^{\mathrm{reg}})
, but since there seem to be no natural constraints on the geometry of
(X,D)
in case
|K_{X}+D+C|=\emptyset
, the classification following this approach would be difficult. Nonetheless, it led to a classification of particular classes of del Pezzo surfaces, which we now briefly review.

Under suitable assumptions, one can use the curve
C
to construct an explicit
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
X
, often of low height. This was done by Kojima in
[
Koj03
, Theorem 1.1(2)]
for del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and index two; and in
[
Koj14
, Theorems 2.1(3), 3.1(2), 4.1(3)]
for those with only one quotient singular point.
Using this structure, loc. cit. provides a full classification in these cases, see Tables
5
and
6
. We note that Lemma
4.4
(16)
,
(21)
,
(27)
gives examples of log canonical del Pezzo surfaces with exactly one singular point which is
not
of quotient type, hence does not appear in loc. cit. We also remark that del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and exactly one singular point are of height at most
2
by
[
PP24
, Corollary 1.3(a)]
, cf.
[
Rus81
]
.

Following a similar approach, Zhang
[
Zha89
]
classified log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one with exactly one non-canonical singularity which has multiplicity
3
(called
dP3-surfaces
). Here the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration constructed using
C
has height at most four. A comparison with our classification, see Table
8
, shows that sometimes it is not a witnessing one, and in fact most dP3-surfaces have
0pt(\overline{X})\leqslant 2
. The remaining ones have descendants with elliptic boundary.

A particular instance of the difficult case
|K+D+C|=\emptyset
, namely the one when
C
meets at least two
(-2)
-curves (called case
IIa
), was classified by Kojima and Takahashi in
[
KT12
, Theorem 4.1]
, see Table
7
. Here the linear system of
2C
plus two
(-2)
-curves meeting it supports a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height two. Moreover, in Theorem 1.1 loc. cit. it is shown that a log canonical del Pezzo surface of rank one has at most
5
singularities; surfaces realizing this maximum are classified in
[
Koj13
]
: they are isomorphic to the ones from Lemma
4.4
(25)
.

Recently, Belousov
[
Bel24
]
used this approach to classify log terminal del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and four singular points (recall from Remark
1.7
that for
{k}=\mathbb{C}
this is the maximal possible number of singularities). He arranged the non-canonical ones in seven series (1)–(7), according to their singularity types. The ones in series (1)–(4) are of height at most
2
, and are isomorphic to the ones from Lemmas
5.15
(6)
,
5.15
(5)
,
4.4
(7)
,
(8)
, and
5.15
(3)
, respectively, cf. Tables
9
,
11
. The ones in series (5)–(7) admit descendants with nodal elliptic boundaries, whose underlying surfaces are of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{A}_{1}+2\mathrm{A}_{3}
and
\mathrm{A}_{1}+\mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{A}_{5}
, respectively.

Remark 7.2
.

Del Pezzo surfaces of index two were classified using K3 surfaces by Alexeev and Nikulin
[
AN06
]
, and, using yet another method, by Nakayama
[
Nak07
]
. The latter method works over any algebraically closed field
{k}
, and led to a classification in case of index three, too
[
FY17
]
. In this approach, to each
\overline{X}
one associates a unique
fundamental triplet

[
Nak07
, §4]
, which can be explicitly described. While this correspondence endows each
\overline{X}
with an interesting geometric structure, it does not immediately yield e.g. the singularity type or the height of
\overline{X}
. Therefore, a direct comparison of our classification with exhaustive lists of fundamental triplets from loc. cit. is beyond the scope of this article.

7C.
Hwang’s cascades of toric del Pezzo surfaces

In
[
Hwa24
]
, Hwang arranged all
toric
del Pezzo surfaces in cascades. A
cascade
is a sequence of toric surfaces such that minimal log resolutions of two subsequent elements are related by a toric blowup. The first element of such sequence, called a
basic
surface, is explicitly described.

Note that since
\overline{X}
is toric, either
\overline{X}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
or
(X,D)
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of height at most two, whose fibers are closures of the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-orbits. It is known
[
OW77
]
that
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
consists of two disjoint sections, and each fiber is columnar, i.e. it is a chain meeting
D_{\mathrm{hor}}
in tips. Since
\overline{X}
is toric,
(X,D)
admits two such
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations. In
[
Hwa24
, Theorem 1.3]
, this structure is used to prove that there are at most two degenerate fibers, and as a consequence,
(X,D)
swaps vertically to a cone over a rational normal curve, see Example
4.2
, or to the surface of type
3\mathrm{A}_{2}
from Example
5.2
(a)
. These vertical swaps correspond to toric blowups in loc. cit. It is further inferred in Theorem 1.4 loc. cit, that
\overline{X}
is as in Lemma
4.4
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3)
or
5.10
(1)
. Moreover, by Theorem 1.7 loc. cit,
\overline{X}
is Kähler-Einstein if and only if it is as in
5.10
(1)
.

[
Koj14
]

this article

singularity

8.
Tables

We now summarize all singularity types of log canonical del Pezzo surfaces of rank one and height at most two, see Theorem
A
. We use conventions summarized in Section
2G
, which we now briefly recall. A singularity type is written as
T_{1}+\dots+T_{k}
, where
T_{i}
is a type of a rational chain fork, or bench, which is a minimal resolution graph of the corresponding singular point. Namely,
[a_{1},\dots,a_{k}]
is a rational chain whose subsequent components have self-intersection numbers
-a_{1},\dots,-a_{n}
;
\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
is a rational fork with branching component
[b]
and twigs
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
; and
[\![C]\!]
is a bench with central chain
C
, see Section
2A
. We recall that a fork
F=\langle b;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
is admissible or log canonical if and only if

(
\dagger
)

\delta_{F}\coloneqq\frac{1}{d(T_{1})}+\frac{1}{d(T_{2})}+\frac{1}{d(T_{3})}\geqslant 1\quad\mbox{and if }\delta_{F}=1\mbox{ then $F$ is not a $(-2)$-fork}.

For an integer
k\geqslant 0
, we write
(2)_{k}
for a sequence consisting of the integer
2
repeated
k
times, which corresponds to a chain of
k

(-2)
-curves. For a definition of
“
*
”
see formula (
1
).
